Yesterday's Ghosts

by thetalee

Summary

Evan Buckley has been a firefighter for a full year now. He's moved back in with his brother, his ex-girlfriend is gallavanting through Europe, he's settled and comfortable with where he is. So of course now is the perfect moment for ghosts from his and Daniel's past to come waltzing into their life once again.

Chapter 1

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Now

After a year of doing this job, and even more years watching and listening to Danny, Buck had discovered patterns. Certain things that, no matter what Bobby might want to deny, caused the calls they were sent out on to just be weird. Full moons, the Santa Ana winds, and, currently, the heatwave smothering the city.

Already, on top of power outages, they’d had to deal with: a tour bus flying off the road to land inside a house, with the tourists-cum-victims asking if someone famous lived there; an exploding pressure cooker at a restaurant, which resulted in a fork impaled in someone’s hand and a bottle of hot sauce sticking out of the shoulder of another; and an exploding manhole that resulted in Buck needing to fish the victim’s arm out of a swimming pool. That last one, according to the captain, was caused by frayed cables overheating and smoldering, the build-up of combustibles, and the sudden influx of oxygen into the enclosed space.

Even before the heatwave, there was something going on with Bobby. The man had been oddly upbeat for weeks. It was weird. Not that Buck was complaining, because this was much preferable to finding him passed out drunk, alone. Again.

Then, in addition to all of that, Danny had been babysitting for Athena Grant. Not often, only a couple times over the summer, but that was still weird. Right? Not to mention how he had plans on both Memorial Day and the Fourth of July. Buck needed to do a reappraisal of his brother’s social life, because clearly his initial judgment of its absence had been colored by his odd hours while bouncing between jobs and the time spent partying with Connor and the guys.

But instead of focusing on figuring out any of the above, Buck decided to double down on his vanity and his attention whore tendencies.

“I got another DXA scan,” Buck announced as he joined Hen and Bobby just before shift started, Hen still in her civvies. They’d been talking about something, but he was too excited to feel bad for interrupting. “And guess who dropped another half percent!”

“What?” Hen stared at him blankly.

“A DXA scan,” he repeated, holding up his phone with the scan on it. “It measures your body fat, you can see your percentage in every part of your body.”

“Oh, yeah?” Chimney was eating something as he spoke. “Do they measure the fat in your head?”

“See, that would be funny, but we’re about a week away from submissions being due for the Hot Days, Smoldering Nights: Men of the LAFD wall calendar.” Buck enjoyed saying the full title a bit too much, probably. “And I’m already at my goal weight, so it seems like my head is clearly working perfectly.”

“Do you really need to use that whole title?” Hen asked, all faux-sweet. “You could just say, ‘that idiotic, reductive, sexist calendar that insults the dignity of this organization and furthers the myth that all firefighters are male.’”

“That’s not any less words,” he pointed out, but it did give him a thought. “If there was a Women of the LAFD version, you and Karen would have it hanging up on the wall in your kitchen.”

She stared at him for a moment, lips pursed, before admitting,

“Not in the kitchen.”

Buck thought they were missing out on a major demographic. Did the people organizing the calendar take suggestions?

“It’s for charity,” Bobby reminded them both. “And they say a man is at his sexiest when he reaches fifty.”

“You’re all wrong,” Chimney broke in to say. “See, I think sorority houses all across this great nation are ready for a new Asian sex symbol. It’s our time.”

“I think it’s great that you’re both going up for it,” Buck told them.

“Oh.” Bobby nodded, like he was suddenly understanding something. “Because you don’t think we have a chance.”

“Did I say that? I mean, yes, they are only taking one candidate from each station-”

“Okay,” Chimney interrupted, “that is a beautiful man.”

They all turned to look at the locker room, where he was pointing. And then Buck’s stomach plummeted while his heart stopped dead in his chest. Through the glass walls, they could see a man in the middle of tugging on his LAFD shirt that looked so much like-

No.

No.

“Where’s the lie?” Hen was clearly oblivious to the crisis Buck was currently going through, “And I like girls.”

“Who the hell is that?” he managed to get out, because it couldn’t be him. It couldn’t be.

“Eddie Diaz,” Bobby answered. Fuck. “Graduated top of his class just this week. Guys over at Station Six were dying to have him, but I convinced him to join us.”

“Why?” Buck bit out. The others laughed at him.

“He served multiple tours in Afghanistan. Guy’s got a Silver Star, it’s not like he’s wet behind the ears,” the captain explained. “Come on, I’ll introduce you to him. He likes to be called Eight Pack.”

“Better drop some more body fat there, butch,” Chimney said, slapping Buck on the stomach, before following Bobby and Hen as they went to greet the new recruit. As they went to greet Buck’s ex-boyfriend, fuck.

Buck lingered behind, in absolutely no hurry to say hello. He watched as they each lined up to shake Eddie’s hand, watched as Bobby looked around and noticed he hadn’t followed, watched as the captain pointed him out. He watched as Eddie spotted him, watched as his face slackened in shock.

That old, desperate need to run bloomed into life once more.

Then

It was Evan Buckley’s eighteenth birthday.

It was also Eddie Diaz’s eighteenth birthday.

Like their seventeenth, and their sixteenth, they were celebrating both of them together, at the Diaz residence. It only made sense, because Evan and Eddie were practically inseparable as it was. If someone needed to find one, they only had to look for the other. And Evan was at the Diazes about as often as Eddie was at the tiny apartment Evan shared with Danny.

The front yard was bedecked with decorations, with a giant tent and tables that would be laden with food. Family, cousins and uncles and aunts and people that weren’t even related but were family nonetheless, had come out of the woodwork and were swarming the place. It was noisy and busy and Evan would usually be in the middle of it, charming anyone and everyone he could.

Usually. Because at the moment, he was taking advantage of the oasis of privacy that was Eddie’s room.

“If you-” Eddie interrupted himself with a gasp. He was pressed against his own wall, head thrown back. “If you don’t stop, things are gonna- Mierda, Evan.”

“What?” Evan lifted his head up from where he was mouthing at Eddie’s chest, the other’s shirt rucked up as far as it would go, to smile impishly at him. “You don’t normally complain this much.”

“One of us has to be the reasonable one,” his boyfriend pointed out, grabbing him by the face and pulling him up. Evan let the shirt drop down as they kissed, at least until his hands started wandering and pushing it up again. And then Eddie pushed him away, batting at his hands. “Okay, no, no. My entire family is out there and Mom’s about to send-”

There was soft thumping at his door, followed by a younger girls voice,

“Eddie! Evan!” Sophia was being far louder than she needed to be for her to be heard through the door. “Mama says to come out and help!”

“We’ll- We’ll be right out!” Eddie called back, and then hissed, “Her. Mom sent her, and now we have to go out there with boners, Evan.”

“I can take care of that,” Evan promised with a smile, leaning in close to whisper into Eddie’s ear. “Your abuela in a bikini.”

His boyfriend groaned in disgust and thumped his head against the wall.

“Yep, that did it,” he said, sneaking one more quick kiss before crossing the room and opening his door. Sophia did not look impressed about the time she had to wait, letting out a huff before turning and flitting away in her dress. Eddie turned back slightly, smiled at him, and went down the hall in the direction of the front door.

Evan, though, did not immediately follow them. Instead, he took Eddie’s spot leaning against the wall and mentally prepared himself for the party. Because out there, outside of this room, they weren’t Evan and Eddie, boyfriends since the time Evan took a chance and kissed him. No, outside of this room, they were Evan and Eddie, best friends since the first day of high school.

Just best friends.

There would be no kissing, no hand-holding. Hell, it was a gamble half the time if an arm around the shoulders would spook Eddie too much. But that was okay, because Evan just had to wait a little bit longer. In just a couple days, the two of them would be in his brand new - slightly used - jeep and heading for the first of many destinations on their road trip. They’d be far, far away from family and expectations.

Evan just had to be patient and wait. With how much he loved Eddie, it was the easiest thing to do.

Now

Being in the truck with Eddie, riding to the first call of the day, was intolerable. Intolerable because the guy had decided to act like he’d never seen Buck before, though that was almost a blessing. Intolerable because, through some slight against the Universe Itself, Buck and Eddie ended up sitting across from each other in the cab. Intolerable because, even after so many years, Buck still wanted to kiss the bastard. And also punch him.

“So,” Chimney said, “Silver Star?”

Eddie glanced at him, and then turned back to the window, where he’d been keeping his gaze the entire ride.

“Yeah,” he said. It was clear as day that he was uncomfortable. Buck had always been the more social one between the two of them, but there was something else to it. He very much did not want to talk about his Silver Star, specifically.

A decade ago, Buck would step in and take some of the attention off him. But that was a decade ago, when Buck was Evan and was feeling far more charitable towards the man.

“You save a platoon or something?” Chimney asked, clearly not getting the hint.

“No, nothing like that,” Eddie said, tearing his gaze away from the window to face him again. He somehow managed to do it without once looking at Buck. “Uh, just a convoy.”

“Eddie.” It was Hen’s turn to try and get the new guy to open up, apparently. “You hear about the hot firefighter calendar?”

She shot Buck a bright and smug smile, but he was too busy trying to kill Eddie with his eyes to really notice it.

“Sorry,” Eddie said. “The what?”

“It’s for charity,” she added. 

Up front, Bobby laughed, and that was apparently the moment Buck reached his limit.

“So is your full name Eduardo?” he asked, because seeing the man again brought up all that old hurt and anger that he thought had been buried. And he always got petty when he was hurt and angry.

Eddie turned and actually looked at him for only the second time that day. His mouth was drawn, lips pressed together.

“No.”

“People ever call you Diaz?”

“Not if they want me to respond,” was his ex’s flat response.

“Something’s got to give. We’ve got Cap, Hen, Chimney, Buck,” he said, gesturing to everyone in turn. “We can’t just call you Eddie.”

“Buck, huh,” Eddie said, and then, “Can’t tell if he’s being serious or not.”

“I always like to operate under the assumption that nothing he says is serious,” Chimney told him, amusing everyone else except Buck. 

Or Eddie, apparently, because he didn’t laugh either. And despite spending the entire ride until then avoiding looking at him, now he wouldn’t look away. Buck just rolled his eyes and finally accepted the fact that Eddie wouldn’t spontaneously burst into flames no matter how hard he glared, opting to gaze out the window until they arrived at the tire shop.

The owner of the tire shop was waiting for them out front when the truck and ambulance pulled up, and he immediately started explaining the situation when they jumped out.

“The air nozzle is embedded in his ass cheek,” he told Bobby as they hurried into the garage. “I shut it off, but I was afraid to move him.”

The victim, Hector, was puffed up like a balloon. They got him off the nozzle, laying him on the side, and tried to give him oxygen and morphine. Tried being the keyword, because the air pressure inside his body was preventing anything from getting in. Unfortunately, the pressure was also collapsing his lungs.

“Buck, get a fourteen inch angiocath,” Bobby ordered, and Buck hurried to the med kit to dig it out. “Start decompressing the pleural cavity.”

Buck, with the needle in hand, started undoing the buttons of Hector’s work shirt.

“Need help?” Eddie offered, already reaching. Buck ignored him, getting the shirt out of the way and the needle in place. “I’d go lower.”

Buck paused, looked up to glare at him. Eddie met his gaze only for a moment before averting his eyes.

“No,” Buck said, barely keeping a rein on his temper, because there was absolutely no way the man was trying to tell him how to do his job. “Second intercostal space, midclavicular line.”

“The chest wall is thinner at the fifth intercostal at the anterior axillary line, less likely to injure any vital organs,” Eddie told him, and then turned to Bobby. “I’ve treated guys with collapsed lungs in combat.”

“Do it,” the captain said.

Eddie held out his hand expectantly and Buck, just barely resisting the urge to stab it, gave him the needle. Then he opened the shirt the rest of the way and got to watch Eddie shove the needle into Hector’s chest. There was a hiss of air, and soon the balloon man was deflating.

Then Buck got to watch and listen as Bobby and Chimney praised Eddie for the good call as they loaded back up. Buck, though, just moved past him and climbed back into the cab, ignoring the way he could feel Eddie’s gaze on his back.

The rest of their shift was not any better. Buck ended up settling on avoidance as a tactic. If they weren’t on a call, he made sure they didn’t share space longer than the time it took to finish a meal. If they were in the truck, he kept silent and tried to find his happy place. And when they were on a scene, he was as professional as he could be. More professional than he thought anyone else would be, in his situation.

Finally, freedom came at the end of the shift. Buck dragged his way through a shower, wasting time in the hopes that Eddie would simply disappear. But of course his luck was not that good today, because the man was still there when he finally forced himself back into the locker room. He ignored him, though, as he got changed into his civvies.

At least until everyone else had drifted out and it was just the two of them, at which point Eddie spoke up with a quiet and desperate,

“Evan-”

“No,” Buck snapped, slamming his locker door closed and shrugging the strap of his duffel bag onto his shoulder. “We’re not doing this, Edmundo. ”

“If you could just let me explain. Please.”

“Explain what?” he asked, venom coating every word. “Hey, have you spoken to Shannon yet?”

Eddie looked startled by the question. Buck let out an ugly laugh.

“Yeah,” he said, nodding. “She came by the other month, wanting to catch up. Imagine my delight when it turned out I wasn’t the only person to flee the state just to get away from you.”

Buck knew whatever expression he wore was not a kind one, his smile twisting his face. He stepped into Eddie’s space, a cruel mockery of how close they used to be, and heard the other man’s breath hitch.

“I was never very good at math, you know that,” Buck told him, “but she and I really only had one thing in common, so I think we can figure out the common denominator here.”

“I’m sorry,” Eddie whispered. Buck shook his head and stepped back.

“Save it for your wife,” he told him, and then left him standing there.

Before their next shift, Buck will talk to Bobby. He’ll ask if there was space on one of the other shifts, or maybe he could transfer to another station. Anything that kept him from having to squeeze into the truck beside Eddie Diaz again.

Then

At some point, Evan had lost track of Eddie as Helena sent the two of them scurrying about with orders. It happened, he thought, sometime during one of the trips into the kitchen to bring out the food. Because it might not be Evan’s house, but he’d spent enough time here over the last few years that he was well past being a guest, and not even it being his birthday would spare him from being worked like a pack mule.

With all the food outside, he went off in search of his wayward boyfriend. Except he was, of course, immediately waylaid by Diazes. One of Pepa’s sons - Rafael or Fernando, Evan saw them so rarely he didn’t bother remembering which was which - made the mistake of voicing an opinion about the recent Alice in Wonderland movie where he could hear. And then, after Rafael (or Fernando) managed to flee, Evan was distracted by Adriana and Sophia, Eddie’s sisters, as they demanded his attention. After them, he got caught by Isabel.

“You have gotten tall,” she said, forcing him to stand in front of her. Her fingers pressed into his arm. Four years of playing high school football meant he was no longer the scrawny kid that he was when he left Hershey. Still, some people would never be satisfied. Exhibit A: Isabel Diaz. “But you could use more meat on your bones.”

“You say that every time you see me.”

“It is always true. How is Daniel?”

“He’s good. We did our birthday thing this morning,” Evan said, and then grinned. “He bought me a jeep.”

“Que dios nos agarre confesados,” Isabela whispered, crossing herself.

“Ha. Ha.” Evan rolled his eyes. “You better pray, your favorite nieto will be in the passenger seat right next to me.”

“You hush now,” she said, smacking him on the arm she’d been squeezing. “The others will be jealous if they hear.”

“Everyone knows he’s your favorite,” he said, laughing. “Where is he, anyway?”

Isabel blinked at him, taken aback.

“You do not know?”

Evan shrugged.

“Helena had us helping with everything, and then Sophia and Adriana distracted me,” he told her. “I lost track of him. He’s slippery like that.”

“I think I saw him going back inside with Shannon.”

“Oh.”

Shannon and Evan were friends mostly because of Eddie. Apparently, she and Eddie had been friends before high school, lost contact, and then reconnected their senior year. At which point, despite Eddie still spending all his time with him, Evan started feeling like a third wheel. It was not helped by the way that everyone around them talked about what a nice couple they’d make, nor by how it turned out that Shannon apparently wanted to be with him.

What other explanation was there for the way she suddenly sprung a prom invite on Eddie? 

But things had been weird between Eddie and Shannon after that night, the pair of them awkward and distant, which Evan did not mind. Not after seeing how close they were to one another when he stupidly decided to go to the dance alone, not after how much it hurt to see Eddie with someone else. Call him jealous, call him possessive, he didn’t care. It also didn’t matter, because in just a couple days they’d be on the road, just the two of them.

Evan decided to go into the house and hunt his wayward boyfriend down. It took a bit, because Eddie wasn’t in the entryway, or the living room, or the kitchen. Finally, he found him and Shannon in Eddie’s room, the door only partially closed. Habit, from Helena’s insistence that the door never be closed when Shannon was in the room with them.

(She never had a problem with the door being closed when it was just Eddie and Evan. Joke’s on her.)

He walked to the door, about to push it open the rest of the way, when he caught part of the whispered conversation. The words stopped him dead.

“What are we going to do?” Shannon was asking, sitting on Eddie’s bed. Eddie himself was kneeling in front of her, gripping her hands, with his back to Evan. He didn’t say anything for a moment, and then he took a deep breath and said,

“We should get married.”

Evan’s heart dropped.

“What?” Shannon gasped.

“Marry me,” Eddie repeated.

“But what about- Evan.” Shannon must have noticed the movement at the door as Evan stumbled back, because her head shot up, eyes going wide when she spotted him.

“He’ll-” Eddie finally realized that Shannon wasn’t looking at him. He followed her gaze, spotted Evan as well. Panic marred his features as he stumbled to his feet. “Evan.”

Evan shook his head as he turned and ran down the hall and out the door. He heard Eddie shouting for him, begging to let him explain, but Evan didn’t stop. He felt disconnected as he threw himself into his jeep, backing out of and speeding down the road. 

At some point he started crying, and could not remember when it happened.

Now

Buck threw open the apartment door, already talking as he walked inside.

“Today has been an utter shit show,” he announced to the room at large, mostly Danny. He tossed his bag in the vague direction of the couch, not bothering to look, and kicked off his shoes next to the rack. “I mean, an utter shit show. You would not believe what I have had to deal with.”

“Buck,” Danny said, but Buck kept talking as he went for their fridge.

“Hell, you’ll probably never guess who’s working at the station now. I mean, of all the stations in this city, of all the fire departments in this state, he had to come to mine? Did I piss off a witch or something?”

“Buck.”

“This is an ice cream conversation, Danny,” Buck said, digging into the freezer. “Please tell me we have ice cream. If not, one of us is doing a store run, because I need some Ben & Jerry’s after today. Seriously, you’ll never guess who-”

“Evan.”

Buck recognized that voice. He hadn’t heard it in person in so very long, hadn’t heard it at all in three years, but he recognized it. The freezer door slammed shut with a thud as he spun around, spotting his sister standing in front of the couch with Danny.

“Maddie!” he cried out, sprinting for her. He wrapped his arms around her and lifted her off the ground.

“Let me go!” Maddie was laughing as she smacked at his shoulders, fixing her shirt when he put her back down. “God, you got bigger, but you didn’t grow up, obviously.”

“I’m sorry, it’s just so great to see you,” Buck said, and then, “Wait, where’s Doug?”

“She left him,” Danny answered for her.

“Finally! What took you so long?”

“What can I say?” Maddie shrugged, glanced at Danny over her shoulder. “Mom was right.”

Danny let out a disgusted groan at the words.

“Never, ever say that again,” he told her.

“Do they know?” Buck asked.

“No one knows,” she said. “Please don’t tell them if they call, I don’t want anyone to know I’m here.”

That wasn’t going to be a problem, considering Buck was fairly certain he hadn’t spoken to their parents since high school. He knew Danny still talked to them on occasion, or at least he used to, but Buck always got the impression that those were cordial check-ins at best.

“Who were you talking about?” Danny asked, diverting the topic away from their parents. “Whatever you were complaining about, the person I’d never guess. Who is it?”

“Oh, um.” Buck glanced between him and Maddie, and then he shook his head. “It doesn’t matter, I’ll- I’ll tell you later, promise.”

Maddie and Danny looked at each other, and then at him, and Buck realized that his sister being back in their lives might not actually be a good thing for him.

“No, no,” Maddie said, grabbing his wrist and dragging him to the couch. “I want to know all about what’s been going on in your lives recently. Starting with, why is there a baseball bat by the door?”

“Habit,” Danny explained, sitting down on the other end of the couch. Buck was pinned between his siblings, something he hadn’t experienced for a long, long time. It was nice. “Now c’mon, tell your big brother and sister. What crawled up your butt today?”

Buck sighed.

“Our new probie, it’s…” He debated what to say, avoiding his brother’s gaze the entire time. A part of him, even after so long, recoiled at the idea of violating Eddie’s privacy. Even if it was just telling his big sister the whole sordid tale. So, a half truth it was. “I’m uh. I’m pretty certain everyone likes him more…”

Chapter 2

Now

It took a lot of verbal finagling to get through the conversation without giving anything away. By the end of it, Maddie accepted everything perfectly well, teasing Buck for being threatened, while Danny very clearly did not believe one iota of the bullshit he was spewing. But he mercifully didn’t press Buck on it. Not then, anyways.

Mostly, though, they spent the evening catching up with Maddie, and then arguing over who would be taking the couch. Danny ended up insisting that she take his bed, talking over her until she just gave in. Buck initially thought it was just him being gentlemanly or whatever, until it was time for work and he found his brother awake in the kitchen area. Buck ignored him for the time it took to shower and change, but it wasn’t something he could do forever.

“I made you coffee,” Danny said, setting the cup down on the kitchen table. It was highly suspicious, and Buck was very tempted to just sprint out the door.

“Thanks,” he said, cautiously creeping over. “What is it?”

“I just told you, Buck, it’s-”

“Yeah, yeah, you knew what I meant.”

“You lied,” Danny said, head tilted curiously. “Last night. Maddie didn’t notice, because it’s been years since she had to catch you at it. What actually happened yesterday?”

Buck took a long drink of his coffee as he debated what to say.

“Okay,” he said. “But first you have to promise me you won’t do anything rash.”

His brother’s eyes narrowed.

“Why?”

“Just promise it,” Buck insisted. “And- And actually, I think we should put the bat up somewhere. And take away your keys.”

“Why?” Danny asked again, the word coming out dangerously quiet.

“Promise first.”

The other man scowled, but he finally nodded and said,

“Fine, I promise.”

“Okay.” Buck set the coffee back down and said, “ThenewprobieisEddie.”

It came rushing out, all in one breath. His brother understood it all the same, the scowl disappearing entirely as his face went concerningly blank.

“What.”

“The new probie,” Buck repeated, not looking at him. “It’s Eddie.”

“Eddie Diaz? From El Paso?”

“Yeah…”

“Your ex-boyfriend? The one who didn’t say anything to you before proposing to that chingada perra? That Eddie?”

“Nice to see the Spanish lessons with Tia Alma stuck,” Buck said. “Yes, that Eddie.”

Danny was silent for a very long time, long enough for Buck to finish the coffee and put the mug in the sink for later. Then he nodded to himself and headed for the door.

“Danny?” Buck asked, following after him. “Are you heading out for work- Danny, no, put down the bat!”

Buck ended up taking the bat with him, when he finally managed to wrangle it out of Danny’s hands. He stuffed it into his work bag and hurried out the door, leaving his brother to sulk. Of course, it drew some curious eyes when he walked into the station, since the handle of it was sticking out of the bag.

“Why do you have a baseball bat, Buckaroo?” Chimney asked.

“To prevent a murder,” Buck said as he pulled open his locker. Eddie, doing a poor job of pretending not to listen at the other end of the locker row, was suddenly looking very worried. Buck ignored him, though. “Where’s Bobby?”

“Office,” Hen answered.

“Awesome.”

Buck hurriedly changed into his uniform, slipping out again before there was any risk of Eddie opening his mouth. The man had stayed silent the entire time, not even looking his way, and that was both the best and worst thing ever. Sharing the same space as Eddie again, sharing the same air, left Buck physically aching.

The door to the captain’s office was open, Bobby sitting at his desk while he smiled at his phone. He looked up, startled, when Buck knocked on the door.

“Can I talk to you for a moment?” Buck asked. He waited for Bobby to nod before he stepped inside, closing the door behind him.

“So it’s one of those conversations,” Bobby remarked. “What is it?”

“Is there room on one of the other shifts?” Buck asked. The captain’s brow immediately furrowed as he frowned. “Or- Or if not, maybe another station?”

“Kid, I asked if you wanted to stay with us when you finished your probation.”

“And I did. Then.”

“So what’s changed? Besides Eddie.”

“I just- I can’t work with him, Cap.”

“Why?” the captain demanded, leaning forward. “Give me one good reason, and I’ll transfer one of you right this moment.”

Buck opened his mouth, and then he froze. Because he had a reason, a very good reason. If he told Bobby that Eddie was his ex, that ex he told him about way back on Valentine’s Day while he was getting ready for his date with Abby, then Bobby would absolutely agree to the transfer. But Buck was almost certain that Eddie was still in the closet, and he still loved the man too much to do something so cruel as to drag him out simply because it would be convenient.

He shut his mouth, he shook his head.

“Then request denied, Buck,” Bobby said. “You have to learn how to work with new people, even when you don’t get along. And besides, I recruited him specifically to partner with you. I thought you two would be a good fit.”

The irony of that, of the captain playing matchmaker for him and his ex, was almost enough to make Buck laugh. He didn’t, though. Instead he simply nodded and left the office, ignoring everyone that seemed even remotely curious as to why he was in there.

He wished, very much, that he managed to develop that callus Danny mentioned so long ago. Most of the time he was able to pack it away and move on, accept the lost causes and focus on the ones he could still do something about. But he had never quite managed to pack Eddie Diaz away and move on from him, no matter how far he ran, and instead of a callus there was only scar tissue.

Seeing him again tore it open.

On any other day, Danny constantly texting him to see how he was doing would drive Buck spare. But today, the check-ins were keeping him sane. Especially when Eddie just so happened to hit the station gym at the same time he was doing reps with the barbell. Eddie started punching on the bag, even doing a fancy little kick, and Buck caught himself rolling his eyes.

Ignoring the other man, Buck took the moment to check his phone and saw that Danny had once again texted him. This time, he snapped a selfie to send as proof of life. Which apparently prompted Eddie into ruining their fragile peace by speaking up.

“You’re in the wrong light, man.”

Buck shot him a glare, noticing for the first time that Chimney was there. The paramedic was watching the two of them with an amused little smirk.

“Some of us,” Buck said, turning back to his phone and Danny’s response, “don’t need lighting to look good.”

“What did you mean?” Chimney asked as he hopped off the equipment he was using. “That the light was wrong?”

“The light in this room is flat and blue, makes you look soft,” Eddie explained. Buck desperately wished he could just tune the man out. Maybe next shift he’ll bring in earbuds, drown out the world with loud emo music like the petulant teenager he never really was. “If you want to look lean and your muscles to pop, you need warm side light. Here, I’ll show you, these are the ones I sent in for the calendar.”

“Whoa.”

Buck looked over to see that Eddie had his phone out, with Chimney peering at the screen and looking impressed. He rolled his eyes.

“Pretty sure it’s cheating to submit pictures by a professional photographer,” he said, because he was feeling particularly bitchy at the moment.

“The photographer’s twelve,” Eddie shot back. “My niece took them.”

Buck scrunched his face up in confusion, doing the mental math on that and failing to come up with any way for it to make sense. There was absolutely no way that Sophia and Adriana popped out a twelve year old in the eight years since he last saw them. Eddie clearly noticed and quickly amended his statement with a sheepish,

“I mean, my cousin’s kid. I just call her my niece.”

Ah, Liliana’s daughter, then. She’d been a rambunctious little four year old running around in a princess dress at that awful birthday party. Buck had forgotten, with how long it had been, that anyone younger was a niece or nephew and everyone older was an aunt or uncle.

“Do you think she’d be willing to take my submission pics for me?” Chimney asked, either oblivious to or just ignoring most of that exchange. “I’m told I photograph like an Asian Fabio.”

Like a- What? Buck had switched to the dumbbells and was about to start his reps, only to immediately drop the weight and fix Chimney with an incredulous look. Because, seriously, what?

“Sure she would,” Eddie said. “Yeah.”

“You really shouldn’t get his hopes up like that,” Buck told him, because he had seen Eddie shirtless with the rest of them. He could admit that everyone else’s chances of being chosen had plummeted. Also, he was kinda worried about his ex’s diet and water intake. Not that he was going to say anything, because screw him. “No offense, Chimney.”

“No offense taken, Evan.”

Some offense was clearly taken.

Eddie had clearly reached the limits of his patience. It thrilled Buck a little, knowing he was still so very capable of getting under the man’s skin unlike anyone else. Under it enough, at least, that Eddie clearly forgot where they were as he walked to stand in front of him and demand,

“What’s your problem, man?”

Buck’s eyebrows went up as he got to his feet. Were they really doing this now? Here? In front of Chimney and everyone else?

“You’re my problem,” he told him, because that really should have been obvious. “Your comfort level.”

“My comfort level,” was Eddie’s dull response.

“Yeah.” Because it absolutely bothered Buck that the man was drifting through this shift, acting like he wasn’t bothered by being in Buck’s presence again. Like they had never been anything at all. Maybe it was hypocritical, because Buck was doing his best to act the same, but it still hurt to watch Eddie do it seemingly without effort when Buck himself was spending every second fighting the urge to run out of this station and away from this city. “You’re- You’re not supposed to just walk in like you’ve been here for years. You’re meant to respect your elders.”

The old joke brought a smile to Eddie’s face. It was quick, gone in a blink, but Buck saw it nonetheless.

“You’re not his elder, Buck,” Chimney chimed in from where he was doing push-ups on one of the equipment. Buck just barely managed to keep from rolling his eyes at the man, because obviously.

“Look, I’m not trying to step on anybody’s toes,” Eddie said, and this time Buck did roll his eyes. “I know your girlfriend recently broke up with you and you’re coming to terms with that.”

Oh, the audacity of this man.

“One, that was months ago, and it was mutual. And two,” Buck said, before turning to Chimney. “Are we gossiping about our coworkers’ dating lives now? Because I can start gossiping.”

It was a shot across both their bows, and the message was clearly received. Chimney’s eyes went wide and he dropped back to the floor before hurrying away to another part of the station. When Buck turned back to Eddie, it was to find the other man taking a step away and ducking his head.

“Like I said.” Buck dropped his voice to keep it from carrying. “You’re my problem. I left Texas to get away from you and from Shannon, and now it turns out you’re both here. I have to come in and see your face and act like it’s all perfectly fine.”

“I’m sorry,” the other man whispered.

“Shove it,” he told him.

Eddie nodded and turned away, drifting silently out of the gym. Buck spotted Bobby watching them and shook his head at the captain, before going back to the weights he’d been about to start on.

Then

Eddie reached down and scooped up the little girl running past in her princess dress. She squealed and giggled as he swung her around.

“And where are you running off so fast?” he asked her. “Where’s your mama?”

“Over there,” the little girl said, pointing to where Liliana was talking to Pepa.

Everyone had come for his eighteenth birthday, Pepa and her brood from California, along with Abuela; cousins and aunts and uncles from across the border and throughout Texas. Nearly his entire family was there. But only nearly, because Dad was, of course, away for work.

On his tenth birthday, his father took him aside and told him it was time to step up and be the man of the house. That morning, there was a phone call where his father pretty much repeated the same thing.

“Tio Eddie, let me down!” the little girl whined.

“I don’t know,” Eddie teased. “I think I might keep you.”

“Please?”

“Oh fine, since you asked so nicely.”

Eddie set her down and she was immediately off like a rocket, a giggling blur of pink frills. Laughing to himself and shaking his head, he looked around for Evan. Just the thought of the other boy - the other man, now - brought a smile to his face and had warmth blooming in his chest.

He spotted him with Adriana and Sophia, the pair of them demanding his attention. Eddie was fairly certain his sisters had a crush on Evan, but his boyfriend refused to believe it when he told him about it. The girls would be heartbroken when Evan and he left, heading southeast for Corpus Christi before following along the gulf coast. Or at least that was the plan, because it was inevitable that something would catch Evan’s eye and Eddie would be dragged along for the ride.

Eddie hadn’t told his parents yet, not about the road trip and not about Evan. They knew he wasn’t going to college; Eddie had to stay and be a man and help provide for the family, after all. The road trip ran counter to that entire notion, and they would lose their minds at the idea of it.

His devout Catholic parents would go apocalyptic if they knew about Evan.

“Eddie?”

He turned, saw Shannon standing behind him. She was pretty today, dressed in a bright yellow sundress with her hair in a braid that fell over her shoulder. She was always pretty.

“Hey, Shannon. I’m glad you made it.”

It wasn’t a guarantee that she would come. Ever since prom, ever since that awful mistake of a night, they’d awkwardly danced around each other at school and tried to avoid one another as best they could.

“Yeah.” Her eyes darted around, landed on Evan in the crowd and lingered before moving on again. “Could I talk to you? Privately?”

Eddie wasn’t certain if that was the best idea. But she looked anxious, which in turn made him anxious.

“Yeah, sure.”

He led her into the house and, when she didn’t look like she would say anything when they paused in the living room, continued on to his room. Habit had him leaving the door partially ajar as the pair of them crossed the distance to his bed, perching on the edge.

“So,” he prompted.

“So, I’m…” Shannon shook her head, covered her face with her hands. “God, Eddie, I don’t know what to do.”

“You’re my friend,” he told her. “You can tell me. Whatever it is, I’ll help you. Evan’ll help you. I know things have been weird since we-”

“I’m pregnant.”

Eddie stared at her, jaw hanging open. Shannon removed her hands to meet his eyes.

“You’re… What?”

“I’m pregnant,” she said again. “I took three tests.”

“But- But- How?”

“How!? How do you think, Eddie!? The pull out method only works if you pull out.”

“No, but you- I thought-” He thought she was on the pill. That night, almost everything about that night, had been her idea, after all. It had all been one drunken mistake, and now it was biting him in the ass. “Are you sure it’s mine?”

The slap to his face should not have caught him by surprise, but it did.

“Yes, I’m sure,” Shannon hissed out.

“Sorry, sorry.” He pushed himself off the bed and began to pace, panic starting to eat away at him. “Dios mio, por favor, what am I going to do?”

“What are you going to do?” she hissed at him. “It’s in my uterus, Eddie.”

“Yes, yes, I know!”

He paced more, trying to think. His father’s voice was in his ear, telling him to step up and be a man. Oh, God. Oh, God. He was going to be sick.

(Mea culpa, mea culpa, mea maxima culpa…)

Finding his courage, Eddie knelt in front of her and grabbed her hands and said,

“It’ll be okay.”

“What are we going to do?” Shannon asked.

He didn’t say anything for a moment, and then he took a deep breath and said,

“We should get married.”

“What?” Shannon gasped.

“Marry me,” Eddie repeated.

“But what about Evan?”

Evan would understand. When Eddie told him about what happened, when he’d explained everything, Evan would understand. Eddie just had to weather the anger, first.

“He’ll-” Eddie started to say, only to realize that Shannon was not looking at him. She was looking past him to his open door. To- Oh, fuck. “Evan.”

Evan, pale as a ghost, shook his head. He turned and sprinted down the hall. Eddie scrambled to his feet and chased after him, shouting for him to wait, to hold on, to let him explain.

But Evan did not stop running.

Now

It was awkward, having his sister in the apartment. Wonderful, but awkward. Daniel hadn’t seen her since that trip to Hershey, when he reported Doug to the cops. Their conversations over the phone, when Maddie started talking to him again, had been short and stilted. Something had broken that day, and Daniel despaired that it would never be fixed.

And yet here she was, sitting on his couch.

“So Buck is still living with you,” she remarked. Daniel rolled his eyes as he poured them drinks.

“He just moved back,” he told her, carrying the glasses over. Maddie took hers from him as he sat next to her. “He was gone for years, so I really don’t mind.”

“Dan…”

“Can we not?” he just about begged. “He’s here, I like that he’s here. He’ll always be welcome to stay with me, just like you are.”

“Fine, sorry.”

Daniel understood what she was concerned about. That his entire life was built around their brother, which… Wasn’t entirely true. Buck was a large part of his life, he’d admit that, but it wasn’t like Buck was his entire life. Sure, yes, he moved states twice for Buck, but what older brother wouldn’t do the same? 

“How long are you staying for?” Daniel asked, changing the topic. “You haven’t really said.”

“I don’t know, I’m just… “ Maddie made some weird flapping motion with her hand. “Passing through.” She took a sip of her drink and her eyes widened. “Okay, I didn’t expect you guys to be drinking wine, much less good wine.”

“Buck bought it,” he explained. “I blame the ex-girlfriend, may she find herself lost without her passport or any of her luggage.”

His sister let out an amused snort.

“Not a fan?”

“Not of her,” he confirmed, because he didn’t dislike the wine. “But she’s gone and no longer a problem.”

“What was so bad about her?”

“Besides the fact that she was thirty years older than him?” Daniel asked. Maddie burst out laughing.

“Oh my God.”

“What made you finally decide to leave?” That killed his sister’s merriment. “You got so angry. Why did you choose now? Why not then?”

“I- I don’t know,” Maddie lied. “It was just… Christmas, I guess. I saw the card the two of you sent me and I just made up my mind.”

Before Daniel could decide whether or not to call her out on what she wasn’t saying, his phone began to ring. It was Austin’s ringtone, and he hurried across the room to grab it.

“Buck?” Maddie guessed.

“No,” he told her, and then answered the phone with a, “Yeah?”

“Your brother and the new guy blew up our bus.”

“They blew up what?”

Chapter 3

Now

Sometimes, the calls that they responded to could get weird. Like, really, really weird. If Buck hadn’t spent years listening to Danny’s work stories from before they both moved, he would’ve thought that Los Angeles was simply like that. And to be fair, this city did seem to have more than its fair share of out-there emergencies. But it wasn’t like the city had an exclusive claim to them, either.

This call was pretty… Unique. Buck was gonna go with unique.

“Is this some kind of militia nut?” he asked as he followed Bobby into the house. It was filled with old war memorabilia: mannequins wearing era accurate uniforms, guns and cavalry swords hanging on the walls beside framed documents and above glass cases of medals and bullets and yet more guns.

The caller was in the back room; Buck and Bobby found him by following his weak shouting.

“What’s your name, sir?” Bobby asked as the two of them dropped down on either side of the man.

“Charlie,” the man answered. He’d put a nice tourniquet around his injured leg. “The damn grenade went off while I was taking it apart.”

“Why were you taking apart a grenade?” Buck asked him.

“I was cleaning it, I’m a collector.”

“Did you pull the pin?”

“Not that kind of grenade,” Charlie said. “It’s a forty mike-mike, practice round for a grenade launcher. I picked it up at a flea market in Brea, for my ‘Nam collection. Must’ve hit the propelling charge with my screwdriver.”

“Lots of shrapnel,” Bobby said, peering at the gnarly leg wound with his flashlight. “And it must’ve nicked his femoral artery. We need to transport him.”

Getting Charlie onto a gurney and to the ambulance was fine and standard, the most difficult part being maneuvering the gurney through the house. Eddie was in the back of the ambulance, and Buck was about to leave the patient to him and head for the truck, when Bobby hit him with,

“I want you to travel with him to the hospital, keep him stable.”

Why him? No, seriously, why Buck? There was absolutely nothing he could provide to this situation that Eddie wasn’t fully capable of doing himself. Bobby had gushed just so much over the man’s work history, he surely knew which of them was better suited for a grenade in someone’s leg.

The captain was clearly doing this on purpose, some twisted attempt to have everyone get along, but Buck couldn’t call him out on it.

“Copy that, Cap.” He made no effort to hide his reluctance, though.

“You’ve got to learn how to place nice,” Bobby told him. “It’s one team.”

Oh, if only he knew. But he never would, because that would require Buck to be far crueler than he could ever bring himself to be.

Buck climbed into the ambulance, the doors slamming shut behind them, and soon they were off to the hospital. His presence was utterly superfluous, with Eddie perfectly capable of handling what needed to be done. Buck was mostly taking care of the bandages on the wound, which the guy was quickly bleeding through, and feeling extra bitchy.

“I guess you’ve seen a lot of shrapnel wounds,” he remarked. Eddie paused in the motion of sticking leads to Charlie’s chest to cut a glance his way.

“My share.”

“You ever seen a guy with a length of rebar through his skull?”

“What are we measuring here?” Eddie asked, turning to scowl at him.

“Dunno. Pretty sure I won our last measuring contest,” Buck shot back, grinning prettily, before turning back to Charlie’s leg. He’d already bled through the bandages and they needed changing. “Hey, how do you guys know if it’s a practice round or a live one?”

Eddie stared at him; Buck shrugged.

“Just curious.” The other man should be used to that, even if it had been years.

“The color of the caps,” Eddie explained, going back to work. “Practice rounds are blue, live ones are gold.”

“Oh,” Buck said. He pointed at the grenade. “Eds? This one’s got a gold cap.”

Eddie’s eyes went wide with panic. And then he started pounding his fist on the ambulance cab, demanding they pull over.

They convened with the bomb squad in the empty parking lot of the LA Memorial Hospital. The engine was parked across the road from them, presumably a safe distance away from being in the splatter zone. Buck could see Jones and the others standing around it, watching.

At the moment, Buck, Eddie, and Bobby were gathered around one of the bomb squad guys as he held up a tablet, looking at the x-ray they took of Charlie through the ambulance.

“So he has a live round embedded in his thigh,” Bobby stated.

“I thought this thing had already gone off,” Buck said.

“The launch grenade has two components,” Eddie explained. “Gunpowder to make it travel, explosive charge to make it go boom.”

“And this one didn’t go boom, why?”

“It’s fitted with a proximity fuse, a little smart sensor that tells the cap it’s traveled a safe enough distance from the shooter to explode. From his hand to his thigh probably wasn’t far enough.”

“Well, we can’t bring him inside a hospital full of people,” Bobby said. “Not with that still stuck inside him.”

“We called the military for help,” the bomb squad guy said. Buck was pretty sure his name was Jim.

“The military?” Buck asked. “Can’t you do it?”

“You can’t defuse a grenade. We need to find someone who knows how to pull it out of him without setting it off,” Jim answered. “They’re sending someone from Pendleton, should be here within the hour.”

That just wouldn’t do, not with Charlie bleeding out like he was.

“He doesn’t have an hour,” Buck pointed out.

“I can do it.”

The three of them - Buck and Bobby and Jim - turned and stared at Eddie, who Buck could tell wanted so very badly to start fidgeting under their attention.

“If he doesn’t go to surgery soon, he’ll die,” Eddie said.

“You’ve done this before?” Bobby asked him.

“None of the guys I served with were dumb enough to shoot a live round into themselves,” Eddie answered. “But I’m familiar with the ordinance.”

Buck continued to stare at him, realization and determination settling over him like an old blanket. Danny was going to be so pissed off when he heard about this, and he no doubt would be hearing about this.

“I’m in,” he said. Eddie shot him a surprised look, but Buck ignored the man in favor of turning to Bobby.

The captain stared at him and sighed, and soon they were gearing up to climb back into the ambulance. Buck wasn’t entirely certain what good the vests would do if the grenade went off, especially since their faces wouldn’t be protected from the shrapnel, but he figured they were more to make everyone else feel better about what was happening. Jim gave Eddie a box and a claw-like tool, along with instructions and advice, and then Eddie was walking past Buck with a pat on the arm and a gratitude-filled,

“See you inside.”

Bobby caught Buck’s shoulder before he could follow.

“You don’t have to do this,” the captain said.

“Yeah, I do,” Buck said. Eddie’s return to his life might’ve reignited all the old anger and hurt, but he still loved him. Which was the entire problem with the situation. But if Eddie was going to be doing something stupidly dangerous, Buck would never forgive himself for not at least trying to help. “You wanted me to play nice. This is me playing nice.”

“Not remotely what I meant.”

“I know,” Buck said. “Now start praying that an explosion takes me out before Danny hears about this.”

Eddie was silently waiting just outside the ambulance. Buck took from him the box that the grenade was to be put into before climbing inside, the other man right behind him.

At Eddie’s instruction, Buck started Charlie on a drip. Soon, the guy went from talking about his life - dead wife, rejected by the marines, became a teacher - to snoozing right through everything. Which was very good for him, because there was no way having that claw dig into his leg was going to be a pleasant experience to be awake for, especially since he needed to be still throughout it. Plus, if this went horribly, he wouldn’t even realize when everything exploded.

“You really do not have to be here, Evan.” Eddie’s words were hushed. Either because of what they were getting ready to do or because of the audience outside the open ambulance doors. “But thank you.”

“Shut up,” Buck told him. He reached down and uncovered Charlie’s leg. “He’s losing a lot of blood.”

“Keep pressure on it,” Eddie instructed. Buck did as he was told and pressed his palms firmly on either side of the wound. “Not too much pressure.

Eddie took the claw tool and stuck it into the wound, which made a gruesome noise. A kind of wet squelch with meaty undertones. 

“There it is,” he said, gripping the grenade with it.

“Alright, awesome. Pull it out,” Buck said. “Come on.”

“I’ve got to be careful,” Eddie explained, face twisted in concentration as he worked the tool. “The sensor measures the distance traveled based on how many rotations the shell made after the launch. The key is not to turn it while pulling it out.”

“Okay, yeah. So don’t turn it.”

“I thought I might spin it like a top, but now that you’ve told me not to turn it…”

“There’s no reason to be snippy.”

Eddie actually took the time to scowl like a grump at him, which was a fair response to that comment. Buck had been snippy since pretty much the moment Eddie stepped foot in the station. But Buck wasn’t going to let him have that, though.

“Eyes forward, Eds. C’mon, you’ve got this.”

“Yeah, yeah.” Eddie focused back on the task at head, sweat beading on his forehead from the stress. With a slight turn and a soft yank, the grenade came free. It was covered in blood and gore. Buck was already letting go of Charlie’s leg and grabbing the container the grenade was going into before Eddie even got out the words, “Grab the box.”

Personally, Buck thought this box should be filled with styrofoam or something. Packing peanuts, at the very least. Instead, it was an empty box much larger than the grenade, which rolled slightly when Eddie put it in. Buck could hear the other man’s breath hitch at the same time as his own, and then he quickly - but very, very, carefully - sat the box down out of the way.

Together, they got Charlie and the gurney out of the ambulance, hurrying him far, far away from the explosive and handing him off to actual paramedics. Buck watched in silence as Charlie was finally wheeled into the ER, letting the tension drain out of his body.

“Thanks,” Eddie said, breaking the silence as he turned to face him. “For having my back in there. And y’know, for keeping Danny from killing me.”

“Don’t thank me for that last one yet,” Buck told him. “He’s at the One Thirty-three. We work scenes with them, sometimes.”

The prospect of that seemed to frighten Eddie more than the live explosive had.

“Nice work,” Bobby said as he joined them. “I’m glad you both made it out of there.”

“He’s a professional,” Buck said. “I was never really worried.”

And then the ambulance exploded in a burst of fire and noise, bits of metal flying everywhere and proving how useless their vests would have been. Buck and Bobby both instinctively ducked, as did pretty much everyone else around them.

“You guys hungry?” Eddie asked, acting unbothered by the explosion. Buck nearly called bullshit on that, but refrained.

“Yeah, sure,” he breathed out, shaking his head as he laughed a little. “I could go for something.”

Bobby looked at the both of them in utter despair.

Then

There was something bothering Evan.

It started, Daniel thought, after Eddie's last baseball game. Daniel tried to make every one of them that he could, just like Evan’s football games. Especially since Evan just insisted on being there to cheer his friend on. Eddie’s parents usually didn’t make it, Ramon on work trips and Helena at home with her daughters. When they did manage to be there, it was almost always just Helena with Eddie’s sisters, and Daniel usually found himself stuck awkwardly conversing with the woman while the girls clambered all over themselves to get Evan’s attention.

Anyways, Daniel thought whatever was bothering his brother started at that last game, a home game. He’d been fine before it, fine throughout it - loudly and obnoxiously cheering whenever Eddie was at bat or threw a particularly good pitch - but then he started acting weird at the very end. When the last inning was played, and their team won, and Eddie ran up to the bleachers to talk to them before going to the lockers. That was when Evan started acting weird, Daniel was pretty sure.

But for the life of him, he couldn’t figure out why.

It wasn’t anything that happened before that point, it wasn’t anything that Eddie said or did. That interaction had been pretty normal. Stupid, they were teenage boys, but normal. But Evan got quiet when Eddie left, and he was quiet on the car ride home, insisting that Eddie sit in the front passenger seat since their team won. Which was not the normal seating arrangement. When Daniel gave them a ride somewhere, they always sat in the back together, goofing off and generally being irritants the entire way.

Almost an entire week had passed since then, and Evan’s behavior was getting even odder. Daniel missed some of it, with work and work, but he got the impression that his brother was avoiding his friend. Not a single afternoon, for instance, was spent at the Diaz home. Eddie hadn’t been at their apartment when Daniel was there, and Alma said he wasn’t there while Daniel was at work, either.

And Evan was being quiet at home, too. Not a moody, sulky quiet, but the distressed sort. Where normally he was vibrant in just about every way, capable of filling up an arena sized space just by himself, the past week had seen him acting so very small in a way he hadn’t since that last school bully in Hershey.

In short, it was worrying. Daniel was worried.

Wishing to nip whatever the hell this was in the bud, Daniel decided to confront his brother about it. He ordered them both take out, he let Evan put off homework while they played a game, and generally did everything he could to put the kid at ease. It worked some, Evan smiled more than he had the entire past week, but he was still subdued.

After the last round, Daniel finally paused the game and moved to sit on the coffee table. Evan, far too familiar with his tactics by this point, avoided his gaze and began fidgeting with his jeans. They were torn at the knees and unraveling at the hems and they were starting to show enough ankle to scandalize a Victorian, which meant it was past time to buy him new ones.

Priorities, though.

“Alright, squirt,” Daniel said. “What’s bothering you?”

“Nothing,” the kid mumbled, finger widening one of the holes in the denim.

“Yeah, I’m calling bullshit. C’mon, I haven’t seen Eddie all week. Did something happen? Do I need to beat him up?”

Evan was quick to shake his head.

“That’s good to know.” Daniel liked Eddie, but he would absolutely beat the crap out of him if he needed to. “What is it, then?”

“I told you, it’s-”

“It’s not nothing, because I’ve also barely seen your smile.”

Evan, somehow, managed to make himself even smaller at that. He wrapped his arms around his middle, hunched his shoulders, ducked his head so low his chin touched his chest. He did everything he could to hide without actually burying himself in the couch cushions.

“Just drop it, Danny.”

“No, I won’t. Because I’m worried,” Daniel said. “I’m serious, do I need to beat someone up? Is there another little shit starting rumors about your birthmark?”

“No.”

“So what’s going on?”

“You’ll think I’m weird,” Evan mumbled, an answer that meant they were finally getting somewhere. But it was also concerning. “That I’m a freak.”

“I already think you’re weird,” Daniel joked. Which was absolutely the wrong thing to say, apparently. The moment the words were out, his brother looked about ready to start crying. Daniel just about threw himself forward, hands hovering over Evan without a clue about what to do to make this better. “Oh, hey, no, no. I didn’t- C’mon, what’s wrong?”

His brother shook his head again. Daniel sighed, sat back on the table. Then he moved back to the couch beside him, throwing an arm around Evan and pulling him into his side.

“Evan Christopher Buckley,” he said, “I loved you even before you were born, and that has not changed. You were made for me, remember? There is nothing, nothing, you can do or say or be, that will make me not love you. Okay?”

Evan trembled beneath his arm and he didn’t say anything for a very long time. But then, still without looking at him, he whispered,

“I want to kiss a boy.”

Oh.

Oh.

“I meant what I said,” Daniel told him, his voice as soft as it could be. “But there’s nothing wrong with that, understand me? There isn’t anything wrong with it at all. Kiss whoever you want. And if someone says something to you about it, I have a bat and I am not afraid to use it.”

That finally got a laugh out of his brother. It was small and wet, but it was a laugh nonetheless.

“And I’ve kissed boys,” Daniel confessed in a hush. Evan, at last, looked up. His blue eyes were wide and shiny, but there was no fear or trepidation now. Just surprise and wonder.

“Really?”

“Yeah, loads of ‘em,” he said, though he definitely wasn’t getting into the context of those kisses. Not with Evan. “Do you want to tell me who the boy is that you want to kiss?”

“No,” was Evan’s quick answer.

It didn’t matter, anyway. Daniel was fairly certain he knew exactly who it was. He really should’ve seen this coming. Maddie would have, if she were here.

“You know what this means, though, right?”

“...what?”

“I need to give you a whole new version of the talk.”

“Danny!” Evan whined, and then started trying to wiggle out from under his arm. Daniel didn’t let him go, though, tightening his hold to keep him in place.

“But first,” he said, “who do you want to know? Or do you even want anyone to know? I can and will keep it a secret if you’re not ready. It’ll just be between you and me, until you are.”

Evan ducked his head again, once more fiddling with threads of denim.

“Can you… Can you tell Maddie for me?”

“Of course,” Daniel was quick to say. “Maybe she can tell me what book to look for at the library, because I was serious about that talk.”

“Danny!”

This time, he didn’t bother keeping Evan from fleeing the room in embarrassment.

Chapter 4

Chapter Notes

okay readers, this one might be a bit of a doozy for some of you
CW for: narrative descriptions of homophobia and what is basically gay bashing (it does not involve Eddie or Buck); talk about canon domestic violence (Doug).

Now

Buck and Eddie did not talk on the ride back to the station, tucked away in the cab of the engine. They did not talk when they reached the station, either, hopping out and going their separate ways for the last little bit of their shifts. But it was inevitable that they would have to, though, Buck knew that much. And so he was not surprised at all when Eddie trailed after him when they’d changed into civvies and everyone was heading out.

“That’s not the jeep Danny got you, is it?” Eddie asked.

“No,” Buck told him, leaning against the side of it. “That one made it through the years of travel, only to give up the ghost shortly after we got here.”

They had mourned, given it a proper send-off to the scrapheap, and then Buck took the money he’d saved up being a vagabond and bought a new one. He missed that jeep, but he did not miss the nights spent curled up in the back of it when he couldn’t find a motel or a cheap place to rent. There also hadn’t been a particularly auspicious start to their relationship, so there was that, too.

“What are you doing here, Eddie?” Buck asked him, and then hurried on when the other man opened his mouth. “I don’t mean here in LA, because I kinda figured that out, since Shannon is here. But I meant here, right now, talking to me.”

“Because I’ve missed you,” Eddie confessed in a whisper. “I didn’t know you were here, I swear, and I definitely didn’t know you were working at this station. But I saw you again and I just- I’ve missed you. I know I fucked up, that I fucked up so much…”

“To be fair,” Buck sighed, leaning against the jeep, “I think I can claim some fault in that. I mean, obviously most of it is yours, but I did encourage you to go to prom with her.”

“You practically said yes for me when she asked.”

“Because you were too busy panicking- Yeah, yeah,” Buck rolled his eyes when Eddie opened his mouth to contradict him. “You don’t panic.”

Nevermind the time Buck had to talk him down from what was pretty much a panic attack, brought about by the very idea of going on an actual date where anyone might recognize them together. That had led to them deciding to keep any romance contained to the apartment or on trips far out of the city, and all other outings together being things easily excusable as two friends hanging out.

“The point, though, is that I- I fucked up,” Eddie said again. “I knew that even before you ran. And I have spent every day since then regretting it.”

Buck could not contain the disbelieving snort he let out at that. He wouldn’t have even if he were able.

“You said yesterday that you wanted to explain,” he said. “But I don’t want to hear it. It won’t make anything better, it won’t make me feel better. You married Shannon, proposed to her when we were still together, and it doesn’t matter why or what was going through your head at the time.”

Eddie opened his mouth, ready to argue. But then he bowed his head and sighed and said,

“Yeah.”

“The thing is,” Buck continued, “Bobby’s not going to transfer one of us without a good enough reason, and us sniping at each other constantly apparently isn’t one. So we’re stuck working together. And I… I’ve missed you, too.”

Before everything else, Eddie had been his best friend. For four years, they did everything together. And then, well. Buck ran, as far and as long as he could.

Danny was going to be so disappointed in him.

“So let’s start over,” he said, and then held out his hand. “Hi, Eddie. I’m Buck.”

Eddie looked down at his hand in silence for a moment, before letting out another sigh.

“Hi Buck,” he said, gripping Buck’s hand tight. “I’m Eddie.”

“It’s nice to meet you,” Buck said, letting go. “But now I’ve got to get home and hope my brother’s snitch hasn’t gotten to him yet.”

“Snitch?” Eddie asked, and then shook his head. “Uh, yeah. Okay. Drive safe.”

Buck wasn’t actually in any hurry to get back to the apartment, but he also didn’t want to be there any longer. He might have said they were starting anew, pretending to be strangers just meeting one another instead of people who used to know each other so very intimately, but that didn’t actually wipe the slate clean. It was a truce that he could keep at the station, but he needed to flee now for the sake of his sanity.

He debated, for a moment, about going out somewhere. Or simply driving around, aimlessly. But that would just be putting off the inevitable, and maybe he would get lucky. Maybe Danny didn’t know about the ambulance yet. Maybe he never would.

“A live grenade?” was the question that greeted him the moment he walked through the door. Buck’s luck had never been very good.

“Hi, Danny,” Buck said, all mock cheer, as he toed off his shoes and then went to drop his bag on top of the washer. “It’s nice to see you too, Danny.”

“You got into an ambulance with a live grenade?” Danny was standing at the kitchen table, bracing himself against it with his hands. On the counter behind him was the bottle of wine that Buck bought the other week.

“I didn’t know it was live.”

“The first time! What about the second time?”

Buck thought about any and all possible answers he could say to that. And then he just shrugged. Danny rolled his eyes before he shoved himself away from the table, grabbed the bottle, and stomped back to the couch where Maddie was sitting.

“Dan has someone spying on you at work?” their sister asked, clearly amused by the whole idea. She held out a glass to be filled.

“He’s not a spy,” Danny grumbled, pouring the wine.

“I have no clue who it is, but I’ve narrowed it down to just about every guy there,” Buck said. He grabbed a glass from the cabinet for himself before joining them, where he held it out expectantly. His brother, though, just shook his head and refilled his own, before sitting the bottle down out of Buck’s reach. “Hey! C’mon.”

“Nope. I’m annoyed with you.”

“I bought the wine!”

“Don’t care. Did you bring my bat back?”

“It’s in my bag,” Buck said, jabbing a thumb in that direction.

“Why did you take it with you?” Maddie asked, craning her head to look.

“To annoy him,” he lied. “Duh.”

“Duh,” Danny agreed, and then reached around Maddie to smack at Buck’s shoulder. “We’re both off tomorrow. Help me figure out where all we’re dragging her to.”

“Oh!” Buck brightened and sat up straighter, setting down his still pathetically empty glass. “Obviously there’s the Walk of Fame-”

“Obviously,” Danny and Maddie chorused.

“But there’s the USS Iowa, and the Griffith Observatory, and…”

Then

“Ha! Suck it!” Evan gloated as Eddie’s avatar exploded on the screen.

After nearly two years of knowing each other, it was more than common for one of them to be at the other’s home. Evan often ended up at Eddie’s - easier for everyone, since the other boy was almost always getting saddled into helping with his sisters or around the house - but Eddie would just as often wind up at the apartment with him and Danny. Evan assumed those were days that his friend, his best and pretty much only friend, needed or wanted a break from his family.

In some ways, the Diaz family was so very different from the family Evan grew up with. Dinners were loud and warm, instead of the quiet and cold and near formal affairs he was used to. When something was celebrated, a holiday or a birthday, distant relatives converged en masse, while Evan had never even met his grandparents on either side. Eddie and his sisters were even allowed to sit in the living room and watch whatever they wanted on the TV, which was…

Well, there was a reason Evan and Danny weren’t familiar with most things that came out before they moved to Texas.

But in other ways, the Diaz family was distressingly familiar. Mostly in the ways that Helena and Ramon treated and spoke to their children, Eddie in particular. They expected and demanded so much of him, forcing him to step in as a third parent while Ramon was constantly gone for work. The first time Evan was at the Diaz house, he’d witnessed Eddie get into a fight with both his parents about joining the baseball team. Something about how he needed to be at home helping his mother take care of the girls.

Eddie won that argument, or maybe he ignored it entirely, because he’d been on the team since freshman year.

Today, it was just the pair of them at the apartment, with Danny going in that morning for a twenty-four hour shift. Neither Evan nor Eddie had anywhere to be: it was summer, so they didn’t have school and it wasn’t time for practices to start for football, yet. That meant they were able to spend all their time on the couch, killing each other in the game and gorging themselves on pizza. 

(Danny always left cash behind for Evan to order himself something whenever he was gone. Even though Tia Alma had started teaching Evan how to cook within months of meeting the Buckley brothers, and still sometimes took it upon herself to make sure they both got actual dinner.)

“You’re such a sore winner,” Eddie complained, miming as if to smack him. Evan just laughed and ducked. “You have an unfair advantage. How often do you and Danny sit here playing this?”

“Not that often,” Evan lied with a grin.

“Oh sure.” His friend rolled his eyes and grumbled, “Pendejo.”

“Hey! I know what that means, y’know!”

“Oh, I know. Cabrón.”

It was Evan’s turn for an eye roll this time.

“Hurry up and respawn so I can kill you again,” he said.

Eddie stared at him for a moment, and Evan watched an all too familiar smile start to bloom across his handsome face. And then his friend was lunging forward, yanking the controller out of his hands.

“Hey!” Evan yelped, grabbing for it and failing. “Give that back, you asshole!”

“Nope.” Eddie shook his head, holding both controllers in one hand so he could use the other to keep Evan away. “It’s mine now.”

With a bellow, Evan tackled him. He got to see Eddie’s eyes go wide before the both of them were wrestling for the controllers, Eddie doing his best to keep them out of Evan’s reach. The pair of them stretched out along the couch, rolling back and forth, until they finally went too far and fell to the floor with a thud that shook the coffee table.

Evan found himself beneath Eddie, the other boy grinning down at him in victory as he held the controllers above his head. And in that moment, he let the intrusive thoughts win. At that moment, he leaned up and kissed him.

Evan had been wanting to kiss his friend for… He didn’t know how long, really. Evan had known he’d wanted to kiss his friend only since last April, when he saw Eddie fresh off the baseball diamond, sweaty and flushed with victory, and he found himself thinking “He’s so hot like that” followed by “Oh God, oh no.”

Evan let the intrusive thoughts win, he leaned up and kissed Eddie, and then sanity struck him like a lightning bolt. Terror and horror came with it.

“Oh God, I’m sorry,” he said, crawling backwards to get out from under Eddie and away from him. Eddie stared down at him in stunned silence. “I’m sorry, I’m sorry.”

Their freshman year, there’d been this boy. Homeschooled until that point, and sheltered more than Evan ever was. His name had been Timothy, and he hadn’t wasted any time telling everyone that he liked other boys in that way. The student body reacted… Unkindly.

Evan never knew Timothy personally, hadn’t shared any classes with him or even the same lunch period. The gossip and rumors made sure he knew what happened, regardless. The way he was turned into a pariah by their classmates and tormented. One particular tale that stuck in Evan’s memory involved Timothy being shoved into the girls’ bathroom, the students responsible making sure to push him back in every time he tried to get out.

The school year started in August.

He was at a different school by Halloween.

The football team had not made their opinions of him quiet, either. Evan heard them in the lockers, before and after practice. They talked about how disgusting Timothy was, what a freak he was, what they would do to the kid if they caught him alone. Some of them bragged about what they’d done to him, turned it into a joke. Words and names were bandied about that Evan knew he could never, ever say in front of Danny.

That was freshman year. And now, the summer before his junior year, Evan had let the intrusive thoughts win. Evan had ruined everything.

Eddie moved, and Evan braced himself for whatever was coming. For Eddie to get to his feet and run out the door, for Eddie to scream at him and tell him how disgusting he was, for Eddie to do something worse.

Eddie moved, set the controllers down on the coffee table, and then he reached for Evan. Evan, in turn, could not help the way he flinched backwards when his (former?) friend’s hand came towards his face. He did not expect the gentle way Eddie’s palm cradled his face, did not expect the somewhat chapped lips that met his.

Oh, Evan thought.

“Oh,” he said, when Eddie leaned back again.

They looked at each other, silent and both on the verge of panicking. And then Evan surged back up again, lips crashing into Eddie’s with much more confidence.

The controllers sat forgotten on the coffee table for the rest of the evening.

Now

The week of shifts following the truce with Eddie were awkward as the pair of them found their new rhythm together. Buck came in, treated his ex like they had only just recently met, and did his best to get on with his day until they were called out. At scenes, Bobby - who had started being unbearably smug about everything - paired them up constantly. That they worked so well together was only surprising to all the people who didn’t know their history.

In their downtime at the station, Buck did his very best to treat Eddie as just another coworker, as a brand new acquaintance that meant nothing to him. He was very careful about it, because he knew that if didn’t watch himself, then they’d very quickly fall into the same patterns as long ago. Already, more than once, they’d found themselves joking and talking like Eddie hadn’t broken his heart and sent him fleeing the state, only to catch themselves and then gracelessly exit the situation.

At the moment, they were in one of those downtimes. Chimney was in the kitchen area, emptying out an entire jar of peanut butter with celery. Hen was sitting at one of the tables, concentrating on today’s crossword puzzle in the newspaper. Buck was sitting next to her, being delightful, though some naysayers might describe it as annoying. He was also conveniently angled so that he could watch Eddie at the pinball machine. More specifically, so he could watch Eddie’s arms flex as he played and also enjoy the man’s side profile.

It was unhealthy and counter-productive to what they were doing, but… There were actually no excuses for Buck’s actions and choices here, he could admit that. He should absolutely stop it, just from a professional standpoint. 

“Alright, everyone, listen up. I’ve got an announcement to make,” Bobby called out as he crested the stairs on the other side of Eddie. He stepped around him to stand in the middle of them, just beside the pool table. “I just got off the phone with the people from the calendar, and they have made their choice.”

“No hard feelings, no matter who won,” Buck said, just to be a little shit.

“That’s good,” the captain said. “‘Cause they didn’t pick you.”

“Obviously it’s a fix.” Buck was neither surprised nor upset about it. “Congrats, Diaz.”

That had Hen laughing silently where she sat.

“They didn’t pick him, either.”

That did have Buck surprised. Also somewhat offended on Eddie’s behalf, as nonsensical as that was.

“Who, then?” he asked. “You?”

“Nope,” Bobby said, and then looked past him.

Buck turned in his seat and spotted only Chimney standing there. It took him a moment to realize what that meant.

“No way,” the paramedic intoned. “You’ve got to be kidding me.”

“Nope. Congratulations, Chim,” the captain said. “Or should I say, Mr. April?”

Well. Plot twist, but okay then.

They swarmed him to give their congratulations, high-fiving him and patting him on the back. Chimney looked about as surprised as Buck felt, and also like he was very much enjoying the attention.

“Am I interrupting anything?”

They turned as one and spotted Athena, in uniform, coming across the loft towards them. Bobby met her halfway. They said something to each other, too quiet for Buck to hear. And then they started kissing. It was not a quick peck, either. It was the kind of kiss one did to prove a point, just shy of being indecent for the public.

Yet another plot twist. And more disappointing than the calendar, because it meant Buck was about to lose money.

“Pay up,” Hen demanded, making grabby hands at him and Chimney.

“Wait a minute. You knew?” Bobby sounded understandably gobsmacked, considering the pair had been creeping around like teenagers having an illicit affair. Buck should know, that pretty much described his relationship with Eddie. 

“Sir, first you two were happy at the same time,” Hen pointed out as she gladly yanked the money out of people’s hands. “And then you were both pissed off at the same time. It wasn’t hard to figure out.”

“And you’re good with this?” Athena sounded worried, which made sense. Hen, as Buck managed to gather, had been her friend for years. Athena and Bobby - Hen’s boss - being together could very easily make things awkward for Hen.

“I’m just mad at myself for not fixing you up in the first place.”
