“Hold on.” A realization had struck Buck. “Did you have Danny babysit for you so that the two of you could…?”

“I knew I could trust him,” Athena answered with an elegant shrug.

Judging from the quizzical look that Hen shot him, Buck knew she was as confused as he was about that statement. But before he could try to probe further, his phone buzzed in his pocket. It was the man in question, and the text had him letting out a groan.

“What is it?” Bobby asked him. On the captain’s other side, Eddie was just as curious.

“Danny’s Mr. July.”

Buck came home to fighting.

It was not often that Maddie and Danny argued. It had always been the two of them - the three of them - against Mom and Dad. Unless it was about Doug, then Maddie was typically on her own, because neither Danny nor Buck could stomach the words to defend that relationship.

But Buck had never seen his siblings argue, actually argue. Not like this.

“You’re just like Mom!” Maddie shouted. Buck winced at the insult, remembered the time he used it, and then winced even harder. “I bet you’re so happy to have been proven right! Go on, say it! Say, ‘I told you so!’”

“I’m not happy about this! Why the hell would I ever be happy about this!?” Danny shouted back, standing on the opposite side of the room from her. “The only thing I’m happy about is that you finally left him! Eight years later, sure, but better than never. But now you’re just going to breeze right back out there, and who cares if we ever see you again, right!?”

What? Buck glanced around and finally spotted Maddie’s bags, packed and ready to go, sitting just beside the door.

“You’re leaving?”

Though Buck didn’t raise his voice, it shut both of his siblings right up. The pair of them spun in place to face him, eyes widening.

“Oh God, Buck,” Danny said, gripping the back of the couch. “I didn’t hear you come in.”

“You’re leaving?” Buck asked again, ignoring his brother in favor of his sister. “Already?”

“The road ahead awaits,” was her cavalier answer.

“Yeah, but…” They’d been arguing about Doug, Buck could gather that much. “But what about the road behind you, though?”

The stubborn jut of Maddie’s jaw was so very familiar. He’d seen it on Danny, and he’d seen it in his own reflection.

“What really happened with Doug?” he demanded, once it became clear neither of his siblings were going to say anything. “Why are you running from him?”

Maddie didn’t look at him, she looked at Danny. There was a silent conversation happening between the two of them, and it irritated Buck to be left out of it. He wanted to know what was going on. How was he supposed to help protect his sister if he didn’t know what she needed protecting from?

“The stuff that Mom and Dad hated about him, that the both of you hated, it all got worse, much worse, over the last year or so,” Maddie finally said. Danny scoffed, something that earned a glare from her and confusion from Buck. “When I threatened to leave him, he threatened to kill me. And he meant it.”

“Jesus, Mads,” Danny whispered, making an aborted step towards her..

“When women in abusive relationships used to come into the hospital, I gotta be honest, I would pass judgment,” Maddie said, not looking at either of them. “Like, ‘why don’t you just leave him?’ And now I get it. It’s like, you can’t even believe it’s happening.”

“Okay, but you did leave him,” Buck said, glancing at Danny for help. His brother was silent, however. “And now isn’t the time to be alone, alright? So you should stay here. Danny and I have room. If Doug comes looking, we both know a lot of cops and Danny has his bat.”

“He doesn’t know the two of you live here,” she said with a shake of her head. “He doesn’t know what you do. It’s a benefit of being married to somebody who doesn’t care to ask questions.”

“That’s perfect!” Buck glanced at Danny again. His brother looked annoyed, though Buck could not for the life of him guess why. “You can start over, I can help get you a great nursing job-”

“No,” Maddie was quick to say. “I miss helping people, but I’d spend every day looking over my shoulder, wondering who’s walking through the door.”

“Okay, so- so you want to help people, but not deal with them face-to-face?” Buck asked. His sister slowly nodded. “Then I think I might have an idea.”

Daniel quietly slipped into his bedroom, letting the door shut behind him. In the living room, Buck was hammering out the details with Maddie about a possible new job. He, though, didn’t particularly care about that bit. Not at the moment, not beyond the fact that his sister was staying.

No, what Daniel cared most about at this moment was the glaring problem that Maddie had just so casually brushed over. Doug might not have cared about where Daniel and Buck lived before, but he definitely would now. They’d be the first place, the most obvious place, for where Maddie would run.

Earlier in the year, Buck had gone viral over a rollercoaster rescue. The “Country Fair Spider-Man” as the news dubbed him. It was those interviews that led to the relationship with Abby and also to the catfisher stealing Buck’s identity. A quick Google search of his name would lead Doug right to Los Angeles.

Eight years ago, Daniel had gone to the cops about Doug. It had been an utter mistake, one that led to his relationship with Maddie pretty much evaporating entirely. If she’d had anywhere else to go, she probably never would have ended up on his doorstep once she finally decided to run.

Today, he took out his phone and wondered if he was about to make the same mistake.

“Daniel,” was how Athena Grant answered. The sergeant struck him as a kindred soul, and he hoped she could be trusted with this in ways most cops couldn’t be. He had trusted her with Buck, after all, and she in turn had trusted him with her own kids. “To what do I owe the pleasure today?”

“I need to talk to you about my brother-in-law.”

Chapter 5

Now

Every night since Maddie arrived at their apartment, Danny slept on the couch while she took his bed. Buck offered to give up his own bed time and again, but his older brother refused each time. Because much like Buck and Maddie, Danny could be very stubborn. Even to his own detriment, because he was looking more and more worn down every morning.

On the morning of Maddie’s first day at her new job as a 911 dispatcher, or at least the first day she’d actually be dispatching, now that she was through orientation, Buck stumbled out of his room to find that Danny was already up and freshly showered. He was yawning his way through a cup of coffee and was somehow looking more tired than when they’d all bunked down for the night. Buck went to bother him, since he could hear his sister in the shower.

“Do you think there’ll be any hot water left for me this time?” he asked.

“Cold showers are supposed-” Danny’s words were cut off by a yawn that had his jaw unhinging like a snake. “Supposed to be good for you.”

“Okay, at this point it’s your own fault,” Buck told him. “I’ve offered to take the couch how many times?”

“‘S fine,” his brother said, like he said every other time before. “It’s a comfortable couch. Broken in.”

“We’re both over six feet tall. It doesn’t matter how comfortable it is to lay on if we don’t fit.”

Danny just shrugged. Buck rolled his eyes.

“C’mon, I know you’re worried about Maddie leaving. I am, too,” Buck said. “But she’s not gonna just leave in the middle of the night.”

Danny’s eyes drifted up from his coffee to fix on Buck. And then he said, oh so quietly,

“You did.”

Buck cringed at the reminder, because he very much had left in the middle of the night, once. It was just after the second Christmas after graduation, the second Christmas he made sure to come back to El Paso for. It was also the last Christmas he spent there.

He’d meant to stay for a while, at least through New Years. But then he ran into his reasons for fleeing the state to begin with. The urge to run overwhelmed him, and he’d been too much of a cowardly piece of shit to give his brother a proper goodbye.

“B- But she won’t. She- She has a job and… And everything…” Buck trailed off awkwardly.

Silence filled the air, broken only by the sound of running water coming from the bathroom until it shut off. Buck could hear their sister moving around, could hear a drawer opening and shutting, followed by the sink being turned on. Quite a few minutes after she was finished in the shower, Maddie walked out of the bathroom in her new uniform, still brushing her hair.

“Okay, I know I’ve asked this before and you lied to me then, too,” she said. “But how stupid do I look? Really?”

Maddie held her arms out to properly display the uniform she’ll be wearing as a dispatcher: a maroon polo shirt with black pants. Abby’s shirt had been blue, Buck remembered, and he wasn’t sure if that was because the uniform had changed in the months since then or if different colored shirts meant different things. Maybe maroon was just what the new people wore?

“Why does it matter if you look stupid?” Danny asked, setting down his coffee. “The people calling you for help aren’t going to care how you look.”

“But I care.”

“Then you don’t look any more or less stupid than you normally do.”

Danny grinned at the affronted noise Maddie made.

“You look fine,” Buck said, just as he had said the last time she asked, and headed for the bathroom to take his own shower. “Hey, did you leave any hot water for me this time?”

Maddie gave an apologetic shrug. Buck rolled his eyes and prepared for yet another cold shower.

After the quickest shower he could manage while still claiming to have showered, Buck hustled his sister out the door. The drive to Metro Dispatch was out of the way for either Buckley brother, in the opposite direction from both the 118 and the 133. Add to it the city’s godawful traffic, and the commute was a bitch. Sometimes, Buck really regretted volunteering to be his sister’s taxi on mornings when Danny worked.

There was a parking garage attached to the building Dispatch worked out of. Buck didn’t drop her off there, though, instead pulling up right in front of the entrance doors.

“Okay, honey,” he said, grinning at her. “Now I want you to have fun and make lots of friends, and did you remember your lunch?”

Maddie glared balefully at him.

“You’re such a brat,” she told him, before hopping out of the jeep.

“Love you too, sis! And start Ubering!” Buck called after her. She flipped him off without even pausing in her stride, and he laughed in response.

He was late getting to the station. Late enough that the locker room was empty as he stowed away his work bag, grateful he had the presence of mind to put on his uniform right out of the shower instead of waiting to change here. Bobby was manning the stove while Hen and Chimney were sitting at the kitchen island, plates of food in front of them, when Buck jogged up the stairs.

“No, it’s a dream, it’s not supposed to be attainable,” Hen was saying, and then pointed at Chimney with her fork. “Top Gun? You can barely drive, you rebar head.”

“Hey,” was Chimney’s offended response, before getting distracted by Buck joining them. “Alright, Buckaroo. If you weren’t a member of the LAFD, what would you be doing?”

Probably what he’d been doing before becoming a firefighter.

“Uh, I don’t know.” He looked at Bobby. “I’m not getting fired, am I?”

The captain gave a firm shake of his head, which did a lot to quell any anxieties before they could come rushing up. Chimney’s quip of “that’s inevitable” was kinda hurtful, though.

“He’d be a Golden Retriever,” Hen said, smiling up at him with a hint of mockery.

“A bartender,” Chimney guessed. “No, no, bouncer at a bar.”

“Bouncer at a strip club,” Hen said, and then high-fived Chimney.

Joke’s on them, he’d actually done all three of those things. He hadn’t particularly enjoyed being a bouncer, but four years of football in high school, combined with his height, meant that everyone except the biggest drunks, the biggest assholes, or the biggest of both, backed down before things went too far. But Buck had done a lot of other things, too, and it kinda rubbed him wrong that it was those jobs that apparently came to mind when they looked at him.

“What’s going on with you, kid?” Bobby asked, setting down the spatula he’d been using to stir whatever was in the skillet he’d been focused on. “You’re late again.”

“Because the traffic in this city sucks, unless you’re driving ten tons of engine with sirens,” Buck complained as he perched on one of the stools at the other end from the two paramedics. “Dispatch is in the complete opposite direction from here. I just told Maddie she needs to start Ubering.”

“How is your sister settling in down there?”

“She’s a Buckley,” he said, a grin starting to grow as he looked between the captain and the paramedics. “She’s practically running the place.”

Hen and Chimney shot him very, very unimpressed glares. In unison, they dropped their silverware onto their plates, which they then picked up to move to the dining table. Buck’s grin got even wider and he turned back to find Bobby silently laughing to himself.

“How is she, really?” the captain asked, because he was wise to the games younger siblings played to torment their elders.

“Uh, nervous.” Probably an understatement: Maddie had taken a giant notebook filled with who knew what in with her that morning, clutching it to her chest like a safety blanket. “Sorry about being late again, but since Danny’s the driver-engineer for his shift, we kinda figured it was more important for him to be there on time.”

“It’s fine,” Bobby said, because he was cool like that. “Just make sure she does start Ubering.”

“Got it, Cap.”

After he ate his own breakfast, Buck headed downstairs. There were chores he could work on, which also conveniently gave him an excuse to avoid being around Eddie. Their truce was holding, and last shift Buck had actually managed to avoid saying more than five words to the man when they weren’t at a scene, not that they had much time to chat then, either. Honestly, the worst was when they were stuck in the cab of the engine together, unable to escape.

After knocking out one thing off his chores list, and with the day promising to be a slow one, Buck decided to work off some extra energy by hitting the gym. He went to the locker room so he could change into his work out clothes, pausing to chat with one of the guys who seemed to have had the same idea. And then the earth tried its best to shake itself apart beneath his feet.

Everything that was not bolted in place fell or rolled. The rack of tanks crashed to the ground and filled the air with fog, the chest of tools went from one side of the truck bay to the other, one of the yellow hoses attached to the banister up above came crashing down. The glass walls of the locker room shattered, spraying everyone nearby with shards.

Buck spared the time to hope that it meant they’d be replaced by something opaque as he rushed out, pushing people ahead of him.

“Anybody else back there?” Bobby shouted, meeting him halfway.

“No, I was the last.”

“Then c’mon.”

The captain grabbed him by the arm and practically dragged him out, the pair of them stumbling as the ground continued to roll. The others were all gathered outside, safely away from anything else that might fall on them, they huddled together until the shaking stopped. The air was filled with car alarms and distant shouting.

Buck took a breath and said, with all feeling,

“Holy fuck.”

Then

Daniel would never claim to be a football fan. Dad used to watch it, and sometimes Daniel would watch it with him, but he never really got it. The players lined up in nice little lines and then burst into motion, throwing the ball down the field and tackling each other. And then, within seconds, a whistle would blow and they’d separate, only to line up and do it all over again. There were four quarters of that, of starting only to stop a moment later and reset everything.

Basketball was more his speed, with the players in constant motion. There was no lining them up like bowling pins to be knocked over and then set back up. There was the ball, there were the baskets, there were the ten guys chasing each other up and down the court as they tried to get the ball into a basket. The action was simple, clean, and it didn’t stop every ten seconds.

He wasn’t sure if Evan actually liked football, or if he just chose the most violent school sport he could find. Daniel wouldn’t be surprised if the latter had been the entire reasoning, because his brother used to find so many ways to get himself hurt. Accidentally, of course, but also quite clearly on purpose. It was the only way that he ever got Mom to show him something close to care, even if she was only interested in keeping her investment safe. At least for a while, until she reached her limit and just started berating him.

When Evan signed up for football back in middle school, when they still lived in Hershey, Daniel had forged their Dad’s signature on the required permission slip. Mom and Dad found out, of course, and threw an utter fit about it, which might have also contributed to why Evan wanted to play it even more. He and Maddie were much alike, at times.

Anyways, Daniel wasn’t really a fan of football, but he was a fan of his baby brother. And Evan happened to be playing football, so Daniel sat his happy little self in the bleachers and screamed himself hoarse. At least he wasn’t alone, Eddie was right next to him and screaming just as loud as he waved a piece of poster board with Evan’s jersey number, 81, drawn on it in their school colors of blue and green..

Back in April, Evan wouldn’t tell him what boy he wanted to kiss, but Daniel had his theories. And since sometime over the summer, the pair had gone from touching constantly whenever they were together to there always being a conspicuous amount of space between them. At least whenever they thought someone could see them.

Daniel would be worried, except they were spending just as much time together. Plus, it seemed to be a mutual effort to avoid touching, because it wasn’t a simple case of Evan shying away from his friend’s touch. Eddie, too, went from being all over Daniel’s brother to acting like he had cooties.

Daniel had his theories, because he wasn’t stupid, but he wasn’t going to say anything. Really, that conspicuous bruise that appeared on Evan’s neck during one of Daniel’s twenty-fours said plenty on its own. It definitely hadn’t come from their coffee table, as Evan claimed, not unless the table grew teeth and started nibbling on people.

One of the coaches called a time out, and so Daniel sat back down. The sign went back down to rest between their feet, avoiding the thing of popcorn and the remnants of hot dogs that Daniel bought the both of them.

“So,” Daniel started, because there was a pause in the game, and because while he might have decided not to say anything, it didn’t mean he couldn’t say anything. “Anything new happening in your life, Eddie? Family? Friends? Relationships, maybe?”

Eddie stiffened in place. The crimson that creeped up his neck wasn’t quite so obvious as it would be on Evan, but Daniel could still spot it. He took a slurp of his coke as he waited for the teenager to come up with an answer, and wondered if he was just being cruel.

“Um,” Eddie said, followed by another “um” before he managed to get actual words out. “I’ve been looking for jobs.”

“I worked all through high school to save up money.” Daniel hadn’t saved up enough money, it turned out, but regretting something didn’t magically change it. “Why now?”

“I’ve been sixteen for a couple months,” Eddie said with a shrug. “And I can drive, so I won’t have to ask for rides.”

Oh, Daniel knew Eddie could drive. He’d been the one to teach both him and Evan, short little fifteen year olds that they were at the time, which had been a more harrowing experience than virtually anything else he’d gone through. He still sometimes had nightmares about it.

“Plus Dad’s real big about me being the man of the house,” Eddie said, dropping his voice in a clear imitation of his father.

In two and a half years, Daniel had only seen Ramon Diaz a handful of times. The man had made it to maybe three baseball games in that period and only one of Eddie’s birthdays, his sixteenth. Suffice to say, Daniel had opinions about him that would continue to go unspoken.

“How do you do it?” Eddie suddenly asked him.

“Do what?” Daniel asked in return.

“This.” The teen waved a hand at everything around them, as if that answered anything. “I know you work a lot of extra shifts, but I don’t think you’ve missed a single one of Evan’s games.”

Daniel hadn’t. It took a lot of wheeling and dealing, a lot of begging and pleading and bargaining, but every Friday that there was a game, he was able to watch Evan play. Even if it sometimes meant he came straight from the station and then went straight back when the game was over. It helped that there was a lot of sympathy from his captain and coworkers, who all knew it was just him and his brother.

That Evan regularly charmed the whole lot of them whenever he came to the station also helped.

“Evan wants me here, so I make sure I’m here,” Daniel said, simple as that. Because it really was that simple.

Eddie opened his mouth, and Daniel could just see another question on the tip of his tongue. But then the players broke their huddles and went to line up on the field again and soon the both of them were busy screaming themselves hoarse once more, the question forgotten.

Now

Eddie Diaz was no stranger to earthquakes. West Texas had them often enough, though nothing to really brag about. The worst had been a 5.7, back when he was a kid. Too young to remember it, even. They were usually small enough to be indistinguishable from a particularly heavy truck going down the road. And there’d been quakes in Afghanistan, too, but the bigger ones usually happened closer to Pakistan from where he was stationed.

He had never seen anything quite like this.

There had been a short grace period of noise-filled calm, filled with car alarms and shouting and dogs barking. Long enough for them to ascertain that the shaking had stopped, long enough to creep back inside and see the damage. And then the station’s alarm began to scream, sending them all scrambling to find their turnouts in the mess, to get the way cleared for the trucks to leave, to get the way cleared for them to get into the trucks.

Destruction was visible throughout the roads they took. There were downed trees and powerlines, along with crashed cars, but there were also pieces of buildings that had been shaken off and were now littering the ground around them. Half of one structure, a parking garage, maybe, had collapsed. It probably said a lot about what they were heading to, that they bypassed that one entirely.

But truthfully, Eddie barely paid attention to the world outside the engine. His focus was on his phone, on the texts that went nowhere, on his rising frustration with his inability to check on that which mattered the most.

“Is everything okay?”

Evan’s - Buck’s, Eddie reminded himself, he goes by Buck now - voice had him raising his head. He wasn’t sure what was more surprising: that the other man had willingly started a conversation with him, or the genuine concern that was visible in his expression. But no, the man had always been that way, curious and empathetic to a fault.

(He wanted so very desperately to reach over and run his thumb over that birthmark, just like he used to do. The urge came on him so strongly, sometimes, strong enough that he had to clench his hands into fists and find something else to do with them.)

“Yeah, uh, there’s no service,” Eddie got out, looking back down at his phone. “Texts won’t even get through.”

“Who’re you trying to get a hold of?”

Eddie felt himself stiffen at the question. Evan- Buck said he didn’t want an explanation about why he’d done what he had, but Christopher was at the heart of it. Christopher and the drunken, panic-filled decisions that led to him.

“My son,” he finally said. “I’m trying to reach my son.”

Eddie wasn’t sure what he expected to see when he risked looking. Anger, maybe. Hurt. What he had not expected was an utter lack of reaction from the other man. Buck did not seem at all surprised to hear about Christopher.

Something must’ve shown on Eddie’s face, because Buck shrugged and averted his eyes.

“Where is he?” he asked.

“School,” Eddie answered, feeling like the ground was shaking beneath him again. 

Had Shannon told him about Christopher, during that visit to the station Buck mentioned? Had she told him about everything?

“I’m sure he’s fine, then.”

“His name’s Christopher,” Eddie blurted out, and that got a reaction.

Buck’s eyes went wide and his mouth parted slightly. He sat there, staring at Eddie in silence, and did not manage to find his voice before the engine was arriving at their destination.

The answer to the earlier question - What would demand their attention more than a partially collapsed parking garage? - turned out to be a highrise hotel, leaning ominously over them. Chimney and Hen arrived before them in the ambulance, and were already gawking up at it when he jumped out of the engine.

 “You ever deal with something like this before?” he asked.

“No,” Bobby answered.

Eddie was momentarily distracted from the deathtrap in front of them by a new commotion. Cops were escorting a couple away, the pair of them struggling to get loose and run back into the hotel. Apparently they were parents, apparently their daughter was still inside.

Eddie would’ve punched the cop, if it was him.

They followed Bobby as a group to the Incident Commander, Chief Williams, who gave them a summary of how things were. Which was basically, Situation Normal, All Fucked Up. Due to timing, they were light on guests, and they’d accounted for most of them and most of the hotel staff. The collapse was caused by a failure in a section of the underground parking garage, which caused it to pancake at an angle.

The only thing keeping the building from hitting the ground was some reinforced steel, hope, luck, a prayer, and some pixie dust.

“A high-rise is supposed to be the safest place to be when an earthquake hits,” Buck said, having finally found his voice again. Eddie already knew that thanks entirely to him, after Buck experienced his first proper earthquake in El Paso, a minor little thing compared to this morning, and proceeded to dive headfirst down a rabbit hole.

There’d been a week of earthquake related trivia that followed.

“Not when you’re built right on top of a fault line,” Chief Williams countered. “And this quake was a seven-point-one.”

“Northridge was just a six-point-seven,” Hen said.

“A seven-point-one makes it the largest in SoCal in twenty years,” Chimney added. “The last one was in Joshua Tree.”

The chief lingered long enough to impress upon them just how much this earthquake had stretched the city’s resources. Then she was being dragged away to somewhere else that needed her attention, leaving the group of them huddled together.

“Alright, here’s how you make it to the end of the day,” Bobby told them. “You don’t worry about the things you can’t do anything about.” That, for some reason, was said while looking directly at Buck. “You focus on one task at a time. I can’t order you to go inside that building, and I’m not going to judge you if you decide not to.”

None of them were going to stay outside where it was safe. Eddie had only known most of them for a few days, but that was already perfectly clear to him. And Buck did follow him into an ambulance to extract a live grenade out of a man’s leg, so.

A cop came and requested their attention, leading them around to show them the side of the building that was looming directly over the street. A man in a bathrobe was pressed against a window, his fear quite obvious even from this far away.

“We could get a bag street-side,” Chimney offered. “In case that window gives.”

“Nobody works under that side of the building,” Bobby declared. “Somebody’s gonna have to get him from the inside.”

Eddie turned his head to find Buck already looking at him.

“Would you say that’s the eleventh floor?” he asked.

“I would,” Buck said with a nod, clearly on the same wavelength. “I bet if we take the ladder to the fourth floor, we could cut the distance in half.”

The pair of them turned to Bobby. The captain, without hesitation, nodded.

“Go.”

Chapter 6

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Now

It did not take very long to shed their heavy turnouts and grab any gear they might need in the hotel: rope, ax, power saw. The basics, basically. The ladder was aimed and extended, leading them directly to a window on the fourth floor. Pieces of paper, blown out from the hotel, fluttered in the air around them as Buck and Eddie clambered up. When they got to the window that was going to be their entry point, Eddie shattered the tempered glass with a Halligan.

There were questions building up in Buck’s throat, growing and multiplying ever since Eddie told him the name of his son. Because, Christopher? Christopher? Buck’s middle name, Christopher? Why the hell would Eddie name his son after his ex?

But Buck couldn’t ask that question at the moment. Climbing into a highrise that was being held up only by its own ghost was neither the time nor the place. Plus, however much the man was trying to cover it up, Buck could tell that he was still anxious about his son.

About Christopher.

(What the hell, Eddie?)

“After Northridge,” Buck said, watching Eddie knock out every shard of glass left in the frame, “FEMA spent two-hundred million dollars retrofitting every school in the LAUSD. Ceiling tiles, light fixtures… He’s in the safest place he can be.”

Eddie glanced back at him, smiled.

“I thought that was a highrise.”

“You’re still a smartass.”

They crawled through the window and into the stairwell on the other side. It was going to be a tough climb, Buck could already tell, because the entire thing was at a very steep angle. Which meant, for half of the steps, he was practically pulling himself up by the railing.

“It’s only six flights,” Eddie said, staring up as far as their flashlights illuminated. “Not exactly Mount Everest.”

“Yeah, except Everest isn’t tilted at an angle,” Buck pointed out. “And it wouldn’t tip over and crush us like a bug.”

Buck got to the landing for the fifth floor exit and made the mistake of letting go of the railing. Gravity immediately sent him falling onto the exit door, shoulder first.

“You okay?” Eddie asked, looking back in concern.

“Yeah.”

“C’mon then, let’s keep going on.”

Buck was, in his humble opinion, doing a really, really good job not asking the questions he was dying to ask. He was being a complete professional, even though it meant they climbed in silence. Which was probably a good thing. This was a difficult enough ascent as it was, they didn’t need to be wasting their breath on anything else.

But Buck was not perfect, he was actually quite far from it. And he was curious and talkative by nature. The chatty cat that kept getting its whiskers burnt off.

He broke at the landing for the ninth floor, two down from the guy that needed rescuing.

“Why Christopher?” he blurted out.

Eddie froze on the steps ahead of him, shoulders rising to around his ears.

“Why not Christopher?” he shot back without even glancing Buck’s way.

“Why not- Seriously, Eddie? Because it’s my middle name. Because it’s your ex’s middle name.”

“I told you,” the other man said, climbing again. “I missed you.”

“Oh, then I guess I should just be thankful you didn’t name him Evan,” Buck said. “God, wait, please tell me you didn’t give him Evan as his middle name.”

“Of course not,” Eddie said, and then immediately followed it with, “It’s Daniel.”

“What the fuck?”

“Yeah, well.” Eddie stopped, turned to face him. “What about you, huh? You weren’t surprised to hear I had a kid. Did Shannon tell you?”

“She didn’t have to,” Buck told him, gripping the railing to keep from falling backwards. The angle was worse on this part of the stairs, and so it was not a good place to stop for a conversation. Damn his impulses. “The first couple of Christmases, I went back to El Paso to spend it with Danny.”

And then he ran into Shannon out and about with Christopher - Jesus, Eddie - and proceeded to run like a cowardly rabbit. He missed the next Christmas, and then Danny came to him the Christmas after that, done with his shit and bluntly informing him that they were both moving to Los Angeles. Buck hadn’t argued.

“You… Came back?” Eddie asked, the words shaking as they passed his lips.

“Yeah, and crept around the city to avoid any and all Diazes.”

El Paso was not small. Sure, its population was only (“only”) a few hundred thousand compared to Los Angeles’ millions, but still. Avoid certain neighborhoods, certain stores, and they could pass by each other like ships in the night. At least in theory.

In practice, Buck’s luck had always been a bit shoddy.

Eddie stared at him for a moment, before he seemed to remember where they were and what they were doing. He shook his head, turned back around, and continued the climb. Buck followed him in silence.

When they reached the right floor, the stairwell exit was angled downward from them. They had to lift the door open, and then cling to the frame to keep from falling or sliding.

“You ready?” Eddie asked him.

“Yeah, on three,” Buck said. “One. Two. Three!”

He let go and then proceeded to do his best to keep from falling entirely. It was a controlled slide across the floor that ended with him crashing into the wall with a harsh thud. He pushed himself off it just as Eddie landed beside him.

Finding the right room, now that they were on the 11th floor, was easy. They called out and waited for someone to answer, which did not take long. It was locked, which meant Buck got to use the power saw while Eddie got the ropes ready and anchored.

If the station had been a mess after the earthquake, this room was far worse. All the furniture was piled against the window, where Buck was assuming the guy was pinned. Huddled and clinging to a pillar, propping herself against a low wall, was a woman. There was a solid counter, or a bar, that Buck carefully slid himself down to as the woman started making motions like she was about to start climbing up to them.

“Ma’am, I need you to just sit tight,” Buck told her, motioning for Eddie to throw him the rope and then join him. “I will come and get you.”

“No offense.” The man’s voice came from beneath all the furniture, so at least Buck’s guess had been correct. “But I think this might be a situation where ‘women and children first’ does not apply.”

“Did you just say, ‘no offense?’” the woman spat. “Everything about you is offensive.”

“First date?” Buck guessed, running the rope first through his harness and then Eddie’s, ensuring they were both anchored securely.

“He wishes!” the woman shouted. “That pig is married with five kids, but it didn’t stop him from asking me to shower with him.”

“Man, have you not watched the news at all over the past year?” Buck asked him. He patted Eddie on the back, who nodded and started easing himself down the steep slope of the hotel floor in the direction of the pinned man.

“Seriously,” Eddie said, “keep up with the times.”

“Fine!” the man said. “I’m a dinosaur, a relic. Just get me out of here!”

Buck decided that Eddie could deal with that absolute delight. He, instead, opted to focus on the woman who wasn’t being offensive by sheer proximity.

“I’m gonna throw you a rope, okay?” he said, holding up the spare. This one, unlike the one Eddie was using, was not anchored in the hallway above them. Instead, it was all on Buck. “You need to try and secure yourself.”

He waited until he got confirmation, a nod and an “okay,” before tossing the end of the rope to her. She grabbed it and started wrapping it around her waist, Buck keeping an eye on her as she did. At least until Eddie suddenly shouted,

“Don’t move!”

Buck’s attention immediately zeroed in on Eddie and the pinned man. From his spot behind, he could almost make the guy out. He could see a hand, at least, and the sleeve of a bathrobe. And while Buck couldn’t see whatever it was that had Mr. “I Don’t Panic” all panicky, he could certainly hear it, the faint sound of glass cracking.

A tug on the rope attached to Ali stole Buck’s attention again. He braced his foot against the counter, leaning back as she started pulling herself up. Eddie was talking to the pinned man, explaining what he was going to do.

And then the world began to shake again.

“No!” he heard Eddie shout and saw the glass of the window finally give out. 

The man fell and so did the pile of furniture that Eddie had been trying to clear a way through. Buck did not hear the man hit the ground. What he did hear was the scream that the woman let out when the rope, which she had obviously not properly secured, came loose and she began to fall towards another missing window. Eddie flew into motion at Buck’s shout, flinging himself and catching her just as she slid into the open air.

Or, Buck assumed that Eddie caught her. The extra weight that he was suddenly supporting made him think so, because he definitely couldn’t see either of them now. He was flat on his back, holding onto the rope that was tethering Eddie with all his strength.

There was motion through the rope, followed by a series of tugs. And then he allowed himself to relax as first the woman’s and then Eddie’s heads came into view over the counter.

“You,” Buck said as he got as close to standing as he could be with this tilt, “are very heavy.”

“Complain later,” Eddie told him. “We’re taking the slow way down.”

Then

Evan always loved Christmas, growing up. He loved the lights and the snow and the presents. Because who didn’t love getting stuff, right? Sure, it took a few years for Santa to start actually reading his list and get him the things he asked for, and maybe his and Maddie’s piles of presents tended to be smaller than Danny’s, but that was fine.

It was the time of year they got the closest to being anything like the other, “normal” families that Evan saw elsewhere. Mom would bake cookies while Dad - and later Danny - would string lights up all over the eaves of the house and around the windows and down the sidewalk. All white, of course, but that was just on the outside. When it came time to help decorate the tree, always a live one, Maddie and Danny and Evan got to make it as colorful and gaudy as possible.

Even after leaving Hershey for El Paso, Evan and Danny kept the tradition up. Every year, Danny would haul two live trees up three flights of stairs, one for them and one for Alma. Colorful lights and garland and anything else that Evan decided they needed would fill the apartment. Danny always managed to make sure Evan had a pile of presents under the tree to wake up to, still from Santa even though Evan had long ago outgrew that particular belief.

And they would always, always send a Christmas card to Maddie.

Evan always loved Christmas, and he always loved celebrating it with his siblings. That was why, that first holiday season after Eddie, he returned to El Paso despite how much he wanted to fly in the opposite direction. He arrived in the middle of December, helped decorate the apartment like always, posed for the Christmas card, and stayed until just after New Year’s.

The fact that he managed to avoid running into a single Diaz that entire time, despite how they tended to all congregate around any day worth celebrating, was why he wasn’t quite so reluctant to return the following Christmas. 

(Evan knew Danny was upset, when the months went by and he showed no signs of settling down again. But his brother didn’t say anything, no in any of their calls or texts or the emails they sent each other.)

That second Christmas, a full year and a half after Evan first fled El Paso, went much the same. He arrived a couple weeks prior to the day itself and spent time leading up to it decorating with Danny and baking with Alma, and a new Christmas card was sent to Maddie. Danny worked Christmas itself, so they celebrated it the day before and then Evan spent the actual day with Alma and her daughters. But this time, instead of creeping about the city like a thief, he was much more free with where he went. 

Careless about it, really.

It was a Tuesday, December the 27th, when that carelessness caught up to him. He was making a trip into the Food King, because they were out of ingredients needed for him to make breakfast in the morning. It was supposed to be a quick run, in and out, to get some milk and butter.

So of course, in the middle of the dairy aisle, Evan almost literally ran into Shannon.

He’d grabbed a half-gallon from the cooler and was turning to walk away when it happened. She came to a stumbling halt and took several steps backwards while he did the same, his back ending up pressed against the cold glass doors.

“I am so sorry,” she said, and then realized who she was talking to. Her eyes flickered up, to his birthmark, and then went wide. “Evan?”

“Hi, Shannon.” He gave an awkward wave as he did his best to put distance between them, sliding down the row of coolers.

“Wow, it’s been…” She let out a breathy laugh. “A while.”

Not long enough.

“Yeah, it- it has,” he stuttered out. “Um. Where’s, um…”

The question trailed off, and Shannon’s face twisted with a frown.

“Afghanistan,” she said. “Army.”

“Oh.”

Eddie had never stated any particular desire to enlist in the years that Evan knew him. But who knew what happened in the year and a half since Evan left. Since Eddie proposed to Shannon.

Evan never once asked himself why Eddie chose her. He never, not ever, asked himself why. That question, like all the various questions he might’ve asked that started with that word, was easily answered: because Shannon was a woman, and Evan was not.

There was an awkward silence as the two of them stood there, Evan doing his best to avoid meeting Shannon’s eyes while trying to figure out how to get out of this the fastest. He was just about to simply start walking in the opposite direction, as quickly as possible, when Shannon spoke again.

“I’m sorry.”

“You’re… About what?”

She looked him in the eye, said,

“You know why.”

“I- You-” Evan stammered out, trying to understand what she meant. Because there was no way that she meant that. No way that Eddie, that anyone, had told her. Was there? “I- I don’t…”

“It was kinda hard not to figure it out, with how you fled town, with how upset you looked when you heard us,” Shannon said. Evan stepped away from her, legs trembling with the need to run. “Impossible not to figure it out, really, with how upset Eddie was when he realized you were gone.”

“And you- you still… Why?”

A noise from farther down the aisle caught his attention. It was a tiny little garbled cry, and it came from a cart just a few feet away that Evan hadn’t even noticed, so focused was he on Shannon. A toddler was sitting in the seat. Evan guessed a boy, based purely on outfit and hair. There was something… off about the way he held his head, about the movement of his arms and legs.

“Oh.” Several expressions crossed over Shannon’s face as she looked between Evan and the toddler. “That’s… That’s uh, that’s…”

“Your son.”

“Yeah.”

“How old is he?” Evan asked. Because from the looks of things, Eddie and Shannon had not wasted time in his absence.

“He turns two in February,” Shannon answered, eyes darting over his face for a sign of… What? 

“He’s- He’s cute,” he managed to get out, mind a screaming mess at the sight of Eddie and Shannon’s child. This, even more than the ring on Shannon’s hand, was a punch to the gut. “I’m, uh. It was…” He couldn’t say it was nice. It would never, ever be nice to see her. “I’m gonna go.”

Evan had to go past Shannon’s cart to get to the front of the store. Even as he gave it a wide berth, he couldn’t help but look at the toddler, at Eddie’s child. He was cute, maybe the cutest baby ever, and that was all Evan allowed himself to think on the topic as the urge to flee finally overcame him.

He practically sprinted through check-out and then sped back to the apartment. But once there, the need to run did not leave him. Seeing Eddie’s happy little family, his wife and son, broke something in him. Eddie himself might not have been there, but that was only a small mercy.

Danny noticed something was up, because of course he did. Danny just noticed things, always had. But Evan lied and claimed everything was fine, and kept saying that throughout the evening until his brother went to bed. And that was then Evan did the unforgivable.

He knew, even as he was doing it, that what he was doing was so very shitty. But he couldn’t stay in this city another night, not now. If Danny was awake when he left, he’d ask why Evan was leaving so early, try to get him to stay longer, and Evan… Evan really didn’t want to look him in the eyes when he explained that he couldn’t stay here.

He made sure his bags were all in order and sat them by the door. He made pancakes for in the morning and put them in the fridge, to be reheated by his brother. Then he wrote a note with an apology that was not nearly enough and flew out the door.

Evan was in Chihuahua City, Mexico, by the time his brother called.

Now

The woman’s name was Ali Martin, and she was being a real trooper about everything. She was barefoot, so she was slower than them and more careful as she waded through the destruction. She was slower climbing down the tilted stairs, too.

“That’s it, you’re doing great,” Buck encouraged her, shepherding her forward as Eddie led the way. The latter would pause every few steps and turn, sometimes propping himself against the wall with his leg, to help keep her from falling and injuring herself. “You’ve done this before, haven’t you?”

“Yeah, it was my major at the Rhode Island School of Design,” was Ali’s sharp retort. Her next words were more contrite. “Sorry. Sarcasm is my fallback for pretty much everything.”

“I totally get it,” Buck told her. “That one was being sarcastic as we handled a live explosive.”

“You said something stupid,” Eddie shot back. “Which is why I’m always sarcastic when I talk to you.”

“Wow, funny.”

Ali let out some laughter as they continued down the stairs, only to suddenly stop. Buck looked at her in concern as she turned her head to ask over her shoulder,

“You think he’ll still be on the sidewalk when we get down there?”

“No,” Buck lied. No one was going to risk working under that side of the building for someone beyond saving until things were properly secured. Not that she needed to know that. “We’re not going out that side, anyway.”

“Let’s hustle up, you two,” Eddie impatiently urged. “We don’t want to be here for the next aftershock.”

“Wait, what do you mean the next aftershock?” Ali asked, worry shaking her voice.

Eddie looked contrite for a moment, and then he was back to hurrying them forward. Not that it mattered, because they barely made it another flight down before it became clear that there was no path forward. The last aftershock had knocked debris loose, and it cut off access to their exit.

“Now what?” Ali asked them, focused on the debris.

“We go back up,” Eddie told her, as both he and Buck peered up the stairwell.

“Back up!?”

“Ladder One Eighteen, respond.” Bobby’s voice crackled through the radio. “This is Captain Nash, I need a headcount.”

“Ladder One Eighteen, responding,” Diaz replied, hand on his radio. “Diaz, Buckley, we’re good, Cap.”

The others from their station responded. Most of the others, anyway. After Chimney, there was a heavy and expectant pause as one of theirs failed to answer.

“Hen, do you copy?” Bobby asked, sounding worried. “This is Captain Nash for Henrietta Wilson, do you copy?”

Hen did not respond. Buck shared a worried look with Eddie, unwilling to even begin contemplating what that could mean.

“Is that a friend of yours?” Ali asked, looking between them.

Neither of them answered. They simply turned their little group around, and started back the way they came.

Chapter End Notes

It's too bad Buck can't do math, amirite?

Christopher Diaz: Sending father figures fleeing the country since 2011.

Chapter 7

Now

Buck walked backwards up the slope and out of the stairwell, using the doorframe to push himself up. He used his grip on Ali’s hand, and the tether they’d wrapped around her waist and then attached to his harness, to help her up. Eddie brought up the rear, stepping around them as Buck unwrapped the tether from around Ali.

They’d chosen this floor because incident command claimed there was a clear way to the northern stairwell, which would hopefully get them to the ground without taking the expressway. Buck was choosing to believe them, because they were paid the big money and they had those fancy 3D maps. And also because it wasn’t like they had very many options to choose from.

“I know I should’ve asked this before we went to the effort of getting here,” Ali said, panting a bit. “But what if this way is blocked too? Can’t you guys get a ladder to one of the windows?”

“Too risky,” Eddie said. “Building’s shifted too much.”

The hotel’s stability was even more questionable than when Buck and Eddie first went in. For the aerial to reach the floor they were on, the ladder truck would need to be parked right next to the building. And if another aftershock struck, or if the building finally lost the struggle against gravity, there’d be no time for it to get clear.

“We got this,” Buck assured her. “We’ll get you out of here.”

Unfortunately, their immediate way forward to the northern stairwell was blocked. Nothing severe, just part of the ceiling and some wooden beams that had fallen. Buck got started with Eddie on moving it out of the way so they could progress. Ali, meanwhile, apparently decided that it was a convenient time to take advantage of the room service cart someone abandoned in the hall, grabbing one of the tiny and overly expensive bottles of liquor to down.

“What?” she demanded when she noticed them staring. “It’s after five, and it isn’t slowing us down.”

Buck was about to say something quippy about it, but another noise caught his attention. Faint, hard to hear, but it sounded like someone yelling.

“Hey, hey,” he said, getting Eddie and Ali’s attention. “You guys hear that?”

They were all silent for a moment, straining their ears to listen. This time, Buck definitely heard it, a man calling for help from the room they were stopped outside of. A Halligan served as a skeleton key, and soon both he and Eddie were sliding down to where a man was lying against a wall.

“I knew help would come,” the man said. His face was covered in blood, trailing from his right temple. He was dressed as a hotel employee, and his name badge read Batari.

“Yeah, we got you,” Eddie said, already falling in what Buck had started deeming his Medic Mode.

“Don’t move, just relax,” Buck instructed. “You need to stay as still as possible, okay?”

“We have to roll him over,” Eddie said. Buck nodded and reached down to grip Batari’s head to keep his neck still. Eddie rolled him onto his side, an action that caused him to scream.

“I- I soiled myself,” Batari confessed. “Not my proudest moment.”

“Forget about it,” Eddie said, yanking off one of Batari’s shoes and then his sock.

Buck left him to it, because he had already guessed what it meant. He gently laid Batari’s head back on the floor and allowed himself to slide down the slope again. As he started searching for something flat and sturdy enough that they could use, he heard Eddie ask,

“Can you wiggle your toes for me?”

Buck was not surprised when Eddie slid down to join him a moment later.

“Definitely a spinal injury,” the former army medic confirmed. “We’ve got to move him out.”

“On what?” Buck asked him. “We don’t have a backboard, and every millimeter counts.”

“Guys, what about this?” Ali, when they looked up, was holding an ironing board.

“That could work,” Eddie said, and then turned back to Buck. “But how are we getting him down the stairwell?”

“Maybe,” he said, an idea forming in his head, “we don’t use the stairwell.”

Eddie was right to be concerned about the stairs. Climbing down them right now was difficult enough, trying to do it while carrying Batari between them would be almost impossible. But the stairs weren’t the only way down, were they?

Together, they got Batari secured to the ironing board. Then they carried- Well, at first one of them pushed him up the floor while the other pulled. But when they got into the hallway again, they picked him up and carried him to the nearest elevator shaft, which required an upward climb. When they got the doors open, putting Batari down to do so in a spot where he wouldn’t be sliding away, Eddie and Ali stuck their heads into the shaft for a look.

“At least we’re not blocked by any debris,” Eddie remarked.

“Or an elevator,” Ali added. Which, somehow, was not a possibility that Buck had taken into consideration, And also, if the elevator wasn’t below them, then…?

Eddie seemed to have the same thought as Buck, because he turned onto his back and looked up. Ali followed his gaze. Buck could see her face suddenly contort with concern.

“How much do you think that weighs?” she asked.

“No more than three thousand pounds,” was Eddie’s casual answer.

Buck poked his head in as well so he could  see what they were dealing with. The way down was clear, just like Eddie said, and the elevator was dangling a few floors above them. It should be safe there, as long as there hadn’t been any flooding to corrode the cables. And as long as the earthquake and aftershocks hadn’t damaged anything too terribly important.

“I like our chances,” he said.

“Of course you do.” Eddie’s eye roll was audible.

Once again, they broke out the ropes. They found a suitable anchor spot in the hall, and then attached a rope to the ironing board and one to Eddie’s harness, running it through Buck’s just like before. Eddie and Batari went down first, Buck feeding rope while Ali anxiously hovered at his side.

“Get down here, Buck!” Eddie shouted, once he’d reached the doors to the floor they’d decided on.

“On my way,” Buck called down as the other man started forcing the doors open. He looked at Ali. “You ready?”

“Do I have a choice?” she replied, to which he only shrugged. “Yeah, okay.”

She didn’t have a harness, and they didn’t have a rescue strap or an extra rope. That meant that when the both of them started down, it was with Ali wrapped around Buck like a koala. In any other scenario, he probably would’ve enjoyed it a lot more. In this one, it just made the task of handling the ropes much, much harder.

And then, because Buck’s luck continued to be shoddy, the building began to shake once again. It sent him and Ali free falling for a few feet before he could control their descent and bring them to a stop. Up above, the elevator groaned and sparked as it began to slide down.

“Eddie!” Buck cried out. “Eddie, kick that door open!”

He didn’t look down to see if Eddie was doing as demanded, focusing instead on double-timing their descent, kicking off the wall and letting as much of the rope go as he safely could at a time.

“Move your ass!” Eddie shouted up, and that was a very good idea.

Buck kicked off once more, letting the rope go so fast it squealed through his harness, and swung them through the open doors. He landed flat on his back, knocking the wind out of him, while Ali hit the ground beside him. Eddie was immediately on him.

“Are you okay?” he asked as he looked Buck over, hands running over him and patting him down.

“I’m fine, I’m fine,” Buck told him, reaching up to wrap his fingers around Eddie’s arms. He had vertigo from how familiar it felt to have the other man’s hands on him like this, how right and comfortable it was. Like slipping into his favorite pair of jeans, the denim worn and broken in. “I told you I liked our chances.”

Eddie’s frantic motions stopped as he directed his full glare down at him.

“Pendejo.”

Buck just grinned up at him.

“I’m fine, too.” Ali’s voice popped the bubble they were in. “Not to ruin your moment or anything.”

Eddie tore himself away, as if Buck’s touch was suddenly burning.

“No moment to ruin,” he said, looking everywhere but at him. “Let’s get out of here.”

Buck sighed and pushed himself up, ignoring the confusion on Ali’s face in favor of helping carry Batari.

Then

It was difficult to plan a date when both people involved were still firmly in the closet. Though, recently, Evan was realizing that he didn’t particularly care to stay that way. The more time went by, the more kisses - and other things - that he and Eddie shared, the more he wanted to climb onto the roof of the school and shout for all to hear that he had the hottest boyfriend. He understood, now, why Timothy had been so open and free about himself in those first couple months of freshman year.

Unlike Timothy, Evan had more than two years of football conditioning under his belt and an older brother that was quite liberal with a baseball bat. He figured he could weather any discontent that came his way about who he kissed. The bigots that targeted Timothy would find that Evan wouldn’t be pushed around as easily nor would he go down quietly.

He and Eddie were not of like minds about this, however. Eddie was very much not ready for his family and classmates to know that he liked other guys. And while Evan would happily throw his own closet door open, rip it off its hinges entirely, he couldn’t do so without possibly dragging his boyfriend out with him. So, Evan remained closeted and dates remained difficult to plan.

For the first few months, Evan tried to sate his inner romantic by trying to spruce things up on the nights Danny was at work and Eddie came over. Candlelit dinners, for example, that sometimes consisted of what Evan cooked and sometimes of what they decided to order in. And while he tried buying his boyfriend flowers at the beginning, Eddie’s reluctance to take them home (and then have to explain why Evan was giving them to him) meant that it didn’t last long.

Hell, Eddie was damned paranoid about the two of them touching in public, nowadays, for fear of anyone getting the right idea. Even when he settled into the idea that never touching just made things more suspicious, he still sometimes got anxious about something as simple as Evan’s arm around his shoulders.

The point is, all of that made actually dating quite difficult. But Evan was, if nothing else, persistent. He badgered and begged and convinced and convinced, until Eddie agreed to go out together for a dinner and a movie. But they couldn’t go to any place fancy, no sir. That would be too obvious. They ended up going to Denny’s. Truly, the most romantic place to eat in the city.

Things went okay at first. They got seated, they ate their food, they joked and goofed off as much as they ever did. Really, they were best friends before they became anything else, and hanging out together was always fun no matter what they were doing. Eddie was so relaxed that he didn’t even mind when Evan started playing footsie with him under the table, simply smirking and rolling his eyes.

But Evan’s luck was a bit shoddy, at best.

“Eddie!”

The sudden cry of his name had Eddie bolting up out of his relaxed slump, his feet disentangling from Evan’s as his eyes went wide.

“H- Hey, guys.”

Evan turned in his seat, spotting the person who had shouted. Todd had been in at least one class with him a semester since they started school, and he was on the baseball team with Eddie. There was another guy with him, also on the baseball team, though Evan could not remember his name. He knew the two girls they were with, though, a pair of cheerleaders named Teresa and Ava.

(Maybe Denny’s was a romantic place to eat?)

“What are y’all doing?” Todd asked.

“Oh, uh, we’re just… We’re just hanging out,” Eddie stammered out. “You know, just… Hanging.”

“Yeah, we’re just grabbing a bite,” Evan said, stepping in. “And we’re gonna see a movie.”

“And then we’re gonna go find some hot chicks,” his boyfriend blurted out. Evan just barely resisted the urge to twist back around to stare at him. “Cause, you- you know, hot chicks… love athletes…”

“Right on,” the second guy - Diego? He looked like a Diego. - reached over to high-five Eddie.

“We should probably pay and get out of here,” Evan said. “Don’t want to miss the trailers.”

“Right!” Eddie sounded relieved. “Let’s- Let’s go.”

They didn’t say anything to each other until the food was paid for and they were climbing back into the truck. At which point Eddie hunched over and pressed his forehead into the steering wheel.

“Lo siento,” he whispered. “I’m sorry, I’m sorry. I don’t know why I said that.”

“It’s fine, Eds.” Evan wanted to reach over and touch him, hug him, do something to comfort him. But he wasn’t sure what kind of reaction he’d receive right now.

“Do you mind if…” Eddie trailed off. Evan knew him well enough to guess the rest.

“If we go back to the apartment?”

“Please.”

“Yeah,” he said, and hoped his disappointment wasn’t too obvious. “Yeah, sure. We’ve got movies there, right?”

“Gracias,” Eddie said with all feeling, sitting up and starting the truck.

Oh well, Evan thought as they pulled out. There was always another day.

Now

Buck and Eddie reached the ground level just in time to hear Bobby request assistance to rescue Hen and be denied by Chief Williams, who ordered everyone to evacuate. The pair double-timed it outside, handing Batari off to the on-site paramedics and briefing them on his condition, before rushing off to confront the chief. Because there was no way that they were going to just leave Hen trapped, and Buck knew that neither Bobby nor Chimney were likely to do so, either.

Unfortunately for the battalion chief, Buck was very, very persistent. She probably gave in and agreed simply to make him go away, which he was not bothered by at all. Especially when, along with a group of volunteers, they managed to free Hen, a little girl, and a tiny dog from the parking garage.

While putting away gear and equipment with Eddie, Buck’s phone began to buzz. He pulled it out to find he was suddenly being flooded with several hours worth of worried texts from Maddie, along with a couple from Danny.

“Hey!” he said, getting Eddie’s attention as he typed back replies and assured them both he was fine. “We’ve got service.”

The other man wasted no time in checking his own phone.

“Everything okay?” Buck asked him.

“Yeah,” Eddie said, typing away. “One of the teachers stayed with him.”

“Good, that’s good,” he said. “Then let’s hurry up and finish this so you can go get him.”

Were it so easy, though. There hadn’t been time to take stock of the station before they were rushing off to the hotel. They came back to find the next shift already arrived and cleaning the mess up inside the station. The mess outside, however, had yet to be touched. And that included a down tree in the parking lot that was blocking a truck.

After they’d showered and changed, Buck came out to find Eddie staring at the truck, hands fisting his hair in dismay. Maybe it was because he had too much heart, or maybe it was because everything they did together over the past day had him sliding back into feelings that he wished he could kill. Either way, he opened his mouth and said,

“I’ll take you.”

Eddie turned, mouth parted in surprise.

“What?”

“Danny’s getting Maddie,” Buck said. “So I’ll take you to pick up Chris.”

“Gracias,” Eddie said with all feeling, grabbing his bag from where he’d let it drop it to the pavement and sprinting for Buck’s jeep.

The drive to Chris’ school was slow and winding, filled with detours to avoid a broken overpass or scenes of accidents. Eddie spent the car ride bouncing his leg and cursing every roadblock - literal or metaphorical - they came across. Taking off his seatbelt before they even came to a stop at the front entrance, he just about tripped over himself getting out. Buck watched as Eddie ran through the open doors and came to a sliding stop on his knees to embrace a little boy with crutches and glasses.

Something in him ached at the sight.

Eddie lingered long enough to talk to the teacher that had stayed, probably thanking him profusely, before carrying Chris out to the jeep. He had his head bent down, whispering something to the kid that had him laughing. The world’s cutest toddler had grown up to become the world’s cutest seven year old, with a mess of curly hair and a beaming smile.

“Hi!” Chris chirped as his father buckled him into the backseat, the crutches laid carefully in the floorboard.

Buck drew a breath and gathered his courage, and then twisted in his seat to smile at him.

“Hey.”

The kid's eyes went wide behind his glasses.

“You’re Dad’s friend!” he said, pointing. “From the picture!”

“Picture?” Buck asked, looking at Eddie. The other man had his shoulders hunched up and avoided his gaze as he closed the door and got back into his seat. “Uh, yeah. I’m… Yeah, I’m Buck.”

“That’s a funny name,” the kid said, giggling. “I’m Christopher.”

“Wanna know a secret?” Buck asked, leaning in. “That’s my middle name.”

“That’s so cool!” Chris said. “Isn’t that cool, Dad?”

“It sure is,” Eddie said, still not looking at Buck.

“Hey, Buck?” Chris asked.

“Yeah?” Buck replied, turning forward once again and pulling away from the curb.

“Do dogs know they’re dogs?”

Buck blinked, looked away from the road long enough for Eddie to actually meet his eyes again and shrug.

“I… Don’t know,” Buck confessed. “I think so? I’m sure I could look it up.”

“Maybe they think we’re bigger, less hairier dogs that walk funny.”

“Maybe!”

The kid was a constant and steady stream of chatter the entire drive, and Buck had no problem keeping up. More than once, he spotted Eddie smiling at him, only for the man to look away when he was caught. By the time they were pulling up in front of Eddie’s place, Chris was still talking and let out a disappointed groan when Eddie started pulling him out of the jeep.

“But I like Buck,” he complained. “Can he come inside? I want to show him my room.”

“Uh.” Eddie shot him a bewildered look over his son’s head. “Maybe some other time, mijo, he has to get home to his own family.”

“Okay,” the kid pouted, and then turned in his father’s arms to wave. “Bye, Buck!”

“Yeah, bye,” was Buck’s weak reply. He lingered long enough to make sure the pair made it in okay, and then he was heading to the apartment as quickly as he could.

Danny and Maddie were sitting at the kitchen table when he walked in, the former with hair still wet from a shower and the other still wearing her dispatcher uniform. They fell silent at his entrance and watched as he beelined for the fridge to pull out an unopened bottle of wine. He didn’t bother with a glass after he popped it open, simply tipped the entire thing back and chugged.

“Jesus, Buck,” Danny said.

“I thought my day was stressful,” Maddie remarked. “Was yours that bad?”

“I met Eddie’s kid,” Buck said, slamming the bottle onto the table and wiping his mouth. “He’s fucking adorable.”

An expression of understanding mixed with concern crossed his brother’s face. His sister, meanwhile, just looked very, very confused.

Chapter 8

Now

Eddie blindly slapped his alarm clock, shutting the damn thing up, and threw the covers off himself. This morning, like all mornings, felt like it came far too early. He had never been the type of person who greeted the rising sun with a smile, not like a certain someone he knew. Evan used to make fun of him for the way he would walk into school, bleary-eyed and grumpy and monosyllabic.

Years of military service hadn’t broken him of his antipathy for the early hours, it just taught him how to bully through it.

He didn’t truly open his eyes until he was pushing open Chris’ door. The sight of his son’s messy curls peeking out from under his comforter brought a soft smile to his face. Eddie regretted so many things in his life, so many of his choices. But some of them he would never undo. He would never, ever give Chris up.

“Rise and shine, little man,” Eddie said as he rapped his knuckles on the door.

His son very much was the type to start every day bright-eyed and bushy-tailed. Chris sat up with a wide smile, tiny arms stretching. From there, they fell into their morning routine: Eddie did his morning exercises while Chris did his stretches; they had their breakfast of cereal, which usually ended up with Chris pouring out a little too much; they took their turns in the shower and then stood in front of the bathroom sink together, brushing their teeth and, in Eddie’s case, shaving.

Getting dressed for the day was always where they got delayed. When it came to some things, Chris was simply slower than others. Eddie was fully ready to go, just putting his watch on, when he checked on his kid. Chris was still trying to get his last sock pulled onto his foot. 

It was instinctive, that step forward to help him. But Chris wanted so desperately to be independent, to do things himself, and Eddie did not take another step. Because he would sooner get shot a fourth (and fifth and sixth) time than even try to stifle his kid like that.

(He remembered the one and only time that Margaret and Phillip came to El Paso. Honestly, Eddie had thought they were dead until that surprise visit, because neither brother ever really spoke of them. One of the biggest parts that stuck out in his memory of it was the fight between Danny and his mother over Evan playing football. The way that Margaret tried to dictate what Evan could do, and the way that Danny raged in response.

Before Chris, Eddie had simply seen Danny as an older brother helping to raise his younger sibling, just like Eddie. After Chris, he came to a whole new understanding of the man.)

Unfortunately, letting Chris go at his own pace meant that they inevitably ran late. Los Angeles traffic certainly did not help, either. It meant that Eddie was definitely running behind by the time they pulled into Abuela’s driveway, and he rushed to get Chris out of the car and then go knock on the door.

“Eddito!” she greeted him when she stepped out. Then she spotted Chris, making his slow way to the porch with his crutches. “Eddie, help him out.”

“He wants to do it.”

She frowned at him, before turning to greet her great grandson with a smile.

“Hola, mi angel,” Abuela said.

“Hola,” Chris chirped back.

“Thank you for taking him,” Eddie said.

“Taking him is not a problem,” Abuela replied, waving his thanks out of the air. “Giving him back, that’s the hard part.”

Eddie chuckled and nodded and started running for his truck, only for Abuela’s aggrieved tutting to stop him in his tracks. In his rush to not be any later than he was, he had forgotten. Like the dutiful grandchild he was, he turned and marched back as Chris laughed, to kiss her on the cheek and allow her to make the cross over him. He didn’t believe in it, but Abuela did and it made her feel better.

And maybe it would be enough to ward off Bobby’s annoyance over his tardiness.

The past month had felt like getting slapped in the face with the past, so it only made sense that they’d eventually have a call that ran with that theme. Buck just wished it involved anything other than this place.

Saddle Ranch was a bar he used to tend at, back when he was still aimlessly bouncing between jobs in the city. It was country themed, complete with a mechanical bull, and the building was made to look like a twisted fairground version of an old saloon. There were appropriately dressed mannequins in the windows of the second floor, and above the entrance was a wooden cowboy on a wooden horse that looked like it was exploding out of the wall. Buck was fairly certain the fact he already owned a cowboy hat was a large part of what got him hired.

Considering the usual clientele, Buck was not at all surprised that they were being called in the middle of the afternoon to rescue someone who got their head stuck in a tailpipe.

“Oh God,” Eddie intoned when they pulled up in the truck. He looked as if he had just seen Hell.

“I used to work here,” Buck confessed. The judgmental expression that appeared on the other man’s face was sadly not unfamiliar to him. “If the floor is sticky, it’s probably just beer.”

“Gross.”

Once they’d grabbed what they needed and headed in, Buck was greeted by exactly what he expected. There was a woman trying her best to stay on the mechanical bull, which wasn’t even bucking that hard, while people cheered on. A group of drunken women was in the way, excited about how “the strippers” were there, and he was fairly certain he felt one “accidentally” grab his ass as he tried to get past them.

Out back behind the bar was where the truck, its tailpipe, and the stuck woman were located. Once the crowd of gawkers got out of the way, Buck could see the woman on her hands and knees, partially beneath a compensating-for-something truck. The truck’s presumable owner was standing right next to it and Buck was absolutely going to label him as Asshole in his head, just off looks alone.

“What’s her name?” Chimney asked as Hen knelt down to start examining the patience.

“Betty,” Asshole answered, only for the trapped woman to immediately correct him with a tinny sounding,

“Jennifer.”

“Sorry, I thought you meant my truck.”

See? Asshole.

“This is your truck?” Bobby asked.

“Yeah, but it’s not like I backed into her,” Asshole said. “She did this out of her own free will.”

“You dared her to,” said one of the two women that had met them outside, one of Jennifer’s friends.

“We were flirting.”

“Your idea of flirting with a girl is daring her to stick her head in your tailpipe?” was Eddie’s incredulous sounding question.

Buck shook his head and busied himself with sliding beneath the truck on the creeper, opting it was better to keep his mouth shut. He held his hand out to Eddie, who handed him a Sharpie. While Chimney chatted with Jennifer, Buck marked off where he was fairly certain her head stopped.

“Why is this tailpipe so large?” he heard Chimney ask while Hen started poking around on Jennifer.

“It’s custom,” Asshole responded.

“Where’d she get those bruises on her arm?” Bobby asked, almost accusingly.

“That wasn’t me.”

“It’s from the bull,” the second of Jennifer’s friends said. “She rode it like, five times.”

“People like to use it to show off their skill for, uh, riding other things,” Buck chimed in, still under the truck. “But they usually end up just getting tossed off. I always managed to stay on, though.”

Riding the bull in there had gotten him a lot of attention, back in the day.

“Did not need to know that, Buckaroo,” was Chimney’s response. Buck grinned, though he knew no one could see it. “There’s no way we’re gonna get a collar on her.”

“I got it,” Hen said, though he couldn’t see from here just what she “got.”

“Get me out of this thing,” Jennifer demanded.

Buck pushed himself out from under the truck and sat up. He noticed Chimney was no longer there and wondered where he went.

“These things are meant to increase the power to the engine,” Buck remarked. He knew a few things about cars and trucks, mostly from hanging around Eddie while he worked at that garage in high school.

“TSA Two Thirty saw should do the job,” Bobby said, kneeling down to peer at the tailpipe.

“Like a knife through butter.”

“Chim!”

“Way ahead of you, Cap.” So that’s where Chimney went, to get one of their turnout coats and the saw. He handed them both to Bobby.

“What!?” Asshole was not happy. “No, I spent twelve hundred bucks on that tailpipe.”

“You might want to close your eyes for this part,” Bobby told him, setting down the saw so he could pull on the coat.

Buck stepped back, because he knew his role in these things. With the captain handling the power tool, and the medical stuff covered by the paramedics and Eddie, he was kinda superfluous at the moment. The first of Jennifer’s drunken friends took the opportunity to swan up to him and hold out her phone.

“Just type in your number,” she said, “and I’ll text you so you have mine.”

He caught the flicker of a scowl on Eddie’s face, gone before it could really settle into place.

“Tempting, but gonna have to be a no, sorry,” Buck told her. “Cap doesn’t like it when we give out our numbers while we’re working.”

“Hi.” The second of Jennifer’s drunken friends had decided Eddie was a good target, apparently. “Do you have Snapchat?”

“No.” Eddie looked uncomfortable, but he was handling it better than the last time Buck witnessed him weathering a girl’s advances. “And uh, I don’t think I’m what you’re looking for, I have a son.”

“That’s great. So do I.”

“I’m gonna puke!” Jennifer shouted from inside the tailpipe.

“You better not, these fire guys are really hot.”

“Alright.” Chimney apparently reached his limit with them, because he was stepping in to shepherd the drunken women away. “Let’s move the peanut gallery a few steps over this way. Thank you. Everybody back up a little, thank you.”

Buck leaned in and whispered to Eddie,

“He’s insecure about not being flirted with.”

“He can have ‘em,” Eddie grumbled. Buck laughed.

The TSA 230 saw did, indeed, cut through Asshole’s tailpipe like butter. $1200 butter that sparked as the spinning blade bit into it, but butter nonetheless. Once the cutting was done, and they could get Jennifer into a sitting position, they had to actually remove the pipe from off her head. They needed lubricant for that, which one of the drunken friends - the one who had flirted with Buck - was a bit too eager to provide. People came prepared to this kind of bar.

(Like he told Eddie, it was probably just beer making the floors sticky. Not definitely.)

Thankfully, there was lube in the paramedics’ bag, a tube of it that did not have a questionable history of use. Jennifer blinked at the sudden brightness when they got her head free, looking a bit dazed as the crowd around them clapped.

“Wow,” she said when she looked up at Buck and Eddie. “You really are hot.”

Buck preened.

“Would you like us to transport you to the hospital?” Hen asked her. “Get checked out?”

“She just needs another drink,” Drunk Friend #1 said.

“What she needs to do is go home.” Bobby was radiating full-on Dad energy. It had been happening more and more over the past few months. Buck wondered if it was because he felt more comfortable around them, or if it was the relationship with Athena getting him to open up more, or a combination of factors. “All three of you do. In a cab.”

The captain bent over at the waist, hands on his knees, to look the still sitting Jennifer in the eyes.

“Listen to me,” he said. “When you sober up, if you have a headache or blurry vision, or you feel confused, you get yourself to a hospital. Understand?”

Jennifer stared at him blankly. And then she broke into giggles. It was probably a fair bet that the captain’s words were completely lost on her.

“So Christopher is the reason you turned her down,” Buck said, bumping shoulders with Eddie while they walked away from the truck and the crowd of day drinkers. “No other reason.”

“That and they weren’t my type,” the other man said, eyes fixed ahead of them. Buck couldn’t help the snort he let out at the words. “Look, it’s complicated when you have a kid.”

“That’s a weak excuse.”
