Eddie stopped in his tracks, turned to glare at him.

“And what about you? If you weren’t working, if Cap didn’t care, you’d just take her up on that offer?”

“Well, yeah.” Buck shrugged. “I’m not dating anyone, or married. Nothing stopping me from showing off my bull riding skills. Though she probably wouldn’t appreciate them as much as, I dunno, the guy that named his truck.”

He was being petty, and he knew that he was being petty. It was partially fueled by the way Eddie was insisting that Chris was the only reason he turned those women down, and partially fueled by how the man got upset at the mere idea of Buck being with someone else. Part of it, too, was old hurts reopened and the fact that Buck was still trying to wrap his head around the fact that the other man had named his son, the son he had with someone else, after him.

Eddie opened his mouth to shoot back, but Buck never got to find out what it would have been. The other man’s phone began to buzz, breaking their bubble and causing the man to take several steps back. Buck hadn’t even realized how close they’d gotten.

“Hello?” was how Eddie answered, already sounding anxious. It was followed a couple beats later by even more anxious sounding, “What? Which one?”

Buck recognized that tone of worry, and he wasted no time shouting for the captain.

They took the ambulance, he and Eddie, while Chimney and Hen rode back to the station in the truck. Much like the drive to pick up Chris after the earthquake, Eddie spent this one bouncing his leg and cursing anything that slowed them down. The moment they were parked, just like then, Eddie was unbuckling and sprinting for the doors of the hospital. Buck followed, though he dragged his heels a bit.

He was not certain how this was going to go for him.

Josephina “Pepa” Diaz was in a ground floor waiting room. Eddie wasted no time cutting a path her way when they got there, and she stood from her chair to greet him. Buck lingered some feet away.

“Tía. ¿Qué pasó? Is Christopher okay?” he asked, rapid fire, as they hugged.

“You mean Prince Charming? He’s peachy,” Pepa assured him, laughing, and pointed to where the boy was laughing with a pair of nurses. “It’s your abuela, she broke her hip.”

“What? How?”

“She was out back on the steps and calling him to come inside, and she lost her balance,” she explained. “Christopher called Nine-One-One. The rescue got there really quick.”

“I want to see her,” Eddie insisted, but his aunt shook her head.

“No, she’s sleeping right now. And who is-?” She was turning as she spoke and she froze as she actually looked at Buck. Her eyes darted to his birthmark and then she was suddenly stomping his way. There was no time for Buck to run or hide before the woman was on him, smacking at his arm as she let out a string of angry, rapidfire Spanish.

“H- Hi, Pepa,” Buck said, pulling in his arms and turning slightly in a futile attempt to give her a smaller target.

“Evan Buckley!” she spat. “Do you know how worried Mama was when Eddie told us you left on your own? Where have you been!? Have you been here, in this city, the entire time!?”

“N- No, just… Just for a couple years- Ow! Pepa!”

“And you!” Pepa snapped, turning on Eddie. The man had been too busy being amused over Buck’s predicament to think ahead, and now it was too late as she began smacking him. “How long have you known he was here!?”

“Since he started at the station,” Buck helpfully chimed in.

“Chinga tu ma- Ow! Perdón, Pepa! Perdón!” Eddie glowered as he rubbed at his arm, grumbling out a petulant “Pendejo” under his breath.

“Oh, save it for when your abuela hears about this,” Pepa tutted, shaking her hand out. “Wait. Buckley. Is he the Buck that Christopher was talking our ears off about?”

“Maybe- Ow!”

Pepa let out a huff and shook her head, before seeming to gather herself.

“Anyway!” she said. “Eddie, you cannot keep leaving him with your abuela. She’s not up to it.”

“I know, I’m sorry.” Eddie sighed and ran a hand through his hair while Buck looked between the two Diazes. “I’m trying to find permanent help, it’s just… There’s too many forms to fill out, it’s worse than the V.A.”

“I can’t believe your gringa ex stuck you with all this.”

“I’m not stuck, Tia.”

“Do you have to go back to work?” Pepa asked. Eddie didn’t say anything, not that he needed to. They all knew the answer to that. “And you’re not stuck. I’ll keep him tonight, but you need to get this figured out.”

Eddie’s grump face was out in full force, though it was wiped away the moment Chris called for him. He turned and wasted no time going to the kid, scooping him up in his arms as Chris laughed.

“It’s been rough, hasn’t it?” Buck guessed as he watched them. He remembered those first few months in Texas, with an apartment devoid of furniture outside of their bedrooms.

“Raising any child alone is rough,” Pepa said, side-eying him. Buck shrunk a little under it, wondering just how much she was judging him for not being there. “My nephew is a saint, but I pray for him anyway.”

Buck wasn’t sold on Eddie being a saint, and he definitely wasn’t going to start praying anytime soon. But as Danny liked to say, his heart was far too big, and he did want to help. Luckily, there was something he could do right away. He excused himself and slipped out into the hall, so he could call Bobby.

Chapter 9

Now

For being the personification of everything that killed Buck’s one and only real relationship before Abby, Christopher continued to be unfairly adorable. Buck had always held a soft spot for kids, from Tia Alma’s daughters to the baby in the pipe he helped rescue, and Christopher just pressed himself right into it. The kid was sunshine incarnate and it was infectious, making everyone around him light up.

Bobby was, of course, perfectly okay with having Chris at the station until Pepa could pick him up. Buck figured he would be, given Bobby’s general nature and what he remembered of Danny’s old crew in El Paso. The look of surprised gratitude that Eddie shot his way, once Bobby said that Buck had called ahead and explained the situation, was almost overwhelming. The hours that followed was perhaps the most fun that Buck ever had at work, with most of that time spent goofing off and playing with an actual child.

It was a legitimate disappointment when Pepa finally did swing by. Everyone was disappointed, Buck was sure.

“I feel bad for him,” he told Maddie and Danny later that evening as he finished making dinner for them. “Eddie, not Christopher.”

“I’m sure,” he heard Danny grumble. He shot his brother a glare, which Danny ignored in favor of taking a swig of his beer. With Maddie sharing space with them, there hadn’t been any Eddie related arguments yet. Yet.

“Christopher’s great,” Buck said, turning his back on both his brother and the argument they were putting off. Not that there needed to be an argument, because he already knew everything Danny would say about Eddie and he also knew he’d agree with them. It didn’t mean he wanted to hear it, though. “He’s smart, adorable, funny in a kid way. He just needs a little extra help.”

“Cerebral Palsy, right?” Maddie asked, popping open the bottle of wine she picked up on the way home. She had asked Buck what kind of wine would pair with the albondigas he was making, but that was very much beyond him, even if he’d come out of the relationship with Abby with a taste for the stuff. As far as he was concerned, there was red wine and there was white wine, and he liked red wine better.

Maddie got white wine, because sometimes she decided to be mean to him.

“Yeah, Tia Pepa said he got stuck in the birth canal and there were complications.”

“Well did Pepa explain what’s happening with the child care situation? ‘Cause there are programs.”

“Has he tried befriending his neighbors?” Danny asked as he leaned against the kitchen table. “Even if you were older, Alma was still there to check on you when I was working.”

“I guess you were a single father,” Maddie said with a frown. “That’s weird to think about.”

“And I didn’t even get there the fun way.”

“Eddie’s working on it,” Buck said as he stirred the pot. “He’s got insurance and there’s other stuff through the city, the state, the county. But the requirements are all different. Applying to one could disqualify you from another. It’s a bureaucratic mess, I can’t wrap my head around it.”

“I was a nurse,” Maddie reminded him. “The only people who can truly navigate a bureaucracy are the people who work inside it.”

“It shouldn’t be that way. Eddie always wondering how to take care of Christopher, and Christopher feeling like a burden.”

“Eddie doesn’t feel that way, does he?”

“You didn't feel that way, did you?” Danny cut in, sitting up with his gaze laser-focused on Buck.

“No!” Buck said, to both their questions. “Never, not even a little.”

“Good.” His brother relaxed against the table again.

“Eddie loves that kid like crazy, he’s a really great dad.”

For some reason, Maddie was suddenly smiling. Buck did not trust that smile, nor did he trust the amusement that was flickering to life behind his sister’s eyes. It did not in any way mean good things for him.

“So,” she said. “When do we get to meet this new crush of yours?”

Danny had been taking a drink and ended up choking on it. Buck hunched his shoulders up around his ears and purposefully turned back to the stove so he didn’t have to look at either of them.

“What?” Maddie sounded confused and amused and a bit offended. “You both know I’m okay with it.”

“Yeah, that’s- That’s not-” Danny was trying to get the words out while sputtering.

“I’m just saying,” she said. “I wasn’t here for Abby, I wasn’t there for that boyfriend you had in high school. I want to be here for this one.”

“Thank you,” Buck said. “But uh, there’s… Yeah, this isn’t something to be here for.”

“If you say so.” Oh, Maddie, if only you knew. “Anyways, I have news! I’ve found an apartment.”

That had him turning back around, so he could join in staring at her with Danny. 

“Already?” Danny asked.

“I saved up a lot of money in my flight fund,” she said. “It’s not far from work. Two bedrooms, secure building. Parking included.”

“So you’re really moving out?” Buck tried to keep the disappointment out of his voice, but knew he wasn’t doing a good job of it.

“I told you both I couldn’t live here forever,” she said. “When I left Doug, I didn’t have a plan. I just grabbed up all my stuff and ran as fast and as far as I could.”

“Sounds like us,” Buck said, grinning at Danny. “He threw darts at a map.”

“I’ll only know this is all real until I’m standing on my own two feet,” Maddie said. “And it’s about time that Dan gets his bed back.”

“I’m fine,” Danny grumbled, though all of them knew he’d be thankful to not be sleeping on the couch. Even if he was too stubborn to let Buck take any turns on it.

After dinner was eaten, after Maddie went to bed, Buck stayed up with Danny. The pair of them sat on the couch, pillows and sheets piled on the floor beside it, and put their feet up as they watched some trashy action movie playing on FX.

“I should be happy for her, shouldn’t I?” Buck said. “I mean, she’s here and away from Doug, she’s building a new life. I should be happy for her. Right?”

“I’d be happier if she stayed here,” Danny said, gesturing around their apartment. “At least she isn’t moving as far away this time, just to Eagle Rock.”

“Maybe without Doug, she won’t just cut us off again,” he said, and then frowned at himself. “Oh. I didn’t realize I was so bitter about that.”

Danny just reached over to pat him on the shoulder.

Then

It was the text from Tia Alma, about the rude couple knocking on his apartment door, that piqued Daniel’s concern. It was the text from Eddie that had him flying out of the station with an excuse about a family emergency. Because it was a text that said people claiming to be Daniel and Evan’s parents were at the door.

Not cutting them off had been part of the agreement that got them to hand over custody of Evan so easily. Daniel had abided by that, because however awful they definitely were, they were still his mom and dad. He called every week, he sent them cards on birthdays and Christmas. All of it done so he could keep them out of their new lives, so he could keep them away from Evan.

There’d been no warning about this visit. Mom hadn’t said anything when he last called, nor had Dad. They hadn’t bothered asking if it was a convenient time for either of them, or if the two of them were even wanted. They decided it was a good time for them to come down, so they did.

It took entirely too long to get to the apartment. Every moment spent on the road was another moment that Evan was with their parents without Daniel there to protect him. The parking job when he got there was the worst he’d ever done, but he didn’t have a single care about that. He just grabbed his bag and sprinted up the stairs to the fourth floor, not stopping until he was flinging their door open.

The first thing Daniel looked for was his brother, sitting on the couch with his back ramrod straight and his eyes fixed on the floor at his feet. Eddie was next to him, pressed as close as he could be, an increasingly angry expression growing on his face. Mom was standing over the both of them, though her attention was clearly focused on Evan. Dad was standing slightly behind her.

Whatever conversation they’d been having was cut off by his entrance. The four of them immediately whipped their heads around, and the relieved expression on both teens' faces just made Daniel angrier. Because Evan shouldn’t need to be relieved that Daniel was there to protect them from their parents’ newest bullshit.

“Mom, Dad,” he said, tossing his bag down on the floor without care. He was still in his uniform, having been in too much of a hurry to change out of it. “You didn’t tell me you were coming down.”

“We thought it’d be a nice surprise,” was Dad’s cheerful response. “It’ll be Thanksgiving in a couple days. So we figured, why not?”

How miserable Evan looked after thirty minutes in their presence should have been the answer to that question.

“A bit of warning would have been nice.” Daniel did his best to keep his tone even. “I could have told you that I work on Thanksgiving before you made the trip.”

“You can take the day off.”

“No, Mom, I can’t.”

“Surely-”

“Dad,” Daniel snapped, cutting him off. “I can’t.”

Agreeing to take someone’s Thanksgiving shift was part of the wheeling and dealing that allowed him to be there for Evan’s games. It hadn’t mattered at the time, because neither he nor Evan really cared about that particular holiday. No one in their family did, so it was a bit absurd that their parents chose it as their excuse to pretend to be familial.

“No need to take that tone with us,” Dad said. “Why do you have a bat by the door?”

“In case we need it,” Daniel said, and hoped they didn’t press on that issue. But of course they did.

“Oh yes, we’ve seen the news,” Mom said. “It’s getting terribly dangerous down here, isn’t it? Maybe it would be safer for you to move back.”

“El Paso is safe, and Evan doesn’t go into Juarez by himself.”

“He shouldn’t be going there at all.” She sniffed disapprovingly. “What if something happened to him?”

Daniel rolled his eyes, because he knew what her actual concern was with that. 

“How long are you both staying?” he asked instead of poking that particular sore spot. “Where are you staying?”

“We checked into a hotel downtown,” Dad said. “We don’t have to be back until Sunday.”

“That’s great!” Eddie suddenly blurted out. “Y’all can come watch Evan’s semifinals with us.”

Evan shrunk in his seat the moment the words were out and it was a feat for Daniel to keep from glaring at Eddie. Mom looked between them all, her frowning deepening.

“Semifinals?”

“Football,” Evan mumbled, not meeting her eyes.

“You’re on the football team?” Mom turned on Daniel. “How could you let him do that? It’s dangerous, Daniel, you know that!”

“He likes playing,” Daniel said, not giving an inch. “He has fun, he’s good at it.”

“Well he’s not going to be playing anymore.”

That was when Daniel lost his temper, when he heard those words and saw the stricken expression on Evan’s face that they caused. He wasn’t sure which of them began to shout first, but all three of them - him and Mom and Dad - were doing so by the end. At some point, a shocked looking Eddie dragged Evan out by his wrist, and Daniel made a mental note to do something nice for him.

The argument was the summation of all their old ones, with some new themes thrown in. Mom wanted to keep Evan wrapped in cotton in case Daniel got sick again. Dad was on Mom’s side, his wife’s happiness the main priority for him while his children’s was a distant second. This time, though, was the addition of how they should never have let Daniel take Evan, how maybe it would be better if he went back to Hershey with them, where they could make sure he wasn’t doing anything “dangerous” and “selfish.”

Daniel probably started shouting then. Their combined volume had been rising rather steadily up until then, but he was pretty sure that was when he officially started shouting at his parents. Because there was no way, absolutely none, that he was going to let Mom get her talons into Evan again. His baby brother wouldn’t survive her “keeping him safe.”

When they finally left, Daniel slammed the door shut behind them and leaned against it as he tried to calm himself down. He only had a couple moments, though, before there was rapping at the door. He wrenched it open again, ready to tell whoever it was to go away, but held his tongue when he saw it was a worried looking Alma.

“Is everything okay?” she asked, peering around him into the apartment.

“Yeah.” He sighed, leaned against the doorframe. “My parents are in town.”

Alma blinked at him.

“I thought they were dead?”

“I wish,” he blurted out, and only felt a little guilty for the sentiment. “They’d be easier to deal with that way.”

She nodded, as if that was a perfectly reasonable thing to say about the people who raised you.

“Where’s Evan?”

“Eddie took him and ran when the yelling started.” Daniel would need to make arrangements for Evan to stay with the Diazes until Mom and Dad went home. Neither boy was likely to argue against the idea.

“That’s good,” Alma said with a nod. “Delia and Celia are at a sleepover until tomorrow. If you don’t have to go back to work, do you want to get drunk and complain about family?”

“God, yes.”

Now

This was possibly the stupidest, riskiest thing Buck had ever done. But he couldn’t just not do something to help. Maybe if it was just Eddie, he could let that hurt and anger take center stage and allow himself to be petty. But it wasn’t just Eddie, it was about Chris, too.

It took some lying and manipulating, because Eddie was rightfully worried about going to Danny’s apartment. Buck simply told him that Maddie was moving out but Danny couldn’t get off work to help. Technically true on the first part, Maddie was moving out, and partially true on the second, Danny was supposed to be at work on the day Buck was inviting Eddie over.

Buck wasn’t really certain how he managed to convince the man. They were barely friends at this point, and Buck was constantly on the verge of verbally knifing him if the conversation went the wrong way. But Eddie had named his kid after him, and apparently still had pictures of him on display, so maybe it shouldn’t have been a surprise.

Eddie crept into the apartment like he was expecting Danny to pop out at any moment with his bat, ready to knock his head off. The apprehension turned into confusion as he looked around the place.

“This is all the same furniture you guys had in El Paso,” he said. “That table is different, but- I thought I was here to help your sister move, where are her things?”

“No, she, uh. She only has a couple of bags,” Buck said, rubbing the back of his neck and shrugging at his curious frown. “I lied. I mean, she is moving, but that’s not why you’re here.”

“This is a really bad place for your brother to murder me.” There was tension in Eddie’s voice, and the smile he put on was more of a grimace. “What’s going on?”

“There is someone I want you to meet-”

“Are you setting me up?” Eddie’s face had gone utterly blank, that grimace-smile wiped away.

“No, no, I-” There was knocking at the door and Buck went to answer, throwing over his shoulder a, “Trust me.”

“I do,” was the whispered response.

“Buckaroo!” Carla cried when the door opened, immediately dragging him into a hug. “Baby, ah! Goodness, I have missed your face.”

“I’ve missed you, too. Come on in.”

He stepped aside, letting her into the apartment. She looked around and let out a snort.

“Yeah, this is definitely a place where two bachelors live,” she said.

“The place I was living at when I met Abby was so much worse. Danny called it a frat house,” Buck told her as he closed the door. “Eddie, this is my friend, Carla.”

“Nice to meet you, Eddie,” she said, shaking his hand.

“Likewise.”

“She is the city’s finest home health care aid,” Buck told him. “She has years of experience with navigating giant bureaucracies, and I thought she could help you figure out how to get Christopher what he needs.”

“I’m red tape’s worst nightmare,” Carla bragged with a laugh. “I’ll get you through this in no time.”

The confused, wary expression on the other man’s face turned to surprised awe.

“Let’s go sit down and see what you’re working with,” she said, walking past Eddie to the dining table. “Besides that perfect bone structure.”

Buck puttered about the apartment as the two of them talked, finding little things to keep him busy. Eventually he pulled out his laptop and set up camp on the couch, finding himself a rabbit hole to fall into. He did it a little too well, because he failed to keep track of the time.

The sound of the door opening had him bolting up.

“Danny!” he screeched. “You’re home.”

“Yeah? Why-” Danny’s eyes landed on Eddie, a cobra catching a mouse beneath its gaze. His hand immediately reached for the bat, making the other man flinch in his seat. The only reason he didn’t grab it was because of Carla, who twisted around in her seat and gave him a smile.

“Oh, you’re Daniel,” she said. “I’m Carla. Abby’s told me about you.”

“She did, huh?” Danny’s walk into the living room was purposeful. Eddie turned to track his movement.

“She did?” Buck asked as he got up. If he was literally standing between his brother and his ex, maybe the blood shed could be delayed.

“Oh yes, something about your brother walking into her work and threatening her with a bat.”

“Danny!”

“I didn’t threaten her,” Danny said, setting his work bag on the washer. “I just made sure she understood that I had one and wasn’t afraid to use it.”

The look he gave Eddie at that was pointed. Thankfully Carla was busy packing things back into her purse and missed it.

“It’s been very nice to meet you,” she said, shaking Eddie’s hand again. “And I’ll call you in a couple days once I’ve got everything we need for me to start.”

“Thank you so much,” Eddie said.

“Of course.”

Carla headed for the door, and Buck trailed after her with Eddie. At the threshold, as she went on out and down the hall, Eddie paused. A visible debate played across his features, and then he was throwing himself at Buck and wrapping his arms around him. Buck froze in surprise at first before he let himself sink into it, bringing his arms up.

It was nice, at least until Danny cleared his throat. Eddie let out a yelp and was fleeing down the hall in an instant.

“Buck.”

“Danny,” Buck said, closing the door. He rested his forehead against the wood so he didn’t have to look at his brother.

“What are you doing?”

“Holding up the-”

“Buck.”

“I could help him!” Buck said, turning around. “I could help him, so I did.”

“Why are the two of you still at that station together?” Danny asked him, arms crossed. Apparently that argument about Eddie was no longer being put off. “If you told Nash about what happened, he’d transfer him. Or he’d let you transfer.”

“I know that,” he said. “But that would mean outing Eddie, and that would be a really shitty thing to do.”

“I could do it.”

“But you won’t.” Buck glared at his brother, who met it without flinching. “I wouldn’t forgive you if you did.”

“Jesus.” Danny shook his head. “You still love him.”

Buck looked away, could not bring himself to lie and refute it.

“You’re going to get hurt,” his brother said. “You’re going to get hurt, and then I’m going to be…”

“I’m not going to leave again,” Buck told him. Well, he mumbled it towards the floor. Same thing, really. “I promise.”

“Right.” The disbelief in his brother’s voice hurt. It was also well-earned. “Find out where the kid goes if something happens to his dad.”

“Danny!”

“What?” Danny shrugged without concern as he went to unpack his bag and throw his uniform into the laundry. “The job is dangerous. You never know when someone’s older brother is going to come at you with a baseball bat.”

Chapter 10

Now

Daniel had only been to Athena Grant’s house twice before, both times to watch her kids while she went out for the evening. With Nash, apparently, but that was none of Daniel’s business. Though it did have the potential to make things awkward when one considered how he threatened the captain just a few months prior.

Had Nash mentioned that to her? He hadn’t said anything to Buck, that much Daniel could comfortably assume, but surely he would’ve said something to his cop girlfriend. Hell, maybe he had and that was why Athena chose Daniel to watch her kids.

A month ago, when he and Buck managed to convince their sister to stay, Daniel went to Athena and told her about his brother-in-law. Last week, Maddie had her ride along with the sergeant, who intended to use the opportunity to get her to open up about her marriage. That was why Daniel was over today, sitting in the den of the sergeant’s house.

“For someone who claimed to not be ashamed to talk about it,” Athena was saying, a mug of something in her hand. She’d offered him something to drink but he’d turned it down. “Your sister sure didn’t talk about it much.”

“I wish I could be surprised,” Daniel said. He wasn’t, though. Maddie refused to talk about the negatives of her relationship with Doug since the beginning, like it was a personal failing to admit that things weren’t absolutely perfect. Or perhaps she still didn’t want to admit that everyone who tried to warn her had been right.

“She was very happy to talk about you and your brother, though. She wanted to know about Abby. And Eddie, for some reason.”

Daniel rolled his eyes.

“She thinks Buck has a crush.” God, if only. Thinking about Eddie made his hands itch with the need to hit something. “I’m pretty sure she feels guilty about not being there much before now, so she’s trying to make up for it. But she didn’t tell you anything about Doug at all?”

“Nope.” The sergeant shook her head. “Just that it was awful. How much does Buck know?”

“That it was awful,” he said. “That Doug was a dick. Maddie lied and said it only got worse in the last year or so. I dunno, maybe she didn’t really lie and it did get worse, she did say he threatened to kill her when she threatened to leave. But the way she phrased things, I know Buck definitely hasn’t realized how long it’s actually been going on.”

“You said you reported it eight years ago,” Athena said, sitting her drink down on the table between them. “I poked around some. There were noise complaints from their neighbors in Boston, complaining about yelling and things breaking.”

“Christ.” Daniel shook his head, hatred and disgust welling up inside him. “I took Buck to visit her, before we moved to Texas. She was freaked out a little to hear we were in town, never invited us over to their place. Neither of us argued at the time, ‘cause Doug was a dick and we didn’t want to deal with him at all. But now…”

“She didn’t want you to know.”

“She lied to me,” he said, staring into the wall in front of him. “Said her bruises came from a patient at the ER. And then she pretty much cut me off when I called it in. I don’t know what she told the cops.”

“Probably what she told you.”

“And I bet they just ate it up, ‘cause Doug is such a swell guy and his daddy had money.” Daniel rolled his eyes and grumbled out, “Fucking cops.”

Athena’s eyebrow went up and her mouth curved with amusement. Daniel wasn’t about to apologize for his statement, though. He hadn’t exactly come out of El Paso with an appreciation for the police, so the sergeant should simply feel lucky and blessed.

“There is something else, however,” Athena said with a sigh, all traces of amusement wiped away.

“And that would be?”

“He apparently went to your sister’s supervisor at the ER, after he realized she was gone,” she said. Daniel’s stomach dropped at the news. “He thought she knew where Maddie went. When she told him that she didn’t, he got violent.”

He closed his eyes, dragged a hand down his face.

“Is she okay?”

“Yes. Security showed up, ran him off. He was asked to take some personal time.”

“So he’s definitely coming here.”

“I’d wager that he’s already here,” Athena said. “That was around the time you first called me about him. But there’s no warrant out for him, he’s not a person of interest in any reported crime, and he hasn’t been reported missing. If I had a picture of him, I could share it with colleagues, but…”

“We don’t have any,” Daniel told her, feeling disappointed with himself. “He’s a dick and we always hated him, so we definitely weren’t keen to have his face in our home. We only have two pictures from their wedding, one just of Maddie in her dress and another of the three of us.”

“Which means all I can do is share his name and description.”

“She claims her new place is secure.” Daniel wasn’t sure how much he trusted his sister’s opinion on that. “Buck’s got that Chimney guy coming over to put in a security system while we help move in furniture.”

They just had to hope that would be enough.

“You have officially reached Fort Knox level security,” Chimney declared as he started climbing down the ladder. He was looking distinctly less unicorn-like than the last time Daniel had seen him, and the scar left behind was rather subtle when one considered that it was caused by a rebar through the head.

“You think it's too much?” Maddie asked, leaning over the railing to look down at them from upstairs. Because her new apartment had two levels, and she could afford to order all brand new furniture for it. Daniel was starting to wonder just how much money she managed to stash away before she ran.

“No,” he shouted up to her, because nothing short of actual Fort Knox would be enough in his opinion. His sister rolled her eyes at him as she started down the stairs.

“Three motion sensors in the living room? Maybe if you’re trying to keep Tom Cruise from getting his hands on your NOC list.” Chimney frowned when Daniel and Maddie stared at him blankly. “Mission: Impossible? No?”

“Haven’t seen it, sorry,” Maddie said with a shrug.

“Travesty.”

“I think I took Buck to see one of those.” Daniel tried to remember. There was something about a rabbit’s foot?

“I’m gonna go see what’s taking them so long with the couch,” she said, walking past him.

Them being Buck and Eddie. Because Buck had asked fucking Eddie to help move their sister in. And then made Daniel promise to leave his baseball bat back home. That did remind him, however, that he left Maddie’s housewarming gift sitting by the front door.

“Maybe we pop the hinges off the door,” he heard Eddie say, and Maddie hurried her pace, reaching them as Buck suggested they used the Jaws of Life.

“No, no,” Maddie said, throwing herself out there before they killed her security deposit. “It has to fit, I measured it twice. Can’t you guys just, you know, turn it around the other way?”

“But then the pizza will slide off,” Buck countered. When Daniel laughed, their sister swiveled her head to glare at him.

“You have pizza on my new sofa?” She spotted it and grabbed it.

“It’s on the plastic.”

Maddie stomped back inside, purposefully stepping on Daniel’s foot when he laughed again. She went back to Chimney, asking him to show her the security app. Daniel, though, lingered by the door. Because he was a bad person who enjoyed tormenting Eddie. The man still tensed up at the sight of him, it was delightful.

“Are you going to help?” Buck asked as he and Eddie started lifting the couch again.

“Nope,” he said. “You managed to get through four years of football without screwing up your knees, you’ll be fine. Remember to lift with your legs!”

Daniel grabbed the gift for his sister, smirking when Eddie yelped and dropped his end of the couch at the sight of it, and went to join her in the living room.

“Mads!” He held up the wooden baseball bat he bought for her. “Housewarming gift!”

“Gee, thanks,” she drawled. She didn’t take it, because her hands were full with the boxes of pizza.

“Never know when someone needs a kneecap busted.” Daniel said it just loud enough for his voice to carry.

“What is with your brothers and bats?” Chimney asked Maddie, before turning back to Daniel. “Did you say Buckaroo played football?”

“He was a tight end.”

Many a joke had been made at his brother’s expense. All of them were made by him, which was perfectly allowed. If someone else made them, though, things might turn ugly. He hoped the expression on his face appropriately conveyed that fact to Chimney.

“Thank you, guys, for helping me on your day off,” Maddie told Buck and Eddie as they carried the couch into the living room. They flipped it up right and set it down.

“Don’t worry about it,” Eddie said, coming over to take the pizza from her. He gave Daniel, and the bat he was holding, a wide berth. “Plates?”

“Countertop, kitchen.”

“Beer?” Chimney asked, handing Maddie the tablet with the security app.

“Also kitchen, in the fridge,”

“Clever.”

Maddie smiled as she watched the man walk away.

“He is so cute,” she gushed.

“Yeah, he gets that a lot,” Buck told her. Daniel frowned, because he didn’t think- “You should meet his kid, though.”

Ah. His brother was never beating the allegation.

"Wait," Maddie said. "Chimney has a kid?"

“No, I- I thought you meant…” Buck trailed off as Maddie walked away, heading for the kitchen with the other two. “Chimney?”

“Why do you two have such awful taste in men?” Daniel asked under his breath. “It’s bad enough that you’re still panting over that one, but now this?”

“He’s not…”

“Did he or did he not constantly lie to his last girlfriend? And wasn’t there something about guilt sex that you were complaining to me about?”

The worst part was that Daniel couldn’t just tell Maddie that he disapproved of her new crush, because there was no faster way to get her to go out with the guy. Daniel was still half-certain that the fact everyone in their family hated Doug was half the draw for her. He just had to hope she had the sense not to jump into anything serious when she literally just fled from an abusive husband.

“Stop tormenting Eddie,” Buck said, changing the topic and basically admitting that Daniel was right.

“No,” he said, and then, “How am I tormenting Eddie?”

His brother glared and pointed at the bat still in Daniel’s hand.

“This,” Daniel said, holding it up, “is your sister’s housewarming present, like I said.”

“Normal people get things like, I dunno. Wine glasses or a plant.”

But normal had never really described the Buckleys, did it?

Their very first call of the day sent them out to a baseball field. Not because of any sports related injury, but because of a news helicopter doing an emergency landing and not quite making the target, ending up sitting in the bleachers. When they pulled up in the engine, there was a group of people cowering behind tables and chairs and anything else they could find, as the rotor blades of the ‘copter sent rocks and clumps of dirt flying through the air.

“The pilot radioed they were having mechanical trouble,” Athena relayed, joining them where they’d gathered on the safe side of the truck. “It looks like he tried to put it down in the field over there.”

Bobby peered around their cover, only a quick look, before pulling back.

“It got close,” he said.

“Not close enough,” she retorted, before going to herd the civilians towards safety.

“Alright, guys, we’re gonna fan out and go behind those bleachers,” Bobby told them. “Eddie, after we get the people out, do you think you can kill that engine?”

“Think so,” the man replied, peering around for a look at what they were working with. “I’m just worried about the dynamic rollover.”

“The dynamic what?” Buck asked.

“We change the weight ratio by pulling people out, the whole thing could tip over,” Eddie explained. “Rotors could snap off.”

And then flying rocks would be the least of their problems.

“Visors down,” Bobby ordered. “Let’s go!”

They sprinted forward, going around behind the bleachers. It put them on the opposite side of where the rotors were digging into the ground, so they were at least safe from launched projectiles. When they got to the end the helicopter was on, they climbed up into the bleachers and crowded on the safer side of the helicopter.

They worked on getting the cockpit doors open, Buck going for the passenger in the back while Eddie went for the pilot. The passenger was a pretty woman with red hair, clearly doing her best to keep from freaking out entirely as he got her seatbelt undone.

“Put your arms around me,” Buck told her as he bent down to scoop her up. “Alright, here we go.”

Of course, the moment he started taking her out, the dynamic rollover that Eddie warned them about almost happened. The helicopter groaned and shifted. The woman slid out of his arms and down the seat. Buck barely managed to catch her by the leg.

The others hopped onto the landing struts of the ‘copter, using the weight to keep it from unbalancing. Buck picked the woman up again and hurriedly backed away. The moment she was clear, Bobby was dragging the pilot out so Eddie could take his place. He did something with the controls, Buck couldn’t say what, and the rotors came to a groaning stop.

With the danger over, the paramedics could come in and look them over. Buck ended up assisting Hen with the woman, while Chimney and Eddie focused on the pilot. He seemed more in need of medical attention than her.

“Pulse/ox is good,” Hen declared while Buck checked the woman’s pupillary response. “We can get rid of this.”

She pulled off the oxygen mask they had on their patient, while Buck bent down to stow the penlight away in the medical bag.

“How are you doing?” Athena asked here.

“I’m not sure,” the woman said. Buck perked up at the sound of her voice, because it was so very familiar… “How’s Trent? Is he okay?”

“Your pilot’s gonna be just fine.”

“Hey, uh. Do me a favor?” Buck asked, butting in. “Say, ‘And on the Four Oh Five, speeds are under five miles an hour, making your morning commute a rough one.’”

“Seriously?” Hen asked, being very judgmental in his opinion. Athena’s stare, too, was extra judgey.

“I- I know her voice,” he said. “Taylor Kelly reporting, right? Skywitness News Eight.”

The helicopter even said Channel 8 News.

“That’s me,” Taylor Kelly said.

“Wow. It’s weird hearing that voice come out of a face.” Buck knew, even before he saw the expressions on all three women’s faces, that it was a really weird thing to say. “It’s a pretty face!”

“Thank you?” Taylor Kelly laughed.

“You have helped me beat traffic in this city more times than you will ever know.”

“Well,” she said. “You might want to Uber tomorrow.”

Somehow, Hen and Athena managed to look even more judgmental.

Chapter 11

Chapter Notes

tw: nonconsensual drug use

(it is That episode, after all)

Now

Buck was outside washing the captain’s truck, almost done with hosing it down to rinse off all the suds, when he noticed the visitors. It was actually hearing someone call for Bobby, saying that the Chief’s office was on the line, that made him look over. Standing there in the truck bay was a man holding a camera on his shoulder and Taylor Kelly, the traffic reporter they rescued from the helicopter.

Ever curious, he dropped the sponge he was holding into the bucket and the (shut off) hose onto the ground, and went to be friendly.

“Uh, hey!” he greeted, getting their attention as he strode over. The pair turned, the cameraman moving so he stood behind and a little to the side of the reporter. Wait, were they recording right now? “Wh- What are you doing here?”

“How was your drive in?” Taylor asked instead of answering, bright red lips curving into a smile as she batted her eyelashes. “Did you miss me?”

“My morning definitely wasn’t the same without you,” Buck replied with a smile of his own.

“Hey, how’re you feeling?” Eddie was over in the station gym, getting his requisite exercise time in, but now he was coming over. He was a sweaty mess in one of his stupid tank tops and Buck hated it. (No he didn’t.) “Any side effects after the crash?”

“I’m great,” the reporter answered. Her tone had shifted, dropping the flirtiness she’d been using with Buck. The others, Hen and Chimney, were making their way from where they’d been doing their checklist chores.  “Thanks to all of you, which is why I’m here. You guys were so amazing. I want to do a story on this firehouse and on the heroes who work here.”

“Yeah, but you do traffic,” Chimney pointed out.

“I’m not quite ready to go back in the sky,” Taylor replied. “So I thought I’d try my hand at telling a story from the ground.”

“And use your newfound fame to put your face in front of the camera,” Hen said with a laugh. Taylor smirked.

“That too.”

“Smart girl.”

“I can’t wait to interview all of you,” Taylor continued. “I want to know why you chose this job, the crazy thing you’ve seen, what it’s like to face death. I mean, Howie, you almost died, right?”

“Actually,” he said, wagging his finger at her. “Nobody calls me that. It’s Chimney.”

“I bet there’s a story there.”

“Not one you can tell on TV,” Hen quipped.

“Well, you must have really impressed the chief,” Bobby said as he joined them. “He said to give you whatever access you need.”

“Don’t worry, Captain Nash,” Taylor said with a smile. “Your story’s in good hands.

The words did not seem to put the captain at ease.

Taylor Kelly proceeded to stalk the station, cornering people for interviews wherever she could. Reactions to the whole thing ran the gamut from unease (like Bobby) to neutral (Hen and Jones) to excited. Chimney and Buck both fell into that category, as did Eddie. Though the latter insisted the whole thing was a farce, he still went out of his way to shower and change, before going back to the gym so Taylor’s cameraman, Bret, could get footage of him at the punching bag.

When the time came for Buck to be cornered, he was in the middle of polishing the engine.

“Firefighter Buckley.” Taylor’s voice had him turning to smile brightly at the reporter. “The County Fair Spider-Man.”

“Oh, man.” Buck laughed. “It’s just Buck. Y’know, I met my last girlfriend because of those interviews.”

“Where is she now?”

“Morocco, last I heard.”

The reporter smiled at the answer.

“So, Buck,” she said, popping out his name. “What made you want to become a firefighter?”

“Honestly? I just kinda fell into it,” Buck said, going back to polishing. If doing so happened to make his biceps flex a little, then it was only incidental. “Well, more like my brother pushed me into it, I guess.”

“What do you mean?”

“My brother’s at the One Thirty-three, he’s actually Mr. July in next year’s calendar-”

“Oh, I know about the calendar,” Taylor said with a chuckle and an eyeroll. “Mr. April up there gave me a free one.”

Considering she didn’t have it with her, Buck wondered what she did with it.

“Well, Danny wanted to be a doctor growing up. But he was younger then, when he took guardianship of me, than I am now, so.” He shrugged. “I’ve done a lot of jobs before this. Traveled around a lot, too. Kinda felt a bit lost, y’know? Aimless. I was even gonna try to be a Navy SEAL, until he talked me out of it. That was when recruitment pamphlets for the LAFD started popping up all over the apartment. I got the hint, and here I am.”

Taylor tilted her head a little. Something like interest glinted in her eye.

“What led to your brother taking guardianship of you at such a young age?”

“Oh, uh. Y’know.” Buck shrugged again as he tried to think of an answer. However pretty Taylor Kelly was, he didn’t feel like talking about his parents in front of a camera. “Reasons.”

The reporter was not very satisfied with that answer, but she seemed to realize that she wouldn’t be getting anything else from him. She lingered to chat with him, or maybe more like flirt with him, until Bret the cameraman made an impatient noise. Taylor rolled her eyes before leaving to find someone else to interview, making some comment about trying to pin down the captain.

Buck watched her go, and then checked the shine of the engine. He spotted Eddie’s scowling face in the reflection before the man spoke.

“Seriously?” Eddie sounded grumpy about something. He’d also changed again and was now wearing the button down uniform.

“What?”

“You just spent ten minutes flirting with the reporter.”

Oh. Wait, really? It was this, again?

“Did you time it?” Buck asked, tamping down on his irritation. “She’s hot.”

“She’s a shark.”

“Sharks are beautiful, misunderstood animals,” he argued. “Also, she’s hot. What, are you jealous?”

Eddie scowled at him and turned to march away. Except that was the moment the alarm sounded, dispatch reporting about an adult male having difficulty breathing. The pair of them jumped into the engine and were soon joined by the others. Bobby looked far more relieved about going out on a call than Buck had ever seen him to be.

Jones drove them an exotic pet shop on Ventura. Apparently, the shop was hosting an eating contest and one of the participants “just wasn’t doing very well.” A short woman with dangly earrings and a clunky looking necklace met them when they hopped out.

A van parked behind the ambulance, Taylor Kelly and Bret the cameraman hopping out, but no one paid them any mind.

“I was told there’s a man here choking to death,” Bobby said, taking the lead.

“No, uh, Gregory says he doesn’t want any help now,” the woman said, holding her hands up as if to stop them.

“Okay, excuse me, ma’am,” the captain said, and then walked around her. The rest of them followed.

“I wonder how many he put down,” Buck wondered aloud to Eddie, the man at his side and matching him step for step. “Joey Chestnut scarfed down seventy-four hot dogs at last year’s Nathan’s Hot Dog Eating Contest.”

Eddie’s expression was a combination of vague disgust, interest, and something like worry. He didn’t ask Buck why he could rattle that information off like that. It wasn’t the weirdest factoid Buck had shared with him, and at least it was relevant to the matter at hand this time.

The contestants (three of them, presumably the ones that hadn’t tapped out yet and not simply the only ones who signed up) were set up at a pair of fold-out tables with palm tree patterned tablecloths. The crowd continued cheering even as Bobby shouted to ask if one of the male contestants, clutching to what Buck was fairly certain was the kind of container live crickets and the such came in, was refusing care. The man, Gregory, waved them away even as it seemed he might be about to puke.

A hissing and sudden movement from under one of the tables had Chimney leaping into Bobby with a curse.

“Relax,” Buck laughed, giving him a pat. “It’s a monitor lizard, not a snake.”

It was even in a little harness with a leash.

“Cute,” the paramedic said, and then asked the short woman, “That thing yours?”

“That’s Claude,” she said, picking up its leash.. “He’s the grand prize.”

That was when the man Buck was assuming to be Gregory fell out of his seat with a groan, hitting the ground with a thump. All humor forgotten, they rushed to surround him. They got the crowd and tables moved out of the way, allowing the paramedics to start working on him. 

“What was this?” Taylor asked the woman. “Like a pepper eating contest?”

“No, protein.”

And then chirping bugs start crawling out of Gregory’s mouth.

“Locusts!” Hen cried, swatting at them as they leaped on her.

“No, they’re actually crickets,” the woman corrected. “We allowed anything that Claude would eat. We thought it might be fun.”

Crickets or not, the guy was not breathing. He went into v-fib and Chimney began doing compressions.

“The blockage is too big, he’s not getting any air,” Hen said, and then held out her hand. “Buck, give me the Magill.”

Buck hurriedly dug the requested forceps out of the medical bag, handing them over. Chimney helped her hold Gregory’s head steady as she reached into his mouth with the forceps. And then pulled out a clump of partially chewed crickets, to everyone’s disgust.

With the blockage gone, they managed to revive the guy. The crowd cheered as Hen and Chimney helped Gregory sit up. At which point he started puking up his meal of insects. Some of which could still hop away.

“All of this for a lizard, huh?” Eddie asked him.

“A monitor lizard and five-hundred bucks,” Gregory said. “And I just lost.”

“Yes you did!!” one of the other contestants gloated. Only for the roaches she’d been eating to come crawling back out of her mouth.

“They were eating crickets?” Danny sounded appalled. He pushed away the dim sum he had been eating up until that story. Buck reached with his chopsticks and poached what was left.

“And roaches!” Buck cheerily added. “Anything that you might feed a monitor lizard, apparently.”

“Disgusting.”

“Eh, I’ve had worse things in my mouth,” he joked, grinning widely when his brother actually laughed. “Seriously, fry ‘em up and season ‘em, they probably wouldn’t taste any different from chapulines. The crickets, at least. I’m not sure about the roaches.”

“What the hell are chapulines?” Regret was already lining Danny’s words, because he absolutely knew he wouldn’t like the answer he got.

“Fried grasshoppers,” Buck answered, laughing at the groan of disgust. “I had some at a soccer game in Oaxaca.”

“Yeah, no. Pass.”

“Hey, I’ve seen you tip back a bag of chips to get at the crumbs,” Buck couldn’t help pointing out. “Do you know how many dead bugs and rat po-”

“Okay! Okay!” his brother interrupted. “That’s quite enough, thank you!”

“I’m just saying, it’s a bit late to be getting queasy about eating bugs, Danny.”

“But I don’t know I’m eating them then, do I?”

The front door opened then and Maddie came stomping in to glare at them, dropping her purse onto the coffee table with her keys.

“Why,” she ground out, “are you two in my apartment?”

“Oh, this is yours?” Danny made a show of looking around and acting confused. “Buck, why didn’t you say we were in the wrong apartment?”

“Are you sure this is your apartment?” Buck asked.

“Yes, I am.” Maddie crossed her arms and glared at her brothers. “The first sign should have been that it smells better.”

“Wow,” Danny said with a shake of his head. “It’s almost as if she doesn’t want any of this food we brought over.”

“More for me, then.” Buck reached over to grab the take-out bag they’d separated for their sister, only for Maddie to yank it away at the last moment.

“You invade my space, I’m getting my food,” she announced with a sniff as she sat down on the other end of the couch from Danny. Buck was on the floor between them, sitting against the couch. “At least tell me you made sure- Yes! Hot mustard.”

She dug it all out, setting it down on the table and slapping at Buck’s fingers every time he made as if to steal any of it.

“Seriously,” Maddie said, “why are you both here?”

“Because you’re our big sister and we missed you,” Buck said, craning his head up to give her his best puppy dog eyes. His sister just put a hand on his face and shoved it away.

“We wanted to check on you,” Danny told her. “See how you’re doing all alone, in a strange new place.”

“I’m fine,” Maddie said. At Danny’s blatant doubt, she added, “Okay, I got woken up the other night because the windchimes on the porch set off a motion sensor. But I’m fine!”

Buck slapped his brother on the leg.

“I told you something was missing!”

“Yeah, yeah, you were right.” Even though he couldn’t see it from this angle, Buck knew that Danny rolled his eyes as he said it. “Seriously, though, Mads.”

“I seriously am fine,” their sister insisted, though even Buck could tell she was looking tired. The kind of tiredness that came from a sleepless and stressful night. “What about you two, huh? Any work stories?”

Danny immediately slapped a hand over Buck’s mouth, who retaliated by licking his palm. The hand went away, to be wiped on the sleeve of Buck’s shirt.

“You look twenty-six, but you act like you’re four,” Danny groused, as if he was any better. “Don’t ask him about any work stories until after you’ve eaten.”

“Danny’s just a wuss,” Buck mocked. “But fine.”

The moment Maddie was done eating, though, he gleefully repeated the tale of the guy who nearly choked to death on crickets. She handled it much better than their brother did.

Everybody loved firefighters, and their job meant they had a lot of grateful people willing to send them things. Cards, usually, though baked goods were common. At the tail end of a twenty-four hour shift, their second with Taylor Kelly shadowing them, they were sent a batch of fudge brownies with nuts. They didn’t last long, with barely any crumbs being left in the plastic container they came in by the time Chimney came to get some.

He was still complaining when they got sent out for a med call. One that Bobby decided to sit out on so he could wrap up the shift. Taylor Kelly, who still hadn’t been able to pin the captain down yet, opted to stay at the station as well.

An odd feeling started hitting Buck during the ride. Everything started looking kinda pretty and sparkly. And there was a sound, or he thought there was a sound, and his eyes were feeling weird. Did his eyes grow ears? It would be so weird if his eyes grew ears, right?

“Man, allergies are going crazy today,” Eddie complained, rubbing at his own eyes.

“You too?” Buck asked as he leaned closer to him. He didn’t have to lean closer, their headsets were magic that let him hear everyone like they were right in his ears. But he wanted to be closer to Eddie. He always wanted to be closer to Eddie. “The index wasn’t elevated this morning. Think it’s a new kind?”

“New kind of what?” Eddie asked, squinting around.

“Of pollen.”

“A new kind of pollen?” Chimney asked. 

“You’re not feeling this?” Eddie asked in return, staring at his arm. It was a nice arm, so Buck stared at it, too.

“No. I do not.”

“I can see the pollen.”

“I can hear it,” Buck said. But he wasn’t hearing it with his old ears, he was hearing it with his new ears on his eyes. He couldn’t hear it when he blinked. They sounded pretty, so he tried very hard to keep from blinking.

They arrived at a palace of color. Buck looked about in awe as he followed Chimney and Hen, Eddie right behind him. Like a train. They were a train! A train going into a very colorful palace with very colorful clouds floating around, tethered to the ground. Buck wanted to untie them because they deserved to be free, but he stopped because of the ladies. They were tiny and just as colorful and giggling.

Buck stayed exactly where he was, because he didn’t want to step on any of the tiny ladies.

“Did these beauty queens shrink?” he asked Eddie, who was also stuck in place as he stared at them. “Or are we suddenly giants?”

He didn’t want to be a giant. Danny used to complain so much about finding pants that fit him. If he was giant, then he’d never find pants that fit. Oh, God, how would he get into the apartment?

Somewhere in the crowd of shrunken ladies, Chimney shouted for someone to bring him his gear. But how could they get to him without crushing the ladies?

“I think he means you guys,” Athena said. Was she a giant, too?

“They’re the tiniest ladies I’ve ever seen,” Buck told her, holding up his fingers to his eyes to show her. “Teeny-tiny.”

“Excuse me?”

“So tiny,” Eddie whispered, starting to backpedal away from them. He hit one of the clouds and was startled.

“Henrietta!” the sergeant shouted.

“Oh, ‘Thena,” Hen said as she floated over. Like a cloud. Was she a cloud too? “You smell like love.”

Buck wondered what love smelled like. Did it smell like Eddie? Like Danny? It didn’t smell like Mom, Buck knew that. Maybe the other clouds would know. He went over to ask them, but they were rude and didn’t answer. They definitely weren’t getting untied now.

People showed up, dressed like Athena, and started tugging at him. Then they started putting cuffs on him, and Buck had played this game before. It was a fun game, but he didn’t normally play it while dressed like this. That was fine, though, he could still play. He put his hands behind his back, like they asked, and shivered when the cold metal got put around his wrist.

Buck and Eddie never played this game in high school. Maybe that was why Eddie was upset.

“What’s going on?” he demanded, pretty face scrunched up. “I don’t like this.”

“Ohhhh, you made him cry,” Buck complained.

“Just breathe, okay? You’re gonna be okay,” Athena told him. “Somebody dosed you with a hallucinogen. We don’t want you to hurt yourself or anyone else.”

“Yeah.” Eddie sniffed. “I like that idea.”

“‘Thena,” Hen said, raising her hands to point at her. “Your head’s beating like your heart.”

“Is it?” Athena asked, but she didn’t seem to actually want to know because then she went away.

The strangers dressed like Athena took the three of them and made them stand beside the engine. They stood in a nice little line, like ducklings, until Buck looked up and saw more clouds floating way above them. These looked different, big and white and fluffy instead of shiny and round and colorful. He pointed them out to the other two and they all looked up. Then they sat down and leaned back to watch them. It made Eddie stop crying at least.

Jones knelt down, frowning at the three of them. He had something to his ear and he was talking into it.

“They’ll probably take all of them in to get checked out,” he was saying. “Of course I’ll stay until you get there. See you then, Dan.”

Dan? Danny?

“Yes, Danny,” Jones said. Was he reading Buck’s mind? “...Sure, we’ll go with that. Just think happy thoughts. Your brother’s on his way.”

Yay!

Chapter 12

Chapter Notes

cw: implied offscreen sexual activity between minors (because I know this bothers some people); nonconsensual drug use (continued on from last chapter)

Now

Daniel did not expect to have to go out on his day off and pick up his brother because said brother was tripping balls. And yet, that’s what he was having to do. He had to wait until Austin could tell him which hospital they were being taken to before he could rush there, though. While he did, he called up Maddie to tell her what was going on.

She found it funny. Admittedly, Daniel probably would, too. Later, after he’d actually laid eyes on Buck and made sure he was as okay as he could be.

The moment Austin gave him the name of the hospital, Daniel was in his car and off like a rocket. The 118’s engine was parked on the fire lane when he got there, and he was almost tempted to park next to it. He resisted the urge, however, and found an actual parking space before rushing into the building for where everyone was being held.

He found Austin standing in the hall, waiting for him.

“They’re in there,” his fake boyfriend told him, pointing at one of the rooms.

“All of them?” Daniel asked, because that seemed to be a small room for their entire shift to be crammed into.

“Oh, no, just your brother and Eddie.”

Daniel resisted the urge to groan.

“Why are they sharing a room?”

“They refused to separate and it was just easier,” Austin explained. “Eddie was crying about the handcuffs and Buck leaving again, and that just upset your brother. So now they’re both in there.”

“Christ,” Daniel said, and then, “Wait, is he still cuffed?”

He understood cuffing them for transport, because Buck and Eddie were both fully capable of manhandling a full-grown adult if they had to. No one wanted to deal with those kinds of muscles in the middle of a bad trip. But there was a certain point where keeping them cuffed was no longer necessary, and if he walked in there to find his baby brother still cuffed like a criminal…

“No, no,” Austin hurriedly answered. “They took them off when they got settled here.”

“Good.”

Taking a breath, and somewhat dreading what he was about to walk into, Daniel slid open the door to the patient room. Both men were easily spotted, sitting pressed together on the edge of one bed with IV lines running from their arms. Buck perked up as he entered.

“Danny!” he cheered. “Eddie, Danny’s here!”

“Danny!” Eddie actually grinned at the sight of him. “Look, we’ve melted together!”

He pointed at where the two of them were touching. Buck nodded like a happy little bobblehead.

“That’s…” Oh, good grief. “Well, you’ve gotta split apart now, so I can take Buck home.”

“No!” his brother and Eddie both shouted, and suddenly they were trying to wrap all their limbs around each other while Eddie started crying.

“You can’t leave again,” Eddie blubbered.

“I won’t! I won’t! We’re melted together, see!?”

Jesus Christ.

Shaking his head at the pair, Daniel retreated back to the hallway. Austin looked as if he couldn’t decide on confusion or amusement as his primary emotion, settling on a mixture of the two.

“I hate them both,” Daniel lied as he closed the door behind him. “Have they been like that the entire time?”

“I think that started sometime during the ride over,” Austin said. “When I called you, they were looking at the clouds. And then Buck was convinced I could read his mind because he didn’t realize he was talking out loud.”

“Did he say anything embarrassing?”

“More embarrassing than his desire to merge with Eddie?” the other man asked in turn. “Pretty sure he was talking to a bunch of balloons at one point, asking them what love smelled like, before getting upset because they weren’t talking back. And no, I didn’t record it.”

“Damn it.”

“I have to get the truck to the station. Tell me when you get him home and I’ll swing by.”

“Thanks for staying with him, Austin.”

“‘Course.”

After the other man was gone, Daniel stuck his head into the room again to check on his brother and the ex. They were still wrapped around each other like octopuses with no idea of personal space, and he wanted very much to walk in there and tear them apart. He didn’t, though, because that would just upset his brother. And Eddie, but that was a concern that came in a distant second place.

Closing the door again, he stepped out to hunt down the doctor and figure out when he could take Buck home. There was a hispanic woman standing there when he turned around, her look of surprise at the sight of him quickly melting into a scowl. And then she smacked him on the arm. Hard.

“Hello to you, too, Pepa,” Daniel said, rubbing at the spot. The lady had an arm on her.

“How many years were you both living in this city, and neither of you bothered to say hello?” she asked. He shrugged at the question, because it wasn’t like he was the one particularly close to the Diaz family. “The lady at the desk told me Eddito’s room is here.”

“They’re sharing,” Daniel said, stepping aside so she could see them through the window in the door. “I think they’re trying to, I don’t know. Melt together.”

The woman rolled her eyes and muttered something in Spanish.

“Do you know what happened? They didn’t tell me much on the phone.”

“Uh, Austin said something about special brownies being delivered to the station. So they’re, uh…” High as fuck, is what he didn’t say.

“They seem to be having a better trip than my last one,” Pepa remarked. She laughed when Daniel simply stared at her. “What? I was not always old.”

“Okay then,” he said and moved on. “Uh, I haven’t talked to the doctor yet about taking Buck home. Though they both got upset when I talked about that, so I don’t know what we’re going to do.”

Pepa hummed.

“That is for later,” she said. “Come, we’ll find the doctor and talk to them first.”

The thing about a drug trip was that there wasn’t really much the doctors could do, not really. Everything pretty much amounted to holding their hand and making sure things didn’t take a bad turn. That meant, at least, that there wasn’t anything keeping Daniel from taking his brother home, since they’d already drawn blood for the tox screen.

No, wait, that was a lie. There was one thing keeping Daniel from taking his brother home: Eddie. The two of them kept clinging to each other, to the point that they couldn’t be separated without the Jaws of Life and the potential for someone to get hurt. Pepa found it funny, at least. She was also the one who came up with the solution of Daniel taking them both to his apartment.

He was fairly certain she was still holding a grudge over the Buckley brothers ghosting the Diazes and this was her petty revenge against him.

Getting both men to his car was actually remarkably easy. He simply took Buck’s hand, and then Buck took Eddie’s, and off they went. The drive, too, wasn’t horrible: the pair of them were crammed into the backseat and ignoring each other’s personal space, so it was basically like being thrown ten years into the past.

It was when they got to the apartment that they hit a snag. Because while Daniel would have very much liked to put them both on the couch and put something bright and colorful on the TV, Buck wanted to show Eddie his new room. This resulted in something of a tug-of-war, which Daniel ended up losing thanks to Eddie helping his brother.

He called Austin for the sake of his sanity. His not-boyfriend was over ten minutes later.

“So you have both of them?” Austin craned his neck to see into Buck’s bedroom. The door was wide open, something Daniel insisted on, showing the two of them sitting in the bed and once again apparently trying to meld or something. They were also tracing their fingers over each other's tattoos, oo-ing and awing at whatever they were seeing.

“Yes,” Daniel said. He sat down at the dining table with a pair of beers for the both of them, his back to the two drugged up idiots so he didn’t have to see it. “This is misery.”

“What is their deal, anyway?” Austin asked. “I thought they hated each other those first couple of shifts, and sometimes they snap at each other enough that I still think that. But other times, they’re best friends.”

“Not my place,” Daniel grumbled, because Buck would get pissy if he outed Eddie to anyone. Even though they were doing that just fine on their own, weren’t they? “How come you’re not tripping like the others?”

“The brownies had nuts in them,” Austin answered with a shrug. “And you know I don’t like ‘em with nuts.”

“And yet you’re not-dating a guy.”

“Nobody’s perfect,” he said, and they both laughed. Though the laughter died out when Austin peered past him with a frown. “Uh, I think they’re making out?”

Oh, God damn it.

Daniel shoved himself to his feet just as there was a loud thump behind him, sounding remarkably like two bodies that rolled off a bed and onto the floor. He didn’t care how much they cried, there was going to be space between them now.

Then

Daniel’s head ached. Being hit in the head by a swinging piece of two-by-four, even with a helmet on, was not fun. Also not fun was the way that he couldn’t be trusted to drive himself home. Luckily, Alma was quite willing to leave work so she could pick him up from the hospital. She even fussed over him a bit, stopping at a drive thru to get him lunch.

“Evan’s gonna worry when he gets home to find you’re not at work,” Alma said as they pulled into their building’s parking lot.

“Probably,” Daniel agreed around a mouthful of fries. “It’ll be fine.”

The elevator was working today, but Daniel didn’t dare try it. Not when its usual noises would be akin to a mariachi band playing in his head. They took the stairs instead, talking and joking as they went. Alma followed him to his door, even though she had to get back to work, and waited while he fumbled his keys out with one hand, the other holding his Whataburger bag.

He walked through the door and immediately dropped the bag and his keys to cover his eyes, letting out a horrified scream. There was another pair of screams from the direction of the couch, accompanied by the sound of someone falling off it to hit the floor.

“What is it!?” Alma demanded, bursting in behind him. “What- Oh!”

Daniel kept his hand up to block his vision as he turned his head. Alma looked about five seconds away from bursting as she backed into the hallway.

“I’ll just uh, I’ll be going,” she said, closing the door. He heard her laughing a moment later, fading away as she left.

“D- Danny,” Evan stammered out. “You’re home early.”

“And you’re not at school,” Daniel shot back. “Neither of you are.”

“Wait.” There was that worry, along with accompanying footsteps coming towards him. “Why aren’t you at work?”

“Evan,” Daniel said, still not looking. “Pants. Both of you, for the love of God, put on pants. And then wash your hands.”

“I don’t know where mine are,” was Eddie’s embarrassed mumbling.

“Find them. Please.”

Daniel kept his eyes locked on the wall beside the door as he heard fabric rustle and someone stomp around in search of clothing, followed by the kitchen faucet being run. It was only until Evan told him they were both decent that he finally gathered up what he dropped and stepped farther into his own apartment. There was one of Evan’s shirts hanging off a dining chair and a pair of briefs that were definitely not Evan’s thrown into a corner.

The boys, meanwhile, looked absolutely ridiculous, half naked with their hair a mess and hickeys visible. Evan was vibrating with nerves; Eddie just looked as if he was waiting for his execution.

“Sit down,” Daniel told them. They did so, perching on the edge of the couch and not appearing at all relaxed. Eddie’s gaze kept switching from his feet, to Daniel, to the door, and then back again. “Christ, Eddie, I’m not going to kill you for defiling my brother.”

“Danny!” Evan squeaked. And then, quieter, “I told you he’s cool with it.”

Ah, right. Because even though Daniel pretty much already knew there was something going on, neither boy had really come out - heh - with it until now. Actually, he was fairly certain Eddie hadn’t come out at all before this moment, not to anyone but Evan. Shit.

“I wouldn’t go that far,” Daniel tried to joke, only to see Eddie flinch. “Yeah, sorry. Just… Both of you, stay there while I go change. And don’t touch my food.”

He sat the Whataburger bag on the dining table and went to his room. While he changed out of his uniform and into something comfier, he could hear the two of them talking quietly. He left them to it, long enough to figure out how he was going to handle this, before stepping back out.

Eddie immediately fell silent. Evan, meanwhile, started peppering him with questions.

“Why aren’t you at work? Are you okay? Did something happen? Did-”

“Evan,” Daniel cut him off as he sat down on the coffee table. “I’m fine. I hit my head, I got sent home.”

“But-”

“I’m fine. You two, though?” He shook his head. “First thing’s first, you’re scrubbing that couch. I am not sitting anywhere that your naked asses or- or- or naked anything have touched.”

Evan and Eddie glanced awkwardly at each other.

“Uhm,” Evan said. “Scoot to your left then.”

“Jesus fucking-” Feeling ill, Daniel did just that, thought better of it and just stood up. “You’re washing the table, too. And- And anywhere else you’ve- Jesus. The both of you are cleaning this entire place, got that? Second thing: You can’t just skip school. If you skip school again, you’re both grounded.”

Evan threw his head back with a groan. Eddie, meanwhile, forgot his terror in favor of confused offense.

“But you can’t,” the kid tried to argue. “You’re not my-”

“Watch me.”

Eddie ducked his head, making Evan snicker. At least until Daniel glared him into silence.

“Third thing,” he said with a sigh. He’d been meaning to have an updated version of The Talk with Evan. Might as well give it to both of them. “Let’s start by talking about safe sex, and then the importance of proper lube.”

“Danny!” they both wailed.

Now

Buck didn’t really remember much from the unscheduled brownie trip. He remembered tiny beauty queens, rude yet colorful clouds, and trying to melt into Eddie. He also remembered making out with Eddie, however much the memory of it made him want to crawl into a hole. So did the other man, he knew, because Eddie couldn’t look at him. Where once they were both getting too comfortable with invading one another’s space again, they were now constantly aware of where the other was and all the ways they intentionally weren’t touching.

Also, Buck was pretty sure he hallucinated Jones in his and Danny’s apartment, and that was just… Yeah. His brain was already weird, and it just got weirder when drugs were added to the mix.

When it was time for Taylor Kelly’s story to air, they all gathered around the television in the station’s den area. Well, almost all of them. Bobby didn’t join them, not that Buck could really blame him. He might not know the exact details of the captain’s own trip, beyond the fact that Taylor and her cameraman were there to record the whole thing, but he did get the impression that it was both very bad and very embarrassing. He also knew it involved Bobby’s late family.

Bobby was in the station’s gym when Buck hunted him down after the piece aired, bench pressing without a spotter.

“You missed a hell of a show,” Buck told him. The captain put the barbell on its rack and sat up with a grunt and a sigh.

“How bad was it?”

“Not bad at all,” he assured him. “You were barely in it. It was, uh, it was nice. Kind of like a puff piece.”

“Really?” Bobby asked, dubious.

“Really! She didn’t use any of your interview or mention the LSD at all.”

“Why would she leave that stuff out?”

Buck honestly didn’t know the answer to that question. The closest thing he could come up with was that she liked him, or at least wanted to have sex with him. Or maybe Taylor was just a better person than they gave her credit for.

Except that she wasn’t, as Buck found out.

He went to the County Health Building, where she was reporting live, and waited until she was done. When he spotted her heading back to the news van, he hopped out of his jeep and strolled over.

“Doesn’t look like you’ll have to worry about traffic anymore,” he said. Taylor looked over with a smile.

“I guess so,” she agreed. “Our department piece was pretty well received, so they’re letting me do more. Nothing major yet, just mosquitoes and neighbors fighting about bamboo, but it’s a start.”

“I’ll miss hearing your voice every day,” Buck told her. “I’m happy for you. And I wanted to thank you for what you did for Bobby, not using any of that stuff he said.”

“Oh, that wasn’t my call. I wanted to use all of it, but I got overruled.”

“Uh.” Buck tried to get his head around that. “You- You were gonna use that?”

“Hell yeah,” Taylor said, no shame in her words. “The only reason we didn’t is because apparently your lawyers are scarier than ours.”

Their lawyers were the city’s lawyers, so they better be scarier.

“Look, your job is saving lives,” she said. “Mine is telling their stories, even the ones they might not want me to.”

“That is some stupid job,” Buck told her, shaking his head in disgust.

“Hey!” she snapped. “I have wanted to be a reporter since I was fourteen. I put myself through college, spent years interning for next to no pay. I did traffic in the hopes that it would lead to my big break, which it did. This stupid job isn’t stupid to me. You don’t have to like it, but you don’t get to make me feel bad about it.”

“If your job involves making a good man feel awful about himself,” Buck said, “then you should feel bad about it.”

“God,” Bret interrupted, coming around from the other side of the van. “Just get a room already.”

They fell into awkward silence, until Buck shook his head again and walked away.

Chapter 13

Chapter Notes

I wish I was capable of planning a fic enough to intentionally have the Halloween episode land on the 13th chapter. As it is, this was serendipity.

Now

It started out as a normal call, or at least Maddie thought that it was a normal call. Admittedly, she hadn’t been doing this dispatcher thing for very long, so she was still getting her scale calibrated for normal versus abnormal, and she knew from her brothers’ stories that normal for Los Angeles probably wouldn’t be normal for elsewhere. But for everything she’d sat through so far, the call seemed very run of the mill.

“Nine-one-one, what’s your emergency?”

“Uh, I was hiking, fell off…” There was static with the man’s words. “Cliff gave out. Can you hear me?”

“I- I can hear you, sir. I’m here,” Maddie assured him as she typed up the details. “Do you know where you fell?”

“I w- I was on the San Ramon Trail. I think…” The high pitch whine of audio feedback made her wince. “Oh, God. I broke my leg for sure. I can see the bone in…”

“I can barely hear you, sir,” Maddie said, doing her best to make out his words over the constant static. “Can you tell me precisely where on the trail?”

“Vista Point, Abalone Cove. I’m bleeding. Please, God, help me. Please-”

There was more feedback, louder than before. It sounded, unnervingly, like a scream. And then the call cut out.

“Hello?” she tried.

But there was no one on the line to respond.

Working with Eddie since they made-out for the first time in more than eight years, while under the influence of LSD, was awkward. For weeks, Buck had been doing his best to keep from letting himself fall back into old and familiar patterns with his ex, had been doing his best to keep himself from getting too comfortable around him. But now, after the make-out session that was thankfully interrupted by his brother and a hallucination of Jones, he’d pretty much tripped over all those boundaries he set up for himself.

It was like, now that his body remembered the feel of Eddie under his hands and the taste of his lips, there was a magnet constantly dragging him closer. Which was absolutely not what Buck wanted, thanks much, because Eddie had torched that ship quite thoroughly. It was just, the more time he spent with Eddie, the more he forgot the years old hurt and remembered why the man was his first love.

At least there was comfort in knowing that Eddie was having the same difficulties he was. The man would often start moving closer to him, an unconscious motion that he would catch himself doing and then jerk away. While the physical space between them hadn’t grown, it had become an uncrossable gulf.

They were repeating a depressingly familiar dance, one that Buck memorized the steps to long, long ago. They kept their hands to themselves, ‘lest anyone got the wrong (right) ideas about what was going on between them. The space between them was always there, it was always respected. And neither of them crossed it, ever.

This time, however, they stuck to their roles even when no one else was looking. Because the dance wasn’t for any viewers, this time around. It was just for the two of them.
