A few days before Halloween, they were sent out because of a hiker that fell off a cliff. The trails the hiker called from were closed down years back, but signs were easily ignored. It took some walking to get to the cliff in question, because they could only take the engine and the ambulance so far. It was a nice day for it though, the baking temperatures of a California summer forgotten in favor of a more comfortable autumn now that they were fast approaching the end of October.

A selfie stick with the cellphone still stuck to it marked where the hiker went over the cliff. Buck frowned at the sight as he and Eddie geared up to go down, something about that nagging at his brain. But there wasn’t time for Buck to linger on it and try to force his brain into figuring out whatever equation it was trying to add up. He pushed it to the back of his mind as he and Eddie abseiled down the cliff.

“Buck, Eddie,” Bobby radioed when they reached the beach at the bottom. “You see anything down there?”

“No,” Buck radioed back. “Just a million-dollar view.”

Something caught Eddie’s attention though, his hand going to his own radio.

“Cap, found something.”

“Is it our hiker?”

The pair of them got down on their hands and knees, pushing away debris that had gathered up to uncover a half-buried skeleton in a small crevice created by some rocks. There was a bag, in relatively good condition, that Buck pulled out.

“A hiker,” Eddie replied while Buck started looking through the bag. “Don’t think it’s the one we came looking for. It’s skeletal remains, Cap.”

“Alright. Flag it but don’t disturb it. We’ll call it in.”

“Unless.” Buck held up the phone he’d found in the bag.

“What, you think a ghost called nine-one-one?”

“Is it any weirder than that werewolf?”

Eddie opened his mouth, probably to start up the years old argument on how werewolves weren’t real and so it couldn’t have been one back then, despite all the people that saw it. Except they got interrupted by a living hiker calling for help. They found that hiker lying on the ground on the other side of the cliff. His face was bloodied and his leg was clearly broken, the bone sticking out of the shin.

“Cap, we’ve got a live one,” Buck reported while the pair knelt down on either side of the guy to examine him.

“Can you tell me your name?” Eddie asked.

“I- Ian.”

“Ian, okay. We’re gonna get you out of here, okay?”

“Alright, guys,” Bobby radioed. “We’re gonna send a basket down to you.”

“How’d you find me?” Ian asked them.

“It wasn’t easy,” Buck answered. “You weren’t on long enough for them to ping your phone.”

“On with who?”

The confusion and memory loss made sense with the visible head wound. Buck was pretty sure he could see the guy’s skull through the gash.

“With nine-one-one,” he said.

“I dropped my phone when I went over,” Ian told them, and suddenly Buck’s brain figured out what was wrong with the math equation it had been trying to solve. The guy’s phone was still on the selfie stick at the top of the cliff! “I didn’t call nine-one-one.”

Buck felt a chill run down his spine as he met Eddie’s eyes. The other man looked equally as unnerved.

“Do not say it,” Eddie hissed at him.

Buck hoped his smirk conveyed the appropriate level of smugness.

“I am telling you,” Buck insisted as he set out the food he just got done heating up. “It was a ghost.”

Once again, he and Danny were invading Maddie’s living space. This time, instead of Chinese take-out, they brought with them dinner that Buck had made. Maddie had opened the door before they could knock or let themselves in, threatened to slam it in their faces, and then let them in with a huff and an eye roll when Danny pointed out that she gave them both copies of the key.

“No, it wasn’t,” Maddie said.

“Yes, it was,” Buck retorted.

“No, it wasn’t.”

“Yes, it was.”

“No- What the hell am I doing?” Maddie shook her head. “Five minutes with you and I’m regressing.”

“Our baby brother’s immaturity is infectious,” Danny said, and shot his brother a grin when he acted all faux-hurt over it.

“C’mon, Mads,” Buck said. Whined, almost. “The only phone down there was in the skeleton’s bag. And there’s no way some other hiker came along, watched him fall, and called it in themselves.”

“Why not?” Maddie demanded to know.

“‘Cause they’d have to be down there, too, to see his broken leg,” he pointed out. “And our guy didn’t see anyone else before we got there.”

“Okay, but it couldn’t have been a ghost.”

“Why!?”

“Because ghosts aren’t real, dummy.”

Buck scowled, even as he loaded a plate up to put down in front of her.

“I never told you about the werewolf, did I?” Danny suddenly asked. Maddie turned to look at him, brow furrowed.

“Like, a guy dressed up as a werewolf?”

Danny shrugged.

“Dunno, don’t think the cops ever caught it to check.”

“Pretty sure it was a naked werewolf,” Buck pointed out. “So, y’know. Not dressed as anything.”

Maddie looked between them.

“Okay,” she said with a sigh. “Tell me about this werewolf.”

Then

Daniel dreaded shifts that fell on the full moon. The crazies seemed to come out in droves on those nights, and even the most mundane calls involved some new flavor of weird. He worked just about every single one of them, however. Because everyone else hated them, too, and were perfectly willing to shove them off onto him. 

He could always use the extra padding to his paycheck, even if he was no longer a probie and as such was being paid more than he was. And besides, every full moon shift he took was another firefighter who owed him a favor when it came time for football season. If dealing with a night of crazies meant Daniel could cheer his baby brother on as he did his best to give other teenagers brain damage, then it was a sacrifice worth making.

The night had just begun when they responded to a motor vehicle accident involving a tree that the driver plowed into headfirst. Normal? No, because amongst all the pieces of car and people, there was porn. Thousands of DVDs of porn. A field full of it, which they had to trek through to find all the remains of the car and the people that had been in it.

That had taken hours, and now they were attending to a fire at the student housing on the University of Texas at El Paso campus. Drunken college students did not mix well with actual lunacy, apparently, and some future genius had the bright idea to microwave a burrito without taking it out of the aluminum foil first. There were sparks, there was smoke, there were flames.

It was simple, a basic in-and-out call, which was clearly just a way to lure them into easing their guard.

The figure was waiting for them when they exited the building and cleared the half-dressed students to go back inside. It was by the engine, just standing there and staring at them. Daniel wondered how the crowd of teens and early twenty-somethings - if he focused too much on how they were just a couple years younger than him, he might go insane - had missed it. 

The shadows made it difficult to actually see much in the way of details, but he could make out a distinct lack of clothes on the figure. There was fur, though, along with pointed ears and something that looked like a muzzle. And its fingers were too long, each one ending in wickedly curved claws.

“What the hell?” he heard one of the other guys whisper, and then shout, “Cap!”

The shouting startled the creature. It yelped out a bark and dropped onto its hands and feet, before bounding off in the direction of the nearby line of trees. Daniel and the others were left to stare after it and do their best to convince themselves that it hadn’t been what it so obviously was.

Evan should have been sleeping. He knew he should have been sleeping, because tomorrow was Monday and that meant he needed to be up in time for school. But he never fell asleep all that easily on nights Danny worked. Not because he was afraid about being alone - even if he was, Tia Alma was right next door - but because he worried about his brother.

It was why he had his new toy. Well, a slightly used toy. Tia Alma insisted on paying him whenever he babysat Delia and Celia, and he saved up for a secondhand police scanner. Now he didn’t have to lie there while his mind conjured up what sort of calls Danny could be going on. He could listen in and know, instead.

Buying it was absolutely proving to be a poor decision, because he’d stayed up listening to it instead of even trying to sleep. He had been texting Eddie, but the other boy had either started ignoring him or fell asleep himself, like the old man he secretly was on the inside. Evan was crafting the perfect mocking text for him to wake up to in the morning, when the scanner burst into life.

“Attention units, be advised there is…” The woman’s voice trailed off. A man’s voice followed it after a few moments of silence, with a questioning,

“Dispatch? Be advised about what?”

“Uh. Be advised there is… A creature. On the UTEP campus.”

More silence as Evan scrambled up onto his knees to stare at the police scanner.

“Dispatch, please repeat. Did you say, creature?”

“Yes,” the dispatcher answered, sounding equally unsure of herself. “Pumper Six-” That was Danny’s! “-reported a creature stalking the campus. Described as… naked and fur-covered, barking, running on all fours.”

“Dispatch, you just described a dog.”

“They also described it as tall and standing on its hindlegs as it stared at them.”

Evan’s thumb danced excited across the keys of his phone, his intent on mocking Eddie utterly forgotten. Instead, the other boy was going to wake up to multiple texts about werewolves and how Danny saw one and how this was the coolest thing ever, why wasn’t he awake? God he was so lame.

“Are you certain that’s what they saw?” another voice broke in to ask.

“We are getting multiple calls about it, now,” the dispatcher responded. “There’s howling in the trees. Uh. There might be… Blood? On its hands? Something red.”

Whoa!

The first man that responded said he was going to check it out, and Evan was left on the edge of his seat. Well, the edge of his bed. The scanner was silent for the next little while, occasionally bursting out with chatter of other units checking in or, to Evan’s increased excitement, updates on the werewolf’s location.

When the officer relayed he was on the scene, Evan nearly tipped over onto his face as he leaned too far off his bed. He clambered down and went over to where the scanner sat on his dresser as he waited.

“Dispatch,” the officer radioed after some time, sounding out of breath. “I don’t know what that thing was, but it was not a dog. It ran off again when I was about to cuff it.”

There were no more reports about the werewolf on the scanner after that. Evan was extremely bleary-eyed when he went to school the next morning, but still chattered excitedly to Eddie about how awesome it was.

(An abandoned house, not far from the UTEP campus, was the site of a drug bust a few weeks later. That the cops found a high-end werewolf costume inside was absolutely a coincidence.)

Now

Carla was a godsend. Not too long ago, Eddie felt like he was fumbling everything, unable to properly take care of his kid. Now he was being given a tour of a very nice private school, with scholarships and grants on the wings that meant he could actually afford to send Christopher. It was such a turnabout from where he’d been a couple months ago that it gave him whiplash.

Sometimes, Eddie really wished he hadn’t made such a mess of things. Not only so that he could have kept Buck with him, but also so he could go to Danny for advice again. How did the man manage to afford being a single parent on a worse salary than the one Eddie currently earned? Eddie had needed to work three jobs in El Paso and he was still always coming up short of what he needed.

Danny had never seemed to struggle as much to make ends meet.

Principal Summers herself gave Eddie and Carla a tour of the school. She was actually friendly, not the customer service friendliness that public school employees put on to hide their burnout. The school was well-kept, none of that aura of poor funding that coated Chris’ current school, or which coated the one Eddie and Buck went to, where it became increasingly apparent at a glance that the schools hadn’t been able to afford to update or renovate in ages. In the case of Chris’ school, probably not since FEMA retrofitted all the schools in the city following Northridge. The whole thing sounded far too good to be true, except that Eddie realized, even as he thought it, that it was true. Because of Carla, and because of Buck.

“Our grounds include two outdoor play areas,” Summers explained to Eddie and Carla, gesturing around as they walked through the courtyard. There were handmade ghosts hanging from the trees. He should send a picture of them to Buck, ask if they were calling 911. Wait, no, he shouldn’t. “State-of-the-art gymnasium, music, theater, the full range of academic offerings, as well as behavioral and cognitive programs.”

Eddie glanced around some more. This all sounded good, very, very good, but her speech wasn’t really addressing his primary concern.

“The programs sound good," he told her. “But Christopher’s happiness is what’s important to me. I want him to feel normal, not like some special-needs charity case.”

“Every child is special,” Summer said. “And yes, some do have additional needs, but they’re all equally our children. They learn as much from each other as they do from us.”

She gestured ahead of them, and Eddie crept closer to one of the classroom doors with Carla. Inside were kids building model houses, kids that were perfectly “normal” and kids as “different” as his own. All of them together, and not a single one batting an eyelash over it.

“I think Christopher would be well cared for,” Carla told him.

“Yeah,” Eddie whispered, and led them back where Summers was waiting patiently. “Okay. Let’s do it.”

“The only thing left would be the family interview,” Summers said.

Eddie felt his heart skip a beat.

“I’m sorry?”

“Christopher’s terrific,” she said, and of course he was. It went without saying, though it should be said often. “We have your information. All we need now is to meet Mrs. Diaz. That won’t be a problem, will it?”

He lied and said that it wasn’t. It was, though. It was absolutely a giant ass problem. And he told Carla as such as they were leaving. Not with quite as strong language, but he figured he got the point across.

“I know Christopher’s mother is a touchy subject with you,” she said, and Eddie almost scoffed. Touchy? Yeah. “But I’m sure if we explain the situation to Principal Summers, we could find a workaround. Do you have a copy of the custody agreement?”

“There isn’t one,” he admitted, not meeting her eyes. “We’re still married.”

Fuck.

Chapter 14

Chapter Notes

content warning: homophobia and the use of slurs

Then

Evan couldn’t say that he and Shannon were friends, not really. Sure, they were friendly to one another, but that was more because Evan was friendly with just about everyone and anyone. It was close proximity due to Eddie, mostly, who’d been excited to reconnect with Shannon again after so long.

Nearly everything that had once been Evan-and-Eddie became Evan-and-Eddie-and-Shannon. Nearly everything, they still had their private moments behind closed doors that Eddie refused to risk anyone else finding out about. But their duo had become a trio, and despite being there first, despite the fact that he was the one Eddie was dating, Evan started feeling like a third wheel.

It was a feeling that was not helped at all by everyone around them. The other guys at school started teasing Eddie about his supposed interest in his friend that just happened to be a girl. Teasing that Eddie only half-heartedly denied, because it was a nice little cover from the truth, wasn’t it? If everyone thought he was interested in Shannon, no one would know who he was actually interested in.

Helena and Ramon, too, were very encouraging about the friendship between the two. There were pointed comments about how Ramon had been quite the lady’s man in his youth, and it was about time Eddie was following his lead. Helena talked about what a nice young lady Shannon was, and how she would make a wonderful girlfriend.

It was the way they said it while either looking right at Evan, or while looking anywhere but, that had his stomach clenching at times. They didn’t know, they couldn’t. But their tolerance for his continued closeness to their son had begun to wane over time, Helena’s smile a bit more fixed whenever Eddie’s plans so blatantly centered around him. There had been an increase in their prying into their son’s romantic life, an increase in the number of questions about if there was any girl he was interested in. They would, oh so casually, talk about how such a handsome, athletic young man like Eddie must have been beating the girls back with a stick.

Sometimes, Evan wondered if everyone looked at him and simply knew. Knew what he was, knew who he loved. The Diaz parents must’ve, even if they didn’t come out and say it. At least they didn’t seem to see it in Eddie.

So everyone around them joked and commented about what a nice couple Shannon and Eddie would make. The way the two of them got along, in a fashion so very similar to Eddie and Evan, just fueled it further. Even Evan could kinda see it, with how it was constantly in his face whenever the three of them were together. And apparently Shannon could see it, too, because one day in the later half of April their senior year, she asked Eddie to the prom.

It was in the middle of lunch, at a table with far too many jocks sitting at it. Shannon walked up to the pair of them, to Eddie, and just straight up asked,

“Would you go to prom with me?”

And Eddie didn’t answer right away. He didn’t say yes right away, like some of the others expected. He didn’t say no, either, like Evan knew he wanted to. Hoped he wanted to, at least. Eddie didn’t say anything, simply stared at Shannon with his jaw hanging a little loose and his grip on the table’s bench a little too tight.

He was panicking, Evan knew. However much he might deny it, he panicked so much about this kind of thing, about people knowing what he was. Evan couldn’t blame him, because he’d seen the not-quite-glares that Helena and Ramon threw at him on the occasions Eddie’s lack of dating life came up. Evan had heard the comments Ramon made, the foul words he muttered in Spanish under his breath, whenever he was home and the topic came up in the news. Evan remembered Timothy, the way he’d been relentlessly bullied and harassed and tormented until he switched schools.

Everyone was staring, waiting, and Eddie was panicking. So Evan slapped a hand on his shoulder, shook it a little, and blurted out,

“Of course he’ll go with you!”

“Y- Yeah,” Eddie managed to stammer out, the words trembling and a little breathless. “Yeah, of course. I’d love to.”

“Perfect,” Shannon chirped with a smile, before going back to her little group of popular girls. They burst into giggles and loud whispers the moment she joined them.

Eddie turned, bewildered and wide-eyed, to stare at Evan. Evan just shrugged, and tried not to dwell too much on the relief that he could see in his boyfriend’s eyes.

Now

Maddie spotted Athena, in uniform, coming out of the elevator onto the dispatch floor. There was what looked like a case file in her hand. Curious, Maddie crossed the floor to talk to her.

“Athena, what are you doing here?”

“I heard about your ghost call,” the sergeant said. Maddie nearly groaned.

“God, Buck won’t let it go,” she said. “It seems like the hiker they rescued is gonna be okay.”

Or so Chimney had told her.

“Yeah.” Athena held up the folder she was holding. “I’m actually here about the one who wasn’t so lucky.”

“Uh, follow me then.”

Terry Flores was the IT specialist. When Maddie hunted him down, with Athena in tow, he was more than eager to help. He claimed a station for himself, and started going through call logs.

“I pulled any call that referenced Vista Point,” he told them. “You’re thinking, what? Six, seven years ago?”

“Our skeleton had a cell, pay as you go,” Athena said. “Untraceable, but the number was reassigned six years ago. I thought that maybe that could narrow the search a little.”

Terry nodded and started typing away.

“Is that all you have?” Maddie asked her.

“That, and this,” Athena said, opening the folder and pulling out large print photos. One was of a skeletal hand, the other a close up of a wedding ring. “Initials on the inside of a band. Six letters of the alphabet. Not much to go on.”

“Your cell phone lead might be,” Terry said, handing the sergeant an ear piece. “I’ve got one here. October Twenty-Ninth, Two-thousand Eleven.”

He hit play and they all three listened.

“Nine-One-One, what’s your emergency?”

“Uh, I was hiking, fell off…” There was static with the man’s words. But that voice, it  sounded just like… “The cliff gave out. Can you hear me?”

“You’re breaking up,” the dispatcher in the recording said. “What’s your location?”

“No,” Maddie broke in, shaking her head. “That’s not right, that sounds like my call from yesterday. Pull it up?”

Terry did, letting it play. Maddie’s voice came through their headsets, but the caller’s was replaced by static. Static that she responded to.

“I don’t understand,” she said. “There was a voice on the other end of the line.”

“The same voice from seven years ago?” Athena asked.

“Yes.”

“So a ghost called Nine-One-One?” Terry sounded skeptical, as he should. Because there was clearly a more rational explanation to be had here.

“He was down there for seven years,” Athena pointed out. “I guess he got tired of waiting.”

Maddie made the mistake of going to Danny and Buck’s after work to tell them about it. Their gloating about a ghost calling 911 was not needed. It also wasn’t true, because ghosts didn’t exist and so therefore could not be calling anyone. It was a system glitch, nothing more, nothing less.

And if she drank enough wine, she might actually believe it.

Eddie had known that Shannon lived in Los Angeles long before Buck told him of their encounter. He had known where she was, known how to contact her, and yet he hadn’t. Not once after she left had he tried to call her.

(He’d tried calling Buck. Again and again and again, until eventually he didn’t even make it to voicemail.)

She had sounded surprised, understandably so, when he finally did call her. Surprised to hear from him, surprised to learn he was in Los Angeles, surprised that he wanted to meet. Eddie Diaz did not panic, no matter how much certain people - no matter how much Buck - liked to say otherwise. But he did get nervous.

He was nervous now, as he opened the door to his home and let Shannon in. 

Shannon did not look much different from when she left. She seemed as nervous as he felt, standing awkwardly on his porch.

“Hi,” he said.

“Hi,” she replied.

Eddie stepped back, let her in, gave her a short one-armed hug. He thanked her for coming, and didn’t argue when she asked to see Chris’ room. He lingered in the doorway while she crept around it, acting like she was invading sacred space.

Shannon picked up one of the newer framed photos sitting on top of the dresser, one of Eddie and Chris that was taken the day Chris was at the station. She didn’t take her eyes off it as she sat down on the bed.

“He’s gotten so big,” she said. “Has… Has he asked for me?”

“Not in a while,” he told her. “He has a lot to keep him busy, though. The move, new school, new city, new people.”

“Sure, yeah. He must’ve been shocked when you said you were leaving Texas.” She got up, put the picture back where it was. “I sure was when you called yesterday.”

“Shannon…” There was a reason he didn’t try calling her all that time. It always turned into a fight.

“So,” she interrupted. “Tell me about this school.”

They moved to the living room, Shannon taking a seat on the couch. Eddie dug out all the information that he’d been given, the folder from the school and the informational pamphlet from Carla. He tossed it down on the coffee table in front of her so she could go through it.

“They want to meet you. They need to meet you,” he clarified, leaning against the wall with his arms crossed. That it put distance between the two of them may or may not have been intentional. “It’s a requirement for admission.”

“It looks… Fancy,” Shannon said, looking through everything. “Not the kind of place I thought you’d be into.”

“Classes are small. Ten kids instead of thirty,” he told her. There’d been thirty kids in some of the classes they’d taken together, their senior year. Crammed into rooms meant to hold only two-thirds as many. “I mean, the art studio… You should have seen him.”

He realized his mistake the moment her face lit up.

“Can I? See him? I mean, this is a big step. Switching schools when he just got here.” She got up and took a couple steps towards him. “Maybe I could talk to him? Make sure it’s what he wants.”

“I don’t think that’s a good idea,” Eddie said, instead of his more instinctive no. “It might confuse him.”

“What’s confusing? I’m his mom.”

“A mom he hasn’t seen in almost two years.”

“My mother was sick,” Shannon said. “She was all alone. You know why I had to go.”

“Yeah, sure,” Eddie admitted. “But we were expecting you to come back. And you didn’t.”

“You could’ve come with me,” she shot back. “But you didn’t want to leave Texas and your parents and your sisters. At least not until it was something that was important to you.”

He was about to come back with a retort, was about to tell her he was trying to do what was best for Chris - just like Danny always did what was best for Buck - when an odd look crossed her face.

“Did you know?” Shannon asked. “That Evan was here, in Los Angeles?”

“No.”

“But if you had, you would’ve come with me. Wouldn’t you? If you had known where he was, you would’ve come straight here.”

Eddie looked away, unable to deny it and unwilling to confirm it. Shannon let out a scoff.

“Of course you would’ve. It was always Evan. Even when he ran first.”

“Hey! Evan is the reason Chris might even get into this school in the first place,” Eddie told her. “From the moment that Evan met him-”

“Evan met him?” Shannon’s laugh was strained. “Evan met our son, but you won’t even let me? Does he know? About prom night?”

“I thought you told him.”

“No. He told you about running into me, didn’t he?” She shook her head. “He saw Chris, I told him when his birthday was. But I remember Evan being worse at math than you, so I don’t know what he knows.”

That was… Eddie wasn’t sure how to feel about that. Was it better that Buck didn’t know? Worse? Buck liked Chris, and Chris liked him. Eddie didn’t want to know how that would change if Buck found out about prom night.

“Look, this isn’t about Evan,” Eddie said.

“It was always about Evan with you,” Shannon spat, already heading for the door. “God forbid you ever have my back.”

“I always had your back.”

Shannon’s laugh this time was bitter.

“No, you didn’t,” she said. “You were in Afghanistan. Even if you weren’t, I wasn’t Evan.”

The door clicked shut behind her.

Then

“Don’t be a fag.”

Eddie winced at the word. It was one of the guys from the football team, Jordan or whatever. He was, like the rest of them, quickly approaching the far side of drunk. Eddie was not too far behind him; the more Shannon pressed against him, the more he drank.

Prom had been excruciating. The days leading up to it had been excruciating. His mother had excitedly shared with the rest of the family that he was taking Shannon, and so he was inundated with relatives prying into his personal life. She had cried a little, at the sight of him in his rented suit. His father had patted him proudly on the back.

Shannon was gorgeous in her prom dress, he couldn’t deny that. Beaming and radiant as her mother, Janet took picture after picture of them. She blushed and tittered when Eddie gave her the corsage that Evan insisted on, melted a little when he kissed her because it was expected of him. It was different from kissing Evan. Softer.

Eddie wasn’t sure he liked it.

The school’s gymnasium was stifling with bodies, and it was decorated with streamers and balloons and cutouts. Eddie thought the theme was supposed to be sea related, but he wasn’t really certain. Time at the beginning was spent mostly along the edges, where Shannon clung to his arm while they both chatted with friends.

There was a bright spot, when Evan showed up. Eddie was surprised, because he hadn’t thought his boyfriend was going to. He felt himself relaxing in the other’s presence until the music changed and Shannon insisted on them slow dancing. Eddie couldn’t deny her, because that was the reason they were there.

He didn’t see Evan again for the rest of the night.

After the dance was over, they got invited to a post-prom party at someone’s house. Eddie hadn’t particularly wanted to go, but Shannon did. They swung by her house long enough for her to change into something less ostentatious, and then went straight to the party.

“Don’t be a fag,” Jordan the football jock spat. One of the others, more into their drink than the rest of ‘em, had draped himself all over Jordan as a joke only to be shoved away.

“Oh c’mon, don’t be like that,” the second guy - Kyle - said from where he’d landed on the floor. He made kissy faces before bursting into laughter at Jordan’s exaggerated disgust. “Do you remember that little queer, freshman year? What was his name?”

“Tommy? Timmy?” his girlfriend, Eddie didn’t know her name, offered. “It was Timothy, right?”

“Right!” Kyle snapped his fingers and pointed at her.

“I remember punching his lights out when I caught him looking at me,” Jordan said with a shudder. “Fucking freak.”

“What happened to him?” Kyle’s girlfriend asked.

“Don’t know, don’t care,” Jordan said. “Good riddance. Right, Eddie?”

“R- Right,” Eddie drained his cup, excused himself to go get a refill from the kitchen. Maybe he should stop, pull back a little. But when he got back to the group, to where Shannon was waiting, they were still laughing about Timothy. He threw back half his drink as he listened to them recount and joke about the way the kid was tormented.

“Eddie?” Shannon was tracing a finger down his arm. He shivered.

“Yeah?”

“Let’s go upstairs.”

Just like that day in the lunchroom, Eddie felt his heart stutter and his throat close up. The others around them were hooting and hollering, because they knew what that meant as much as he did. Better, probably, because he and Evan rarely came to these things.

He hesitated, glanced at the people around them, the ones who had just been laughing about pummeling Timothy for looking. He wondered what they would think if he told Shannon, “no.” He wondered what they would do, if they knew about him and Evan. If they even thought they knew about him.

“Y- Yeah, okay,” he stammered out. He emptied his drink again and sat it down on the table with all the other cups. “Sure.”

She smiled and took his hand, leading him to the stairs. Behind him, Kyle and Jordan wolf whistled.

Chapter 15

Now

Through a little bit of begging, Eddie managed to get the evening of Halloween off. He’d have to work Thanksgiving, but that was fine. His kid wanted the two of them to dress up and go trick-or-treating, and Eddie would happily move the world to do that. It wouldn’t always be possible, he knew, and there would be some holidays where he would simply have to bite his tongue and find a way to deal with it. The fact that he could spend this Halloween with Chris was something he very much intended to take advantage of.

Carla was in the kitchen, putting her things away in her bag, when Eddie stepped into the dining room to show off his costume. She took one look at him and laughed.

“Are you some kind of pirate?”

Pirate? What? No.

“Snake Plissken?” He took out the toy gun from its holster and struck a pose with it. Carla just stared blankly at him, clearly not recognizing the name. “Escape from New York? Kurt Russell? John Carpenter’s masterpiece?”

She was worse than Buck and Danny. He remembered almost being personally offended by the fact that neither of them had seen it. Offended enough that he made Danny rent both it and Escape from LA for a movie night. That was back when Blockbuster was still a thing.

God, he felt old some days.

“Right, right. Well, I prefer Tango and Cash myself. But you look cute, so I approve.” Carla laughed and walked past him into the dining room. She glanced at his face and came to a stop. “Though I’m disappointed that costume didn’t come with a smile.”

Eddie sighed, pushed his eyepatch up so it was no longer covering his eye.

“I had a fight with Shannon,” he explained. “When I asked her to help with Christopher and the school, she asked to see Christopher first.”

“And you said no?” Carla looked at him as if he was insane. “Okay, if you want her back in your life, you’re gonna have to ease her into it at some point.”

“Who said I want her back in my life?”

“Please,” she scoffed. “So you decided to leave Texas, and you just so happened to choose a job working thirty minutes from where your ex lives?”

Eddie barked out a laugh, because… Wow. Carla just did not know. He’d ended up choosing a job where his other ex worked. It was almost enough to restore his withered faith and convince him that God really did exist. And also that He hated him, specifically.

“Ignoring the fact that mi abuela y tia live here, too,” he said. “LAFD are the best in the country. In the world.”

“If she’s gotten herself back together, you know what’s best for your son,” Carla argued. “And that’s for his mother to be back in his life, sooner than later.”

“Is it?” Eddie asked her. “Is that what’s best for him?”

“A boy needs his mama.”

Eddie laughed again.

“Danny and Buck moved to get away from their mother, so I don’t think that’s true.” He ignored the curious frown she shot him. “Besides, what if she screws up again?”

“Both of you have grown up a lot of the years,” Carla said. “And I don’t know about Buck-”

“Buck?” Christopher asked, making his way into the room. He was dressed like Wolverine, complete with dog tags, plastic claws, and glued on sideburns. “Is he coming, too?”

It was amazing what kind of impression a single car ride could make on a kid. It was probably the fact that Buck had listened to and talked with him like he did everyone else. Chris adored him and decided he was his best friend. Buck just seemed to have that kind of effect on (most) Diazes.

“Sorry, mijo.” Eddie crouched down beside him. “Buck’s working tonight.”

“Aw. Can we send him a picture of our costumes?”

“Of course, baby.” Carla was already taking out her phone to do just that. “Now show us those muscles and go, grr.”

Then

Before that day, Eddie had thought that Evan and Danny’s parents were dead. Neither of them talked about their parents, and there were no family pictures that included them. There were a few of their sister, living way up in Boston, but not a single photo of their mom or dad. Considering that Danny was raising his teenage brother on his own, it was reasonable to assume that something had happened to their parents.

So imagine Eddie’s surprise when a make-out session with his boyfriend was interrupted by rude knocking that turned out to be the not-so-dead Buckley parents.

What followed was as informative as it was appalling. It had seemed okay at first, or at least not horrible. Sure, they insisted on calling him Edmundo instead of Eddie, but that was tolerable for the sake of being polite. Then they started in on how rude it had been of Evan, to make them wait so long in the hallway. Which, fine, they probably should have answered the door earlier. They’d simply been a bit too busy with each other.

Things got worse as they went on, with Margaret’s comments coming out cutting. Evan, always talking, always bright, always full of motion, fell silent and still and dull. Like he was trying to disappear, like he was hoping he’d turn invisible and his mother would leave. Eddie was quickly reaching his limit when Danny finally came home, bursting through the door like some action hero. 

And then Eddie made the mistake of mentioning football, of running with the assumption that like any decent parents, they’d want to see their son play. Danny did, after all. Danny bent over backwards to be at every one of Evan’s games. But it turned out to be a faulty assumption, with Margaret trying to forbid Evan from playing and Danny immediately shutting that down.

The argument that followed, between Danny and their parents, was not pretty. Nor was it quiet. It was around the time the shouting really started, when the Buckley parents talked about taking Evan back to Pennsylvania to keep him safe, that Eddie grabbed Evan by the wrist and dragged him out of there.

Evan hadn’t resisted. He remained silent all the way to the pick-up truck, the one that wasn’t really Eddie’s but which only he ever drove. It was unnerving, the way that half an hour in the presence of those people had seemed to smother everything Eddie loved about the other boy.

There was a park, not far from where Danny and Evan lived. Eddie didn’t intend to drive there, wasn’t really paying attention where they were headed because he was so focused on getting Evan away from those people. They’d spent a lot of time at that particular park, however, before they got their license and they weren’t bound to what was within walking distance, so perhaps it only made sense.

When they got there, when they parked, Evan immediately hopped out and ran off in the direction of the swings with Eddie following a bit more sedately behind him. They were both getting too big, too tall, for the swings. It didn’t stop them from sitting down, though. And for once, Eddie did not worry about who might see them when he took Evan’s hand in his own.

“So,” Eddie said, breaking the silence. “Those were your parents.”

“Yup.” It was the first word Evan had said since they left. Since before they left.

“I thought they were dead.”

The other boy broke out in surprised laughter. It was nice to hear.

“No, they’re just in Pennsylvania.”

“And they uh. They don’t want you playing football,” Eddie said. Evan winced. “I’m sorry. I shouldn’t have said anything to them.”

“Not your fault,” Evan mumbled, digging the toe of his shoe into the dirt. “You didn’t know.”

“Why don’t they?” Eddie asked. “I mean, my mom and dad, they thought baseball was a waste of time.”

“Yeah, I remember. Something about how you should be at home, being the man of the house.”

“Right… But uh. But your mom, she seemed worried about you.”

Evan let out a snort filled with bitter amusement.

“Nah, she’s just tired of me being selfish,” he said. “She doesn’t want me getting too hurt that I can’t help Danny. Can’t really blame her.”

“Selfish?” Eddie stared at him, not really comprehending. “How could football… What?”

“Dunno.” Evan shrugged, swung their joined hands a little. “If Danny gets sick again, it’s my job to help him. So like, I understand why Mom gets upset that I’m doing something that might, y’know, stop me from doing that. But she just wants to stick me behind glass and forget about me until then. It’s why Danny and I moved out here.”

“If Danny gets sick again?” Eddie asked.

“Yeah. He was sick as a kid. It’s why Mom and Dad had me, so I could make him better,” his boyfriend said. “It’s what I’m supposed to do if he gets sick again. It’s cool. At least I’m useful.”

That sounded… Eddie wasn’t really certain. It sounded the exact opposite of cool.

“You’re always useful,” he said, not knowing what else to say.

“I know, that’s what I just said.”

“That’s… That’s not what I…” Eddie trailed off and shook his head. He didn’t know what to say. There were words, three of them, that sat in the back of his throat and tried to crawl their way out every time he spoke to Evan. But they were too big, too important, too scary.

So he didn’t say anything, and Evan didn’t say anything. The two of them sat on the swings in silence, holding hands, until Danny texted with the request for Evan to come pack a bag and then go stay at Eddie’s house for the next few days.

Neither of them argued against the idea.

Now

Eddie sat in his truck outside of Durand School, anxiously waiting. The school had called to inform him about Shannon’s interview with the principal. So here he was, waiting for her to come out while his guts felt like they were twisting in his stomach.

He didn’t know if Carla was right or wrong. He didn’t know if Christopher was better off with his mother, or better off without her. Carla didn’t know, either, not really. She didn’t know Shannon at all and barely knew him. Eddie had brought up Buck’s mother while talking to the health aide, but it felt wrong to compare Margaret to Shannon. He’d only met her and her husband the once, but it was enough for him to know why Danny had taken Buck so far away.

Shannon was not Margaret, but did that mean Carla was right? 

Movement at the school doors caught his attention. She saw Shannon in a bright yellow sundress stepping out with Principal Summers. They shook hands, said their goodbyes, and then Summers was going back inside while Shannon headed across the parking lot.

Eddie hopped out and went to intercept her. She slowed when she saw him, her expression closing off.

“Hey.” He felt a bit awkward as he began to walk her to her car. “The school called to tell me about your appointment, I thought I’d head over.

“Well, don’t worry,” she said. “I didn’t tank the interview. At least, I don’t think I did.”

“I’m sure you did great. I appreciate it, Christopher will, too.”

“That’s why you’re here?” They reached her car and she started to open the driver’s door. Eddie caught it before she could, closing it again. Shannon glared at him. “To pass along thank-yous from my own son?”

“When you left, I understood. Or I tried to,” he told her. “You were taking care of your mother, I was taking care of Christopher. We’d already drifted apart, the actual distance didn’t matter. But I always thought you’d come home, that we’d fix things. But you didn’t. So, I guess, just… Why?”

“I didn’t know how,” Shannon admitted. “The longer I was gone, the harder it was to come back. To face you, and to face Christopher, and…” Her voice broke. “He must hate me.”

“He doesn’t hate you for leaving.”

“No! No, because I did this to him,” she said. Eddie stared at her, bewildered. Did what to him? “I have relived every moment of that pregnancy so many times, just trying to figure out how it happened and what I did wrong. And I thought I could make up for it, so I did all this research, finding new treatments and different therapies. But it was all still so overwhelming and exhausting, and I just… I needed a break. So then the mother who hurt her kid left him.”

She started crying, now. Big globs of tears that ran down her cheeks. Eddie was moving before he consciously realized it, wrapping his arms around her.

“I’m sorry,” she said, but it was muffled by the way her face was pressed into his shoulder.

“You didn’t do anything wrong,” Eddie told her. “What happened, it wasn’t your fault.”

Shannon sniffled and pulled away, wiping at her face.

“I know that,” she said. “I just… I don’t feel it.”

“Christopher loves you,” Eddie said, taking her hand. “And he misses you. And… And I miss you.”

He did. She’d been his friend and his partner, someone to lean on after his shitty choices lost him Buck and Danny. She’d been there with him, the two of them suddenly thrown into parenthood far before either of them were ready. At least until his shitty choices led to her leaving, too.

“I missed you,” she said. 

And then Shannon was leaning forward, her lips crashing into his. Muscle memory had him kissing back, and it was just like he remembered. As soft as he remembered. But then his brain caught up with his actions and he broke away, stepping back and shaking his head slightly.

“Uhm,” was the most intelligent thing he came up with to say. “Uh. We… We’ll talk, okay?”

“Yeah.” She nodded, smiling shyly. “Okay.”

Eddie opened the car door for her, an action that made her smile widen, and then stepped back as she climbed in. He lingered, watched her start her car and drive away, before going to his truck, hoping the entire time that he wasn’t making a mistake.

Buck squinted at Danny. More accurately, he squinted at the book his brother was reading while they lounged on the couch. The two of them were marathoning horror movies together while going through a bag of discount candy. Or at least Buck was marathoning horror movies. Danny had pulled out a rather large textbook in the middle of Freddy Krueger murdering a pool party’s worth of teenagers. His nose had been stuck in it ever since.

“What?” Danny demanded, not even looking up from the book.

“Whatcha reading?” Buck asked, unwrapping a piece of candy to pop into his mouth.

“None of your business.”

“C’mon,” he whined, pulling his knees under him so he could lean into his brother’s space better. Even as Danny shied away with a scowl, Buck could see the diagram on the page he was on. “Is that the paramedic textbook?”

“It is.”

“Why?” Buck asked.

“Because I wanted to be a doctor,” Danny said with a shrug. “And this is easier than spending the next eight or more years juggling school and a full time job with odd hours.”

“Okay. But why now? Why didn’t you just become a paramedic when we were in El Paso?”

“Because being a firefighter meant I got paid more,” his brother answered. “And, I don’t know. It’s kinda Maddie’s fault.”

Buck frowned and waited for that statement to be built upon. It wasn’t, though. Danny just silently refocused on the textbook again.

“So are you not going to be driving the rig anymore?” he asked.

“I am. I’ll just be doing that plus this.”

As he opened his mouth to bug his brother with yet more questions, someone started knocking on the door. A light, polite rapping. The two brothers frowned at each other, curious as to who it could be. Buck unfolded himself to get off the couch, managing to keep from falling onto his face, and went to answer it.

Carla’s smile greeted him.

“Oh, uh, hey. Come on in.” He opened the door wider and stepped aside so should get past him. “Is everything okay?”

“Everything’s fine, hun,” she assured him. “Hello again, Daniel.”

Danny gave her a wave, still frowning a little.

“Not that it isn’t great to see you,” Buck said. “But uh, what’s brought you over?”

“Curiosity,” Carla answered. She was looking around at the apartment, more than she had the day Buck invited her over to introduce her to Eddie. Her gaze paused at every picture that hung on the wall or was displayed on a shelf. “I was talking to Eddie before they went trick-or-treating on Halloween.”

“Thanks for the pictures, by the way.  They were adorable.”

On the couch, Danny rolled his eyes. Buck had maybe spent too long shoving said pictures into his face after he got home.

“You’re welcome. Chris insisted,” she said. “But I wanted… Actually, I’m not really certain why I’m here. Curiosity, like I said. Eddie and I were talking about Shannon-”

She was interrupted by Danny letting out a disgusted scoff. When they both looked at him, he was shaking his head as he flipped a page in the textbook.

“That,” Carla told Buck. “That’s why I’m curious. Because there’s a picture on Eddie’s mantle of the two of you. Rather young, too.”

“Oh.” Buck already knew about the picture. It was the picture Chris had recognized him from, when they met after the earthquake. “It’s on his mantle?”

“It is. I didn’t really connect it at first, but that birthmark of yours…” Carla shook her head. “You two knew each other. Before he came to Los Angeles.”

“Unfortunately,” Danny chimed in. Buck shot him a scowl, but there wasn’t any point in denying it when Carla had seen photographic evidence.

“Danny holds a grudge,” he said. “Why, uh. Why are you asking? Wait, why were you two talking about Shannon?”

Last Buck knew, Eddie hadn’t seemed interested in reconnecting with his wife. Though it had always been a matter of time, because he very much doubted it was a coincidence that Eddie showed up mere months after Shannon. Even if Eddie had known Buck was here before that surprise meeting, Buck knew the man wouldn’t have come for him. Shannon was the easier, more acceptable choice, after all.

“Curious, like I said.” Carla shrugged. “And the new school we’re trying to get Chris into needed to interview her. Eddie said she did it today.”

“Oh, well, that’s… I hope Chris gets in.” The words sounded lame to his own ears. In the corner of his vision, he saw Danny give him a worried glance. “Uh. Have you heard from Abby lately?”

Carla frowned at him.

“Yeah,” she said, the word coming out slowly. “Her brother wants her to stay with him for a bit…”

Chapter 16

Now

There was downtime at the station and Daniel was taking advantage of it. He was sat in the 133’s den, feet propped up on the coffee table with the paramedic’s textbook open in his lap. The pages were littered with colorful post-it notes, and there was a notebook on the couch cushion beside him. He’d started the habit of reading through a chapter once and then going through it a second time, taking notes to make better sense of what the pages said as well as help him remember it.

“This whack-job,” Mendoza said, waving the remote around. On the television, some reporter - Daniel was fairly certain it was the same one that ran that story on his baby brother’s station - was talking about a lady holding up traffic on the freeway. “If I was Norman, I’d keep looking the other way and move on.”

Daniel let out a grunt of acknowledgement, not really paying attention. The deadline to apply for the next round of paramedic training courses was coming up and while he knew he didn’t need to be through this book by then, obviously, he still wanted to get as far ahead as he could. 

This was all Maddie’s fault, coming back into their lives and talking about being a nurse. Nagging and reminding him that he’d wanted to be a doctor once upon a time. She’d tried convincing him that it wasn’t too late to go back to school, and he knew that it wasn’t. He also knew that, as he’d told Buck, he had no interest in spending the next eight or more years trying to fit college courses around his shift schedule, or trying to fit his shift schedule around college classes.

Maybe if he’d already had his bachelor’s degree it wouldn’t seem so daunting. As it was, getting certified as a paramedic was quicker and easier, not to mention kinder to his schedule, and the costs were covered by the department.

“Oh, hey.” Mendoza smacked him on the arm, jolting him. Daniel raised his head to glare, but the other man wasn’t even looking at him. “Look who’s on the scene.”

On the screen, Buck and Eddie - Daniel’s sneer was entirely reflex - were on the overpass that the woman was on the side of. As he watched, his baby brother was strapping on a harness and his helmet. And then the woman was tearing open the bathrobe she was wearing and the camera cut away for a few moments.

By the time it cut back, Buck was straddling the guardrail right above where the woman was pacing. Buck started climbing down as others dragged an airbag out from under the overpass to where the woman would land. Which upset the lady. Enough so that she pulled a gun out of the pocket of her bathrobe.

Daniel dropped the textbook, bolting up right. He could do nothing but watch as she first shot the airbag and then pointed the gun at his baby brother.

Buck was not exactly a stranger to guns and gun related violence. Hershey and El Paso had been safe, relatively, but Juarez was just starting to have its problems while he was going to high school. He could remember, on the nights they had the windows open to get some breeze, listening to the distant and not-so-distant pops from across the border. While Los Angeles was better, it was still Los Angeles. And none of this touched on all the various places Buck had been to in the years between El Paso and LA.

This was the first time he’d ever had a gun pointed at him.

There was a woman on the side of an overpass, demanding to be seen. Her name was Lola, and there was a hand-painted sign stuck up behind her, demanding for Norman, her husband, to see her. She was wearing a bathrobe and shoes, and nothing else: when she tore it open, she flashed everyone down below.

Most of the 118 were down there, Bobby running point with Athena while Chimney and Hen oversaw the inflation of the airbag, tucked away where Lola couldn’t see. Buck and Eddie, meanwhile, took the ambulance up onto the overpass itself, parking above her and getting geared up so Buck could climb down to her. When he was ready, he perched on the guardrail and waited for the word to be given.

It came shortly after Athena told Lola that Norman was on the way. He clambered down as Chimney and the others dragged the airbag out. And that was when Lola pulled out her gun. She shot the airbag, once, twice, three times. Everyone on the ground scrambled for cover, the cops taking position behind their cruisers with guns of their own.

Then the gun was pointed at Buck. He might have peed a little as he threw his hands up.

“Lola, listen to me very carefully,” Athena said through the speaker of her cruiser. She and Bobby were looking very worried down there, which did not help Buck’s nerves at all. “You now have every firearm in the Greater Los Angeles area pointed at you. If you continue to threaten that man, they will fire. Do you understand?”

The seconds that followed were tension-filled, with the gun trembling in Lola’s grip and Buck praying that Danny was busy somewhere far, far away and was not watching. Finally, after far too long, Lola dropped her arm. But she still held the gun, and so Buck did not relax all that much.

“I don’t want to hurt anybody!” she cried. “I just can’t handle being ignored anymore!”

“I know exactly what you mean,” Buck blurted out.

“Come on,” Lola scoffed. She gestured at him with the gun, and he could not help flinching. “Yes, I’m sure you get ignored all the time.”

“I used to do things like this as a kid,” he said. “I- I mean, not exactly like this. I’d climb up a tree or get onto the roof of the house, the higher the better, and then I’d ‘accidentally’ fall. Sometimes it really was an accident.” Lola looked horrified. Buck wondered what Eddie was thinking, if his expression was at all similar, but didn’t want to look away from the unstable woman with the gun to check. “Sometimes I’d do other things, dangerous stunts on my bike, mess up a trick on my skateboard. Get hurt. Then my mom would come running and fuss over me. It was the only time she’d fuss over me. The rest of the time, I was just… I was just there, a part of the scenery, something to be ignored.”

There was the wailing of approaching sirens, and the both looked down as an officer on a bike came to a stop inside the police cordone. As they watched, an older man hopped off and started talking to Athena while they fitted him with a vest. When that was done, the sergeant brought him over to her cruiser and handed him the microphone of her cruiser’s radio to talk.

“Lola, honey, it’s me,” the man, Norman, said. “I’m here, and I see you.”

“Liar!” Lola shouted back, swinging the gun around. Buck flinched back. “You look right through me! You don’t even notice me, I’m just scenery to you!”

“Honey, listen to me, I’m here and I see you. And I need you to put the gun down, okay? Put- Put the gun down and let them bring you to me,” Norman urged. Buck was a big fan of that idea. Huge fan. He was nodding like a bobble head as the man spoke, hoping that Lola would agree. “Then we can spend the rest of our lives together, and I can show you how much I love you.”

“I don’t believe you!” she shouted down, quashing Buck’s hopes on that front. “You don’t even know who I am anymore! I mean… I mean, I don’t even know…”

Buck kinda sympathized with her. To a point. He’d been there before, where he’d felt lost and unsure about himself. Before Danny had thrown him a lifeline. Down below, a visibly confused Norman was talking to Athena and Bobby. He was just raising the mic back to his mouth when his wife started talking again.

“I’ve been a mother for eighteen years, and now that Zack’s gone, I don’t remember who I was before that! Back when you used to look at me!” Lola shouted down. Below them, Norman suddenly pulled out his phone and started fiddling with it. She shook her head at the sight. “Maybe you had the right idea.”

Lola made for the edge of the platform they were on. Buck lunged for her, grabbing her by the wrist.

“No. No, no, no,” he said, shaking his head at her. “I did not have the right idea. Kid me absolutely did not have the right idea.”

Music began to play, Norman holding the mic to his phone. Lola stared at Buck for a moment before looking down.

“That song,” she whispered. “He remembered. It was our first date.”

The music, plus some more words from Norman, was enough for Lola to allow Buck to get them both up to the overpass proper. Eddie checked her over while they waited for the others to join them and Buck allowed himself to relax, now that the gun was out of Lola’s hands. Athena showed up with Norman and allowed the two their touching reunion moment, before ruthlessly arresting the woman.

Buck, who still remembered having the gun pointed at him, did not feel an ounce of sympathy. Especially when his phone began to ring in his pocket.

“Hi, Danny-”

“Are you okay!?”

“So I guess you saw the news.”

“I’m fucking serious,” his brother said. “Are you okay? Who the fuck am I kidding, you just had a gun pointed at your head. I’m going to talk to Mehta, and then I’ll see you at your station.”

“No, no, Danny. I’m fine. I swear, I am telling you the truth. I’m fine,” Buck said, doing his best to get his brother to believe him before the man crashed into the 118 like an overprotective hurricane. “Would it help if I had Cap take a picture of me to send to you?”

“...Yes.”

Buck rolled his eyes and handed his phone over to a silently laughing Bobby.

“Do you want to talk about it?”

Buck closed his locker and glanced over at Eddie. It was just the two of them left in the locker room, everyone else having already changed into their civvies and headed out. Buck had lingered in the showers, still a bit shaken by having a gun in his face, and let himself relax under the hot water for maybe a bit too long.

Eddie, he was pretty sure, had waited specifically to ambush him.

“Talk about what?” he asked. “The gun?”

“No,” Eddie said. “Well, yeah, sure. That too. But I meant… You kinda admitted to a total stranger that you jumped off roofs to get your mother’s attention.”

“Only a couple times.” Buck shrugged. “That was ages ago, man, before you even met me. It’s fine.”

The other man shook his head at him.

“It is a miracle you survived childhood,” Eddie said. “Hell, it’s a miracle you survived yesterday.”

Buck was fairly certain Danny deserved most of that credit, along with Maddie. 

“Yeah, well.” He shrugged again. “Hey, did Chris get into that school? Carla told us Shannon had to interview.”

“Oh, uh. Y- Yeah.” Eddie blinked, ducked his head a little. “He starts next semester.”

“Kick ass.”

“Yeah. Hey, did you wanna…” He trailed off, but Buck still knew him well enough to know what the question was.

“I’m hitting a bar with Chimney,” he said. “I’d invite you, but I’m betting you need to get home and let Carla go.”

Besides, Buck very much did not want to go hang out with Eddie at his place. Or, well, he very much did, and that was entirely why he very much shouldn’t. Even if not going meant not getting to hang out with the world’s coolest kid.

Chimney was huffily waiting for him outside, when Buck finally made it to the bar. It was a badge-and-ladder bar, the one with the karaoke that they frequented a lot as a team. The moment they stepped foot inside, he caught people eying him up and preened a little. Even if they did just see him as a piece of meat, at least they thought he was an attractive piece of meat.

That was a really weird thought to have.

They chatted as they found a table to sit at, with Chimney pulling out his phone the moment they did. Buck frowned at it, because while hanging out with him at a bar was better than risking any mistakes at Eddie’s or resisting the urge to bother his brother as he studies, being ignored in favor of a phone was not his idea of a good time. There was a blond woman licking the salted rim of her margarita glass while maintaining strong, intimate eye contact with him right over there. She wasn’t likely to ignore him.

A waitress popped up, shielding him from the blond woman’s view.

“What can I get you guys?”

“Uh, two IPAs, please.”

“And a glass of chardonnay,” Chimney added, not even looking up from his phone. The waitress nodded and left, leaving Buck to frown even harder at the other man.

“Who’s the wine for?” Because he knew it wasn’t for Chimney.

“Maddie.” He finally put down his phone and waved to someone across the room. “She was parking.”

That was Maddie. He was waving to Maddie.

What.

“You invited my sister?”

“Hi!” his sister chirped as she joined them, claiming the seat beside Chimney. “Sorry I’m late. I don’t know why I get on the freeway, I always regret it.”

“Because you are an eternal optimist,” Chimney said. “Are you hungry?”

“Oh, yeah.” Maddie threw her purse across the table so it landed on the bench beside Buck. “Should we get that thing we got last time?”

“Last time?” he asked, feeling unmoored. What the hell was going on here?

The waitress returned with their drinks, putting the wine down right in front of Maddie like this was perfectly normal. Chimney then ordered the combo platter that they apparently got last time - last time? - and the waitress left again. None of this helped Buck understand anything about his current situation, especially the way Maddie and Chimney let out little laughs as they clinked their drinks together.

“Buck!” Maddie said, so sudden that he jumped a little. “How are you feeling? Are you okay?”

Buck blinked at her and then scowled at Chimney.

“Did you invite me out just so my sister could check on me in person?” he demanded. The other man looked away as he drank his IPA. “I already had Danny threatening to come down to the station.”

“I invited him, too,” Maddie said. “He said he was busy.”

Buck scrunched his face up at that. He kinda wished Danny had come out, because as time went on he started getting flashbacks to third wheeling in high school. He wasn’t sure if this was better or worse than back then. At least, not until the two got up to do karaoke together and he had to listen to the pair duet a love song.

This was Hell, and he needed more alcohol.

When he hit the bar for another IPA, the bartender gave him a glass of something else entirely.

“Uh, what’s this?” Buck asked.

“Bourbon, neat,” the guy said, and then gestured behind him. “The lady said real heroes don’t drink pale ale.”

Taylor Kelly, sitting on the other side of the bar, smirked at him as she drank her own bourbon. She was a much better alternative than being a witness to whatever the hell was happening between his sister and his coworker, plus she was kind enough to buy him a stronger drink, so he went over and sat beside her.

“I see Chimney’s got a girlfriend.”

“No, he doesn’t.” It was a weak denial and Buck knew it. “That’s, uh. That’s my sister.”

“Oh, sorry.” She glanced over to the stage. “They’re kinda good.”

“It’s weird,” he said, and really wanted to pretend it wasn’t happening. “So uh. What’s a fancy TV reporter like you doing in a place like this?”

“Stalking you, of course.”

Taylor said it so matter of fact, with such a straight face, that for a moment Buck found himself once again wishing Danny was there. Not to suffer with him, but to save him from whatever this was. 

“I’m kidding,” she said with a laugh. He relaxed a little. “The station is right around the corner, and I like to keep my ear to the ground. What about you? You come here a lot?”

“Sometimes,” he admitted. “Places like this one. I actually used to bartend a lot, so…”

“You must’ve had no problem getting tips, then,” Taylor said with a smile, leaning forward to put her hand on his thigh.

Buck smirked and threw back the last of his bourbon.

They ended up in the women’s bathroom, in one of the stalls. It was much better than being a third wheel.

Austin sat the beer down on the table and Daniel reached blindly for it, attention focused on rereading the notes he had written down earlier in the day. He heard the other man laugh as he patted around, and then the bottle was being pushed into his hand.

“I’m surprised you’re not out hunting down Lola,” Austin said, popping the cap off his own drink. “Y’know, for threatening to shoot your brother.”

“She’s in lock up,” Daniel said. He was fairly certain part of the reason Athena arrested Lola was to keep her safe from him. Or at least that’s what he inferred from the way the sergeant immediately informed him that Lola was in police custody and being charged for assault. “‘Sides, I’m busy with this.”

“You have time,” he pointed out. “You should save that kind of studying for when you get to the actual exams, not the sign-up deadline.”

“Yeah, but I like this stuff. I always did.”

“Why didn’t you do something like this before, then? Why just a firefighter?”

Daniel took a swig of his drink as he thought how to answer that. Normally, he wouldn’t hesitate to tell him. But it involved his brother, and Austin working with Buck complicated things a bit. 

“I was going to,” he finally said. “Remember I told you I had leukemia as a kid? So I spent a lot of time in the hospital. It made me want to be a doctor, the way they helped me. And I had this whole plan laid out before I was even in high school, what med schools I wanted to go to and everything.”

“What happened?”

“I realized I had to choose,” Daniel said, twisting the beer bottle where it sat on the table. “I could go to college, go to med school, go do what I always wanted to do. Or, I could get Buck the fuck out of Hershey and away from our parents. It wasn’t that hard of a choice.”

Maddie nagged at him about it, even when she herself didn’t go back to school until she moved to Boston. It was the entire reason he applied for colleges he already knew he would never step foot in, because she pushed him into it. She simply never understood that leaving Buck behind with their parents was not an acceptable option, because she had always only ever seen the best of them. Even when their worst was so blatantly on display in front of her.

“And then in Texas,” Daniel continued. “You could either be a firefighter or a paramedic, not both, and being a firefighter paid more. Then when we came here, I’d just… Forgot.”

“You forgot,” Austin repeated.

“Yup.”

He’d had other things on his mind, he figured he could be forgiven about forgetting a childhood dream.

“Your life is very depressing,” Austin said. Daniel burst out laughing. “I’m gonna order a pizza, any preferences?”

“If you put pineapple on it, I’m breaking up with you.”

“Oh no,” the other man drawled. “And then I’d have to find someone else to fake a relationship with.”

Daniel threw a post-it note at him. It fluttered in the air and came to a stop a good foot away from its target.

Chapter 17

Now

They’d intended to crash Maddie’s night at her place. It had been too long since the last time they’d done it, just before Halloween, and so it seemed like a perfectly good time to do it again. Except, when they got there and started letting themselves into her apartment, they both received a text from her calling them idiots and informing them that she was at Chimney’s place.

Daniel had never been to Chimney’s, never had any need to visit his baby brother’s coworker. Buck had, back before he got disinvited from watching games there with everyone else so Chimney could try and score with one of the catfishing victims, so he was the one that drove them over.

“Why is she here?” Daniel asked as they got off the elevator and headed for the right apartment. “How long have they been hanging out?”

“Hell if I know,” Buck answered with a shrug. “He invited her to the bar the other night. Screw you for not coming, by the way. I had to suffer through that alone.”

“I was busy.” Daniel was pretty sure his evening spent eating pizza with Austin was much more preferable to whatever hell his brother went through that night. “‘Sides, the last time I went to a bar with you, you disappeared for half an hour and came back with a hickey.”

Buck rolled his eyes and juggled the large container of pozole so he could raise his fist, rapping his knuckles against the door. Their sister answered it, as confidently as if it was her own apartment, and that did not serve to improve Daniel’s mood. Chimney greeted them from farther inside, where he was wiping down silverware in the cramped little kitchen space.

“We brought food,” Buck said as he stepped inside. “Not sure if there’s enough for four people, but…”

“It’s fine, we have Chinese on the way,” Chimney said. “Drinks?”

There was already a glass of white wine sitting on the tiny kitchen table, next to Maddie’s phone. Daniel eagerly accepted the offered drink, not even minding the way it was poured into a glass before being handed to him. He sat at the dining table and watched as Buck heated up the pozole, moving around the kitchen with all the awkwardness he would expect from someone who wasn’t there often.

It made Maddie’s lack of it all the more apparent. Especially when there was knocking at the door and she, once again, answered it. This time she paid the delivery guy and brought back bags of food. She started pawing through them, looking increasingly agitated.

“Damn it, they didn’t put it in here again,” she complained, only to light up when Chimney placed a small bowl in front of her with a,

“Ta-da.”

“You made me hot mustard,” Maddie gushed.

“I just can’t anymore, with the sad face every time they forget it, so…”

Daniel shared a look with his brother that neither of the other two noticed. Said brother just shrugged at him, apparently not as concerned about what they were witnessing as Daniel was. Weirded out, obviously, but not concerned.

“Why so much food?” Buck asked them.

“It’s Buffriday,” Maddie answered, like it explained everything.

“Buffet Fridays,” Chimney expanded as the two of them carried the food, along with plates and the silverware he’d been wiping down, over to the dining table. “It’s the one day we indulge in your sister’s complete inability to choose what she’s in the mood for.”

“Yeah, so we order everything.”

“And because of your sister’s hatred for leftovers, I don’t have to buy groceries. It’s a win-win situation.”

Daniel glanced between them, threw back what was left in his glass, and went to get a refill. He paused beside Buck, who was checking that the pozole was heated enough.

“What the hell is this?” he hissed at him.

“They were like this at the bar,” Buck whispered back. “At least I’m not suffering alone this time.”

“Why do you both have such terrible taste in men?” Why couldn’t either of them be interested in someone like Austin? Daniel wouldn’t have to worry so much then.

Buck rolled his eyes and carried the pozole over to the dining table to be set down with the rest of the food. Chimney immediately spooned some out into a bowl, which he then handed off to Maddie. Daniel bit his tongue as he retook his seat and plated his own food.

“I still can’t believe you can cook,” Chimney said. “I mean, Hen told me, but…”

“I know, right?” Maddie said with a laugh. “But Dan, you should’ve come out with us. It was fun, you missed us singing karaoke.”

“Did I miss it, or did I dodge it?” Daniel asked. “I told you, I was busy.”

“Busy with what?” his sister asked, and then lit up. “Oh, are you dating someone!?”

He rolled his eyes at the question.

“Danny hasn’t dated anyone since that lawyer last year,” Buck oh-so-helpfully told them. “What was her name?”

“Attica. We’re still friends.” For a definition of “friends.” She’d occasionally call him up because she wanted someone to listen to her complain and there wasn’t anyone else available. Mostly, though, it was just sex.

“Ah,” Chimney said with a nod. “So you’re less of a manwhore than our Buckaroo.”

Daniel froze with his glass halfway to his mouth. He set it down and turned in his seat and glared. Chimney’s eyes went wide.

“Not- Not that there’s anything wrong with that,” he backtracked, nervously chuckling as he did so.

“We went to do a song together and came back to find him gone,” Maddie told Daniel. “He showed up twenty minutes later, claiming he was in the bathroom.”

“I was,” Buck said with a shrug. Daniel rolled his eyes. “There was a line.”

“Uh-huh, sure.” Their sister scoffed.

“What about you two, huh?” Buck gestured between Maddie and Chimney with his fork. “When did you two start dating?”

Maddie and Chimney looked at each other and laughed, and then the denials started coming.

“That’s crazy we’re just…”

“Just friends who hang out.”

“Yeah, just hanging out.”

“Because I answer the door at all my friends’ places,” Daniel quipped. He did at Austin’s, but that was a totally different animal.

“You don’t have any friends,” Maddie said with a scowl. “Shut up.”

“Wow, rude.”

“No, no.” Buck shook his head. “You guys sing karaoke, you sing love songs, together. You do Buffridays-”

“Often enough that he knew you’d be upset about the hot mustard,” Daniel broke in to add. Buck pointed at him excitedly.

“He made you hot mustard,” he said. “Come on, you’re a couple.”

Maddie grabbed for her wine while Chimney took a bite of egg roll, neither of them looking at anyone else in the room. Nor did either of them argue the point.

The awkward silence lingered for the rest of the meal, until Daniel and Buck both decided it was time to get out of there. Maddie immediately latched onto the idea and left as well, which was good. Because Daniel wanted to have words with his sister.

He caught her by the wrist as they left the building, letting Buck go on ahead to the car. She looked up, confused.

“What?”

“What?” he repeated, voice low so it didn’t carry to their brother. “What are you doing, Maddie?”

“You’re the one who stopped me.”

“We weren’t wrong up there, about the two of you.” Daniel let go of her wrist. Maddie huffed and looked away from him. “You literally just ran away from your asshole, abusive husband. And you can lie to Buck about how long it was happening, but I know it was a lot longer than that.”

“What’s your point, Dan?” she snapped.

“My point, Mads, is that it’s too fucking soon to be doing whatever the hell you’re doing,” he said. “Just the other month, we had to beg and convince you to stay here instead of running off again. And now you’re jumping headfirst into a relationship with the first guy who smiles at you?”

“We told you, we’re just friends,” Maddie said, scowling. “Stay out of it.”

“The last time I listened to you and stayed out of-”

The sound of a car horn interrupted him. He twisted around to glare at Buck, who was half-hanging out of the car window as he gestured at them impatiently. Maddie, unfortunately, took her chance to flee from the conversation.

Daniel huffed in irritation and went to drive both himself and Buck home.

Sometimes, the calls they responded to were tragedies with their endings already written.

The shift after the Buffriday at Chimney’s, the 118 was sent to an accident involving an old man pinned to a gate by his car. His husband was the one who found him, the one who called it in, the one who told dispatch that the victim wasn’t breathing. There wasn’t much of anything that they could do when they got there.

It looked as if they were getting ready for a trip, with bikes on the back of the car. There were photos spread about the pavement, too, knocked out of an album that had hit the ground. While the others - Bobby and Eddie and Chimney - got the deceased victim free, Buck was tasked with keeping the husband company, the pair of them sitting on the back of the ambulance.

“When we got married, we thought, what the hell? We have so little life left, we might as well live,” the husband, Thomas, told him as they watched the others work. “That was Mitchell, always daring the clock. And me, I always followed along. All those foolish things we did, we only ever wanted to go together. That’s love.”

“I’m sorry, I really am,” Buck said, and knew his words didn’t mean shit. “I’m kinda jealous, I- I thought I’d found something like that, once.”

“You thought?”

“Yeah, uh.” He glanced at Eddie, helping pull the white sheet over Mitchell. “Back in high school. We were just kids, and uh… And he was closeted, and terrified over anyone knowing.”

“What happened?” Thomas asked, voice soft and painfully kind.

“He married a girl,” Buck said, forcing a laugh. “I actually wasn’t all that surprised, looking back on it. I think I was kinda expecting it.”

“Well, let this old queer tell you something.” Thomas laid a wrinkled hand on his knee and squeezed. “You don’t find something like that, you make it. Step by step, memory by memory. You’ll make your own, too.”

He smiled at him, though it dropped with a sigh when he looked over at the sheet-covered shape of Mitchell.

“Son, you mind if I have just a few moments alone with him?”

“Of course, here.”

Buck helped him up and guided him to his husband with a hand hovering over his back, and then went to go pick up the photographs scattered everywhere. There was one, a polaroid of the pair of them looking not much older than Eddie and Buck had, before their lives split apart. He was stuck staring at it when he heard a soft, muffled thump.

Thomas was bent double, with his head pressed to Mitchell’s chest. Buck thought the man was just in mourning, but when he went over to check on him, he found him still. Deathly still.

“Eddie!” he heard himself shout, even as he rolled Thomas so he was flat on his back. “Eddie! Cap!”

The other two hurried over, Eddie dropping beside him with his medical bag while Bobby straightened Thomas’ legs out.

“I don’t know what happened,” Buck frantically explained to them, pressing his palms together over Thomas’ chest. “He was talking, he was responsive, and then…”

“Alright, start compressions,” Bobby said, though Buck was already in motion before the words left the captain’s mouth.

Buck only paused when Eddie put the respirator bag to Thomas, and then went right back to work. He kept doing it, quietly begging Thomas to stay with them, until Bobby placed gentle hands over his own.

“Buck,” the captain said. “He’s gone, kid.”

He sat back with a sigh and his eyes caught on the sight of Thomas’ hand still gripping Mitchell’s. Mitchell dared the clock, Thomas followed along. They went together.

Buck was silent and shaken for the rest of the shift, enough so that the others noticed and sent him worried looks. It wasn’t the shock and horror of Devon, but Thomas simply keeling over out of heartbreak was not something Buck could simply pack away and move on from. It did not help that he was still so very jealous of the two old men, for the life they lived that was so very close to the one he himself used to fantasize of with Eddie.
