What they used to whisper about in the safety of their darkened rooms, back when hopes and dreams had yet to be dashed upon the rocks.

And speaking of Eddie, the other man was continuing his habit of lingering in the locker room at the end of their shift to ambush him. He tried to, anyway. Buck noticed what he was doing, this time, and was quick to hurry out, but Eddie followed and caught up to him in the parking lot.

“What do you want, Eddie?” he demanded without turning around. At least until the other man failed to answer and curiosity got the better of him.

Eddie was standing there, hand outstretched and eyes squeezed shut.

“Eds?”

“Come back to mine,” Eddie blurted out. Buck let out a sigh.

“Eddie, come on…”

“Chris still wants to show off his room to you,” he said. Buck shook his head. “Fine, then… Then I’ll go home, pick up Chris, and go to yours and Danny’s.”

“That’s not- Eddie, come on,” Buck said again. “What are you doing? What are we doing?”

“I told you, I missed you,” Eddie said. Begged, almost, for him to listen. “You were my best friend. You’re still my best friend. And… And I missed you, and I’m worried about you, and I don’t feel right going home and not making sure you’re okay.”

Buck closed his eyes and tried to find the resilience, the reserve, to say no. To do the smart thing, to shoot Eddie down and go home. But it simply wasn’t there.

(Danny was going to be so very disappointed in him.)

“Fine,” he breathed out.

“Thank you,” Eddie said. “I’ll get pizza on the way. The, uh. The old usual?”

“Yeah, sure.”

He watched Eddie practically run to his truck, before turning back around and continuing on to his jeep. The entire drive he felt a mixture of dread and excitement. It only got worse as he got into the apartment, as he threw himself down on the couch beside Danny and silently stole the remote from him. They sat there together, in companionable silence, until Buck’s phone chimed with a text from Eddie, saying he was almost there.

“Um,” he said. His brother looked over and frowned.

“Um?”

“I, uh. I think I did something stupid.”

“Must be a day ending in Y,” Danny muttered. Buck glared at him for it. “How stupid?”

“Very stupid. I- I, uh… Eddie’s coming over.”

“What.” The word was icy as it left Danny’s mouth.

“He’s bringing Chris. And pizza.”

“What.”

“He’s almost here.” Buck focused on the remote in his hands, fidgeting with it, instead of looking at his brother. He could hear the disapproval just fine, he didn’t need to see it.

“Buck,” Danny said. It came out like a sigh. “What are you doing?”

“I don’t know,” he quietly admitted. “I missed him.”

An arm wrapped around his shoulders and pulled him in, until his head was on Danny’s shoulder.

“You’re going to get hurt.”

“I know.” And Buck did know that. It was inevitable. There was no way through this where he didn’t end up hurt. But then, he did have a history of doing stupid things for precisely that result. “I’m not going to leave again. I know you don’t believe me, but I won’t.”

“I’ll behave for the kid,” Danny promised. “But I’m not going to be nice to Eddie.”

It was more than Buck expected, honestly.

“He’s the coolest kid, I’m telling you,” he said, getting up when there was a series of tiny knocks at the door.

“Pretty sure I met the coolest kid years ago,” Danny said. “But sure, I can pretend.”

Buck shot him a grin, before throwing open the door.

Chapter 18

Then

Evan always loved Christmas, growing up. The lights, the snow, the presents. He loved the way that it was the only time of year that they ever acted like a normal family. They’d even sit in the living room in front of the TV, together, and watch whatever Christmas movie was playing. It was one of the few times, outside of when he got hurt, that the way Mom acted towards him was like all the other mothers he saw. She would bake cookies and let Maddie and Evan both help, actually laughing at the messes Evan made in his excitement.

The rest of the year, he had to beg and bleed for any scrap of attention and care from her, but on Christmas it was always the best gift he was given.

When Maddie moved to Boston with Doug, Evan’s love for the holiday season took a few hits, but it stayed strong. Mom baked cookies and she let Evan help, though her tolerance for his messes had waned considerably by that point. Danny and Evan decorated the tree together, and they all sat and watched Charlie Brown and the old claymation movies.

This was going to be their first Christmas in El Paso, their first Christmas with just the two of them, and Evan wasn’t sure how it was going to go. The first few months after they moved had been… Not all that bad, but different. Sure, at the beginning they ate a lot of take-out and microwaved meals, and maybe they didn’t get a couch until September, but it wasn’t bad.  

But Evan wasn’t sure what they were going to do for Christmas this year. It wasn’t like they had the things to bake cookies, or even someone for Evan to bake them with. Danny probably would, if he asked, but he’d never been good at it. And they hadn’t brought any decorations with them, as far as he was aware. Not that they had a house to decorate.

Their first Christmas in El Paso was going to be different, it was going to be just him and Danny, and Evan… Evan was trying to not be a downer about it. Because he loved Christmas, and he loved his brother, and the entire rest of the year was so much better than it had been in Hershey. 

There was thumping in the hall outside their door. There were a lot of odd noises in this building, most of them coming from the odd people that lived in it. Evan learned to not pay them any mind, usually, except it was right outside their door. And Danny was supposed to be coming home soon.

He opened the door, poked his head out, and spotted his brother immediately. He also spotted the live tree, still wrapped in twine, leaning against the wall.

“Hey, squirt,” Danny said in greeting, before knocking on Alma’s door.

Evan liked Tia Alma. That was Spanish for “aunt,” and he’d never met any aunts or uncles before. She wasn’t much older than Danny, but she acted like how he thought an aunt would act. She brought them food, sometimes, and had started teaching Evan how to cook. He’d babysit for her after school, when she wasn’t home from work yet, and she’d pay him for it.

When she opened the door, and was greeted by Danny shoving the tree at her, she looked very confused.

“Dan,” she said. “What is that?”

“It’s a tree,” he said, and then to Evan, “Ours is still downstairs.”

“Yours is downstairs?” Alma looked at the tree, then at Danny. Her face contorted into a scowl. “Daniel Buckley, chico loco. What did you do?”

“I bought you a tree, duh. Ev, grab that tree stand?”

The tree stand, a green, plastic thing, was right by the door. Evan hadn’t noticed it at first, distracted as he was by the tree. He picked it up and Danny pretty much pushed his way into Alma’s apartment. Her twin daughters, Delia and Cela, were sitting on the couch and staring at them with wide eyes and open mouths.

“You can’t- You shouldn’t have-” Alma was saying, shaking her head in dismay. “Daniel.”

“Where do you want it?” Danny asked, ignoring her protests. “By the window? In the corner?”

Alma glared at him for a solid moment, muttering something in Spanish under her breath as she did. She was teaching Evan, but he was still learning and so he didn’t really know what she said. He recognized the cuss words, though. Those were the first ones she taught him.

“By the window,” she spat, throwing up her hands.

Danny smiled brightly at her, and Evan hurried to place the stand where she said. He helped keep it steady as his brother shoved the end of the tree into place and then tightened the screws to keep it from falling over. Then, he stood back and watched as Danny took out his pocket knife and snipped the twine, letting the branches spread out.

“There,” Danny said, putting the knife away. “Okay, I gotta bring up ours and then everything else.”

“Dan…” Alma shook her head again. “Delia, Celia, can you get out the box with the decorations, please?”

The little girls nodded and jumped to their feet, racing for one of the closets. At the door to the hall, Alma stopped Danny from leaving right away. They talked a bit, too quiet for Evan to hear, before his older brother shrugged and left.

It took several trips for Danny to bring everything up. Besides a tree and a stand for it to go in, he’d also bought decorations. Several decorations. Bags and bags of them. Lights, ornaments, garland. Even a box of candy canes. They spread them out on the floor of their apartment around the tree, once it was in place.

There was also a shoe box. Danny brought it out of his room and sat it down with everything else. More decorations were inside, handmade and clumsily crafted. Most of them were made with popsicle sticks, a tree painted green and a snowflake painted white. Each of them had Evan’s name written on the back, along with a year.

“I think I kinda went a bit overboard,” Danny said, a bit sheepishly. He held up a box of lights. “Tree then windows, then… Wherever else we can stick ‘em?”

Evan tackled his brother in a hug, and then started tearing open packaging with glee.

Now

Eddie was no stranger to making poor life decisions. The opposite, actually, because it seemed like he was making them constantly. So many of them involved Shannon, the most recent being the decision to have sex with her. Again.

He collapsed onto the bed, careful to make sure he didn’t crush her. The pair of them were sweaty and panting from exertion. Shannon hadn’t even managed to get fully undressed, still wearing her bra and panties.

“We suck at this,” she said once she caught her breath.

“Really? ‘Cause I feel pretty good about how that went down.”

“You know what I mean.” She laughed and rolled so she could put her chin on his chest. “We say we’re gonna talk and work things out, but we just keep ending up like this.”

“It’s a different way of working things out.”

It wasn’t like Eddie meant for it to keep happening. Each time, he really did intend for them to talk. Just talk. But each time, without fail, they ended in bed together. Usually in bed together, occasionally in a backseat.

The problem was that talking with Shannon always turned into arguing with Shannon. Every time, without fail. It happened back in El Paso, before she left, and it continued to happen in Los Angeles.

Sex was easier. The only shouting and yelling that happened during sex was the good kind. The fun kind. Neither of them walked away frustrated or angry, no feelings were hurt, no doors were slammed on the way out.

Alright, maybe Eddie was doing it on purpose. Just a little.

“You’re getting the tree today?” Shannon asked.

“Yup,” he said. “The annual tradition of convincing him that, no, a sixteen-foot tree does not fit in the house.”

“I caved one year,” she said. He laughed. “That last Christmas, before you got back. He hadn’t been feeling well, and we missed you. When he looked up at that giant tree, I couldn’t say no.”

“Where’d you put it?”

“The backyard.” Ah, of course. “But we didn’t have enough ornaments, so we just loaded it up with lights and ribbon. And I got the sleeping bags out, and we laid under it, just staring up at the branches for hours.”

“He must have loved that.”

“He did.”

A glance at the alarm clock told Eddie that they were running out of time to enjoy the afterglow. Pushing himself up, and dislodging Shannon in the process, he rolled out of bed. His pants were on the floor and he tugged them on, ignoring the way she watched him with interest.

“You know, he still won’t tell me what he wants for Christmas,” he said. “I’ve tried everything. Evan couldn’t get him to open up.”

Shannon rolled her eyes at the name and sat up..

“Well if Evan couldn’t,” she grumbled as she reached down and fished for her own pants. “Have you tried Santa?”

“The creepy guy at the mall with the fake beard?” Eddie gestured at his own face for emphasis. He grabbed his shirt from where it was hanging off a lamp and tugged it over his head. “No.”

Shannon, in the middle of doing up the buttons of her jeans, turned to laugh at him.

“Oh, my God. You still haven’t let that go? Look, just because you got scared and threw up all over Santa, that doesn’t mean-”

“First off,” he said, cutting her off. “That was very wrong of mi abuela to tell you that story-”

“Actually, it was Evan who told me.”

“Then it was wrong of her to tell him, and fuck him for telling you,” Eddie said. “And second, no, I wasn’t scared. Okay? The old guy smelled bad, and I had a stomach flu.”

“Two years in a row?” Shannon held up two of her fingers for emphasis.

“Yes. It was very-”

They fell silent and still as they both heard the front door open, followed by Christopher and Abuela shouting for him. Eddie vaulted over the bed, nearly crashing into Shannon, in his hurry to get to the bedroom door.

“Why is he here?” Shannon asked in a whisper. “I thought you were picking him up.”

“I dunno,” he admitted. That had been the plan, and he was pretty sure he hadn’t heard his phone go off with anything alerting him to a change. “Look, I’ll distract them, you head out the back.”

“Whoa, whoa, wait.”

“What?” he hissed impatiently, hand on the knob.

“You want me to sneak out?” she asked. Eddie nodded, gestured at the window as there was a tiny knock at the door. Shannon stared at him. “That is our son out there. We’ve been trying to figure out how to tell him, maybe this is the moment.”

“This is not the moment,” Eddie told her. He wasn’t certain there ever would be a good moment. “You have to go, okay? You have to go.”

He opened the door, just wide enough to poke his head out. Chris was in the hall, and he smiled brightly at the sight of him.

“Hey!” Eddie slipped out the rest of the way, thankful his son was too trusting to be suspicious, and herded him down the hall. “I thought I was supposed to pick you up at Abuela’s.”

“Oh, he was so excited about decorating, he couldn’t wait to get home,” Abuela told him. She tapped him on the arm and he obediently bent down and kissed her on the cheek. “Did you have company?”

“What?” Eddie asked. She sent a pointed glance at the pair of glasses sitting out on the coffee table, left behind by him and Shannon in their frenzied rush for the bedroom. Fuck. “Oh! Oh, uh… You know I’m not the best housekeeper. I, uh. I had Buck - Evan - over last night.”

An odd expression crossed her face at that, but he didn’t have time to decipher it.

“Everyone else has gotten to see him again,” she tutted. “Tell him to stop hiding from me.”

“I will be sure to pass that message along.” He scooted the glasses back, to make room for the pair of shopping bags that they’d brought in with them. “Show me what you guys got.”

While Chris and Abuela pulled things out, he spotted Shannon lingering in the other room as she watched them. He gave her a look that he hoped conveyed the instruction to get out, and got a scowl in return.

Eddie didn’t relax until he heard the soft click of the back door, a sound that thankfully went unnoticed by both Abuela and Chris.

The Los Angeles Fire Department did an annual toy drive every holiday season. They rotated which station hosted it, and this year was the 118’s turn. That meant that the station was decorated from ceiling to floor, everyone was wearing Santa hats, and they were offline while they entertained the crowd of people coming in and out with donations.

Buck did not mind this one bit. Whatever the opposite of a Scrooge or Grinch was, it was him. He honestly couldn’t understand how someone didn’t bounce around throughout the holiday, because just the sight of anything Christmas made him light up inside. He and Danny broke out the decorations at 12:01, December 1st, and Buck had been blasting Christmas music ever since.

For the toy drive, while some of them were tasked with giving tours, like Jones with the engine, Buck was working the refreshments table with Chimney. The other man kept giving him dirty looks, probably because Buck was continuously singing off-key to the music they were playing. But it was making all the kids laugh, so he’d get over it.

The singing came to an abrupt stop when he looked up from pouring a cup of punch and held it out to the next person in line. A person that happened to be Shannon, holding a toy fire truck. He yelped when he saw her, nearly spilling the punch.

“Hello, Evan,” she said, stepping to the side. “Don’t worry, I’m not here for you. Though, I can’t say I’m surprised to find that you’re both here.”

“Wasn’t my choice,” Buck said as he handed the punch to the person who’d been behind her. “Cap wouldn’t let me leave.”

“I’m sure you tried real hard.”

It was a different attitude from when she first showed up at the station, months ago. Then, Shannon had been here to see him, though Buck wasn’t really sure why. Maybe she had wanted to reconnect. Maybe she felt like they were kindred spirits, with the way they both fled Texas to get away from Eddie.

This time, Buck was getting the impression that she came to pick a fight.

“Shannon.” They both turned at Eddie’s voice, spotting the man frozen a foot away. He was staring at them in obvious terror, eyes bouncing between the two of them. “What are you doing here?”

“You won’t answer my texts,” she said, stepping forward and shoving the toy fire truck into Eddie’s chest. “Or return my calls.”

“This is not the place,” Eddie hissed, taking the toy and dropping it into the bin with all the other donations.

“Maybe it’s the perfect place,” she shot back. “We can actually have a conversation that doesn’t end up with us in bed.”

Shannon said it without once looking Buck’s way, but it felt like the words were a stab to the chest, regardless. Which was stupid, because Eddie was his ex, and her husband, for the past eight years. There was absolutely no reason for Buck to feel hurt about the revelation that they were apparently sleeping together.

There was absolutely no reason, either, for the almost guilt-stricken look that Eddie shot Buck’s way.

“Follow me.”

Eddie turned and marched off towards the locker room, Shannon following with an aggravated huff. Buck turned in place tracking their movement and watching through the glass walls as they started arguing. For once, he was actually thankful for the stupid design decision.

“Was that Eddie’s wife?” Fuck! He’d forgotten all about Chimney. The other man was staring at him, his curiosity plain to see on his face. “Did she call you Evan?”

“Um.” Buck did not want to have this conversation with Chimney. “Did she? I- I don’t… Uh. Hey, wh- when did you want to get Maddie that Christmas tree?”

Chimney did not look like he was going to take the bait, but they were thankfully interrupted by an adorable little girl waddling up to them with a toy dinosaur. Which meant Buck had an excuse to not only act like the overgrown child that he was, but to do it while ignoring all his problems. Sometimes, he thought there might actually be a loving entity watching over him from on high,

Eddie closed the glass door behind Shannon and purposefully did not look in Buck’s direction to see if he was watching them. He’d felt Buck’s eyes on him the entire way here, so he knew that he was. His heart nearly stopped at seeing him and Shannon together, and shame threatened to strangle him when Shannon blurted out about them sleeping together.

Eddie didn’t know why he felt shame. She was his wife, after all. But something about it felt far too close to the weeks following that awful prom night.

“I can’t do this,” he said, turning and heading to the other doors to close them, too. “Not now.”

“Then where and when?” Shannon demanded. “It’s been almost two months, and right now? Right now I feel further away from him and from you than I ever did when we weren’t speaking. Are you ever going to let me see Christopher?”

“Of course.” It tasted like a lie even as he said it. “Eventually.”

“Eventually?” Shannon echoed, watching as he paced the length of the room to the door they’d entered through. “Does he even know I’m here?”

Eddie sighed, his hand on the door handle. He so very much wanted to flee from this argument, but he knew she’d just follow him. And he wanted even less to have this argument where everyone could hear.

“I didn’t want to confuse him,” he told her. “Not until I was sure.”

“Of me? Because you seemed pretty sure when we started having sex again.”

“Is that how you see this?” Eddie demanded, walking back to her. “A business transaction? What, you sleep with me so I let you see Christopher?”

“The other choice was to do whatever the fuck you’re doing with Evan,” Shannon snapped. Eddie reeled. “No, I thought it was a- a reconciliation. Right up until you hid me from our son.”

“I didn’t know what else to do!” he nearly shouted, catching himself at the last moment. At a lower volume, he repeated, “I didn’t know what else to do. And I’m not keeping you from our son like some kind of punishment.”

“Whatever I need to do to prove myself and to fix things, name it-”

“Build a time machine.”

Shannon glared at him, lips pressed into a flat line.

“Doing whatever we’re doing and hiding it from him? That’s not fixing anything,” she said. “It’s just starting to make everything feel more broken.”

“You keep looking to me like I have the answers, and…” Eddie shook his head. “I don’t.”

“No, you just have the power,” she pointed out. “I’m the one who screwed up, the one who left. I know I don’t get to have a say. I know, okay? So I'm following your lead, just tell me where we’re going. When will you be able to forgive me?”

“I can forgive you,” he said, heading for the exit. “I’m just not sure I can trust you.”

“But you can trust Evan,” Shannon said, bringing him to a stop with his hand on the door handle.  “And he left long before I did.”

Eddie turned his head to glare at her.

“Evan left because I broke his heart,” he spat. “You left because you needed a break from your son. You’re not the same.”

He threw open the door and stomped out. Shannon did not follow.

Chapter 19

Chapter Notes

Content Warning: Doug and domestic violence in the flashback at the end.

Now

Buck was very much regretting having agreed to go Christmas tree shopping with Chimney. Oh, sure, at first he’d been very eager to go along with it. In fact, he’d been the one to bring it up as an idle thought during shift as he complained about how Maddie’s apartment was utterly devoid of seasonal cheer. She loved the holiday as much as he and Danny did, so it wasn’t right that there were no decorations. Everyone around him was at least a little familiar with his love for the festive season, especially Eddie, so most of the others simply laughed or ignored him.

Chimney had leaped upon the idea of surprising Maddie with a Christmas tree, when Buck joked about just buying her one. Buck had obviously been all for it and they worked out a time when they were both off at the same time she was, so she’d be home when they surprised her. They could decorate it together, just like when they were kids.

It’d be great.

But that had all been before Shannon showed up at the station again. Before she called him “Evan” in front of someone whose nosiness was only rivaled by Buck’s own. Before she made it perfectly clear to anyone paying attention, even with just a couple sentences, that she had more than just a passing familiarity with him. That he had more than a passing familiarity with her.  

The other man’s curiosity had been further inflamed by how Eddie avoided them both, after Shannon left. If there was anything that Buck’s coworkers lived for, it was learning all about other people’s drama to gossip about. And Chimney had accidentally been exposed to the potential for a lot of juicy drama to gossip about.

“She knew you,” Chimney said as Buck followed him down rows of trees. “She called you Evan.”

“I- I don’t… Hey, what about this tree?”

Chimney paused, looked at it, scrunched up his nose.

“Maybe if we were shopping for Charlie Brown,” he said, and then glanced at Buck. “Please tell me you know that movie.”

“Of course I know Charlie Brown,” Buck said with a scoff.

“With you Buckleys, I can never be sure,” the other man said as they resumed their search. “But no, she knew you. Eddie’s wife knew you and called you Evan. I haven’t even heard your brother or sister call you Evan. The only people who I’ve ever heard call you Evan was-”

Chimney paused again to turn at him.

“Was she one of the women that the catfisher was talking to?” he asked, and then answered himself before Buck could take advantage of that explanation. “No, she knew you too well for that.”

“We barely even talked,” Buck pointed out.

“Yeah, but she knew you,” Chimney said. “She said she wasn’t surprised to find you both working at the station. Implying that she didn’t know you and Eddie worked together before- Oh my God, did you sleep with Eddie’s wife!?”

“What!?” Buck sputtered. “No! What? Why would you- What?”

“Well it fits!”

“No it doesn’t. If anyone, it sounds like Eddie’s the one having an affair with her. Which is weird, right? She’s his wife. And ‘sides, if she was someone I hooked up with, she wouldn’t be calling me Evan.”

Chimney squinted at him, clearly having a raging debate in his head. Then he asked,

“I’m going to regret this, but what would she call you, then?”

“Firehose.”

“Gross,” the other man said, his face contorted in disgust.

“You asked!” Buck said. “C’mon, we’re here to pick out a tree for my sister, hurry up.”

“I am, I’m multitasking. I can do that,” Chimney told him. He had his phone out as he talked, tapping away.

“Are you…” Buck peered at the screen. “Are you seriously googling Christmas trees?”

“I want to buy Maddie a gift,” Chimney said. “And I want it to be perfect.”

“It’s a tree. It just has to be big and green, and able to fit through her door,” Buck pointed out. “I’m gonna go find someone to help us. You stop overthinking and just pick one you like.”

It didn’t take too long to find someone that worked at the tree farm they were at, who could cut down the tree that Chimney would hopefully have chosen by the time they got there. Buck was on the way back to him, the employee busy helping a happy little family of three, when he spotted someone amongst the trees that should not have been there. It was just out of the corner of his eye, so it wasn’t a good look at him, but it had been enough.

Buck froze in his tracks, turned, spotted the guy moving through one a row of evergreen trees down the way. A guy that looked so much like Doug from this angle, but it couldn’t be Doug. Doug was in Pennsylvania.

Wasn’t he?

He ran after him, this person who looked like Doug but couldn’t be, weaving around couples and children and trees. He ran after him because he needed to be sure, needed to reassure himself that it wasn’t Doug. But the guy took another turn amongst the trees and, by the time Buck got there, was gone.

It couldn’t have been Doug, Buck decided as he went back to Chimney. Maddie said that Doug had no clue where he and Buck were, what they did. It was just some random guy that looked like Doug from the side.

At least Chimney had finally chosen a tree while he was off chasing ghosts.

Daniel was just done tugging on his boots and about to grab his coat when someone knocked at his door. It was timid sounding, like whoever was on the other side wasn’t sure about what they were doing. He shrugged his coat on with a frown and went to answer it.

He immediately slammed the door shut.

“Jesus fucking…”

Taking a moment to reign in his temper, because he actually had somewhere to get to, Daniel opened the door again.

“I deserved that,” Eddie said.

“You deserve worse,” Daniel told him, shouldering his way past and locking the door behind him. “Buck’s not here.”

“I know,” the little shit said. “To both.”

“What do you want?”

“I need advice.”

Daniel stopped, turned, stared at him.

“You’re coming to me for advice?” he said, and laughed a little. It wasn’t a friendly laugh. It actually had Eddie flinching a little. “I’m not exactly your biggest fan, right now. I’m so far away from it that it’s not even funny.”

“I know,” Eddie said again. “But you’re the only one I could think of.”

“Seriously?”

“Danny, please.”

Daniel looked at him, actually looked at him. Eddie looked as desperate as he must’ve felt, to come to him for help. It was enough for Daniel to actually feel pity for the guy. Just a little.

“Fine,” he spat. “What is it?”

“Shannon-”

“Jesus Christ!” Daniel threw up his arms and started stomping for the elevator. Eddie hounded him.

“I reached out to her because I needed help getting Christopher into his new school,” he hurriedly explained. “And then she kissed me, and we’ve been having sex-”

“Any good will I felt towards you? Gone, Eddie. Gone. Why are you telling me this?”

“Because she wants to see him again,” Eddie said, slipping into the elevator with him when the doors opened. “Christopher, I mean. I’m sneaking around his back, keeping her from him. And she’s demanding to see him, and I have Carla telling me that he needs his mother, and I… I don’t know what to do.”

“Carla said that?” Daniel asked as he hit the button for the ground floor. “That he needs his mother?”

“She said it was what’s best for him, that a boy needs his mama.”

“So we know how little Buck told her about Mom.”

“That’s what I said.” Eddie chuckled a bit, and then sobered up. “I don’t know what to do, Danny. I don’t know what’s best for Chris.”

Oh holy shit, that’s what this was? He came to Daniel for parenting advice? It was a bit flattering, especially since the guy knew the reception he would receive. Daniel’s respect for Eddie rose a little from the rock bottom it sat at for the last eight years.

“Why did she leave?” Daniel asked. The elevator doors opened. He stepped out and headed for the parking lot, Eddie following behind him. “Shannon, I mean. Why did she leave?”

“I left first,” Eddie said. Daniel frowned at him over his shoulder. “The army. I enlisted right after we got married. And then I reenlisted when he got diagnosed. Without talking to Shannon first.”

“Kinda shitty,” Daniel said. “But I get it. Buck was a healthy teenager, and I still struggled. An infant with health issues, on top of everything else? Why wouldn’t you stick with it?”

“It paid for his surgeries.”

“Yeah, I’m not really sure I’d classify that as ‘leaving.’ God knows it’s not much different from what I did.” The last sentence was said under his breath, but he knew Eddie still heard, judging from the way he tilted his head in question. “You didn’t say why she left, though.”

“She needed a break-”

Daniel swung around.

“From her son!?”

“It was… Stressful. And her mom was sick again. She wanted us to go with her, but when I told her I needed more time, she just… Left anyway.”

“Why didn’t you go? Why didn’t you follow her?”

“I’d just gotten discharged.” Eddie reached up to rub at his left shoulder as he said it. He dropped his hand when he noticed Daniel looking. “Like I said, I needed time.”

“And now you’re both here, and she wants back in his life, and you’re not letting her because…?”

“I don’t trust her,” Eddie admitted. “I can forgive her, but… But I can’t trust that she won’t do it again.”

Daniel hated that he could sympathize. He did not want to be sympathizing with Eddie Diaz. When they got to his car, he didn’t get in right away. Instead, he leaned against it as he talked to Eddie.

“When I took Buck and ran,” he said, “it was because being in that house with our parents, with our mother, was killing him. I mean, you’ve met them. Even if it was just the once. Imagine fourteen years of that.”

“He’s mentioned climbing onto the roof and falling, before,” Eddie said. “He said sometimes it was an accident.”

“Yeah.” The word came out in a whisper while Daniel clenched his fists, tight enough that his fingernails dug into his palms. “Because Mom only really cared about her investment. And I knew leaving with him was the right thing to do even before we did it. I knew it the moment I talked to him about it, because of how the mere idea of leaving our parents behind made him so much happier.”

“Okay,” Eddie said. “So…?”

“So, talk to your fucking kid, Eddie,” Daniel said. “Ignore Carla’s bullshit, ignore what anyone else is saying - except me, you listen to me - and talk to your kid. Does he want Shannon back in his life?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then find out.” Daniel yanked the door open to his car and got in. He was about to close the door when a thought struck him and he was leaning halfway out again. “Oh! And stop having sex with Shannon.”

Eddie stared at him.

“What?”

“Stop having sex with Shannon,” Daniel repeated. “Whatever the fuck you two have got going on, it needs to stop. I mean, ignoring how much it’s gonna hurt Buck, if he finds out-” Eddie winced at that; Daniel narrowed his eyes at him. “Ignoring that, it’s not helping matters. It’s just gonna complicate everything, make it all worse. Why did you start having sex with her again, anyway?”

“It was easier,” was Eddie’s sheepish answer. “Easier than talking, or arguing.”

“So much easier, you’re asking for advice from someone who wants to take a baseball bat to your patellae.” Daniel rolled his eyes and pulled his door to.

Eddie was still standing there even as Daniel was pulling out of the parking lot.

Buck and Chimney beat him to Maddie’s. They were just getting the tree off the roof of Chimney’s car when he came to a stop beside them, and he oh-so-helpfully stood aside and jeered as he watched them struggle. Buck flipped him the bird.

Even without the cameras that Chimney installed at Maddie’s door, there was no way they would have been able to stealthily get a large fir inside while she was home. Especially since Buck insisted on shouting out “Ho ho ho!” as they did so.

“Oh, wow.” Maddie looked shell shocked as she came down the stairs. “A Christmas tree.”

“Yeah,” Chimney said as they set the tree down. It came with its own stand, a pair of boards crossed over that were attached to its stump, so there wasn’t a risk of it falling over. “Buck said you didn’t have one yet.”

Daniel stepped out of the way as Buck went to go get whatever decorations they’d bought. It was probably more than would fit on the tree, knowing his brother, but that was okay. They’d just spread it around the apartment.

Or maybe it wasn’t okay, because Maddie was not looking particularly happy about this surprise. Which was weird, because all three of them loved the holiday.

“Is this okay?” Chimney apparently took note of her apprehension, too. “Is this the wrong kind of surprise?”

“No, no,” she said. “It’s sweet. Really, I love that you guys did this.”

“You just didn’t want it. That’s okay, we shouldn’t have assumed.”

“I just really wasn’t going to do much Christmas this year-”

“What the fuck,” Daniel blurted out. “Why?”

“All- All my decorations and stuff are back in Pennsylvania,” Maddie said.

“And that is why we have bought everything you need,” Buck announced as he came in with shopping bags. There were only four of them, so either Chimney did the shopping or Buck somehow didn’t go overboard for once. “We have lights, we have ornaments, we even have…” He sat the bags down on a table and pulled out- “An angel for on top of the tree.”

“No,” Maddie snapped. She tore the tree topper out of Buck’s hands and threw it back into the bag. “Angels are creepy.”

“Since when?” Daniel demanded, trying to tamp down on the instinctive anger over Maddie’s actions. Buck’s confused and hurt expression was not helping. “You always loved the one we had growing up.”

“Since- Since now, okay?”

“You know what, I think we might have overstepped,” Chimney said. “Maddie should be able to pick out her own decorations. We’ll just… We’ll just bring this back to the store.”

“What are you talking about? You can’t return a tree,” Buck said. “Come on, you always loved Christmas, right? The three of us always decorated the tree together. You even made your own bows.”

“I’m not really feeling the holiday this year,” Maddie said. Behind her, Chimney was motioning for Buck to shut up, but he stopped when Daniel glared at him. “Okay?”

“No, it’s not okay,” Buck insisted. “What’s going on with you?”

“I don’t want to talk about it.”

“Too damn bad,” Daniel said, and got a scowl in response.

“Guys, it’s fine,” Chimney said. “I’m just gonna take away the evidence before this, uh. Turns into a crime scene? Okay?”

He lifted up the tree and awkwardly shuffled out the door with it, leaving the three siblings alone.

“So, you hate Christmas now?” Buck asked Maddie as he gathered the shopping bags back up. “Something else I can thank Doug for, huh?”

“No, I don’t hate Christmas,” Maddie snapped back. “But I’m starting not to like you very much.”

“Hey!” Daniel barked, too angry to feel much guilt for the way his sister flinched. He jerked his head at Buck, who stomped out of the apartment in a huff. “I don’t care how angry you are, you do not talk to him like that.”

“The two of you can’t come into my house and act this way.”

“It’s Christmas,” he told her. “The first Christmas with all three of us together. And Buck, our baby brother, all he wants to do is celebrate that with you. Because he loves this holiday, and he loves you, and he has missed celebrating it with the both of us.”

“Well I don’t want to celebrate Christmas with the two of you,” Maddie said.

“Fine. We’ll just celebrate it without you. Again,” Daniel said, heading for the door. “Just like we’ve had to every year since you started choosing Doug over us.”

She didn’t say anything as he slammed the door shut behind him.

Then

Maddie was in hell.

Doug hadn’t liked the way she’d decorated the Christmas tree. In particular, he’d said the angel tree topper was ugly and creepy. It was the same one they’d had for the past five years, when they bought it to replace the star he’d thrown at a wall. But this year, he’d decided he didn’t like it, nor had he liked any of the other ornaments, nor had he liked how she arranged those ornaments on the tree. Or maybe he’d just wanted an excuse to be angry so that he could take it out on her.

The tree was now on its side, where it was knocked down after Maddie stumbled into it when Doug backhanded her. There were pieces of glass all over the floor that she needed to pick up, but the broom and dustpan had conveniently gone missing. That was Doug, too.

Her hands were bleeding from a dozen shallow cuts, with slivers of glass stuck in some places. There was blood running down one of her cheeks, too. Maybe from one of the branches or maybe from one of the pointier ornaments, she wasn’t sure.

She was just gathering up some of the bigger, intact ornaments when she spotted it. Laying on the floor with the rest of the wreckage was this year’s Christmas card from her brothers. It had come in just that day, she hadn’t the chance to put it safely away with all the others before Doug threw his fit. When she picked it up, she did it with care: her hands were bleeding, and she did not want to dirty it.

It was a picture of Dan and Evan- No, of Dan and Buck. He was going by Buck now, according to Dan in thir last conversation over the phone, as shallow as all the others they’d had since she’d last seen him. It was Dan and Buck posing in front of their Christmas tree, grinning cheesily for the camera. Right above Dan’s shoulder she could see one of the ornaments that Buck brought home from school, Rudolph’s head made out of popsicles sticks and a red pom pom and a pair of googly eyes.

Below the picture, in Dan’s deliberate handwriting, was a wish for a merry Christmas and the hope that she’d visit them soon.

Heavy, foreboding footsteps had Maddie hurriedly tucking the card into the pocket of her sweater. She cringed as Doug came into view, smiling so very sweetly. He crouched down in front of her and she flinched when he reached for her. The touch didn’t bring pain this time, except for the deliberate way he pressed down on her cut cheek as he stroked it.

“Doug,” she started to say, but he hushed her.

“I know you tried so hard,” he said. “It just wasn’t right, it wasn’t perfect.”

Doug gripped her face, pulled her in for a hard kiss to her forehead.

“You’ll get it right,” he lied. “Someday you’ll get it right.”

And it was a lie, Maddie knew that far too well by now. Nothing would be right in Doug’s eyes, nothing would be perfect enough for him. He would always find something, some infinitesimally small detail, that he decided wasn’t good enough. And then he’d take it out on her.

It was all an excuse to take it out on her.

Once, years ago, Dan had seen what was happening and he had tried to help. And Maddie, in response, had shoved him out of her life. There had been reasons why, but they did not matter. All that mattered is that she should’ve taken the hand he offered, should’ve gone to Texas with him and left this hell behind.

Maybe it was too late for her to get out, now. Or maybe it wasn’t. The Christmas card in her pocket, like all the others before it, came with the offer for her to visit.

Chapter 20

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Now

When he was given the invitation to visit Santa at the Grove with Eddie and Chris, Buck almost refused. He should have refused. Whatever the hell he and Eddie were currently doing, Buck had no business being a part of something so family-oriented. He could easily imagine the way Danny would roll his eyes when he heard, and he knew that going would just be a complicated form of self-harm.

So of course Buck ended up sitting on the edge of the dancing fountains, just him and Eddie, close enough for their arms and shoulders to brush every time they moved, while Chris stood in line to tell the fat man in red what he wanted for Christmas.

“I offered to wait in line with him.” Eddie had his phone out and was taking pictures. “But he said this is, uh. Private.”

He shouted Chris’ name to get the kid to look their way. They both waved to him, making the kid throw back his head and laugh.

“I admire him,” Buck said. “I love the way he always wants to do everything on his own.”

“Yeah.” Eddie tucked his phone away, shoulders suddenly going stiff. “So, uh. Not gonna say anything?”

“No,” he immediately said. And then, in an attempt to feign ignorance, asked, “About what?”

“You know what about.”

“No,” he said again, quieter this time. “She’s your wife, Eddie.”

“Yeah,” Eddie said. “Christopher doesn’t know. Or, I don’t think he knows.”

“Wow.” Buck shook his head.

“Yeah,” the other man agreed. “She wants back in his life and I don’t know if I should let her or not. Danny told me I needed to talk to Chris, find out what he wanted.”

“That’s…” Buck blinked, turned to look at him. “You went to Danny for advice? And he didn’t kill you? Wait, when did you go to him for advice?”

“The other day, he was heading out the door,” Eddie answered. “I think… I think I’ll talk to him after this. Bring it up, try to feel him out. I don’t even know how I’m gonna bring it up, though.”

“You could always just say, ‘hey, buddy, d’ya want to see your mom again?’” Buck offered. “If he starts crying, the answer’s probably no.”

They both burst out laughing at that, though it didn’t last long. Eddie sobered up first, and the serious expression that fell over his face quickly had Buck following suit.

“What?” he asked.

“I’m sorry,” the other man said.

“Sorry about what?”

“I slept with her.”

“Yeah, Eds,” Buck said, looking away. “I know.”

“No, Buck. I slept with her,” Eddie said. “Prom night. I slept with her on prom night.”

Buck closed his eyes and took a breath.

He let it out.

He opened his eyes again and looked at Eddie.

“Why?”

“Does the why matter?”

“Yes!” Buck snapped.

“I was drunk,” Eddie said with a shrug. “We were all drunk. We were at a party at someone’s house. Everyone around us was talking about what happened to Timothy, Shannon said she wanted to go upstairs, and I…”

“You panicked.”

Eddie opened his mouth, prepared to argue like he always did. But this time, he didn’t. He simply closed his mouth and nodded, not looking at Buck.

“Yeah,” he admitted after a moment. “I didn’t want them to know. About me, about you. And I was… Afraid. I was afraid that if I didn’t go upstairs with her, didn’t have sex with her, that they’d somehow figure it out. Which, it’s ironic really.”

“What do you mean?”

“I mean that having sex with her is how she found out.”

“What, were you that bad at it?” It was a lame attempt at a joke, but it was the best Buck had at the moment. Eddie threw him a mock glare.

“I said the wrong name.”

“Oh my God.” Buck covered his mouth, but it didn’t do much of anything to stifle the giggling. “I’m- I’m sorry, I- You said my name while having sex with her. Oh my God.”

“You don’t know that, maybe I said Mr. Henderson’s.”

Buck scrunched up his face. Mr Henderson, their 12th grade English teacher, had been ancient. The kind of ancient that had them all taking bets on which class period he’d keel over during. Even one of the assistant principals took part.

“Don’t be gross,” he said. “How, uh. How did she…?”

“As upset as anyone else would be, I figure,” Eddie said. “But she, uh. She didn’t… I mean, I was terrified she’d run downstairs and tell everyone. But she didn’t. We just avoided each other afterwards.”

“Yeah. Up until you proposed to her.”

It came out more bitter than Buck intended it to. Next to him, Eddie winced and ducked his head.

“Why now?” Buck demanded. “Why did you tell me about this now?”

“I tried telling you before,” Eddie said. “I told you I wanted to explain, you said you didn’t want to hear it. Danny slammed the door in my face.”

“Explain?” Buck shook his head. “That’s what- What, so, the two of you had sex on prom night, so you just had to marry her?”

Eddie shook his head. He was smiling, but it was small and sad looking.

“You are so bad at math,” he said. “Buck, when’s Christopher’s birthday?”

“It’s in February,” Buck immediately answered. “Why-?”

His mind whirled. He held up his hands and started counting on his fingers as Eddie watched, very clearly amused.

“She told you she was pregnant,” Buck whispered. “She told you and you panicked. Again.”

“I had to step up and be a man,” Eddie said with a shrug. Buck rolled his eyes, because those were very much Ramon’s words coming out of his ex’s mouth, and then he punched Eddie in the shoulder. Hard. “Hey!”

“Did you not listen to Danny!? How could you forget his safe sex talk? It was humiliating!”

“I was drunk!”

Buck shook his head.

“You should have talked to me.”

“I tried-” Eddie said, but Buck cut him off.

“No! Before you decided what was best for everyone. You should have talked to me, your boyfriend, before getting engaged to someone else.”

“Would it have made a difference?” Eddie asked.

“Maybe! But we’ll never know now, will we? Because you…” Buck trailed off as he spotted Christopher being escorted to them by a lady in an elf outfit. He shoved at Eddie to get his attention, the pair of them getting to their feet.

“How’d it go, pal?” Eddie asked his kid.

“It went great,” Chris answered with a smile.

“So, what’d you ask for?”

“Can’t tell,” the kid said. “Santa said he’d work on it.”

“Oh, man,” Eddie groaned. Buck knew he’d been hoping that Chris would tell him, so that he could make it happen. “Okay, let’s go.”

He scooped the kid up in his arms and headed for where they parked. Buck lingered behind a bit, watching them. His mind was still stuck on everything he’d learned, on the fact that Christopher was the reason why. Or, not the reason, but at least the impetus. 

“You two have an adorable son.”

Buck blinked and turned around. It was the lady dressed as an elf, her name tag proclaiming her to be Blair.

“Um.” What did he say? Christopher wasn’t his. Eddie wasn’t his, not anymore. But also… “Thank you.”

And then he hurried off after the two of them, wondering what Danny would say if he came home with enough eggnog to get Santa and all his reindeer wasted

Their bedtime routine was, well, routine. Eddie got Chris changed into his pajamas, made sure he brushed his teeth, tucked him into bed and read him a story. The entire time, he wondered how to bring up Shannon.

Did he go with Buck’s joke of a suggestion? Just simply ask if Chris wanted to see his mom again? No, easing into would be better. Right? Less of a chance to upset him that way. Right?

“Dad.” Chris’ voice yanked him out of his thoughts. The blanket was pulled up to his tiny little chin, but he was still wearing his glasses. “Is Santa real?”

“Why? Did someone say he wasn’t?” If someone told his baby boy that Santa wasn’t real, Eddie had no problem asking to borrow Danny’s bat. “What do you think? Do you think he’s real?”

“When you were gone,” Chris said, “I asked Santa for you to come back. And you did.”

“When I was in Afghanistan?” he asked. Chris nodded. Well, Santa could’ve been a tad bit gentler in his methods. Oh, wait. This was the perfect opportunity to find out what Chris wanted from Santa, wasn’t it? “You know, buddy, Christmas wishes aren’t like birthday wishes. You can say them out loud and they’ll still come true.”

“I asked him to find Mom,” Chris said. “Will he?”

Oh.

“You know what?” Eddie reached up, ran his fingers through Chris’ hair. “I’m sure he will.”

He leaned forward and pressed a kiss against his son’s forehead, before slipping the glasses off him and putting them safely on the table by the bed. Then he wished him a good night and slipped out of the room, turning off the light. In the hall, he leaned against the wall and prepared himself for the conversations he needed to face in the very near future.

Eddie took out his phone and stepped into his own bedroom, pushing the door closed behind him. It rang in his ear for a bit, long enough to wonder if he was going to be rightfully ignored, before Buck finally picked up.

“Yeah?”

Eddie blinked at the sound of his voice.

“Are you drunk?”

"A little,” Buck admitted. “We have eggnog. What is it? What’s up?”

“Uh, well.” Eddie rubbed at his face. “You can tell Danny that he was right.”

“Right about…? Oh. Oh! You asked him about Shannon?”

“I didn’t have to. She’s what he asked for from Santa.”

“Oh. Shit, man.”

“Yeah, uh. Would you…” It was on the tip of his tongue to ask if Buck wanted to be there on Christmas morning, an irrational and stupid urge. Why would Buck want to celebrate with him and Chris and Shannon, when he could spend it with his family, instead? “Would you mind helping me do last minute tree shopping?”

“You don’t have a tree yet!? Eds, it’s almost Christmas.”

“Yeah, no, I- I do. But I need to get one to put in the yard now. Surprise him on Christmas morning.”

“Okay, sure. Uh. We have that half-shift on Christmas Eve,” Buck said. “We can hunt one down after?”

“That sounds good.”

They talked a bit more, before saying their goodbyes and hanging up. Then, he pulled up another contact. She answered quickly.

“Hey, Shannon…”

Eddie felt a bit ridiculous, hiding in his own house from his own son. But it was worth it for the sight he saw when he stepped out to join him. Chris was smiling so very brightly at him over Shannon’s shoulder.

The night before, with Buck’s help, they got the tallest tree they could find on such short notice and set it up in the front yard. Buck had even stayed to help decorate it, after Chris went to bed, before going home to his and Danny's apartment. The morning of, Eddie got up even earlier than he usually did, so he could move all the presents from their hiding places to under the tree.

There was a bit of awkwardness when Shannon arrived, shortly before Chris would be waking up. Because the first thing she did after they hugged in greeting was move to kiss him. He almost let it happen, before jerking back and stepping away from her.

“We shouldn’t,” he told her. “Not until… Not while we’re figuring out what we’re doing.”

“Seriously?” Shannon had said. “You’ll fuck me while hiding me from Chris, but now that you’re letting me see him, kissing’s off-limits?”

“I don’t want us-” He gestured between the two of them. “-to get in the way of what’s best for our son.”

And she had huffed, but she hadn’t argued. Not then, anyway, not when they both knew Chris could be waking up at any moment.

Now, Eddie stepped off the porch and walked up to the pair of them, Shannon holding Chris and hugging him tight, and pressed a kiss into his son’s hair. He stood there for a moment, arm on top of Shannon’s on Chris’ back, simply soaking in his child’s happiness. Then he stepped away.

“What do you say we see what else Santa brought you?”

“Yeah!” Chris cheered, wiggling to be let down. Once Shannon set him on his feet, he plopped himself down in front of the tree and the pile of presents under it. Shannon eased herself onto the grass behind him, legs stretched to either side of him.

“Uh, this one.” Eddie knelt down and grabbed a rectangular present that was on top, the wrapping paper decorated with snowflakes. The person that wrapped it had forgotten to get tags, and so simply wrote on the paper with a marker. “Isn’t from Santa, actually. It’s from Buck.”

He handed it to Chris when the kid made grabby motions with his hands, and watched as Shannon helped him unwrap it. If she had any thoughts about Buck giving their son a present, she didn’t voice them. 

“It’s a book,” Chris announced, looking at it curiously.

“Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland and Through the Looking Glass,” Shannon read off. “It has pictures.”

“We can read it later, bud,” Eddie said, and then snagged another present to hand him. “This one actually is from Santa…”

Back had high hopes for this Christmas at the beginning of the month. Now? Now, as much as he didn’t want to admit it, he was feeling a bit disappointed. Because he’d been looking forward, so very much, to celebrating with both of his siblings again. That wasn’t happening, though.

He and Danny had both been given invitations to celebrate at Athena’s, but they’d turn it down because, according to Danny, they had “other plans.” Buck assumed he meant with Maddie, but nothing about those plans apparently changed even after their sister made it clear she’d much rather enjoy the holiday with them absent. The fact that Chimney wasn’t going to be at the Grants, either, or so Hen let slip, was probably just a coincidence. Chimney could very well be spending the day with the Lees, the couple who weren’t his parents but also were his parents.

Convincing himself of that was a bit difficult when he was having flashbacks to his childhood and all the times Maddie had ditched them for Doug. Especially after Danny ended his phone call to her with a very familiar scowl.

Regardless, their Christmas morning was spent opening presents. Including the ones labeled as “from Santa” that Danny still got for Buck, even though he was twenty-six and had stopped believing in the guy long before they left Hershey. They called Tia Alma, whose daughters were graduating high school in May, and chatted with her. At one point, while lounging on the couch while a Christmas movie played, Danny’s phone rang.

Buck recognized the ringtone, and it soured his good mood. His brother didn’t even look at the caller ID before taking it to his room and shutting the door, because even now he tried to keep as much distance between Buck and their parents as possible.

While Danny was gone, Buck scrolled through his phone, smiling at the pictures that Eddie had sent him of Chris tearing into his presents. Well, he mostly smiled. He still wasn’t quite sure how he felt, with everything Eddie had dropped in his lap while the kid talked to Santa. He knew he was a little hurt, and that he was a little mad, and he was trying very hard not to dwell on the what if of things.

Shortly before noon, Danny bullied him into changing out of his pajamas and into actual clothes, before they both got into Danny’s car. No matter how much Buck badgered him, Danny didn’t give an answer as to what they were doing. Even after they pulled up in front of another apartment building and Danny told him to move to the backseat.

“Why?”

“Because I said so, that’s why,” Danny said. “Now move.”

With an eye roll and a huff, Buck did so, getting out and then squeezing himself into the backseat. He hated sitting in the back, he always felt like he was folded in half. Danny fiddled with his phone, texting someone, and then stowed it away as the passenger door opened.

“Hey,” Jones - Jones - said, holding up bags of presents. “Pop the trunk?”

“‘Course,” Danny said, reaching down to do just that, like he hadn’t driven Buck into the Twilight Zone.

Buck twisted around as best he could, watched Jones put the bags away like this was a normal, every day occurrence, and then turned back to lean into Danny’s face.

“What!?”

“Sit back,” his brother said, pushing at his face. Buck smacked his hand away.

“No, seriously, what!?”

“Hi, Buck,” Jones said, sliding into the passenger seat and closing the door. “Merry Christmas.”

“Uh, yeah,” Buck said, a bit dull with his surprise. “Merry Christmas.”

“You guys’ sister isn’t coming?”

“No, she isn’t,” Danny grumbled. “Pretty sure she’s spending today with that Chimney guy. Maddie doesn’t really believe in the whole ‘bros before hoes’ idea.”

“Ouch.”

“Yeah, yeah,” Buck said. “What is going on?”

“We’re going to his parents,” Danny answered as they pulled away. “Okay, important things for you to know: they think we’ve been dating for the past, uh…”

“Did you forget our anniversary?” Jones asked, holding up a plastic candy cane filled with Skittles. There was a bow wrapped around it.

“No,” Danny said, yanking the container of candy out of his hand. “Maybe. When did we say it was?”

“I think three years next month.”

Buck’s head swiveled on his neck as he stared at them both in complete confusion.

“Three years- You’ve been dating for three years!? But- But- Attica?”

“No,” Danny said, like Buck was being stupid. “They think we’ve been dating for three years.”

“Who’s they?”

“My family,” Jones answered.

“Okay, no. No, no, no.” Buck stuck his head between them and made time-out motions with his hand. This was honestly the most he’d ever heard Jones say to anyone in one sitting. Wait, wait, did this mean the Jones he saw while tripping on LSD was the actual Jones? Did Jones see him making out with Eddie!? “How the hell do you two even know each other?”

“I went through the academy with a guy at his station.”

“And you’ve been dating for three years.”

“Fake dating,” Danny said. “I show up at family functions and get fed.”

“And my mother’s stopped trying to set me up with anyone,” Jones added with a shrug. “It’s a win-win.”

Buck sat back, digesting this information. When a realization struck him, he shot forward again to shove an accusing finger in Jones’ face.

“It’s you!” he nearly shouted. “You’re the spy!”

“Sit back before you make us crash.” Danny shoved at his chest and Buck allowed himself to be forced back into his seat. “As I was saying, important information for you to remember: we’ve been dating for three years, his mom’s Tabitha and his dad’s Alan. Cynthia is his sister, she’s married to Emmett, their boys are Dean and Sam-”

“She was a bit obsessed with the show,” Jones cheerfully announced.

“His brother Ethan is married to Irene, their daughter’s Rosemary. Any questions?”

“Yeah, I’ve got several,” Buck said. “Mostly, what the fuck, Danny?”

Chapter 21

Now

“Stop staring at me.”

Buck closed his locker as he continued to stare at Jones. The driver-engineer had come into the locker room while Buck was getting ready and he couldn’t not stare at him. Buck’s entire worldview had been fundamentally altered, his metaphorical table knocked over and leaving him scrambling to pick everything up again.

“I will not,” he said. “I watched you make out with my brother under the mistletoe.”

Jones rolled his eyes.

“We did not make out.”

“I’m pretty sure there was tongue!”

“There was no tongue.”

“I saw him grab your ass.”

Jones paused in buttoning up his shirt, looked up as if he was trying to remember, and then shrugged. Because Danny had grabbed Jones’ ass while they kissed under the mistletoe, to the soundtrack of Jones’ nephews making exaggerated sounds of disgust and Mrs. Jones - “Call me Tabitha, dear” - cooing, and it had been weird and uncomfortable for Buck to witness. The entire day spent with Jones’ family had been weird for Buck. 

“I feel like I’ve fallen into Wonderland.”

“I saw Hen and Chimney going upstairs when I came in, “ Jones said as he closed his own locker. “So there’s your Tweedledee and Tweedledum.”

“Okay, first, they’re from the Looking-Glass world, not Wonderland,” Buck corrected him, following the guy out of the locker room. “Second, when did you get a sense of humor!?”

“I’ve always been funny.”

“You have not! You never talk!”

“There’s no one worth talking to in this place.”

“Oh my God.”

The usual suspects were all upstairs, gathered around the kitchen island. Eddie had his phone out, no doubt sharing the multitude of photos he took of Chris opening presents that he’d sent Buck. Bobby looked up at the sight of him coming over, Jones heading for the other end of the loft.

“How was your Christmas?” the captain asked.

“Weird,” Buck announced, and then pointed at Jones. “He made out with my brother under the mistletoe.”

The others all turned their heads to look at Jones, who stopped and let out a sigh.

“We didn’t make out,” he said, without turning around.

“He grabbed your ass!”

Once again, Jones just shrugged.

“I’m sorry,” Eddie said, pocketing his phone and pushing up from where he’d been leaning against the kitchen island. “Danny grabbed his ass?”

Hen shot Eddie an odd look at that, quick and gone again. Buck wouldn’t have noticed it, except that the other two, Bobby and Chimney, were bouncing their gazes between him and Jones. He wondered what was up with that look.

“Yeah!” he said, instead of dwelling on that. “Apparently he’s been dating-”

“Dan and I are not dating,” Jones cut in. He’d turned around, arms crossed over his chest, apparently having accepted the fact that he was not actually escaping this conversation.

“Chimney makes my sister hot mustard, you bought my brother his favorite candy,” Buck shot back, and then refocused on the others. “Whatever, he’s been “fake” dating Danny for three years.”

There were copious amounts of finger quoting as he said the word fake.

“And you didn’t know?” Hen asked.

“No! I walk into his parents’ house, because that’s where we ended up going for Christmas, and his mother is excited to finally meet me-”

“I’m sure that didn’t last long,” Chimney joked. Buck scowled at him.

“Those were her exact words,” he said, opting to ignore the dig. “That she was excited to finally meet me.”

“Dan talks about you a lot,” Jones said.

“And! And! He’s the spy! He’s the one that keeps tattling on me to Danny!”

That made Bobby’s eyebrows shoot up, before his whole face contorted into a frown that he directed towards Jones. Jones, for his part, just scratched at his ear and didn’t meet the captain’s eyes.

“I’m not sorry.”

“You should be!” Buck said. “Do you know how much Danny bitched at me for that call with the grenade?”

“A lot?” Eddie guessed.

“A lot,” Buck agreed.

“Well,” Bobby said. “Did your sister go with you?”

“She didn’t want to celebrate with us this year,” Buck answered, very purposefully not looking at Chimney.

“That sucks, man,” Eddie said, utterly oblivious to the paramedic’s increasingly apparent discomfort. “I know how much you were looking forward to that.”

“Yeah. Hey, did Chris like his presents?” Buck was still trying to figure out his feelings in regards to everything Eddie had told him, but he’d already decided that the kid did not deserve any of his ill will. The blame for everything that happened was a burden for the adults to bear, not a child who hadn’t even been born yet.

“He did. Especially the Legos from Danny. And he demanded we read from the book before bed.”

“Alice in Wonderland is a classic.”

“Huh,” Jones said, still standing a bit away from everyone with his head tilted a little as he looked at the two of them. Eddie glanced at him before turning back to Buck. And then his eyes went wide.

“Was he actually there when we were…?”

“Tripping balls because of the special brownies?” Jones finished for Eddie. “Yup.”

Eddie’s eyes, if possible, went even wider as his face paled.

“Well, this morning has been illuminating,” Hen announced as she got off her stool. “I’m gonna go start on checking the ambulance stock before we get called out. Buck, come help me?”

“Uh.” Buck blinked, looked around him. “Sure?”

He followed her back downstairs to the ambulance, making a stop to grab a clipboard to keep track of the inventory. She opened the back doors for him before climbing in behind him. Then she shut them and sat down, making no move to start going through the supplies.

“I thought…” Buck trailed off as she stared at him. “What?”

“Eddie was showing us pictures earlier,” she said.

“The, uh. The ones from Christmas, right?” he asked, a bit unsure of what this was about. “Chris was adorable in his little robe, with the hood and the ears. Wasn’t he?”

“He was,” Hen agreed. “You know what else caught my attention?”

“...What?”

“The woman in the pictures with him,” she said. Buck could swear he felt his heart stop at the words as he collapsed on the bench behind him.

“Hen, I- Wh- What woman?”

“Eddie’s wife,” she said. “When Chimney told me about her coming here during the toy drive, and how she called you ‘Evan,’ I thought, ‘huh, that’s weird.’ And then when I actually saw her, I couldn’t help but notice that she looks exactly the same as the Shannon who came to the station looking for you, way back in the spring. Your ‘ex’s ex, apparently.’”

“Hen…”

“Who knows? About you and Eddie?”

Buck let out a sigh.

“Danny and our old neighbor,” he said. “They both, uh. Walked in on us. Maybe Jones, because he was helping Danny babysit us while we were high and we, um. Started making out. Shannon. And now you.”

“Five people?” she asked. “That’s all?”

“You can’t tell anyone.” Buck was almost begging. “Not Athena, not Chimney, not- not anyone.”

“He’s not out, is he?”

“No. He’s never- Everyone else found out by accident. Please, Hen.”

“Of course I won’t, that’s a cruel thing to do to someone who’s not ready,” she said, reaching out a hand to squeeze his knee. Buck just about collapsed in his relief. “But if you don’t mind me asking, what happened? You said Shannon knows, and that it wasn’t a good break up. Which totally explains the way you acted when he first showed up. We all thought you were just being a brat.”

“Yeah…”

Buck chewed on his lip as he avoided looking at her, fingers tapping away on the clipboard that was still tightly clutched in his hands. The opportunity to vent to a sympathetic ear did sound very appealing, and she’d already figured out about Eddie entirely on her own with the various clues she’d been handed. He knew, too, that he could trust her to keep a secret. At least about something like this, anyways.

“Remember when I told you I went to high school in Texas?” he asked, and waited for Hen to nod. “I met him on my first day. He was in almost all of my classes, and we hit it off. Best friends pretty much immediately. He went to my football games, I went to his baseball games, we were constantly at one another’s homes. We even had joint birthday parties. Then one day I kissed him and he kissed me back.”

“But it didn’t last.”

“No, uh. Look, it was Texas during the late aughts, right? I mean, El Paso wasn’t bad, the Sun City Pride started while we were going to school. I went to a couple with Danny, but Eddie never went. But um. His entire family’s Catholic, and his dad had… Let’s say opinions on how men are supposed to be. And then there was Timothy.”

“Timothy?” Hen tilted her head in patient intrigue.

“A kid our freshman year, homeschooled before switching to public. He came out, and… Well, he had to switch schools before the semester was even halfway through.”

“Oh.” She looked heartbroken over someone she had never even met.

“Yeah. So Eddie, he wasn’t eager to come out to anyone. Terrified of it, actually. And then when Shannon came along our senior year, started hanging out with us. His parents… I don’t really know what they knew. I mean, I’m pretty sure they knew about me, but I don’t know if they knew about us and were in denial of it, or if they just thought I was that queer kid helplessly in love with their completely heterosexual son and at risk of corrupting him.”

Hen’s eyebrows went up at the L-word, but thankfully didn’t say a damn thing.

“So they were very happy when Shannon started hanging out with us, started talking about what a cute couple she and Eddie would make. They’d say it in front of me a lot, so they absolutely knew about me. And Shannon apparently agreed with them, because she asked Eddie to our senior prom.”

“And he said yes.”

“I said yes for him,” Buck said. He shrugged at the taken aback expression on her face. “The bitch asked him in the middle of lunch, at a table full of jocks. Some of them were the ones who tormented Timothy. He panicked, I rescued him. Like a bro.”

She let out a laugh at the use of “bro” in the context.

“And?” she prodded.

“And that was that.” Buck shrugged again. He wasn’t sure about telling her how things ended, or why. Not when he was still coming to terms with it. “Can we talk about something else, now?”

“Sure,” Hen agreed easily. She took the clipboard out of his hands and took the pen out of her breast pocket. “We actually do need to make sure we’re stocked in here.”

“Yeah, okay,” he said, pulling open a drawer. “Hey! Did you know Jones’ name is Austin?”

Hen just rolled her eyes.

Daniel watched Buck work at the stove as he drank his beer, mentally preparing himself for the shift ahead of him. Tomorrow was New Year’s Eve, and Daniel was unfortunately stuck working it. He was far too aware of how much it was going to suck. His phone pinged on the table and he rolled his eyes at the message that came through.

“Is that your “fake” boyfriend again?” Buck asked, pausing what he was doing to throw up scare quotes.

“Pretty sure I’m the fake boyfriend in this non-relationship, actually,” Daniel said as he texted Austin back. Austin had New Year’s Eve off and was viciously mocking him for it. “What are you doing tomorrow?”

“Connor and the guys invited me out.”

“Whoever you bring back, keep it in your bedroom, please.”

“Yeah, yeah. Hey, should I ask the same of you and Austin?”

There was absolutely no need for his brother to say the man’s name like that.

“We’re not dating-”

“Right, that’s crazy, you’re just friends who hang out,” Buck said, and then threw him a look over his shoulder. “Where have I heard that before?”

“Do not compare me and Austin to whatever bullshit Maddie’s got going on,” Daniel grumbled. “It’s not the same.”

“You literally took me to meet his family,” his brother said. Daniel scowled. “So how does this work?”

“How does what work?”

“You and him.” Buck turned off the burner, moved the skillet to the side, and then turned around. “You were dating Attica last year. Does he just… Not mind? Is this like, an open thing? Does he go out with other people, too?”

“It’s not a thing at all, that’s the point. We aren’t actually dating,” he said, for what felt like the thousandth time. What part of this was his brother having trouble understanding? “We’ve told you, it’s just to keep his parents off his back. He’s not interested in dating anyone, he doesn’t, y’know. Swing for any team.”

“Uh-huh.” Buck clearly still wasn’t understanding. Daniel rolled his eyes as he got up to grab plates from the cabinet. “Hey, uh. There’s no way Doug followed Maddie here from Pennsylvania, right?”

Daniel almost hit himself in the head with the cabinet door.

“What!?” he demanded, turning around. “Why?”

“I just…” Buck rubbed at the back of his neck. “When Chimney and I were getting that tree for Maddie, I… It’s stupid, nevermind.”

“No, it’s not stupid,” Daniel said, feeling his heart pounding in his chest. “What is it?”

“There was a guy. I didn’t get a good look at him, but… He looked kinda like Doug,” Buck said. Daniel took a breath and did his best not to react too obviously. “But that wasn’t- I mean, Maddie said he didn’t know we were in LA, right? That he didn’t ask questions. So… Nevermind, it was stupid. It was just someone that looked like Doug, I guess.”

“It wasn’t stupid,” Daniel told him, turning back to grab plates with shaking hands. “Maddie’s safe. We’ll make sure of it.”

He was already mentally penning an apology to Athena, for the late call she was about to get.

Chapter 22

Now

Nash did not look particularly happy to see Daniel, when he opened the door. Which meant that Nash definitely hadn’t forgotten their conversation last year about Buck. Always good to know.

“Hi.” Daniel was all cheer as he said it. “I was wanting to talk to the sergeant.”

“She’s not in.”

“I can wait.”

The captain glowered, but he was a midwesterner through-and-through. As much as he would probably love to tell Daniel to go away, the manners were ingrained. Nash stepped aside, waving Daniel in and closing the door behind him.

The inside of the Grant residence hadn’t changed much from the last time Daniel was here, after Maddie’s ride-along with the sergeant. That was also to talk about Doug, come to think of it. He couldn’t immediately pick out anything that was obviously Nash’s, so despite getting engaged after only half a year, there was some feet dragging in other fields. Or maybe the captain just didn’t have much to move in. Buck had mentioned how sadly empty the captain’s apartment seemed, when he saw it.

What Daniel did notice, however, was the smell of food. Nash brushed past him to the kitchen, leaving him behind in the living room. The captain bustled around, seemingly content to pretend Daniel wasn’t there as he carried a bundt cake over to the dining table. Daniel was almost tempted to make some comment about hosts and guests and manners, because he could be as much of a little shit as his brother, but the front door was opening again before he could.

“We’re here!” called down a man that Daniel didn’t recognize.

Nash poked his head around and answered with,

“They’re not here yet.”

“You can still make your escape,” May said, her and Harry following behind the man Daniel assumed to be Michael, their father. “Hey, Dan.”

“S’up, squirts.”

“Just put your stuff away before your grandparents get here,” Michael told his children, and then held out his hand to Daniel. “So you’re the guy Athena trusted with our kids.”

“Yessir,” he confirmed as they shook hands. “I haven’t been needed since she stopped sneaking around.”

Michael laughed and then joined Nash in the kitchen.

“Oh, this is nice,” he said, looking at everything laid out on the dining table. “Very impressive.”

“Impressive is what I’m going for, so thank you,” Nash said. “Can you stay? Will you join us?”

Daniel refrained from pointing out that he hadn’t been invited to stay.

“Oh, hell no,” Michael bluntly stated. “Beatrice has never been a fan of mine. You have no earthly idea what is about to happen to you, do you?”

At Nash’s confused look, Michael let out a laugh.

“Tell them I said hi,” he said, heading for the door. “Dan- I can call you Dan, right? I suggest you run away with me. Now. Before-”

“We’re here!” Athena announced as she came through the door. Michael looked panicked. May and Harry rushed to greet their grandparents while Athena made her way into the living room. “You still here? I thought you’d drop the kids and run.”

“They wouldn’t jump out of the car while it was still rolling.”

Athena rolled her eyes and hurried to Nash’s side to grip his hand and drag him over, looking very much like she was walking him to their joint execution.

“Remember,” she was saying, “no matter what happens, they leave on Saturday. Daniel, why are you here?”

“To talk to you,” he answered with a shrug, watching her march pass. “I chose a bad time for it.”

“You really did.”

“Beatrice, how’re you doing?” Michael greeted the older woman coming down. “It’s good to see you.”

“Michael, what a pleasant surprise,” was her happy-seeming response. “I didn’t know you’d be here.”

Judging from Michael’s look of bewilderment, Daniel figured he was not expecting that warm of a welcome. He, meanwhile, was stepping as far away from this mess as he could, perfectly content to just watch. Other people’s family drama was much preferable to his own.

“Uh, Mama, Daddy,” Athena said, getting her parents' attention. “This is Bobby.”

The captain gave a lame, “Hi.” Everyone stood there awkwardly for a moment, before Athena’s father stepped forward and held out his hand.

“Pleasure to meet you, Mr. Carter,” Nash said, shaking it.

“No need for that, Christian names’ll do. Call me Samuel.”

“Will do.”

“That’s a good looking spread,” Samuel commented, peering around Nash and Athena at the dining table. He went over to it, his grandchildren trailing after him. Beatrice lingered behind.

“He living here already?” she bluntly asked her daughter.

“Uh, no, ma’am,” Nash answered, even though the question hadn’t been directed at him. “Not until after the wedding. And it’s a pleasure to finally meet you.”

“You’re very… Tall,” Beatrice said, and Daniel wondered what descriptor “tall” was substituting for. “So, Michael, is this your husband?”

The question was accompanied by a pointed glance at Daniel, who awkwardly smiled and waved.

“No, that isn’t Glenn,” Michael said, shaking his head. “And Glenn and I aren’t married.”

“Of course, you’re barely divorced,” Beatrice said with a dismissive sniff. “Who’s this, then?”

“Daniel,” he answered. “I just chose a really bad time to come talk to your daughter about something.”

“He threatened Bobby’s life once!” Michael blurted out, pointing at him.

“I never once mentioned killing him,” Daniel said with a smile. Nash’s own grin had gone rictus. It looked a bit painful for him to be holding, actually.

Beatrice raised an eyebrow and let out a hum, before joining her husband and grandchildren in the kitchen. The only one who relaxed in her wake was Michael, who had been edging for the stairs the entire conversation. The moment her back was turned, he was hurrying up them.

“I’ll walk you both out,” Athena announced, jerking her head for Daniel to follow her to the door, as well. Nash’s smile dropped in a moment of panic and betrayal.

Michael did not wait for her. In fact, he was already out the door by the time Daniel and Athena reached it. The man simply waved as he threw himself into the car and pulled out, clearly eager to get as far away from his former in-laws as he could. Which was understandable, if Beatrice’s rather cutting comments were an indicator of how she usually treated the people around her.

“I did wonder if Nash told anyone about that,” Daniel said as he stepped out onto the Grants’ porch. The Carters? The Grant-Carters?

“It’s why I trusted you with my babies,” Athena said as she pulled the front door to. “You chose a really bad time for this, Daniel.”

“Trust me, I know. Sorry.” He shrugged apologetically. “I haven’t seen my parents in person in nearly nine years. Even longer for Buck.”

The sergeant furrowed her brow at that, before shaking her head.

“Unfortunately, it’s also a waste of time,” she said. “I don’t have anything new for you.”
