“It’s been three months since I told you that Buck saw him. How is there nothing?”

“Since Buck thought he saw him,” Athena pointed out. “And without a picture-”

“I’ve tried! It’s like the guy has no social media.”

“Without a picture,” she continued on, as if he hadn’t spoken, “I can only give out a description. And ‘a tall caucasian male with short dark hair’ doesn’t exactly narrow it down.”

“What about ‘exudes an aura of dickishness’ and ‘has a punchable face?’”

Athena just gave him a flat look.

“Did you ever try talking to Chimney? Ask him if he saw anyone that had a ‘punchable face’ or otherwise matched Doug’s description?”

“I’d have to try and catch him alone for that, and then he’d probably want to know why I was asking. Worse, he mentions it to Maddie, and then off she runs somewhere I can’t keep her safe.”

“You don’t know that.”

“Yeah, I do,” Daniel said with a sigh. “She and Buck are both runners. It’s a miracle neither of them legged it back in September.”

“Well,” Athena said, visibly choosing not to pry into that, “they’re both here now, there’s been no sign of Doug Kendall since before Christmas, and I have my parents inside, with my mother no doubt tormenting my fiance in my absence.”

“Was that a hint for me to go?”

“Yes.”

Daniel barked out a laugh.

Buck held up the boxing pads, catching his sister’s punches. When Maddie brought up the idea of learning how to defend herself, Buck had been all for going to the gym with her. So had Danny, unsurprisingly. He’d been the one to teach Buck - and Eddie - how to properly throw a punch, after all.

Since Buck told him about the apparition of Doug at the tree farm, Danny had gotten a bit overprotective. One or both of them were regularly dropping in at Maddie’s apartment, regardless of whether or not Chimney was over. Buck didn’t argue, because he never did take much convincing to visit his sister. And if he got to irritate her a little, well. He was a little brother, after all. He may or may not have still been a bit salty over Christmas, when he agreed to it.

“There you go, just keep that tight. Nice,” he encouraged her. “You are on fire with that sassy new haircut.”

“Sassy?” Maddie asked, breathing a little hard from exertion.

“Yeah.” 

His sister had cut her hair, so that now it brushed her shoulders instead of falling down her back. Buck thought it looked good.

“Okay, let’s work on that two-three-two combo.” He pantomimed punching the air. “We want you to be ready with that devastating hook if you ever see Doug again.”

“Whoa, that’s not why I’m doing this.”

“Really?” Buck didn’t believe that for a moment. “You don’t want to kick your abusive ex’s ass someday? ‘Cause I would.”

“I’m not doing anything for Doug anymore, okay? This is just the next chapter in Maddie’s fresh start,” she said, bouncing in place as she spoke. He snorted at her use of third person. “Come on, haircut, apartment, dispatcher of the month. I don’t know if you heard.”

“Only a thousand times,” Buck said, holding up the pads in front of him again. “C’mon.”

“Is that what this is about?” Maddie asked as she started punching again. “I’m thriving, and you can’t handle it.”

“I’m great.”

“Oh, yeah, you’re doing great.” The sarcasm in her tone had him scowling a little. “You’re still living in your big brother’s apartment after twelve years, and you’re tagging along on your crush’s family outings to the zoo.”

“Your trash talk sucks, and I don’t have a crush on Eddie.”

He did go on that trip to the zoo, though. It was the month before. Eddie had extended the invite to him, ostensibly on behalf of Chris, who wanted to introduce his best friend to his mother. Buck suspected it was also because the space between him and Eddie had loomed rather large ever since Eddie confessed about prom night. Regardless, it had been a very, very awkward experience for all of them except Christopher, who was just happy to be there with everyone.

Buck spent the most of the visit pretty much ignoring Eddie and Shannon in favor of Chris, and did not feel any guilt for it.

“Even Dan’s got whatever he’s doing with that Austin Jones guy,” Maddie said. “Even if he keeps denying it.”

“What about you, huh?” Buck asked her. “What’s your excuse?”

“What are you talking about?”

“How’s Chimney?”

Maddie glared and started punching again. He grinned as he caught each one in the pads, because he could feel how much force she was putting in them now. It seemed he struck a nerve.

“We’re friends,” she said.

“Except, oh, you want to date him.”

“I never told you that.”

“You didn’t have to,” he told her, laughing at the face she made. “Your fists told me enough, with how hard you started swinging once I mentioned his name.”

“Shut up,” she groused, feinting a swing at his face. He leaned back with a grin.

“Okay, lemme ask you this,” Buck said, dropping his arms. “If you’re doing so well, if you’re “thriving”-” He tried doing finger quotes, but it was a bit difficult while wearing the boxing pads. “Why can’t you just date the guy?”

By the time they left the gym for the day, Maddie still hadn’t given him an answer beyond “I don’t know.”

Then

Daniel knocked on the door and waited for the person on the other side to shout for him to come in. It swung open easily, revealing his sister fretting in front of a full-length mirror. She spotted him in the reflection and she turned with a joyful squeal.

“Dan! You made it!”

“I wasn't going to miss my big sister’s wedding,” he told her, bending slightly to give her a hug. “You look very beautiful.”

“Thank you,” Maddie said. She lifted the skirt of the wedding dress a little to give a mock curtsy as she laughed. “You clean up nicely yourself. I can’t wait to see Evan in his suit. When I tell him he looks good, will I be telling the truth or lying my ass off?”

“He thinks he looks good,” Dan said with a shrug. “He spent twenty minutes in front of the mirror taking pictures of himself to send to- To er, the boyfriend.”

“Oh, could he not make it?” she asked, looking genuinely disappointed. “I was looking forward to meeting the boy who my baby brother is dating and whose name no one will tell me.”

“He’s not out still, so that’s confidential information,” he told her. “He stayed in El Paso.”

Honestly, Daniel had not wanted to fight Eddie’s parents to get permission for the kid to come to Pennsylvania. Not with the way he knew, just from what he’d heard from Evan and how he himself had heard Helena and Ramon talk, that his parents expected him to stay and shoulder some of their responsibilities for them. Ramon was in Alabama right now, or Daniel thought he was, and so Eddie was once again being shoved into the role of man of the house in his father’s absence. Thankfully, Eddie himself had been reluctant to come, not that Daniel could blame him any.

He was a little relieved, not that he’d admit as much out loud. Not only did Eddie not coming mean Daniel didn’t have to pay out for another plane ticket, but he also didn’t have to sleep in a hotel room with two hormonal teenagers that were dating each other. He’d end up having to share a bed with one of them, because to hell if he was going to risk being woken up by that, and he quite liked being able to spread out in his sleep, thanks.

“It’s probably for the best,” Maddie said. The words came out quiet.

“What? Does Doug have a problem with Evan?”

“He doesn’t know about Evan.” She didn’t meet his eyes as she admitted it. “But his dad… Like I said, it’s probably for the best.”

Daniel scowled. He was very much here under protest, supporting his sister’s bad decisions. If Doug or his father said anything, anything, to or about Evan, Daniel would not hold his tongue. He could hold it now, though.

“I noticed Mom and Dad weren’t out there,” he said.

“I invited them, they told me I was making a mistake,” Maddie said, fingers digging into the white fabric of her dress. “Don’t say it.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“But you agree with them.”

Daniel just shrugged. It was one of the few, maybe even the only, things that the Buckley sons and their parents agreed on. Doug was a massive tool and Maddie could do so much better than him. Daniel just could not see whatever it was that his sister saw in the man.

The music that had been playing outside Maddie’s room changed, pipe organs hitting recognisable notes. She took a breath, flipped down her veil, and gathered up the bouquet of flowers from where they were laying. Then she slipped her arm around Daniel's, letting him lead her out.

Doug had gone for a traditional church wedding, so Daniel had to walk her down the aisle past rows of people he either didn’t know at all or barely recognized. Some of them were friends from high school, some he thought might work with his sister, but their parents were a glaring absence in the front row. 

Evan was at the end of the short line of groomsmen, fidgeting in his suit. He noticed Daniel looking at him and made a face. Maddie didn’t quite manage to stifle a giggle while Daniel rolled his eyes.

With his sister escorted to where Doug was waiting, Daniel’s job was done. He ignored the slimy smile the man sent his way as he went to stand by his brother. They elbowed each other like children, until the pastor cleared his throat and began to speak, at which point they hurriedly stood to attention. Or at least as close to it as Evan was ever capable.

“We have gathered in the presence of God,” the pastor said, “to give thanks for the gift of marriage…”

Chapter 23

Chapter Notes

content warning for blood, the aftermath of violence. y'know, the canon goodies.

Now

It had been said before, it could stand being said again: sometimes, the calls that they responded to could get weird. Today’s example: a shark attack on the freeway. As in there was currently a shark, in the middle of the road, chomping down on some guy’s arm while a group of people held it down and others stood around to gawk. Buck’s response to seeing it, when he and the others arrived on the scene, was a simple and concise,

“What the hell?”

“Tiger shark. We were transporting her for release into the wild,” a woman explained, one of the people holding the shark down as it struggled. The logos on some of their jackets identified them as being from the California Marine Life Center. “Truck jackknifed.”

“Jaws!” Bobby barked out.

“Yeah,” Chimney agreed with a laugh. They all stared at him. “Oh! Right, those Jaws.”

“I’m on it,” Eddie declared, turning to grab them from the truck, brushing against Buck on his way by.

Chimney crept closer to their patient, and the shark that was currently clamped down on the man’s arm. There was blood everywhere, and the man occasionally let out a scream when the shark gave a particularly strong jerk.

“How’d his arm get in there?” the paramedic asked.

“Diego was trying to get what water was left in our pump system,” one of the other CMLC people answered. “She can’t breathe without it.”

“How much time does she have left?” Hen asked, lifting her head from where she’d been digging through her medical bag.

“Minutes,” the first woman responded.

It seemed kinda cruel to just wait while she drowned in the air. 

“Do we wait that long?” Buck asked Bobby, lifting up the halligan he was holding. It wasn’t a vorpal blade, but neither was the shark the Jabberwock. “Or just, y’know-”

“No, no, don’t kill her!” shouted Diego, the guy whose arm was currently being eaten by the shark. “She’s come so far.”

“Okay, we’re gonna do this really carefully,” Bobby said as Eddie arrived with the hydraulic spreader, a tool which was notorious for its careful and gentle use. Not.

Buck wasn’t really certain how they were going to get Diego’s arm freed without either injuring his arm further or breaking the shark’s jaw. The teeth he could see were curved, and he was pretty sure they were serrated. They’d have to get that jaw open wide. And again, the hydraulic spreader was not a precision tool, it wasn’t meant to be careful or gentle. It was meant to tear apart pieces of wrecked vehicles to free the people inside.

Regardless, Buck knew his role here. He knelt down beside the shark to help hold her still while Eddie took the spreader to the shark’s mouth. His view of the shark’s mouth, and Diego’s arm, was blocked by both Bobby and the Jaws of Life. But he watched as Diego managed to pull his arm free with pained grunting and whimpering.

He stepped away from the shark, now that Diego was no longer sharkbait, and stood aside as the paramedics got him situated on the gurney. Even as they were bandaging his arm, which had more holes than a pincushion, the guy was begging them not to let the tiger shark die.

“I thought sharks were beautiful, misunderstood animals,” Eddie said as he came to stand beside him. “But you were ready to Old Yeller the thing.”

“That one’s a beautiful, misunderstood animal that was suffocating while deepthroating a guy,” Buck pointed out.

Eddie narrowed his eyes at him and then smirked. He opened his mouth to say- What, Buck didn’t get to find out, because he was cut off before the words could even come out.

“How much does she weigh?” Bobby called to the CMLC people, who were desperately pouring water over the shark.

“She’s not fully grown,” one of them shouted back. “Around Seven-Fifty.”

Bobby nodded and turned his head, brow furrowed in thought. Buck followed his gaze to the ladder truck. More specifically, to the ladder itself. Which, coincidentally, had a tip capacity limit of 750 pounds.

The captain nodded as he reached his decision and said,

“It’ll be fine.”

And that was how they ended up with a shark dangling from the end of their ladder, like a carrot on a stick, as they drove to the ocean.

At this point, Buck had to accept that his actions and choices were amounting to an intricate form of self-harm. He was no stranger to it, though this time he wasn’t intentionally putting himself in harm’s way so he could pretend his mother’s fretting was because she cared about him. No, he didn’t even have that excuse going for him now.

Once again, Eddie extended an invitation to go somewhere with Chris and Shannon. Once again, instead of doing the sensible thing and turning it down, Buck threw on his shoes and headed out the door. The look Danny gave him on the way out was ignored, because he already knew his brother’s opinion on what he was doing. He didn’t need to hear it again, thanks.

This time, instead of the zoo, Buck was meeting them at the Pan Pacific Park, just north of the La Brea tar pits. Specifically the playground at the park, Renee’s Place. It was one of the Shane’s Inspiration playgrounds that dotted the city, designed with kids like Chris in mind, so he had no doubt that it was Carla who suggested it.

Buck spotted Eddie and Shannon sitting at a picnic table beneath the shaded pavilion. They were sitting on opposite sides from one another, Shannon was angled towards Eddie while Eddie was angled towards the playground. There was a small, red cooler on the ground by his feet. They were talking, neither one looking quite as awkward as they had during the zoo visit. He came to a stop on the path and simply looked at the pair.

What was he doing? He knew how this was going to end, how it had already ended. The best case scenario, he ended up with something worse than what they had during high school. It was much more likely, though, that he’d be given a front row seat to Eddie and Shannon’s reconciliation. And then he’d be stuck with the exact thing he spent so long running from.

Buck almost left at that moment. He was actually taking a step back, was half-way turned as he started composing an excuse in his head to text Eddie, when Chris spotted him from the playground and shouted his name. That had Eddie turning as well. The man grinned when he saw him and waved, which meant Buck’s window for flight was closed.

“Hey, man,” Eddie greeted him when he reached the picnic table. “I was starting to wonder if you got lost on the way here.”

“Nah, you know how traffic is like in this city,” Buck said. He glanced at his choices for seating and went with the bench Eddie was using, making sure to leave as much space between them as he could. “Hey, Shannon.”

“Hello, Buck,” she said. Her eyes flickered down to his neck and drawled, “That’s professional.”

She was talking about the hickey picking out of his collar. It was left over from the night before, when he once again went out with Connor and them. He was doing that more and more these days.

“Uniform will cover it up,” Buck told her.

Chris came trundling up to them, crutches tapping against the pavement between the pavilion and the rubber that playground equipment sat on. He was smiling brightly, as always, and his hair matted down a bit with sweat. He leaned heavily against Buck’s back when he reached them.

“Hi, Buck,” the kid chirped.

“Hey, kid,” Buck responded, twisting around so he could mess Chris’ hair up. “You’re sweaty.”

“He’s been running around,” Eddie said, reaching into the cooler and digging around. “Want a juice, Chris?”

“Yes, please.”

Buck helped the kid get himself situated on the bench while Eddie pulled out a juice box. For some reason, before punching the straw into it, he caught Shannon’s eye and waggled it at her with a smirk. It had her throwing her head back with a laugh.

It was an inside joke, Buck realized with a pang. He wasn’t sure why he was surprised that they had inside jokes. They were a married couple, after all. Truthfully, Shannon had more history with Eddie than Buck did. She’d been friends with Eddie before Buck ever came into the picture, and she was there after he fled El Paso. Of course they would have inside jokes that he wasn’t privy to.

It still sucked to witness firsthand, however, and once again left him wondering what the hell he was doing there.

“So, Chris,” Buck said, flailing for something to distract him. “How, uh. How was school? Learn anything cool?”

“We talked about the moon!” the kid said, and then happily chattered about everything he learned while drinking his juice box. It was a good ten minutes where Buck didn’t have to worry about the awkwardness he was subjecting himself to, at least until Chris finished his drink and demanded his father push him on the swings.

Buck watched the pair of them go and found himself softly smiling at the way Eddie’s hand hovered just behind Chris’ back as they went down the ramp into the playground area.

“At least I know what’s going on now,” Shannon remarked, a bit snidely, as she grabbed the discarded juice box and tossed it into a small bag of trash.

He blinked at her across the picnic table.

“Uh. What’s going on?”

“You and Eddie,” she said, which did nothing to solve his confusion. “At first, Eddie would rather fuck me than talk to me, and I thought, okay, that’s fine. But when he finally lets me see my son again, even kissing is off limits.”

Buck was entirely unaware that they weren’t sleeping together any longer. Eddie hadn’t mentioned it, and so he had simply assumed. Though it still didn’t tell him what the hell he had to do with anything.

“And then,” Shannon continued. “He starts inviting you to family trips. The park, the zoo-”

“Hold on,” Buck interrupted. “I thought Chris wanted me to come to the zoo?”

Shannon rolled her eyes.

“Eddie asked and Chris got excited at the idea.” Huh. “Then you come today with a hickey. Which really does explain pretty much everything.”

“Woah, woah, woah, time-out,” Buck begged, making the motion with his hands. “Do- Do you think…? Shan, no, Eddie and I aren’t- No, you sunk that ship. This?” He pointed at the hickey. “This is from the bar last night, some guy named- Er…”

“Er?”

“...I don’t think I actually got his name?”

Shannon rolled her eyes.

“So why are you here, then?” she asked.

“Because Eddie invited me?” It was a lame answer, and it came out as a question.

“Why?”

Buck could only shrug, because he didn’t have a better answer for her.

“I didn’t know what to say,” Bobby said. “I figured I should stay out of it, but then…”

“Mama got mean,” Hen said with a chuckle.

They were clearing the table after lunch at the station, the three of them: Bobby, Hen, and Buck. Bobby was telling them- Well, Bobby was telling Hen, while Buck eavesdropped, about the fight between Athena and her mother.

“Look, there’s a whole spectrum of mom A-types,” Hen told the captain as they carried dishes to the kitchen island. “You got the cool mom, the helicopter mom, the tiger mom. And then you got Beatrice.”

Buck wondered which category his own mom would fall under, or if she would be considered an “A-type” at all. She probably came the closest to being a helicopter mom, with the way she tried to keep him safe from harm. But that would also imply that she cared about him otherwise.

“The terrifying mom,” Bobby said.

“She named her daughter after a Greek goddess.”

“Yeah, Athena, goddess of wisdom.”

“And war-”

“Strategic war,” Buck broke in. The other two turned their heads to stare at him and he felt his shoulders creep up around his ears under their attention. “Ares was brute force, Athena was strategy.”

He may or may not have had a Greek mythology phase as a kid, and Danny may or may not have encouraged it. There may or may not be worn copies of the Percy Jackson books on his shelf as a result. Buck wasn’t admitting to anything.

“The point is,” Hen said, tearing her eyes away from Buck to focus on Bobby again. “Beatrice had big dreams for Athena and even bigger expectations, but she knows how this world works. How hard Athena would have to fight for everything. So child-rearing was less about raising a daughter and more like training a warrior.”

“Oh!” Buck said. “Athena was an investment. I get that.”

“You do?” Hen asked, sounding dubious. Bobby shook his head at her, a deep furrow between his brows.

“So no shopping trips then?” Bobby asked, clearly trying to move the subject back on topic. “Manicures? Long talks over cups of tea?”

Hen laughed at him.

“Maybe in a TV commercial,” she said. “But in real life? No one gets under their daughter’s skin like a mom.”

“What am I supposed to do, then?” Bobby asked her. “Just forget all the mean things she said to her?”

“You said your piece,” she said. “You had Athena’s back, and props to you for that, but yeah. They’ll work it out, or they won’t. And if you try to stand between them, you’ll get cut down in the crossfire.”

“Your mom ever say those kinds of terrible things to you?”

“Oh no,” Hen said, shaking her head. “My mother said a whole bunch of other, different terrible things.”

“And you forgave her?”

“She’s my mother.”

Bobby stared at her for a moment, head tilted to the side.

“Buck,” he suddenly said. Buck lifted his head up from the cups he was stacking.

“Yeah?”

“What would you want someone to do?” the captain asked, ignoring the way Hen glanced between them. “If your mom was talking to you like that?”

“Historically, I hid behind Danny,” Buck told him, carrying the carefully balanced stack of cups to the sink. “Or, I mean, he put himself between me and her. And then he took me and ran- Hey, have you thought about taking Athena somewhere until her parents leave?”

“Considering she chose a stakeout over going home last night,” Bobby said, “she might actually be up for that.”

“Hey, Buck.” The sound of Eddie’s voice had him turning on a reflex. The man was standing at the loft’s railing, looking down onto the bay. “Your sister’s here.”

Ever curious, Buck went to take a look, Bobby and Hen following him. Peering down into the truck bay as well, he spotted his sister talking to Chimney. She had an official looking manilla envelope clutched in her hands, and as they watched she leaned forward to kiss Chimney on the cheek.

A smiling Maddie turned around and left, without even once looking Buck’s way.

Ever since Buck told Danny about how he thought he saw Doug, they’d started making a point of dropping in on Maddie even more regularly than they already did. Sometimes she was happy to see them, sometimes she was irritated, sometimes they got to ruin one of her absolutely-not-a-date dates with Chimney.

This time was not like that. This time, Buck knew she was going to be out or about to be heading out. He wasn’t there to drop in on her or anything like that. He was simply bringing over a batch of sopaipillas that he had made. The last time he’d made them, they’d been eaten before she got a chance to try one, and she’d complained endlessly.

He figured a batch of them would be a nice surprise for her to come home to, after her first actual date with Chimney.

Finding Chimney laying in the walkway, bleeding from multiple stab wounds, was not a nice surprise.

The container of sopapillas hit the ground, followed by Buck’s knees. He fumbled to get his phone out and dialed 911, turning it onto speaker and setting it on the ground. The moment the call picked up, he was rattling off Maddie’s address and the situation.

“Okay, sir, can I get your name?”

“Evan Buckley,” he answered, scrambling to get his jacket off. He shoved it against Chimney’s stomach. “A firefighter with the One Eighteen.”

“Buck?”

“Yeah.” It must’ve been someone that Maddie knew, or maybe Abby. “Victim is Howard Han. He’s got profuse bleeding to his abdomen. Breathing is labored, might have hit a lung. C’mon, Chim, rebar to the head didn’t kill you…”

“RA Unit’s two minutes out. Do you know how this happened? How long has he been down?”

“I don’t know,” Buck said. “I just got here, I found him like this. Given the amount of blood, I’d say minutes, not hours.”

“How’s his pulse?”

Buck reached up, pressed his fingers against Chimney’s neck and held his breath as he felt for it.

“Weak,” he said. “But it’s there.”

“Jason…”

“H- Hold on, he’s… Chim? It’s me, it’s Buck,” he said, putting his weight against the jacket and the wounds beneath it. He could hear sirens approaching. “I need you to stay with me, okay?”

“Jason took her,” Chimney slurred, head lolling.

“Who’s Jason?”

“Maddie…”

Oh, God. Oh, God. He’d been so focused on trying to keep Chimney from bleeding out that he hadn’t even spent a moment to think about- Oh, God.

“Where’s Maddie?” Buck asked. “Chimney, where’s Maddie?”

But the man had slipped back into unconsciousness.

The moment the paramedics were there and taking over, Buck was grabbing his phone and rushing for the apartment. The door was already open and he shouted his sister’s name as he went in. She wasn’t in the living room or the dining room, she wasn’t upstairs. She wasn’t anywhere to be found.

The apartment was empty.

“She’s not here,” he relayed into the phone, feeling lost and untethered. He wanted to start crying for Danny. “She’s gone, she’s- Oh, no.”

“Buck, what is it?” the dispatcher asked. Buck ignored him, focused instead on the things still laying on the coffee table. There were Maddie’s keys, there was Maddie’s purse and, on the ground as if it had been carelessly dropped, there was Maddie’s phone. “Did you find something?”

“All of her stuff is still here.”

Chapter 24

Chapter Notes

Content Warning: Domestic abuse and domestic violence. If you would prefer to skip over those parts, start reading at "Maddie’s apartment was no longer empty."

Then

 Maddie fiddled with the speakers, humming contently to herself. Doug was in a good mood, which meant she was in a good mood. He’d gotten a promotion at work, and that was worth celebrating.

“Babe!” Doug shouted from the kitchen, accompanied by the sound of drawers opening and closing. Maddie froze at his shout, but the fact that he used a pet name - and wasn’t slamming anything - put her at ease. “Where’s the thing?”

“What thing?” she shouted back.

“That fancy opener that I took out a loan for!” Another drawer was opened and he said, a bit quieter, “Screw it, I’m going old school.”

Maddie hit a button on the computer and music started playing. She went into the kitchen, where Doug had finally found a corkscrew to open the bottle of champagne in his hands.

“I made a mix, some of your favorites,” she told him, because her goal for the day was to make sure his good mood lasted. “Sam Cooke, Marvin Gaye.”

“Nice. Greatest love songs ever written, and every one of them-” Doug’s arm flexed as he popped the cork out of the bottle. “-reminds me of you.”

“You’re sweet.” He was always talking to Maddie so sweetly, even when he… Wasn’t being sweet.

“I am, it would appear, the heart surgeon that stole your heart.”

“Not just surgeon.” Keep him happy. Compliment him, make sure he remembers why he’s in such a good mood. Feed it, fuel it, keep it going. “The chief of cardiothoracic medicine. I’m really proud of you.”

“Second youngest in the state, which my father would file under the “Close But Not Quite” Department.” 

The bitterness in Doug’s words raised the hair on the back of Maddie’s neck and sent a thrill of fear down her spine. She hid it by grabbing the wine glasses out of the cabinet. These wine glasses weren’t fancy or overly expensive. They were plain, just simple glass. They weren’t valuable, they weren’t sentimental, so they were safe.

If an accident happened, because she was always causing accidents, Doug wouldn’t be as upset with her if they were lost.

“But screw him, right? We’re celebrating anyway,” Doug said. “Put those Two-Buck Chuck glasses away. An occasion like this requires something more distinctive.”

He reached into one of the other cabinets, higher up, and grabbed a pair of wine glasses from the set they kept in there. Maddie faltered at the sight of them. They were fancy, crystal. Heirlooms. Not safe.

“Wow.” Somehow, she managed to keep her voice from shaking. “We haven’t used those since the wedding.

“Yeah, one of the few things I have left of my mother.” Doug held one of the glasses up to look at it, running his finger around the lip. He looked at her, the false warmth disappearing from his eyes. “Toast softer this time, the chip’s still there.”

At the wedding, she’d been a bit too exuberant, a bit too focused on making a show of how happy she was to be marrying the love of her life. The tiny piece of crystal had made a little clink as it hit the table, as loud as a bomb going off. Doug hadn’t said anything then, not in front of all their guests, not in front of Dan and Evan who were sitting right there with them.

The make-up she wore when they went on their honeymoon had been heavier than when Dan walked her down the aisle.

“This promotion, it’s not because of some good luck glasses,” Doug told her as he poured them both champagne. “It’s because of my good luck girl.”

Maddie’s smile felt more like a grimace as she picked her glass up and clutched it tightly in her hands. When they made their toast, she held her glass perfectly still while Doug clinked his against it.

Doug’s good mood carried them into the back yard, the music still playing. Doug placed his wine glass on the railing of the porch while Maddie clutched to hers, at least until he took it from her hand and set it down on the patio table.

They danced, Maddie somehow able to forget for a moment that she needed to be careful. That Doug’s good moods never lasted. He twirled her, the pair of them laughing, and then he twirled her again. She stumbled into the table.

The glass hit the ground and shattered.

Maddie’s stomach plummeted.

Slowly, aware of the danger she was in, she turned to face Doug. He wasn’t laughing anymore.

“Accidents happen, right?” The edge in his voice had Maddie’s breath catching in her throat. “My mother always used to say that an accident is just an opportunity to learn.”

Maybe it was okay. Maybe the good mood he was in before, his joy over the promotion, maybe it would keep him from being too angry with her this time.

“The problem, Maddie, is that you never do.”

She saw the hit coming, and knew better than to try and avoid it.

Now

The palm striking against her face had Maddie cringing against the passenger door. There was a trickle of blood running out of her nose and over her lips. It mixed with the tears that would not stop. She tasted salt and copper.

“Damn it, Maddie!” Doug snapped, both hands on the wheel again. Maddie watched them out of the corner of her eye, for when one came her way again. Not that trying to avoid the hits had ever done anything except make them land harder. “This is your fault. I told you, I warned you, that you couldn’t leave me.”

“Is he dead?’ The question came out as a sob. Doug hadn’t let her check on Chimney when he dragged her to the car. He’d been laying, unconscious and still, in a pool of his own blood.

“If he’s not, he soon will be. He’s not our problem anymore.”

“Why? Why would you do that? He’s- He’s kind, he’s good.”

“You think I don’t know that? I didn’t want to hurt him,” Doug told her. “I liked Chimney, we were friends.”

Maddie slowly turned her head to look at him, horror filling her veins. He knew Chimney’s name. He knew his name, he said they were friends.

“You cloud me, Maddie, you fog up my head. Always have. If you’d have just stayed…”

“Doug, how long have you been here?” she asked, though she was terrified to hear the answer. She’d been free. She had been free. Hadn’t she?

“Evan Buckley, the County Fair Spiderman,” Doug mocked. “Went viral because he climbed a rollercoaster and saved almost everyone. Can’t forget about the poor kid he let die. I always told you he wouldn’t amount to much.”

“Doug…”

“I needed to make sure you were safe,” he said. “I saw you with Buck and with Dan- Dan, who was always trying to get you to leave me, so I bet he was real happy. But I thought, alright, fine. She needs time, she needs space. She’ll come home. She always comes home. But then Chimney canceled our pool game because he was going on a date with you, and I knew that I waited too long.”

“No.” Maddie shook her head, for all the good that denial had ever done her. “No. Can you just pull over? We can talk, it’s not too late.”

“Of course it’s too late!” Doug snapped. She flinched. “A man is dead because of us, Maddie. Because of you. There is no coming back from that.”

Maddie’s apartment was no longer empty. There were cops and crime scene investigators scouring the inside of it, as well as the walkway and the courtyard of the building. They had arrived as the paramedics were loading Chimney into the ambulance, shortly followed by Athena and Bobby. The captain and the sergeant were flanking Buck now, as he stood on the back porch and tried to get the detective to listen.

“It was Doug,” he insisted. “Doug Kendall. He told Maddie he would kill her if she left him, he did this.”

“You said he lives in Pennsylvania,” the detective said, looking back through the notes he’d taken. “He knows she was here?”

“No, she- she…”

“Yes,” Athena said. Buck stared at her, bewildered. “We assume he does, anyway. He’s been MIA since September, after he assaulted her former boss.”

What was she talking about?

“We’ll take a look at him,” the detective said. “But before he lost consciousness, your friend said he was attacked by a Jason Bailey. Either of you know who that is?”

Buck looked at Athena, and then at Bobby, because he sure didn’t. The captain shook his head.

“I don’t think so,” Bobby answered. 

The detective nodded and turned to go back inside. Buck chased after him. He barely took note of Bobby and Athena following.

“Wait, wait,” he begged, hounding the detective as he went through the apartment towards the courtyard. “He had a new friend, some guy he was, uh. They played pool. I never met him, but now I know why. ‘Cause it was Doug, he was getting close to Chimney so he could get close to Maddie.”

The detective didn’t say as they reached the courtyard. Buck looked behind him to Athena and Bobby, hoping for support. He only got more silence.

“You don’t believe me?” Buck asked, knowing the answer already. “Chimney’s phone is right there! Check for yourself.”

It was sitting on the ground where it had been dropped, a yellow triangular marker emblazoned with a giant 1 right beside it.

“Phone’s locked,” the detective said, rubbing at his forehead like he had any right to be getting frustrated. “Can’t open it without the victim’s consent.”

“They’ll subpoena his phone records,” Athena told him. “But that’ll take time.”

“Maddie doesn’t have time!” Buck snapped at her. “Come on, Doug did this! He almost killed Chimney, and he… He could kill her next.”

“But he didn’t,” Bobby said. Buck threw up his hands and turned away. The captain stepped forward and gripped his shoulder, forced Buck to look at him. “Just listen to me, okay? Maybe you’re right and it is Doug. He found out where Maddie was and he came here and hurt Chimney, but Maddie’s not here. If all he wanted to do was kill her…”

“Then I would have found her, too.”

“But you didn’t. She was alive when he took her, and that’s what we’re gonna hold onto right now.”

He wasn’t sure how that was supposed to make him feel better. No, he did know. It was supposed to reassure him that she was alive, that there was hope of rescuing her. But what good did that do when they weren’t even listening to him about Doug? What good did it do that she was still alive when she was trapped with Doug again, beyond Buck’s reach?

“Did you know, too?” he blurted out. “That Doug was missing, that he might’ve followed her here. Did you know? Did Danny?”

Bobby frowned, that whole-face frown that he did, and shook his head. Behind him, Athena simply pursed her lips and did not answer. Which meant that Danny absolutely knew. And he hadn’t told Buck.

Well, fine. Buck could just add that to the list of things he had to be pissed off about today. It sat right below Maddie being taken, competing for second place with the way the people around him didn’t seem to be listening. They were toeing the line, keeping to the rules, refusing to use what was right in front of them, and all the while Doug was taking Maddie farther and farther away. Because maybe Doug wasn’t here to kill Maddie, like he’d threatened, but him taking her alive was hardly any better. Not if the cops weren’t going to actually do anything to save her.

Chimney’s phone sat on the ground, taunting him. Danny would probably be upset with him, but Buck didn’t exactly care right then.

“I, uh. I have to call Danny,” Buck said. “My- My phone, I think I left it inside, after I called Nine-One-One. Can I go get it?”

The detective rolled his eyes and made a shooing motion at him.

“Just don’t touch anything else in there.”

Buck nodded and went as if he was heading into the apartment. Instead, though, he stepped around the corner of the entryway and waited. The moment he was sure no one was watching, he hurriedly snatched Chimney’s phone off the ground and stuffed it into the pocket of his pants. Then he made a show of coming back, as loudly and as obviously as he could.

“I’m gonna head to the hospital,” he announced. “I- I want to see Chimney.”

“I’ll drive you,” Bobby said. Buck opened his mouth to argue, but then just nodded in agreement. He probably wasn’t in the state to drive safely.

Calling Danny along the way and telling him was one of the hardest things Buck had ever done. Even if Buck was angry at Danny for not telling him about Doug, the way his big brother’s voice broke at the news had him wiping at his own face.

At the hospital, Buck managed to give Bobby the slip when the captain stopped to update Hen and Eddie. Chimney was still in the ER, on a ventilator while they pumped him full of blood until he was stable enough for surgery. That was good, because things could have gotten very awkward if Buck had to talk his way into an operating theater. 

He hovered at the edges once again, waiting until the nurse doing her rounds moved past. Then he darted forward and through the curtains that surrounded Chinmney’s bed.

“I’d say I’m sorry, but I’m not,” Buck told him, pulling out the phone. He grabbed the man’s hand. And then he froze when the nurse suddenly came back.

“Sir,” she said, “you’re not supposed to be in here.”

Well. He had always been told that he was an overdramatic little shit.

“Oh, oh, God, please don’t let him die!” Buck cried out, throwing himself on top of Chimney and conveniently covering the phone from sight as he pressed Chimney’s thumb to it. He ignored the nurse tugging at him, begging God as loud as he could, until he heard the faint click that meant it was done.

“Sir, you have to go,” the nurse told him.

“Okay, okay, I’m going,” Buck said, pushing himself up. As soon as he was in the clear, he pulled up Chimney’s contact.

Except he wasn’t in the clear.

“You know, Detective Marks didn’t believe me.” That was Athena. With two uniform officers. And she did not look happy. “He said no one could be that stupid. I told him he didn’t know you.”

Well. Fuck.

“He took her.”

There had been moments before in Daniel’s life, horrible moments, where he was left with the cold realization that he’d messed up. When they first moved to Texas and he realized he hadn’t been anywhere near ready for it. When Maddie stopped answering his calls after he reported Doug. When Buck asked why it mattered if he’d wanted to have sex with that therapist or not.

This, right now, was simply the newest in a conga line of moments where he was forced to accept that he had, once again, fucked up.

Daniel rushed through the hospital hallways, searching for where they were keeping his brother. Buck had gotten himself put into hospital jail, something Daniel would make fun of him for later. Much, much later. He just wanted to find his baby brother, lay eyes on him and reassure himself that at least one of his siblings was okay.

He found him in a hallway just outside the ER, sitting on a bench with Eddie. There were uniformed officers by the exits; Athena and Nash were huddled a bit away from him, the former with her phone pressed to her ear.

“Buck!” Daniel cried, running to him. His brother and Eddie both hurried to their feet. Daniel pulled to a stop when he saw Buck’s shirt. “Is- Is that-?”

Buck glanced down at the blood on the front of his shirt and then frantically shook his head.

“No, no, this is Chimney’s.”

Daniel let out a relieved sigh, but he still wanted to make sure Buck was okay. He reached forward to pull his brother into a hug and pat him down to check for any injuries. But the moment he did, Buck stepped away from him , almost bumping into Eddie.

“Buck…?”

“Did you know?” Buck asked him, voice quiet and shaking. His eyes were red rimmed and his face tear-stained.  “You did, didn’t you?”

“Know what?”

“That he was here!” his brother snapped. “That Doug followed her here!”

Daniel’s voice caught in his throat when he opened his mouth to answer. He ducked his head, unable to meet Buck’s eyes.

“I didn’t know,” he whispered. “Not… Not until you told me you saw him.”

“You said Maddie was safe!”

“I- I tried,” Daniel told him. “I went to Athena as soon as Maddie told us about Doug. And I went to her again when you told me you’d seen him here. I promise you, Buck, I tried.”

“Why didn’t you tell me!?”

“I was…” Daniel shook his head and shrugged. “I was trying to protect you.”

Buck glared. And though he didn’t say anything, the words “just like Mom” hung in the air between them all the same. An unspoken accusation that Daniel could not defend himself against.

“I spoke to Detective Marks,” Athena said, coming to stand beside them. Nash was right at her side. “He’s not happy with you.”

This time, it was Buck’s turn to duck his head as he stammered out,

“I- I just…”

“You just broke chain of custody,” the sergeant said. “You unlocked Chimney’s phone without his permission. Marks can’t use any of it now.”

“I’m sorry, okay? I was trying to help.”

“I know,” Athena said. “Let’s go, the car’s out front.”

Daniel frowned, instinctively moving to put himself between the two. The sergeant just rolled her eyes.

“I’m not arresting him,” she said. “We’re going to find your sister.”

“No,” Daniel immediately said, before Buck even had the chance to open his mouth. “Absolutely not.”

“Danny-”

“I said no, Evan.” He reached up to hold his baby brother’s face between his palms, rubbed his thumb against the birthmark he thought had looked so neat when he first saw it. “You are going to stay right here, do you understand me? Doug has Maddie, and I can’t- I can’t worry about her and worry about you. I can’t. So you are going to stay right here, where I know at least one of you is safe. Please, please, stay here where I know you’re safe.”

His voice cracked on the words at the end. Because he was holding onto his cool by his fingertips, and he was only managing that much because he knew where Buck was and that he was alright. Taking him on a hunt for their sister and Doug, when the man had already left one person for dead? No. If he was on shaky ground now, just worrying about Maddie, he didn’t want to know what he’d do if something happened to Buck. If he had to watch something happen to Buck.

“Okay,” Buck whispered. “I’ll stay.”

“Thank you.” Daniel pulled his head close to bury a kiss into his hair. “I’ll bring her back, I promise. I’ll bring her back. And I’ll make sure Doug can’t ever hurt her again.”

The last part was whispered so quietly that he doubted even Buck heard him. Daniel glared at Eddie over his brother’s bowed head, until the man nodded to the unspoken demand, and then he let his brother go.

“Whichever one of you is coming,” Athena said, interrupting their moment, “let’s go.”

She barely waited before marching through the double doors for the exit. Daniel hurried after her only after he was sure Buck wasn’t going to try and follow them anyway.

“Where are we going?” he asked her.

“Your brother thinks Chimney’s new friend, Jason, was actually Doug,” Athena explained. She held up the phone she was holding, which wasn’t the one she’d been using when he walked in. “If he is, then we can use this phone to find him. With a little help.”

“You said they couldn’t use the phone.”

“They can’t,” she said with a smirk. “This isn’t my case, I’m just a concerned friend of the victim.”

“Gonna be faster than doing it the official way,” Daniel remarked. “Is it wrong that I’m a little bit proud of him?”

“Yes.”

“Daniel.” That was Nash. Daniel turned to see that the captain had followed them, the double doors still flapping as they closed. “Here are the ground rules-”

“No,” Daniel said, cutting him off, and then told Athena, “I need to grab something from my car.”

Athena frowned, but didn’t argue. In fact, she even drove him to where he’d parked. And though she looked very, very regretful when she saw just what he pulled out, she still let him tuck it away into her own back floorboard.

Chapter 25

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Now

They’d redone the dispatch floor since Daniel had last visited. They had literally redone the floor. Along with the walls, and the ceiling, and… Pretty much everything had been redone. Sue, Abby’s former supervisor, met him and Athena in the lobby and rode up with them in the elevator.

“I’m not sure how we can help, Sergeant,” she said after Athena explained why they were there. “But whatever you need.”

“Athena, please. I’m off duty.”

Sue led them across the floor to one of the dispatchers wearing blue, sitting at his station and focused on his screen.

“This is Josh,” she said. At her words, his head jerked up to look at them before he got to his feet. “Josh, this is Athena, and Daniel, Maddie’s brother.”

“Any word on her?” Josh asked after they’d shaken hands. Daniel just shook his head, and watched the man deflate a little.

“Police don’t have much to go on yet,” Athena said. “And-”

“And sitting around, waiting on them wasn’t going to get us anywhere,” Daniel said. Athena glared at him for it. He just shrugged at her.

“I’d like to ask you some questions,” she said, turning her gaze back to Josh. “Unofficially.”

“Sure,” the dispatcher said. “What do you want to know?”

“When people dial Nine-One-One, you can see where they’re calling from, right?”

“If they’re falling from a landline, absolutely. Cell phones are trickier,” Josh told her. “We don’t get an automatic address.”

“Our system has to digitally request the phone’s location from the cellular network,” Sue explained.

Athena nodded and pulled out a phone, the screen lighting up to show the contact details for someone.

“This is Chimney’s phone,” she said, holding it up. “We think he was in contact with his attacker.”

“Maddie’s husband,” Sue guessed. Daniel nodded.

“According to Buck, Chimney just thought it was some guy named Jason,” he said. Something that could’ve been prevented if they’d had a picture of the asshole to show everyone. Daniel wasn’t sure he would ever stop kicking himself for failing there.

“Now, I’m wondering, hypothetically,” Athena said. “If this number called Nine-One-One and hung up, is your system able to request location data after the call disconnected?”

“Hypothetically, sure,” Sure said. “But we’d-”

“Of course we could.” Josh cut her off, taking the phone from Athena. “You know, it’s funny. I’m pretty sure I took an emergency call from this number earlier.”

Oh, Daniel liked this guy.

“Are you sure?” Sue pressed. The question, Daniel knew, wasn’t “was Josh sure he took a call from that number?” No, the question was, “was Josh sure he wanted to risk his job, if not more, on a lie like this?”

“I’m sure.”

“Okay. Then let’s make a manual request.”

Josh did not waste any time before throwing himself back into his chair and typing in the information.

Afterwards, when they had a general idea of where the phone was, they thanked Josh and Sue before hurrying back to Athena’s car. Daniel texted Buck an update, because he knew it was killing his baby brother not to know. And also, maybe, a little bit because he was feeling guilty about keeping secrets.

As they drove, Athena called up the detective on Maddie’s case,and informed him that he would soon be getting a call from Sue with the lead. She also encouraged him to consider it an anonymous tip and to not look a gift horse in the mouth. When he agreed to meet them both there, she hung up.

“You’re quiet over there,” she remarked, glancing at him.

“Thinking,” Daniel said, tapping his phone against his knee. Buck hadn’t responded. He wasn’t used to utter silence from his brother. “Or, trying not to think. Trying not to imagine what we might find when we get there.”

“Don’t count your sister out just yet.”

“She was my best friend, growing up,” he said, turning his head to look out the window. “There’s only about a year between us, so we pretty much did everything together. Even after I got sick, after Buck was born, we were still close. We practically raised the little brat together.”

“You did a good job,” Athena said. “Even if he is an idiot that occasionally steals evidence from crime scenes.”

“This is my fault.” Through the reflection in the window he could see the way the sergeant gave him a quick and dirty scowl. “I chose Buck over her.”

“Explain.”

“When I first found out about… This. About what Doug was actually like, beyond just being a dick. I made a choice. And I chose Buck over her.” When Daniel looked, it was clear that Athena was not content with that explanation. He knew, though, that she probably would not appreciate what he meant. “When I first found out, I told you I went to the cops. But what I wanted to do was kill him. To beat him to death with my own hands.”

“Understandable,” the sergeant said with a slow nod.

“But it was too risky. I went to the cops because I didn’t know if I could get away with it. If I went to jail, then I couldn’t be there for Buck. But if I’d just… Maddie would be safe. Maybe she’d hate me, maybe I’d be rotting in a cell. But she’d be safe.”

Daniel looked out the window again.

“I chose Buck over Maddie,” he said. “So this is my fault.”

“Are those Chimney’s parents?”

Buck looked up at Eddie’s question, to see who he was talking about.

“Oh, no. That’s Anne and John Lee.”

The couple was with the others, listening to Hen regale everyone with the story of Chimney’s first time babysitting her and Karen’s son, Denny, when he was an infant. Buck would have been with them, but he wasn’t feeling like good company at the moment. So instead, he kept to himself on the edge of the room, alone, while he worried and fretted and dreaded what Danny might find.

Well, he would have been alone. Except Austin Jones, quiet and so easy to miss, was only ever a few seats away. And Eddie had attached himself to Buck’s side after finding him in “hospital jail” and hadn’t moved more than five feet away since, something that had Hen shooting him occasional (and meaningful) looks, except for when he ran to his truck to grab Buck one of his spare work tees to change into.

The one Buck had been wearing, with Chimney’s blood staining the front, was bagged and tagged and probably put into evidence.

“Where are his parents, then?” Eddie asked.

“Uh, his dad’s all the way in Korea, I think,” Buck answered. “His mom’s dead. That’s where the Lees came in, they took him in.”

“Have you ever met his dad?”

“Nope. Not sure the guy even bothered to pick up the phone when Chimney got that rebar through his head.”

Buck had been playing with his phone constantly, twisting it around and around. Usually, the anxiety running through him now would have him looking up anything and everything in between irritating Danny over texts. Except his phone’s battery was not infinite, and his spare charger was in his jeep. Which was, in turn, back at Maddie’s apartment.

Now, his phone let out a chime and he hurried to check it.

“Anything?” Eddie asked, staring at him expectantly. 

Even Austin, who’d been skimming through out-of-date magazines for the past hour, looked up in interest. Buck wondered what Danny had told him before running off with Athena, or if Austin had been left as much in the dark as he had been.

“They, uh. They think they got a lead on Doug’s location,” Buck told them both. “One of Maddie’s coworkers “remembered” the number from an earlier call and helped them.”

He didn’t know who Josh was, aside from what Maddie had told him and Danny, but Buck already decided the guy was gonna be his newest friend.

“That’s good,” Eddie said. His hand hovered over Buck, like he wasn’t sure where to put it, before finally landing on his knee to give it a squeeze. “They’ll find them both.”

“Yeah,” Buck whispered, leaning his head back against the wall. “Yeah. I guess I should just… Be thankful he’s telling me what’s going on. It’s more than what he was doing before.”

“You know, I don’t think I’ve ever seen you yell at Danny like that,” Eddie said. “Even when we were kids, the two of you never fought. Not even when he was threatening to ground us for skipping school.”

Eddie must’ve forgotten that Austin was there, as Buck had done so many times in the past. The two of them didn’t talk about before around the others, not at the station, not in the trucks. Hell, they barely talked about it when it was just them.

The hand on Buck’s knee hadn’t moved, either.

Austin himself was sitting very still, though Buck caught his eyes darting their way. How much gossip had the man managed to gather, just by being quiet and easily forgotten?

“He knew Doug was here,” Buck said. He dropped his phone in his lap and stuffed his hands into his pockets. His leg, the one that didn’t have Eddie’s hand on it, started to bounce. “He knew Doug had followed Maddie here. And he didn’t say anything.”

“He told Athena.”

“He didn’t tell me. I saw Doug before Christmas, but I just brushed it off because I didn’t know. I told myself it was just someone that looked like him.”

“So what would you have done?” Eddie asked him. “If you had known?”

Buck shrugged, because he honestly didn’t know. He could understand not telling Maddie, because it took so much to convince her to stay in the first place. If she’d known Doug was in the city,  Buck knew she would’ve run again. And then when Doug caught up to her, no one would even know.

It was what Danny and Maddie had been arguing about, when he came home from the station to them fighting. Maddie was packed and ready to leave, and Danny wanted her to stay. Or, no, when Buck walked in, Maddie had been accusing Danny of being happy that he was proven right. And Danny, in turn, had said that the only thing he was happy about was that she finally left Doug.

Eight years later, but better than never.

Buck lifted his head off the wall, to stare blankly into the space in front of him.

“I think…” The words were lead on his tongue. “I think Danny knew about Doug.”

“That’s what the fight was about,” Eddie said, face scrunched up in confusion.

“No, no.” Buck shook his head. “I mean…”

He trailed off. Eddie sat there, patiently waiting for him to finish the thought. Except Buck couldn’t bring himself to say the words. Realizing that Danny might have known about Doug for more than eight years left him feeling numb.

Numb, and yet still anxiously worried about Maddie.

“Hey, hey!” a man said as he walked into the waiting room, loaded down with bags. Buck didn’t recognize him, but he did recognize Harry trailing behind him with a box in his hand. That, plus the way Hen got up to greet him, told Buck who it was.

“That’s Michael,” he told Eddie as they got up to go over. “He’s Athena’s ex-husband. They got divorced last year.”

“Why?” Eddie asked him.

Buck paused and looked at him. The fact that it was Eddie asking, considering everything with Shannon and Chris, was enough to bring a faint smile to his face.

“They got divorced ‘cause he’s gay and he didn’t want to hide it anymore.”

Eddie blinked at him and let out a quiet little, “Oh.”

Daniel knew, even before Athena brought them to a stop, that they hadn’t caught up to Doug.

The location they got from Josh’s tactical interpretation of the rules led them to a stretch of highway in the desert. There was a livestock trailer pulled over on the shoulder, flanked by cop cars. Daniel was leaping out of the car while it was still moving, and could hear the driver claiming that,

“It’s not my phone. I don’t know where it came from.”

The man he was talking to, dressed in plain clothes so probably Detective Marks, turned when he heard Daniel and Athena making their way to him.

“Who’s this?” was the first thing he asked.

“The other brother,” Athena explained. The detective rolled his eyes.

“Whatever. We found the phone,” Marks said, holding it up for them to see. “No sign of our suspect or your sister.”

“How does Doug’s phone end up in his truck?” Daniel asked.

“He says he hasn’t a clue,” the detective answered. “Left Salt Lake City yesterday, been on the road ever since.”

Pennsylvania might not have been a shining beacon when it came to its public schools, but it didn’t have to be for Daniel to be smart enough to realize that the guy clearly wasn’t telling them everything. Actually, the school district Daniel graduated from was actually pretty good. But still, the point was, it didn’t take a well-educated genius to figure out that things weren’t adding up.

Athena was clearly in agreement, because she was stepping around the - clearly not a well-educated genius - detective to ask the truck driver,

“Sir, did you make any stops?”

“Gas station,” the driver answered. “Just before sunrise.”

“Did you see anybody else there?”

“I saw a couple going into the store when I was leaving.”

Daniel closed his eyes and breathed through his nose. A couple going into the store. He saw Maddie going into the store. She was alive. His sister was alive.

“He knew we could use the phone to track him,” Athena said, turning around to speak to both Daniel and the detective. “Probably dropped it in the truck to throw us off.”

Doug was an abusive asshole and a smug bastard, but he wasn’t stupid. They were stupid for not thinking of this as a possibility. Not that it would’ve changed much.

Resisting the urge to punch the side of the truck, he turned on his heel and started back for Athena’s car. The sergeant herself lingered long enough to get the information of which gas station that the driver stopped at, and then she was quickly slipping into the driver’s seat. Neither of them spoke as she made a U-turn, heading back the way they came.

Chapter End Notes

There was no Maddie POV in this chapter because there's limit to how much domestic abuse-related misery I can make myself write.

Chapter 26

Chapter Notes

content warning for canon typical violence

Now

It was amazing how quick a kid with crutches could be, and how stealthy he could move. Shannon swung by the waiting room to visit, and having to interact with her for any length of time was always awkward for Buck. At least on this occasion she brought pizza and Christopher.

The hungry masses fell upon the boxes of pizza like a pestilence. Buck was worried someone would end up losing an arm, and half-expected it to be himself when he reached in to snag a couple slices. He handed one to Eddie, purposefully not looking at Shannon as he did so. He was being so dutiful about tuning out her and Eddie’s conversation, actually, that he completely missed it when Christopher slipped away.

So did they, at least, because they looked very startled when he broke in to ask,

“Hey, where’s Chris?”

Buck watched the pair of them glance about, appearing increasingly worried. At least until Austin spoke up around his own slice of pizza, pointing down the hall and saying,

“Pretty sure I saw him go that way.”

Eddie and Shannon turned in that direction and then shared a look of frustration, before Eddie was hurrying off. Buck was quick behind him. The fact that they were heading for Chimney’s room, where Bobby reported he’d been taken to recover and wake up from surgery, was probably not a coincidence.

When they got there, they found the kid sitting next to the bed and looking happy as a clam. Bobby was there, as well.

“Chris!” Eddie hissed quietly, clearly trying not to disturb Chimney.

“So he didn’t know you’re here,” Bobby said, sounding amused.

It became clear quickly that Eddie hadn’t needed to be quiet, because Chimney was wide awake. Well, maybe not wide awake, the guy appeared to be more than a bit groggy. But he was awake. Buck grinned at the sight, feeling relieved.

“Hey, man,” he said, coming to stand at the foot of the hospital bed.

“Welcome back.” Eddie went to Chris, leaning over the kid’s chair. “We were worried about you.”

“I wasn’t worried,” Bobby lied. “I’m starting to think he’s invincible.”

“Like a superhero?” Chris asked.

“Just like a superhero.”

“Okay, buddy, let’s let Chimney rest,” Eddie said, helping Chris stand up. “We can visit him later.”

“Don’t eat the meatloaf,” was the kid’s stern instructions to Chimney. “That’s how I lost a tooth.”

Chimney smiled faintly.

“Thanks for the tip, buddy.”

Eddie led Chris out of the room, hand once again hovering over the kid’s back. Buck watched them go, but didn’t move to follow them. Once they were gone, Chimney rolled his head to face Bobby and asked,

“Where’s Maddie?”

Bobby didn’t answer. He looked at Buck instead, who let out a sigh and collapsed into the chair Chris had been sitting in. Then the captain gave Chimney a pat on the shoulder and stepped out of the room, closing the door behind him.

“Buck.” Chimney rolled his head to stare at him. “Where’s Maddie?”

“We…” Buck wet his lips. “We don’t know.”

“You don’t know,” the man dully echoed. “Jason took her.”

“We think… We think that Jason was Doug.”

“Oh, God.” The monitor tracking Chimney’s vitals let out a warning beep as his heart rate ticked up.

“Hey, hey.” Buck reached over and grabbed his wrist, giving it a squeeze. “Danny went with Athena to find them. He promised to bring her back.”

“And you…” Chimney paused to swallow; his mouth was probably dry. “You didn’t go with them.”

Buck pulled his hand back and shrugged.

“Danny told me to stay.” He was fairly certain Danny had been about to cry as he begged him to stay. Buck couldn’t remember the last time he’d seen his big brother cry. Or if he’d ever seen him cry. “Oh, uh. I stole your phone.”

“You stole my phone.”

“And I, um. Used your thumb to unlock it while you were unconscious?” Buck ducked his head in embarrassment, rubbing at the back of his neck. “Sorry about that.”

“Why?” Chimney quietly asked.

“‘Cause you had Doug’s number on there, and I knew they could track it,” he explained. “But the cops were following all those rules, y’know? And it was going to take too long before they could help Maddie. So… I took your phone.”

“Did it help?”

“Yeah.”

“Then I think I can forgive you,” Chimney said, laughing a little.

“Hey, um.” Buck’s knee bounced a little. “The Lees are here, outside with everyone else. Do you want me to…?”

“Actually, do you mind sending Hen in here first?”

“Yeah, sure.”

Buck got up to go tell her she was being asked for. When he paused in the doorway and looked back, Chimney was silently staring at the ceiling.

Then

Evan did not like his sister’s boyfriend.

Maddie talked about him constantly, it felt like. How cute he was, how smart he was, how cute and smart he thought she was. Evan caught her on the house phone, once, gushing to her friends about making out with him. He didn’t understand what was so great about making out, it looked gross.

Anyways, Maddie talked about the guy a lot, and so their parents insisted he come over for dinner. Evan hated when they had guests over for dinner, because Mom and Dad fussed at him about behaving even more than usual. Usually they didn’t care, too used to him to do more than just tut disapprovingly. But when guests were over, not even hiding behind Danny helped him avoid being snapped at for moving too much or talking too much.

Doug did that, too. He didn’t snap at him, like Mom and Dad did. He would just look at Evan and say something like,

“Do you even breathe?”

Or,

“Didn’t you ever learn to sit still?”

Or,

“I guess you don’t have an indoor voice.”

Or, once, when no one else was paying attention, not even Danny,

“My father would smack me if I acted like you.”

And they didn’t sound mean when Doug said them, but they felt mean. Danny must’ve thought they were mean, too, because he would glare and snap at Doug, and that would upset Maddie. And the comments had Evan shrinking in on himself and not knowing why. It never lasted, though, because he could never stay quiet or still for long.

Dinner itself was awful, because instead of sitting between Danny and Maddie, like he always did, he ended up between Danny and Doug. Doug, who glared every time Evan accidentally kicked his chair and made another mean comment whenever Evan talked too long or too loudly. But mostly Doug was busy talking to Mom and Dad, so Evan just tucked himself into Danny’s side and ate his food as quickly as he could, in the hopes that he could be excused.

After dinner, and after Doug left, Maddie had a fight with Mom and Dad. Because they didn’t like Doug, either. They thought he was too old, not that Evan understood why that mattered. Doug sucked, but he wasn’t as old as Mom and Dad were, or even Evan’s teacher. But they were fighting, and they were getting loud, so he ran for Danny’s room.

Danny was reading on his bed when Evan crept inside. Danny patted the spot next to him and Evan hurried over, clambering up to sit beside him.

“What’re you reading?” Evan asked, craning his head to look. It didn’t look like it had any pictures in it, so it must’ve been boring.

“The Hobbit,” Danny answered. “Are you hiding from the fighting?”

“Yeah. Mom and Dad don’t like Doug,” he told him. “I don’t like him either. He’s a dickhead.”

“You’re seven!” Danny yelped. “Where did you learn that word?”

“School,” Evan cheerfully answered.

“It’s a bad word, don’t say it again,” his brother said. “And he is a dickhead.”

Evan giggled and laid down on Danny’s pillow. 

“Why does she like him?” he asked.

“I don’t know,” Danny answered. “Girls are weird.”

“Girls are gross.”

His brother laughed at him.

“I’ll remind you that you said that,” he said. “Don’t worry, she’ll forget about him.”

“I hope so,” Evan grumbled. “I hope he goes away forever.”

“Amen,” Danny agreed, and turned the page in the book.

Now

Daniel and Athena were not the first ones at the gas station. Someone had found the clerk dead outside the store, and now sheriff deputies were unrolling yellow tape around the entire area. There was security footage that showed parts of what happened. Daniel ached at the sight of his sister being dragged around by the bastard.

“We only have eyes on the pumps and the registers,” Detective Marks told them. “Best we can guess, she goes out the back window, husband catches her.”

“Yeah, cashier sees something wrong, he goes to help,” Athena said, pausing the footage of the man pulling a revolver out from under the register. “Winds up getting shot with his own gun.”

“Guess he thought he could handle it on his own.”

“Sir.” A sheriff’s deputy appeared at Marks’ side, holding out some papers. “Found this in the bathroom.”

Marks took them and thumbed through.

“It’s a rental car agreement,” he said. “For a dark gray, four-door sedan, rented near Burbank Airport. Looks like she started to write ‘help’ on the back. I’ll put out an APB.”

God, Daniel loved his big sister. She had just given them so much. Terrified and probably convinced no one was coming, she was still giving them exactly what they needed to find her.

“We know where he rented the car,” Athena said, taking the rental agreement from the detective to look through herself. “Maybe we can use that to track him.”

“Rental car companies don’t use GPS tracking,” Marks said. “It’s too expensive.”

“For a company that can afford the insurance to cover it if something happens,” Daniel pointed out.

“And if you’re Bob with only twelve cars in your fleet,” Athena said, “you might want to know where they are at all times.”

Marks nodded in agreement, taking the rental agreement back and pulling out his phone. He stepped out of the store, already talking to someone. In the meantime, Daniel started the security footage over, paused it, started it again.

He watched his sister - alive, alive, alive - as she entered the store and went to the bathroom and was dragged back to the rented car. He watched it once, watched it twice, watched it three times, and was about to go for a fourth when Athena’s finger pressed against the power button for the monitor. The screen went dark, leaving only his reflection.

“That isn’t helping,” she told him.

“She was here,” he said. “She’s alive. She told us how to find them.”

“Marks is on the phone, getting us that GPS location. Let’s get back to the car and on the road.”

“Do we know which direction to even go?” Daniel asked, even as he trailed after her back to where she parked.

“My thought is,” Athena said, “that he’s running scared. He’s not thinking about where to go right now, just that he needs to get space between him and us. Most of the time, people running scared like that go in a straight line for as long as they’re able.”

“So we’re just going to follow the road until that detective actually gives us something.”

“That’s right.”

Well, it beat standing around while they twiddled their thumbs.

Athena pointed the car away from Los Angeles and started driving. Marks apparently had the same idea, or maybe he was just trusting the sergeant’s instincts, because Daniel saw his car not far behind them in the side view mirror. Daniel himself texted Buck an update - that Maddie was alive, that he’d seen her, that they had a better way of finding her - and then spent the rest of the ride in silence.

Eventually, after far too long, Marks called them. The rental place did use GPS for their cars, and they did have the current location of the one rented under Doug’s name. The road they were on didn’t take them there, so Athena was wrong about that. But there was an exit for Big Bear just ahead, probably the exact one that Doug had taken.

By the time they arrived at Big Bear, the local cops had located the sedan. They didn’t find Doug, nor did they find Maddie. There was a rental office for cabins not far from where it was found, and they met Marks outside.

“We found the kidnapper’s abandoned vehicle nearby, so we’re looking for a couple,” the detective explained to the rental manager. “They may have come in earlier, looking for a last-minute rental.”

“No, it’s been quiet today,” the lady said, shaking her head. “Nobody’s come in.”

Athena held up a brochure from the display stand by the door.

“Are these all the properties that you manage?” she asked.

“Those are just the ones we highlight,” the manager replied. “There’s a magazine inside, it has all the listings.”

“How many cabins are there?” Daniel asked her, huddling under his jacket as best he could. There was fucking snow on the ground. More snow than he’d seen since the last Christmas he spent in Hershey. He was dressed for late winter by the coast in Los Angeles. He was not at all prepared to be trekking across a snow capped mountain.

Back in the day, he wouldn’t have been bothered by this sort of weather at all. Living in Texas, and then southern California, had ruined him.

“Three-hundred.”

He and Athena shared a look, because that was a lot. Three-hundred cabins were too many for them to go door-to-door.

“Do you have a map?” he asked. “Of where all the cabins are?”

“The magazine has a map,” the lady said, sounding unsure. “Or at least it shows where they’re at.”

“What are you thinking?” Athena asked him.

“I’m thinking that Doug abandoned the car,” he said. “So they’re going through a snowy forest on foot, and there’s no way either of them came dressed for snow, either. And I’m also thinking that Doug’s not going to risk stopping at a cabin that’s obviously occupied.”

“The lake’s just a short hike away,” the manager said. “And all the cabins there are vacant.”

Daniel didn’t even look Athena’s way before hurrying back to the car. She was opening the driver’s side door just as he threw himself into the passenger seat, and they were soon taking off once again. Unfortunately, while the lake was a short hike away from the rental office, the road they had to take was not so direct. Marks called them on the way, and told them to go to a specific cabin. For reported gunshots.

The sheriff’s department was already there, and Marks was not far behind.

“Somebody’s been here,” Athena remarked as she looked around the inside of the cabin. The door had been kicked in, the wooden frame splintered where the latch broke. It was hanging wide open when they arrived. “And it looks like they left in a hurry.”

“They were here long enough to start a fire,” Daniel said. At this point in his life, he was intimately familiar with the smell of smoke and of something burning. There was just ash and charred wood in the fireplace now, though. And while a broom, a shovel, and a pair of tongs were hanging from a rack by the hearth, the poker was conspicuously absent.

“Question now is, which way did they go?”

That was a very good question, but Daniel knew they couldn’t have gotten far. He went to Athena’s car, but this time he didn’t climb in. Instead, he opened the backdoor and reached into the floorboard, pulling out the baseball bat he’d thrown in there at the start of this journey.

“And just what do you think you’re going to do with that?” Athena asked him, as if she didn’t already know the answer. 

Marks stepped out onto the porch, walkie in hand and already talking, though he fell silent with a scowl when he saw the bat.

“Well, Sergeant, I’m-” Daniel was saved from fumbling for an excuse by a shout of a deputy, calling out,

“I’ve got blood out here!”

He’d heard people joke about Buck being a Golden Retriever before. Daniel wasn’t sure what kind of dog he’d be compared to, but he felt like one now. He’d immediately turned in the direction the shout had come from and took off in a sprint, easily side-stepping Athena’s hand as she reached out to grab his sleeve.

The only good thing about this snow was how easy it made spotting the blood, once he was looking for it. A splatter of scarlet against white there, another farther away. And there, too, the snow was kicked up ahead of him in places, like how he was leaving it behind him in his rush.

Through a break in the trees ahead, there was a clearing. And in that clearing he saw movement, black against the white of the snow and the greens and browns of the trees. It stuck out like a sore thumb. She stuck out like a sore thumb.

“Maddie!” he screamed. She stopped, and even at a distance he thought she looked shocked to see him.

“Dan!?”

Daniel rushed for her, clumps of snow flying in his wake. He reached her just in time to drop the bat to the ground and catch her before she could join it.

“Where are you hurt?” he asked, lowering them both so they sat on their knees. There was blood all over Maddie, she was practically covered in it. She’d been keeping her hand pressed to her side. When he felt around there, she flinched and his hand came back wet. “Okay, okay, it’ll be okay.”

“I didn’t give up,” Maddie sobbed into his shoulder. “I- I didn’t give up.”

“Of course you didn’t,” he told her, and looked back the way he came to see if anyone had followed him. Athena was right there on a walkie as she stared up at a helicopter flying overhead. “Maddie, I need to see, okay? I need to see where you’re hurt. Athena! Get me a first aid kit!”

“I have someone coming,” the sergeant told him. “Maddie, where’s Doug?”

That was a question that Daniel was wondering, too. His fingers itched to pick up his bat at the mere mention of the man’s name, but no. Maddie needed him. His sister was here and she needed him.

“He’s- He’s back there,” Maddie said, pointing back the way she came. “I… There was a knife. I stabbed him. I think I killed him.”

“Good,” Daniel growled out. He pushed her back a bit so he could look her over. There, in her side, was a stab wound that was steadily bleeding. There was a nasty slice on her thigh, as well. “Athena, where’s- Oh.”

Detective Marks had appeared, along with a pair of deputies. He held out a first aid kit taken from one of the deputy’s cruisers, which Daniel grabbed and began digging through. The deputies hurried in the direction Athena directed them, Daniel biting back the urge to tell them to make sure, but she and the detective both lingered with him and Maddie. By the time an ambulance, called for by Marks, arrived at the cabin, Daniel had put the recent paramedic classes to use. His sister was still pale and hurting, but he didn’t think she was about to bleed out on the forest floor.

His baseball bat was left behind, and he just had to hope that Athena was kind enough to bring it back to him.

“Tired,” Maddie whispered. She was looking concerningly pale on the gurney, and her eyes had slipped closed. Daniel, out of love and affection, pinched her arm. Hard. There might have even been some twisting action. Her eyes snapped open with an expression of outrage. “Ow! Dan!”

The paramedic that was in the back with them appeared very concerned when he raised his head from the paperwork he was filling out.

“No falling asleep,” Daniel told her, reaching into his pants to pull out his phone. “You know the drill. You’ve gotta be awake so you can talk to Buck. And because I’m feeling nice, you can talk to Chimney, too.”

“Chimney?” Maddie blinked, eyes going wet with tears. “Chimney’s alive?”

“Oh, God.” He hadn’t even hadn’t considered for a moment that she would’ve been thinking the worst. “Yeah, he’s fine, he’s awake. Pretty sure Buck missed you guys by minutes.”
