Maddie sniffled and smiled, and then smacked at him.

“Hurry up and call Buck.”

Daniel rolled his eyes, grumbled about older sisters being bossy, and did as she bid.

Buck was pacing the waiting room while he chewed his nails to the quick. Most of the others had gone home, after they got the chance to see for themselves that Chimney was awake and they all knew he would be okay. The only ones remaining were their core little group, him and Eddie and Hen and Bobby. Though Bobby was once again sitting with Chimney, so it was really just Buck in the waiting room with Eddie and Hen.

It had been far too long since he’d gotten an update from Danny. It had been far too long since his brother’s quick text telling him about the security footage of Maddie. He was beginning to regret listening to his brother and staying behind. More than he regretted it at the start, anyway.

His phone’s ringtone was loud in the quiet surrounding him. Eddie and Hen both immediately zeroed in on him at the sound of it, and they watched as he scrambled to get it out of his pocket. The moment he answered it, he was rattling off questions like a machine gun as he paced.

“Danny! Did you find her? Where is she? Is she okay? Is-”

“Evan.”

Buck came to a halt, relief buckling his knees so that he had to lean against the wall.

“Maddie?” The sound of her name had the other two jumping to their feet and coming to stand beside him. “Y- You’re okay?”

“I’m… I’ll be okay.”

“That’s…” Buck reached up, wiped at his face. “Good, I’m glad. I was worried.”

“I’m not sure which hospital they’re taking me to. Dan’ll tell you, probably.”

“I- I’ll come see you as soon as he does,” Buck promised. “Uh. Chimney’s awake, Bobby’s in there with him. Do you, um. Would you like to…?”

“Please.”

The hand holding his phone was shaking. When did that start?

“I’ll bring her to him,” Hen said, gently taking the phone out of his hand. 

Buck smiled gratefully at her, because his knees felt like jelly and he wasn’t sure how he’d make the trip. He watched her go, still using the wall to support his weight, and felt Eddie wrap an arm around him to pull him close.

“She’s okay?” he asked.

“Y- Yeah.” Buck wiped at his face again. “She’ll be okay.”

“Good.”

Buck pressed his face into Eddie’s shoulder, simply breathing and letting himself savor the feeling of relief that coursed through his veins.

Chapter 27

Then

The silence coming from Evan was deafening. He wasn’t answering his phone, he wasn’t responding to texts. Eddie might as well have been screaming into the void, for all the good it did. He knew that the other was angry, knew that he had fucked up. But if Evan would just listen to him, let him explain, let him start begging for forgiveness, maybe…

Eddie knew it wouldn’t magically fix everything. But Evan would understand, he knew that he would. If only he would listen. If only he would give Eddie the chance to explain.

No one knew about Shannon yet. Eddie was putting off that announcement for as long as he could, even though he knew there was a time limit for how long they could hide it. His parents would absolutely notice if he suddenly got married to someone with a baby bump, after all. But for the moment, he had the convenient excuse of waiting until his father got back from his latest work trip.

Less than a week, until he had to weather his parents' anger and disappointment.

After Evan left their birthday party, after Evan fled, Eddie should have chased after him. But Shannon was there, his entire family was there, and they’d want to know why he left his own birthday party to chase after him. So he stayed and fielded the repeated question of “where’s Evan?” with bullshit answers. 

It had been three days since then. Three days of utter silence from his bo- God, from his ex -boyfriend. Three days where Eddie argued with himself again and again if it was better to give Evan space or if he should rush over there.

On the morning of the third day, the day after he was supposed to go on the road trip with Evan, he broke. After yet another sleepless night, he got into the pick-up truck and drove to Danny’s apartment. He didn’t go to church, he didn’t believe like his family did. But he still found himself praying as he climbed the stairs to the right floor, because it very much did feel like he was walking through the valley of the shadow of death.

Eddie stood in front of the door, gathered his courage, and knocked. He kept knocking when no one answered right away, feeling frantic now. Finally, finally, the door was thrown open to reveal an angry looking Danny.

“What,” he snarled, “do you want?”

“Is Evan here?” Eddie asked. “He’s not answering his phone.”

“Gee, I wonder why that would be,” Danny said. “Don’t you have a fiancée to plan a wedding with?”

The words might as well have been a punch for the way they landed.

“Please,” he begged. “I- I need to talk to him, I need to explain.”

“Good luck with that, ‘cause he’s not here.”

“Not here?” Eddie asked. “What do you mean, not here? Where is he?”

“Pretty sure he’s somewhere in Arkansas, right now.”

“He- He left?”

No, that- That wasn’t right. Evan wouldn’t have just left. He wouldn’t, not without at least saying goodbye. He wouldn’t.

“Were you expecting him to stay, Eddie?” Danny asked. “You’re getting married. To someone that isn't him. You expected him to stay here and watch it happen?”

“Danny, please, you don’t understand-”

“And I don’t care.”

The sound of the door slamming shut seemed to fill the entire hall and it left an echo in his ears. Eddie wasn’t sure how long he stayed there, simply staring at nothing as the world crumbled beneath his feet.

Now

“Okay, so every unit is different,” Buck said, scrolling through the listings on his phone. “But I think I kind of like this one the best.”

He held up his phone to show Maddie, sitting across from him. They were at a diner together having breakfast before they both had to go to work. Buck had been looking through apartment listings the entire time, something which his sister was kindly tolerating.

“Nice,” she said. “You should totally rent that one.”

“Me?’ Buck blinked at her, lowering his phone. “Why would I…?”

“Because I have my own apartment,” Maddie said. “You live with our brother. Except for recently, when you’ve been camped out on my couch.”

After Maddie was released to go home, Buck had packed a bag and sat himself down on that couch. Partially because it made him feel better, knowing that his sister wasn’t alone, and partially because… Well, he hadn’t properly spoken to Danny since he learned about Doug. Since he learned that his brother knew Doug was coming to Los Angeles, was in Los Angeles, and hadn’t told him. Since he learned that his brother had known about Doug and about Maddie and hadn’t said a word to him.

They’d interacted, it was impossible for them not to interact with the way they both shadowed Maddie’s hospital room at the beginning, but anything that wasn’t focused on their sister had been shallow at best.

“You don’t think it’s weird?” Buck asked, brushing away those thoughts. He was trying very hard not to think about why he was, technically, fighting with his brother. Even though they weren’t really fighting.

“Yes. That’s why I think you should get your own place,” Maddie told him. Buck sat back in the booth and stared, deadpan, at her, because they both knew what he’d meant with his question. “Okay, fine. It’s a little weird to go back to my apartment, but I’m not just gonna force myself out of it because of him. He’s taken enough, already.”

“Mads, I love you,” he said, something he felt he didn’t say enough to her. “But we have to go through the backdoor now, because neither of us can stand seeing the stain in the walkway.”

When Maddie was first released and Buck brought her to her apartment, they’d started through the front. Only they both spotted the stain of red that no one could quite remove from the brick and came to a stop, unable to not stare at it. It hadn’t been a problem before, Danny had been the one to go over and fetch clean clothes for Maddie, but it was definitely one now.

“Okay, there are… Hurdles,” Maddie hedged. “But they’re getting better. I’m getting better.”

“Better enough to go to work and get on with life like nothing happened,” Buck said. Like she hadn’t been kidnapped by her abusive asshole of a husband, dragged into a forest, and then had to kill the dickhead to escape. All the while believing her current boyfriend had been left to bleed to death. “Not better enough to sleep through the night, though.”

Maddie pursed her lips as she scowled at him.

“And how do you know that?”

“‘Cause I’m awake, too,” he casually confessed. “Your couch is not comfortable to sleep on.”

“No one’s making you sleep on it.” She rolled her eyes. “Go back to Dan’s if you’re that bothered by it.”

“I don’t wanna.”

Maddie narrowed her eyes at him, her sisterly instincts clearly sensing something was afoot.

“Why?”

“We’re not talking about my problems,” Buck said. “We’re talking about yours and how you’re pretending everything’s already fine enough that you can go to work and handle other people’s problems.”

“I am not-” She let out a huff. “I will still see my therapist, I’ll go to my support group, but I have to start putting little pieces of my life back together, and work is a very big piece.”

“It seems like, maybe, you’re moving too fast. You need time to heal.”

“I’ve been healing,” Maddie replied. “For weeks. I am sick of resting, and I am tired of talking about my feelings. I just want to go back to the real world.”

“Even if you’re not ready?”

“Once my life is normal again, then I’ll feel normal again.”

“Maddie,” Buck said, and tried to think of the gentlest way he could phrase this. He came up short, so he just forged ahead. “You killed Doug, because he was trying to kill you. I- I don’t think…”

“I know,” his sister snapped. 

He raised his hands to ward off her anger. She closed her eyes and took a breath,

“I know,” she repeated, a bit calmer. “But I am just… I just want everything to go back to normal. To how it was before he…”

“Okay,” Buck breathed. “So where are you gonna take Chimney on your “second first date”?”

The scare quotes probably weren’t necessary, but he threw them up anyway.

Maddie’s face dropped and she admitted,

“I have no idea.”

Today was going to be a very long day, Buck knew. A fun one, but a long one. See, every once in a while they were the subject of a school field trip. The station would go offline, they’d set up folding chairs in the bay and drag out equipment for demonstrations, and then a hoard of kids would excitedly rush inside.

This time around, it was a class from Meadowbrook, Harry Grant’s school, and it was Harry’s class that was sitting through Bobby’s lecture. Buck and Eddie were flanking the captain, dressed in full turn-outs minus the helmets and gloves. Buck had been chosen because he actually enjoyed doing this kind of thing. Eddie, meanwhile, was simply at the bottom of the station food chain as a probie.

Currently, Bobby was telling the assembled kids what to do and what not to do in the case of an emergency, mainly,

“Don’t panic, because panic can be more dangerous than flames, toxic fumes, or a sucking chest wound.” The captain turned his head to look over his shoulder. “Guys?”

That was their cue. Together, Eddie and Buck wheeled the television-on-wheels forward as Bobby stepped to the side.

“Cap’s a little intense, huh?” Eddie commented under his breath.

Buck chuckled a little and pressed the power button. The screen lit up to show a placeholder image welcoming Meadowbrook School to the station.

“He takes these talks very seriously.”

“All you need are three numbers,” Bobby said. “Can anyone tell me what they are?”

“Nine-One-One!” the entire class chorused.

“Yes! Now, does anybody know what happens when you dial those numbers?” Only one hand shot up. “Harry Grant.”

“They call a fire truck,” the boy cheerfully answered.

“Okay, but what if…”

Buck tuned the captain out a bit, because he’d heard all of this before. He spotted Athena making her way inside. She came to a stop next to Michael, who was helping to chaperone all the kids, and started talking. Buck perked up a bit at the sight of her, because she had become one of his favorite people.

The first time he saw her again after Maddie was found, Buck had barrelled into the sergeant. He was wrapping his arms around her and thanking her repeatedly before his brain caught up with his actions. The only reason he didn’t apologize and flee was because she started hugging him back before he could.

Buck decided he liked Athena’s hugs.

“Now, what if I told you that a big computer brain helps you when you dial Nine-One-One?” Bobby asked the kids while Eddie went over to the laptop connected to the television. Unlike Buck, who simply had to stand there and look pretty while the captain talked, Eddie had to be pretty and functional. “It’s called CAD: Computer Aided Dispatch. Let’s say you, Harry, call in an emergency. Can you name one?”

“The library catches fire and all our homework assignments are burned up.”

“That doesn’t sound like an emergency to me,” Buck said under his breath. It had Eddie stifling a laugh and Bobby shooting him a mock scowl.

“Alright, so when you dial Nine-One-One, the dispatcher types in your address into the computer,” Bobby explained. Eddie put the info into the demo version of CAD that was currently on the screen. “CAD automatically finds your school, then finds the nearest station, and…”

On cue, the station alarms rang.

“Station One Eighteen, Station One Eighteen.” Hen was above them at the loft railing, using a bullhorn. Austin Jones, looking so horribly bored with everything, slid down the firepole. “Please dispatch a fire engine and an ambulance to the Meadowbrook School Library immediately. Harry Grant didn’t do his homework.”

After the laughter died down, Bobby tasked Buck and Eddie with giving them a tour of the trucks. This was Buck’s favorite part. He bounded down the aisle and past the rows of chairs, excitedly getting his half of the group on their feet and chasing after him.

They started at the ladder truck, where he let them clamber through the cab and try on the headsets. He even let them play with the sirens and the horn, because every kid should get the chance to be obnoxious and loud whenever possible. After they were done there, he dragged out a spare set of turn-outs and let the kids try them on.

They were, of course, adorable in the oversized coats and helmets. Not that he would ever tell them that, because he remembered being that age. He would’ve been outraged if someone dared to call him cute. No matter how true it was.

Buck was a bit sad when it was time for them all to go. Hanging out with kids was fun. Hanging out with them while getting to show off how cool his job was, was even better. It was definitely better than having to put everything back in their proper place, at least.

The clean-up was almost always the worst part of anything fun.

“You looked as if you were enjoying yourself,” Eddie said, joining him at the turn-out lockers to put away their coats and such.

“I love kids,” Buck said without an ounce of self-consciousness. “They’re fun.”

Eddie snorted in amusement.

“Clearly. Hey, uh. Shannon and I were gonna take Chris to the Science Center and maybe the Natural History Museum, do you-”

“What the hell are you doing, Eds?” Buck blurted out. He didn’t mean to, honestly. The question popped into his head and then shot itself from his mouth. 

The other man looked taken aback by the question.

“What am I doing?” he asked, sounding a bit unsure. “Um. Asking if you wanted to come with us?”

“Yeah, no, I get that,” Buck said. “I meant- Okay, first you invite me to the zoo with the three of you-”

“Chris wanted you to come with us.”

“Only because you brought it up to him!” he pointed out. “Shannon said it was your idea first! And then you started inviting me to park outings, and now a museum trip, and- Eddie, what are you doing?”

“I…” Eddie blinked, once, twice, three times. “I just figured you’d want to come with us.”

“You’re inviting me out to places with your family.”

“God, you sound just like-” 

Eddie cut himself off so harshly that Buck worried he might have literally bitten his tongue. The other man glanced around them and then grabbed Buck by the shirt sleeve, dragging him to the locker room. It was so similar to what he did with Shannon, just prior to Christmas, that Buck was momentarily thrown.

“You’re sounding just like Shannon,” Eddie said, once he made sure the doors were closed and they wouldn’t be overhead. “Yes, I’m inviting you with us, what’s the problem with that?”

“What’s the problem with…?” Buck shook his head and laughed. “Eddie, what part of- I thought these things were like, part of you and Shannon mending bridges or something. The two of you figuring your shit out for Chris.”

“They are!”

“So what am I doing there?”

“Having fun, I hope,” was the grumbled answer.

“Eddie. You cheated on me,” Buck said, and tried not to feel vindication from how he flinched. “You cheated on me with her. And then you got married to her because you knocked her up. And now you’re… You keep inviting me out with the three of you, like we’re all some ultra-modern family. You, your wife, your son, and your ex-boyfriend.”

“Buck…”

“What do you want?” Buck bluntly asked. “What is it that you want from this?”

He watched Eddie’s mouth open and close, but nothing came out of it. No explanations, no excuses. Just silence. After a moment, Buck nodded and turned on his heel. Eddie did not follow him out of the locker room right away.

He climbed the stairs, spotted Hen sitting by herself at one of the tables, and promptly collapsed into the chair beside her. His head made a thunk when it landed on the table.

“Problem?” the paramedic asked, setting down her phone. She was probably texting with Chimney.

“So many problems,” Buck answered, the words a bit muffled by the pressed wood.

“And which one has you like this? Nevermind, I think I can guess.”

He lifted his head and spotted Eddie at the top of the stairs, giving him a hangdog look. The expression cleared when Eddie realized that Hen was watching him as well, and he hurried over to where the couches were.

“He keeps inviting me out with him and Chris and Shannon,” Buck said, keeping his voice low as he dragged a hand down his face.

“And you keep going.”

“I never claimed to be a genius,” he pointed out. “In fact, all of you have called me an idiot at least once a week since I started here.”

“You know,” she said, picking up her phone again when it chimed. “You never did tell me how it ended.”

“Badly enough that I fled the state of Texas.”

Hen pulled a face and gave a slow nod.

“Listen up everyone!” Bobby shouted, coming to stand at the railing of the loft so everyone could hear him, including anyone downstairs. “I just got off the phone. CAD is down.”

“Down?” Hen asked. “What do you mean, down?”

“I mean it is non-operational,” Bobby said. “They can take calls, but that is all. There is no computer assistance at this moment. So we-”

The alarm sounded and everyone scrambled for the trucks.

“We are in Tactical Emergency Mode!” Bobby shouted above the din. “That means we will not be at the station today. We’ll be driving all around the district to make sure we are ready for anything that comes out way.”

“Cap, you do realize we work here, right?” Hen pointed out. “We’re trained professionals, not fifth -graders.”

“Yeah!” Buck added, laughing at the sheepish look on Bobby’s face before climbing into the truck cab.

Today was going to be a very long day, and probably not a fun one.

Chapter 28

Chapter Notes

Content warning for... I'm gonna go with, difficult birthing scenes?

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Now

With the CAD system down, what followed was a cavalcade of human errors. After Bobby reported their current location to Dispatch, they were sent to Hillcrest for a cyclist that was struck by a vehicle. Except that whey they got there, they found the 122 already handling it.

It did not help that in Los Angeles, the streets did not always make sense. There were entire streets that were broken up by whole blocks, for example. Like West 50th Street, it extended from Main until it was interrupted completely by Route 110, with another section starting on the other side until it hit Hoover. And then you had to turn north onto Hoover and hit the next left to get back onto West 50th Street, while going south and taking the right put you on West 50th Place. Then, you followed that West 50th Street until you, once again, had to turn north onto Normandie Ave and take the next left.

That portion of West 50th Street goes all the way to Western Ave until, for a change in pace, you have to turn south onto it and then take the first right for the next section of West 50th Street. And then that one takes you all the way to South Gramercy Place, where a naive fool might believe it stopped completely. Except they’d be wrong, because there was another section of West 50th Street that was about nine streets away from there at Crenshaw High.

That was one street in the city.

Basically, it was inevitable for them to be sent to the wrong place. Frustrating, but inevitable. Buck just tried his best not to think about the costs of the human error as they pulled out of the empty lot they’d been sent to and headed for the high rise they were meant to be at.

They were trying to respond to a woman in labor. When they finally arrived and walked into the lobby, they found her on the floor with her husband hovering by her side.

“Where the hell have you been?” the man demanded, rightfully upset.

“We’re fighting a system outage, sir,” Bobby explained to him as everyone got to work. “We apologize for the delay. Hen you’re up. Eddie, start fluids.”

“We can’t just have a baby in the lobby, that’s now how this is supposed to happen.”

“Roger,” the woman said, exertion and stress layering through her voice. “This is pretty much the way it’s happened through human history...”

The last word trailed off into a pained groan.

“Hi, I’m Hen. What’s your name?”

“Sonia. Thirty-nine weeks pregnant, thirty-eight years old. Which makes me a geriatric pregnancy,” the woman said as Buck got the blood pressure cuff on her. “God, I hate that word.”

“Word doesn’t matter,” Bobby told her. “You’re still gonna get a baby out of it.”

“I can’t believe I’m finally gonna meet him. Here. In the lobby.”

“You’re doing great,” Hen assured her, crouched between her open legs. “Now, on this next contraction, we’re gonna push, okay, Sonia?”

Sonia didn’t say anything. Buck looked up from where he was tracking her vitals to find her staring ahead of her with fear and worry.

“There’s something wrong with the baby,” she announced, sounding so very certain.

“No, no, no,” Hen said. “Your baby’s fine. You’ll be able to see for yourself in just a minute.”

“No! No, this was a mistake. All of it. Roger was right to panic. We can’t do this, I can’t do this. I shouldn’t have this child.”

“No, Sonia, look at me. Look at me,” Hen demanded. “All you gotta do is push. All you need to do is push. Push.”

Sonia took a breath and she nodded. She screamed and she pushed, she screamed and she pushed, Hen encouraging her the entire time while Roger and Eddie clung to her hands. She let out one long wail that trailed away, only to be joined and then replaced by newer cries. Sonia fell back, dropping Eddie and Roger’s hands, while Hen wrapped the baby up in a blanket.

“Baby’s got a set of pipes,” Eddie commented, going around so he could utter the umbilical cord.

“We’re gonna take you to the warmest place in this lobby, okay?” Hen told the baby, shuffling forward on her knees. “Which is gonna be right…”

She trailed off. There was something wrong with Sonia.

“Hen.” Buck held out his arms. “Hen, I've got the baby.”

Buck took the baby from her and cradled him in his arm. His other hand was needed to get Roger to back up, the husband demanding to know what was wrong and what was happening. And Buck could understand that, but he also knew that Roger was in the way while everyone needed the space to work.

“Hey, listen, she is in good hands,” Buck told him. “Okay? But right now, he needs yours.”

He practically shoved the baby into Roger’s arms, bullying him into cradling and supporting the crying little thing. Behind him, Eddie and Hen were working on Sonia and trying to figure out what was wrong.

“Blood pressure went off a cliff,” he heard Eddie say.

“Cyanosis,” Hen said.

“Can someone please tell me what’s happening here?” Roger demanded, raising his voice to be heard over his son.

“Your wife has gone into shock,” Buck explained.

Eddie announced that he couldn’t find a pulse, Hen declared cardiac arrest and started doing compressions. Bobby had them get her loaded up for transport, and while Buck wanted a separate ambulance for the baby - there was no telling what might happen with him, since his mom was in so much trouble - but there was no time to wait for one. Which was how he and Eddie found themselves in the cab of the fire engine with Roger, the newborn’s screams competing with the sirens.

It was a ride that played hell on Buck’s nerves, the worry about the mother combined with the wailing of the baby. And when Buck was anxious, he talked.

“He’s uh, he’s adorable,” he said. “And very loud.”

“Yeah,” was Roger’s absent reply.

“Um.” Buck rubbed at his mouth. “Eds! Did we ever tell you about the pregnant yoga class?”

Eddie’s eyebrows shot up and he shook his head.

“I don’t think so, no.”

“Okay, so, it was the full moon and those shifts are always insane,” Buck said. Rogers lifted his head a little to give him his attention. He even caught Austin glancing at him in the rearview mirror.  “And it’s just Bobby and me at first, because we’re so busy. So we get there and there’s a surrogate, six months pregnant, who’s stuck in the Locust Pose because of a slipped disk…”

Buck’s words filled the cab as they drove to LA General, the little one’s wails gradually quieting down. By the time they were pulling in right behind the ambulance, he was telling them how none of the new mothers would name their kids after him or Bobby. He didn’t look Eddie’s way for that part, all things considered.

Getting out of the truck took some maneuvering, with Buck taking the baby from Roger so the guy could get out without worrying about dropping him. Then he handed him back and clambered out as well. When the three of them - Roger, Buck, and Eddie - made it to the ambulance, they were unloading Sonia and it was… Very silent.

Silent except for a dreadful drone.

“How is she?” Roger asked. Then he took in the grim expression on everyone’s face and demanded, “What’s wrong?”

Bobby simply said,

“I am so sorry.”

A heartbroken Roger went to his wife’s side, everyone stepping aside to give him space. Buck peered into the ambulance; Hen looked distraught as she got up to watch as Roger put the baby on his mother’s chest. The baby fussed, and then…

The long drone stopped, replaced by rhythmic beeping.

Then

There was something wrong with the baby.

Eddie would say it was a normal pregnancy before that point, except he didn’t really have anything to compare it to except for what he saw on TV. What he did know was that it was taking forever, and that Shannon’s grip on his hand was threatening to break bones he would desperately need when he had to go back to Afghanistan. He’d gotten leave to be here, because he would not like his father, unable to even be there to welcome his children into the world.

But he’d assumed that it was a normal pregnancy, up until Shannon let out a scream that was different from the ones before. It was right after the doctor said the baby was crowning, that she could see the head. And then the face of the doctor, whose name Eddie never caught, dropped into a mask of seriousness as she and the nurses went into an agitated flurry of motion.

“What is it?” Eddie demanded while Shannon caught her breath. “What’s wrong?”

“The baby is stuck,” the doctor explained. Beneath the canopy of the sheet they had covering Shannon’ lower half from view, she did something that made Shannon scream again. “The shoulder is caught on her pelvic bone.”

“So get it-” Shannon interrupted herself with a grunt of pain. “Get it unstuck.”

“I’m trying,” was the terse response. After several more tense moments, she barked out, “Help me with her legs.”

While Eddie could do nothing but watch, bewildered, the two nurses who had been assisting the doctor suddenly grabbed Shannon’s legs. They flexed them sharply towards her chest. The sheet fell and Eddie could see some of what they were doing better, including the way one of the nurses put her fist just beneath his wife’s stomach.

“Okay, mom, I’m going to have to reach in,” the doctor said.

“Reach in!?” Shannon screeched.

“This may be uncomfortable,” the doctor said. Whatever she did, it somehow had Shannon squeezing even tighter on Eddie’s hand, to the point he was certain he felt bones grinding. “Okay! And…”

Eddie held his breath and waited, waited, waited for-

There was a tiny cry.

“It’s a boy!” the doctor happily announced while the nurses lowered Shannon’s legs again. She wrapped him in a blanket and brought him over to lay him on Shannon’s chest.

“We have a son,” Eddie breathed, looking at the tiny bundle in awe.

“He’s beautiful,” Shannon said, and he could only agree.

The doctor put a clamp on the umbilical cord and then another, and then held out a pair of surgical scissors to Eddie.

“Would you like to cut the cord, Mr. Diaz?”

“Oh, no, no, that’s okay,” he said, shaking his head. “You go ahead, I’ll just…”

“You’re a combat medic, but this makes you squeamish?” Shannon mocked. She was sweaty and clearly exhausted, but that wasn’t stopping her from roasting him.

“Come on, Dad,” the doctor urged. “You won’t hurt him, trust me.”

“Yeah, Dad.”

He liked the sound of that.

Cutting the umbilical cord was not as easy as they made it sound. Afterwards, when it was his turn to hold him, all Eddie could think was that it was worth it. Anything and everything was worth it for this tiny little life, his son, in his arms. But oh did he wish Evan was there to see, because he knew Evan would fall in love with him just as quickly as Eddie.

Oh.

They didn’t have a name yet, hadn’t been able to come up with one. Not Evan, but…

“Christopher,” he said, feeling it out. Shannon, looking half-asleep, smiled up at him.

“I like it,” she said. “Christopher Diaz.”

“I’m your dad, Christopher,” he said. Wide eyes blinked up at him, though they probably weren’t seeing much. “That means I’ll take care of you.”

Now

After a quick stop back at the station for refuel and resupply, and to watch Bobby and Michael get into because… Buck wasn’t sure, his attention was split between actually making sure they could do their job and eavesdropping on the two men, but he was pretty sure Michael was feeling territorial over Harry. Buck was opting to stay out of it, because while he might be the curious cat, he was currently dealing with too much familial drama of his own to even try sticking his whiskers into that mess.

They didn’t have much time to rest, and they were soon back on the road. It almost beat out the time the EMT gods took him saying the Q-word out of context and punished the entire station for it. It was during one of the infinite loops through the city streets, waiting to be flagged down for an emergency, that Buck’s phone rang. He took off his headset to answer it.

“Chim?”

“Yeah, Buck. Where are you guys?”

“Uh, we are…” Buck peered out the window. “Crossing Robertson for the three hundred and twentieth time.”

“Get over to Doheny Park, man. The whole damn neighborhood’s on fire. Looks like a gas main break.”

“You heard it on the scanner?”

“No, I’m watching it on the news right now. Nobody’s over there, not a single unit.”

Buck dropped his phone without hanging up, grabbing his headset and shoving it back on over his head.

“Cap! Doheny Park is going up in flames! Chim thinks it’s a gas main break.”

Even before Bobby gave the order, Austin was already hitting the sirens and taking the next turn to head that way.

It looked like a warzone straight out of a movie. Ash and smoke was everywhere, and there were occasional plumes of fire coming out of a manhole. Bobby requested assistance, because this was far beyond what they could handle on their own, but the day’s insanity meant they wouldn’t have any back-up for a while.

The biggest fire was the house at the end of the cul de sac. When they came to a stop just before it, a man ran up to the fire engine and started banging on it for their attention.

“You’ve got to help!” he shouted at them when they hopped out, and gestured at the burning house. “My son is trapped upstairs!”

“Do you know where?” Bobby asked him. “Which room?”

“No, no, I haven’t seen him.”

“Cap!” Eddie shouted, from where he stood closer to the building. “I see him! Upstairs window!”

“Alright.” Bobby turned to Buck, who was hovering at his shoulder. “Get the ladder up to the house.”

Buck nodded, rushed to where the ladder truck was parked while Bobby started giving orders over the radio. A quick conversation had it pulling closer to the house while he hung off the side of it. He kept an ear out for what was going on around him, hearing Eddie’s report that the hydrant had been taken out and so they were shit out of luck for more water.

With the ladder truck in position, they extended the aerial. Buck clambered onto it, riding it up to the second floor of the burning house. At a window with a broken pane, he spotted the soot-covered kid he was meant to be rescuing.

“Stay right here!” Buck called out to him as the ladder extended farther. “I just need you to hold on, okay?”

Soon, he was at the window. So close to it he could touch it.

“I told you,” he told the frightened boy. “Everything’s gonna be just fi-”

First, there was a creaking noise. And then Buck felt the metal buckle, bend, break. The ladder snapped with a crack, half of it swinging down, and he had to scramble to grab onto it before it sent him crashing into the ground. It left him hanging just a few feet above the ground, a short enough drop that he felt comfortable with just letting go and landing on his feet.

“You alright?” Bobby asked, rushing to his side.

“Yeah, yeah, I’m good, I’m- Uh, Eddie?” Buck cut himself off to stare at Eddie, who was scaling the side of the house with the use of the drainage pipe. “Eddie! What are you doing!?”

There was, of course, no answer. Because Eddie seemed to actually be incapable of answering that question. Buck was left to watch from the ground as his ex-boyfriend made his way onto the roof.

“If I did that,” he said to Bobby, standing beside him and watching as well, “you and Danny would take turns yelling at me.”

Bobby didn’t defend himself, because they both knew it was true. On the roof, Eddie tied a rope around the chimney and then swung through the broken window Buck had very nearly been able to reach. Without a mask on, so if Bobby didn’t yell at him, Buck was absolutely going to.

“Cap, I got him,” Eddie radioed a minute or two later. “Alive but unconscious. I’m prepping for evac.”

“Cap! Gas guys just showed up!” Hen shouted, running up to them. “They’re shutting it down. All neighbors are present and accounted for. Two more engines are rolling in, but it makes no sense if there’s no water pressure.”

An explosion from the house had them all ducking.

“Cap!” Eddie again. “Negative on evac. Pinned down, south side of the house.”

Okay, to hell with this. Buck could deal with being yelled at.

“I’m gonna grab an extinguisher,” he told Bobby, already moving. He gestured at the broken ladder as he spoke. “I can make that jump, and then I can help Eddie!”

“No, wait,” Bobby said. Something about his voice and expression had Buck pausing.

There was the sound of propellers, getting closer, getting louder. He could see the plane in the sky, the fat tanker they used for waterbombing. It wouldn’t simply put out the fire, it would crush it. Along with anyone that wasn’t sheltered.

“Everybody take cover!” Bobby screamed, motioning for everyone to move. “Under the trucks, now!”

Buck rushed to get everyone close to him under the ladder truck, joining them in cowering beneath it. When the water came, it was like Poseidon’s own hand slamming down. The truck creaked and bounced while curtains of water hit the pavement around them, splashing back up to sting his face and neck.

It didn’t last long, although it felt like forever. When Buck crawled out from under the truck, there was smoke and steam everywhere. And silence.

“Eddie, do you copy?” Bobby demanded over the radio.

Eddie did not answer, and Buck was about to rush into the destroyed house to drag him out. He was stopped, though, by the man himself coming through the broken door, carrying the unconscious boy. He set him down onto a backboard so Hen could get him resuscitated and coughing.

Buck threw an arm around his shoulders and shook him.

“What’s up with the Spider-Man routine?”

“I don’t know,” Eddie said. His face was covered in soot. “I just did it. And prayed a lot.”

“Yeah, yeah.” Buck nodded. Then he smacked him on the back. Hard. Hard enough to be felt through the turn-out coat and leave his palm stinging.

“Hey! Pendejo!” The other man glared at him.

“That wouldn’t have hurt if you’d been wearing your O2!” Buck snapped. Maybe he was being hypocritical, but sue him. He grabbed Eddie by the shoulders and manhandled him towards the ambulance, ignoring the man’s complaining the entire time.

Chapter End Notes

The things I look up for fanfics...

Chapter 29

Now

Apparently, the problem with the CAD was caused by an electrical fire in the building where the communications equipment was located. Things overheated and everyone got screwed six ways to Sunday as a result. Once that problem was finally recognized and addressed - which would have happened right away, except the call was declared low-priority and resources were stretched thin due to the CAD system being down; oh, the irony - things were quickly fixed and everything was soon back up and running.

The aerial on the ladder truck was still broken, though. The end of it drooped down in front of the windshield like… Well, it looked as if it needed a little blue pill. Bobby had a mechanic from the LAFD’s garage come over to take a look at it. Buck watched from the loft, empty except for himself, as Marty crouched at the broken end of the ladder and inspected it.

“There was no warning sign, it just broke,” Bobby told the mechanic. He was at the other end, at the base of the ladder. “The worst possible moment.”

“Eh, budget cuts,” Marty said with a shrug, before making his way to the captain. “The way we’re going, half the city is gonna be held together with paper clips and duct tape.”

“Hen thinks it’s chewing gum and spit.”

They both laughed, but Buck wondered if he should speak up or not. He’d had a theory ever since they were given time to breathe and for him to think. He didn’t really want to lay the blame on anyone’s shoulders, but, well. Bobby had been the one to decide that hanging a shark from the end of the ladder while they carried it to the ocean was a good idea.

While the shark’s weight might have been within the ladder’s limits, said ladder wasn’t meant to have that much weight hanging off the tip for that amount of time. Buck figured there were microfractures that grew and grew every time someone climbed up, until…

Also, it wasn’t quite the worst possible moment. It could have happened after he got the kid. Then they would have both been sent tumbling down, except there would have been no way for Buck to catch himself and keep a hold of the kid.

“I’m afraid you’re gonna be down a ladder for a few days,” Marty said. “I’ll bring in a backup, but there’s a reason it's a backup.”

“Paper clips and duct tape?” Bobby guessed.

“I could throw in some gum, if that’d make you feel better.”

Buck watched Bobby chuckle at the joke before he started climbing down.

“Thanks, Marty,” the captain said.

Buck pushed himself off the loft railing he was leaning on, done eavesdropping, only to find himself face-to-face with Eddie. He leaned back against the railing with a sigh, spreading his arms out behind him and gripping the edge.

“Hey, Eds.”

“Hey,” the man replied, sounding awkward. “How uh, how’s Maddie? Today was her first day back, right? It was a crazy day for it.”

“She handled it, she’s going on that second first date with Chimney,” Buck told him.

“Right, yeah. And uh, how ‘bout Danny?”

“Danny is not on a date with Chimney,” he said, and then shrugged at the look Eddie gave him. “I haven’t talked to him today. Pretty sure I saw Austin on the phone, though, ratting me out about the ladder. You could ask him.”

Eddie tilted his head, either in confusion or concerned or both.

“You haven’t talked to Danny today?”

“Yeah, no, it’s…” Buck flapped a hand in the air. “It’s nothing. Don’t worry about it.”

“Wait, have you and Danny not talked since-?”

“It’s nothing,” Buck repeated, a bit more firmly. He really did not want to talk about this with Eddie. “What did you want?”

“What?” Eddie blinked at him.

“What did you want?” he repeated. “You looked like you wanted something.”

“Oh, um. I was just…” The other man rubbed at the back of his neck. “You never really said if you wanted to go to the Science Center with us or not.”

“Oh, Jesus Christ.” Buck threw his head back with a groan. “And you never told me! What do you want from this, Eds? What do you want from me?”

“I…” Eddie ducked his head, swallowed. “I don’t know.”

“Yes you do,” Buck shot back. “You know exactly what you want. You just…”

He trailed off, shaking his head.

“Do you want me to just keep pretending like I don’t know how you taste?” he demanded, keeping his voice low so it didn’t carry. “Do you want me to keep tiptoeing around Shannon? Who, by the way, thought we were fucking behind her back. Am I supposed to be Chris’ fun uncle? Come on, man. Tell me what you want from me.”

“I don’t know,” Eddie said again.

“Liar.”

“Fine!” the other man snapped. “What do you want?”

That was an easy answer. Buck had known the answer to that question the moment he first laid eyes on Eddie again, even so many years later.

“You.”

Eddie blinked at him, the definition of gobsmacked.

“Buck…”

“You asked, I answered,” he said with a shrug. “See how easy that was?”

Pushing away from the loft railing once again, Buck stepped around Eddie and headed for the stairs.

Then

The first guy that Evan dated after Eddie was a year after Shannon, when he really should’ve been ending the road trip and heading back to El Paso and back to Danny. He didn’t, though. Instead, he had followed the Gulf Coast into and down Florida.

His name was Scott. They met in Key West. Evan had decided to go on a whim, because he had the extra cash and he was already in southern Florida. There was camping in one of the nearby keys for cheaper than a hotel, so Evan rented a space and slept in his jeep. It wasn’t comfortable, not really, but by that point he was very used to it.

He met Scott at Mallory Square, his first day there, because he’d heard that you could see the green flash as the sun was setting. Scott was visiting from Washington with a couple of his friends and invited Evan to sit with them. They hit it off, they flirted, they made out in a corner. And Scott stayed with him, even after his friends left for the bars that nineteen year old Evan couldn’t drink at.

They met up again the next day, and the day after that.

It wasn’t really much of a relationship. Both of them knew it wasn’t going to be forever, that neither of them would be sticking around for longer than a few days. Still, though, they made the best of it. And it was so different from being with Eddie.

The first day, they went to the Butterfly Conservatory. Because, according to Scott, he wanted to go but his friends thought it was lame. Evan didn’t know if that was true, or if he just remembered that Evan mentioned wanting to go through it the night before. It amounted to the same thing, so he didn’t think it really mattered. They met up outside and were about to head in when Scott did something that Buck was not expecting, not in a hundred years.

He held his hand.

Evan came to a stop and simply stared at it. Scott looked at his face and then started taking his hand away, apologizing.

“No, no,” Evan said, holding his hand tighter. “It’s just- My last boyfriend. He, uh…”

“Closet case?”

“Yeah.”

“His loss, then,” Scott said with a wink, raising their hands so he could kiss the back Evan’s.

They held hands the entire way through, even with all the people around them. Scott even, at one point, pulled him to the side of the winding route through the garden so he could kiss him, just because he could. And then laugh at the butterfly that had landed in his curls.

Scott was like that every time they went out somewhere. He would hold Evan’s hand and he would kiss him and wrap an arm around him, completely uncaring of where they were or who saw. On the haunted bus tour, on the train tour, on the beach and at the museums. It didn’t matter if Scott’s friends were with them or if they were surrounded by total strangers. If they went out together, there was no pretending that they weren’t together. Hell, they even went to a drag show, where they all laughed at how flustered Evan got when one of the queen’s practically climbed into his lap.

The week did not last long enough. Evan spent the final night in Scott’s hotel room, and the next morning kissed him goodbye on the street while the crowd moved past and didn’t even blink an eye. It was the most wonderful feeling in the world.

Evan left the island feeling freer than he had in years.

Now

Maddie was in the kitchen, drinking coffee, when Buck came in from his shift. Her answer to his greeting was just a grunt.

“Yesterday was insane,” he said, hopping up on the counter. She scowled and smacked at him, but he just ignored it. “Nice catch about the electrical fire, by the way.”

“Thank you.”

She seemed subdued this morning, which had Buck frowning in concern.

“Everything okay?” he asked. “Couldn’t sleep again?”

“Yeah,” she said, and then shook her head. “No, I couldn’t. I want to move.”

“Uh, okay.” Buck pulled out his phone. “I can help you go through listings.”

“Sure, that would be great.”

“How, uh. How did the date go last night?”

“It…” Maddie sighed, set down her coffee. “Well, first, I didn’t even set up a reservation, so we just ate at his place. And then… I think we broke up.”

“Oh.” Buck didn’t really know what to say to that. “You think…?”

“Well, we said that we just needed a minute but…” She shrugged. “One more thing Doug gets to take from me.”

Buck reached over and wrapped his arms around her, pulling her in to squeeze her tight. She didn’t even pretend to put up a fight.

“I was gonna go back to the apartment today, but if you need me to stay here…”

“No, no, get off my couch,” Maddie said as she pulled away. “But do I get to know why you’ve been avoiding him?”

“I was just… Angry at him, about something.”

“Must’ve been something big for you to be holding it against him this long.”

“Yeah, it was,” he said, and then, “Hey, uh…”

Maddie quirked an eyebrow and tilted her head.

“Hey, uh, what?”

“Um…” Buck was going to ask her about Danny and about Doug, but at the last moment decided that maybe it wasn’t the best time for that. “Just, you know our couch is always free. If you want to crash over there.”

His sister narrowed her eyes at him, clearly not believing that was what he meant to ask.

“I might.”

They spent the rest of the morning chatting while Buck gathered his things that had migrated across her apartment in the time he’d been camping on her couch. He’d learned from his time with Abby that he could practically move into a place without even realizing it, and that was never more apparent than when his sister came back from the bathroom with a bag of things belonging to him that she practically threw at him. After everything was gathered up, he checked once again if she needed him to stay, and then he was on his way back home.

Danny was sitting at the dining table, a sad looking bowl of cereal in front of him. He looked up when the door opened, spoon frozen in the air between bowl and mouth.

“You’re home.” He sounded like he was trying for casual and missed by a mile.

“Yeah,” Buck agreed, setting his bags - the one he took to work and the one he packed for Maddie’s - down on the floor. He’d go through them and throw things into the wash later. “Uh. Maddie and Chim broke up.”

“She told me,” Danny said, putting the spoon back in the bowl. “I can’t even be happy about it.”

“Do you dislike Chimney because he’s interested in our sister or some other reason I’m not aware of?” Buck asked, genuinely curious.

“You and Austin have both told me too much about his last relationship for me to ever like the idea of him with Maddie,” his brother said. He’d gradually looked more and more relaxed as they talked. “I heard about the ladder. You okay?”

“Yup. Caught myself, didn’t fall that far.”

“Good, good.”

Buck nodded in agreement as he pulled out the chair across from Danny and sat down. He leaned back, raising the front two legs and earning a scowl from his brother. Which he dutifully ignored, as always.

“Eddie tried inviting me to another family outing.”

“Where to this time?” Danny asked, going back to the cereal. It was the off-brand fruit loops, the same kind they’d been getting since they first left home.

“Science Center,” Buck answered. “I’m not going.”

“Oh? It learns.”

Buck rolled his eyes.

“I asked him what he wanted from me.”

“And?” Danny prompted. “What did he say?”

“He didn’t say anything.” Buck shrugged. “I told him what I wanted. He just… Never answered.”

“Well, he probably wants things to go back to the way things were before he fucked up.” Danny’s eyes were focused on his cereal, which he was just pushing around and around in his bowl. “You know, if I had to guess.”

“Probably,” Buck agreed. “I love him, but I don’t think I love him enough to go back to that. I don’t think I love him enough to hurt myself like that.”

His brother raised his eyes to stare at him, head tilted. He looked surprised and pleased.

“Huh.”

“Yeah,” Buck said. “How long did you know about Doug and Maddie?”

Danny sighed and pushed the bowl out of the way so he could put his elbows on the table and bury his face in his hands. Buck could watch the way he practically collapsed in on himself at the question.

“I visited Hershey the August after you left on your road trip,” he said, lifting his head up so Buck could hear him. “I saw the bruises then.”

“More than eight years.” Buck let the chair drop forward. “More than eight years. What the hell, Danny!? Why didn’t you tell me?”

“Because I…” Danny threw his hands up. “Because I was trying to protect you, alright?”

“No, not alright! You should have told me! You knew, and you…”

“I tried,” his brother said. “I called the cops, I reported it. She…”

“She, what?”

“Lied to them, I guess,” Danny said with a shrug. “I tried to get her to go back to Texas with me. She cut me off entirely for a year.”

Buck didn’t understand.

“Why? Why would she…?”

“I don’t know, Buck. I don’t even think she could tell you why.”

“Okay, okay. But you should have told me, Danny,” he said. “Eight years. You let me keep thinking that everything was fine for eight years. You shoulda told me.”

“I know, I should have,” Danny quietly agreed. “I was just… Trying to protect you.”

“I’m not a kid, I don’t need you to protect me.”

“You’re still my kid brother,” Danny told him. “And… And hell, you’re my kid. I practically raised you since you were in diapers. I’ll always want to protect you, I can’t just… Turn that off.”

“You should have told me,” Buck repeated. “If not before, then when Maddie got here. And you should’ve told me you knew Doug was gonna come.”

“You’re right,” his brother said. “I’m sorry I didn’t. I’ll try to do better.”

Buck nodded, wiped at his nose, and got up to throw his clothes into the wash.

Chapter 30

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Now

It had gotten easier to talk with Shannon in the months since Christmas, conversations no longer leaving Eddie feeling like they were constantly on the verge of another shouting match. Sometimes, though, they got on a topic and it was like they hadn’t made any progress. Shannon pushed and he pushed back and before long, it was like those days before she left. Eddie tried, he really did, especially when Chris was just within earshot.

At the moment, Eddie was carrying everything Chris would need to spend the night with his mother to Shannon’s car. They had met at Abuela’s for breakfast before Eddie needed to rush off for a shift. And apparently Shannon was worried about how many shifts he had been working.

“Yeah, I’ve been picking up a few extra shifts here and there,” Eddie told her as he used her key to unlock the trunk. “It’s not a big deal.”

“It is a big deal,” Shannon argued. “Surfing lessons for a hundred-fifty an hour?”

“The guy’s giving me a discount,” he said, putting everything into the trunk.

“Carla, the private school, physical therapy, the afterschool program,” she listed off. “Eddie, I know what all that costs.”

“It’s under control,” he insisted. “Don’t worry.”

Sure, okay, maybe it wasn’t as easy as Danny made it look, but Eddie was handling it. He had to handle it. He was the father, the husband, the man of the house. It was on him to provide and take care of everything. And his son needed the physical therapy and a school that didn’t just leave him behind, and he wanted the surfing lessons and so, by God, Eddie provided and took care of everything.

“Every time we talk about money, you tell me not to worry,” Shannon said. “Guess what? I worry.”

“Don’t.”

She opened her mouth, ready to keep pushing. But they were interrupted by Chris stepping out onto the porch to tell them he was ready to go. Eddie closed the trunk, relieved to have an excuse to cut the argument off at its knees, and helped his kid get into the car. He lingered in the yard, waving at them until they were down the street, and then got into his truck to head for the station.

When he arrived, he hurried through the bay for the locker room, noting in passing that they had a new ladder truck in the bay. The only one in there was Buck, the man turning to give him a wane smile as he tugged on his button-up. Eddie started to greet him only for the words to catch in his throat when he saw yet another mouth-shaped bruise on the other man’s collar bone.

Eddie had seen several dotting the man’s skin since New Year’s while they changed before or after a shift, all of them in places that would be covered up by their uniform. The sight of them made something white-hot rear up in his belly like an angry snake.

“You’re cutting it close,” Buck said, buttoning up his shirt and covering the bruises from view. “Careful, Hen’s cranky today.”

“I’ll keep that in mind and try to avoid her, then,” Eddie said, forcing himself to move and head for his locker.

“Oho, no, sir.” Buck shook his head with a laugh. “She’s already recruited us both to help decorate.”

“Great,” Eddie said, rolling his eyes as he opened his locker.

“Hey, uh. Did Chris have fun at the Science Center?”

“He did.” Eddie risked a glance at the other man, saw him squinting at the mirror attached to the inside of his locker door as he fussed over his hair. “You should’ve come with us.”

“I’ve been there before,” Buck said. “It’s a nice place for a date.”

Eddie bit down the reflex to ask if Buck knew that from experience, to ask if he took Abby there, or Taylor Kelly. Or maybe whoever left that mark on his collar bone. Nothing good would come from going down that path.

“You still haven’t given me an answer, by the way,” Buck said, closing his locker with a clang. “Try not to take too long in here, Hen will come hunt you down.”

The locker room was very silent after he left.

Hen was most definitely on the warpath when Eddie, dressed in his uniform, finally joined everyone else. At one point, he was fairly certain he was gonna witness her strangle Buck with a streamer. Instead, she just made the two of them fetch the ladders so they could hang up a banner under her critical eye.

“Buck, you’re lopsided,” she complained, arms crossed and scowling.

“Should we even be doing this?” Buck asked. His end of the banner - which was keeping score of Chimney’s brushes with death - was indeed quite a bit lower than Eddie’s.

“Yes.”

“Chimney said he didn’t want us to make a big deal about his first day back.”

“He didn’t mean it.” Hen’s tone brooked no arguments. “That is way too low.”

“Okay, first of all,” Buck said, lifting his end of the banner up way too high. “You cannot expect me to hang something straight.”

Hen’s face was pinched as she looked between him and Eddie, before she said,

“You’re only half gay, dig in and find your straight side.”

The way Buck’s nose scrunched up at that was adorable.

“How would I even know which side that was?” he asked, and then shook his head. “Anyway! I’m just saying, maybe we should respect the guy’s wishes.”

“You know what we’re not doing? Respecting wishes,” Hen said. “We’re hanging a banner, we’re having cake, we’re gonna show him how much we love him.”

The last part of her statement was snapped out. It left Buck awkwardly glancing at Eddie for support, but Eddie just laughed and shook his head.

“Lower,” Hen ordered.

Eddie taped his end in place, since Hen didn’t have any problems with its placement, and climbed down to start on the balloons with her. Filling them up with air and then tying them off. Marty, the mechanic from the LAFD’s repair shop, finished showing off the new ladder truck to Bobby and came over.

“This is festive,” he said. “Need a hand?”

“I know you got the wind,” Hen said. “But tying it would be a thing, huh?”

“Because of the arthritis?” Marty lifted his hands and wiggled his fingers. “Not gonna be a problem.”

“Well I’ll be damned. Steroid shot kicking in, huh?”

“No, ma’am.” The mechanic shook his head. “I finally took my crazy sister’s advice, went to see her acupuncture guy.”

“You went alternative medicine? I’m shocked.” In the few interactions Eddie had had with the guy, he seemed as skeptical about those things as Eddie himself did.

“Don’t be, that didn’t work either,” Marty said as Buck, finished hanging the banner, came over as well. “But he then recommended me to a kind of witch doctor. You won’t believe what he put me on.”

Eddie saw Buck open his mouth, no doubt to ask, but he got interrupted by Athena rushing into the station with a cake box in her hand.

“Hey, y’all. I’m not late, am I?”

“You’re right on time,” Bobby told her, going to her and kissing her on the cheek.

“Let’s see,” Hen said, pulling off the lid of the cake box. The cake inside was just plain white, with nothing written or drawn onto it.

“Kind of a letdown, isn’t it?” Buck was peering over Hen’s shoulder at it. “Last time we had a cake in the shape of his head with a licorice rebar through it.”

Hen half-turned to scowl at him.

“Yeah, but this time he only got stabbed.”

“There’re knives in the kitchen,” Eddie pointed out. “We could take one and stab it into the cake.”

Buck snapped his fingers and pointed at him in excitement, looking ready to rush up the stairs and grab one for that very purpose. Hen’s scowl deepened and she stuck the lid back on the box, before taking the entire thing from Athena.

“Or, we could not do that,” she said as she marched for the stairs. “Where’s Jones? Jones! Go make yourself useful and get out the plates!”

“She seems a bit on edge today,” Athena commented as she watched her go.

“She’s cranky,” Buck said.

“I heard that!” Hen snapped from the stairs. “Everyone get your asses upstairs so we can have some damn cake when Chimney gets here.”

Eddie and Buck looked at Bobby, who simply gave them a shrug.

“You heard the woman,” the captain said, and made a shooing motion with his hands. “Go.”

In the middle of the afternoon, hours after they greeted Chimney back to work and Hen calmed down, they were called to a bank. Two people in a medical emergency. When they arrived, there was an armored truck sitting out front. The driver started banging on his window when he saw Buck going by, opening a tiny little slot in the door to talk through.

“Can you tell me what’s happening?”

“Don’t know, sir,” Buck told him. “Just got here.”

“I can see that! Do you know what the call was?”

“Two people down in the bank.”

“Down?” the driver repeated. “Like dead down? Injured Down? Depressed down? My partner’s in there.”

“Was your partner exhibiting any kind of symptoms of anything before entering the bank?” Buck asked.

“What? No.”

“Does he have any allergies or medical conditions?”

“No!” the driver said. “He eats better than I do, he’s pescatarian.”

So the guy knew absolutely nothing useful, then.

“Okay, uh. You just sit tight!” Buck told him, before hurrying off. He was fairly certain the guy said something back, but he couldn’t really make it out.

When he got inside, he found everyone gathered around one of the victims. From his uniform, the partner of the guy in the armored truck. Buck passed on what little info he got from the guy’s partner, and then Bobby and Hen went to check on their other victim in the bank vault. Eddie trailed after them, because this guy was pretty well covered.

When the three of them came out, Bobby looked - and sounded - worried. More than he should’ve been for a typical medical call.

“Everybody, stop what you’re doing right now,” the captain ordered. “Chimney, Buck, step away.”

Buck shared uneasy looks with Chimney as they did as they were bid, taking several steps away from the downed man.

“We are now preparing for a possible chemical exposure incident,” Bobby told them all. “That means nobody in, nobody out. We can’t risk spreading whatever may be present here.”

The door to the vault let out a warning chime and began to close. Hen rushed in, Buck assumed to grab the second vic before he was trapped. Bobby and Eddie ran to stop her, or maybe to help. But the door closed in front of them, with her on the other side.

So to sum it up: they had two people down with what Eddie and Bobby thought was a nerve agent; they had Hen and one of their vics locked inside a vault that wouldn’t open again until tomorrow; oh, and actually that was three people down - Bobby, using the security feed in the bank manager’s office, just saw Hen collapse with the same symptoms.

The bank employees who weren’t sick got disposable masks and then thrown into a side room to be out of the way as Bobby called it in. After that, they put on their SCBA gear and bright yellow cleansuits. Buck and Eddie grabbed the power tools and started trying to force their way into the bank vault, motivated by a video feed set up on a nearby computer that showed an unmoving Hen lying on the floor. By the time the LAPD arrived, with SWAT and HAZMAT people, they hadn’t made any progress.

Turns out, their power saw could not open all doors as he had initially assumed.

“Not even a scratch,” Buck complained to Bobby when the captain led one of the HAZMAT guys to the vault door.

Bobby stomped to the computer to check on Hen and the bank manager, but Buck knew nothing had changed for the good in there.

“Two people, seventy-two breaths a minute,” he said, obviously thinking aloud as he did math in his head. Buck could never. “We need to drill into that vault and get some air flowing.”

“Doors are wired, Cap,” Eddie told him. “Running hot.”

Anyone that tried cutting through that vault could easily hit a live wire, and then they’d have yet another person down. Buck would much prefer going the rest of his life without knowing what several volts of electricity going through his veins would feel like. He could imagine it well enough, thanks.

“We need to cut the power,” Bobby agreed. “You two, go get the drill and the O2 tanks. I’ll send Chimney to the circuit breaker.”

Buck and Eddie nodded before rushing out for the trucks. Outside, the HAZMAT people were setting up decontamination tents in the parking lot while the police had cordoned off the entire area. The driver of the armored truck honked and waved Buck down, but between the clean suit hood and the SCBA mask he was wearing and the thickness of the truck’s glass, there wasn’t really much of a conversation.

The guy looked incredibly frustrated when Buck left, not that there was anything he could do about that.

They got the oxygen tanks inside along with the drill they would be using to poke a hole through the vault door, the bit as long as Buck’s leg. Lights were brought in and fixed to the wall around the area in preparation for the darkness that was about to fall over them. Around them, the HAZMAT people were still taking readings of the air with detectors that Buck wasn’t entirely certain was actually giving them anything they wanted to know. If he only went by the increasingly frustrated way one of them waved theirs around.

Bobby took a final look at the video feed showing Hen and the bank manager, neither of whom had moved the entire time.

“Okay, we’re going dark,” he said, grabbing his radio. “Chimney, hit it.”

The lights shut off section by section as, elsewhere in the building, Chimney hit the switches in the circuit breaker box. The only illumination they had were the portable lights they brought in, the flashlights attached to their suits, and the red emergency lights that came on automatically. Once the constant low buzz of power was gone, a buzz that Buck only noticed upon its absence, and Chimney gave them the all-clear, Eddie put the drill to the vault door. He hit the trigger and sparks began to fly.

It was not a fast process. The vault door was thick, layer after layer after layer of tempered steel. It was designed to keep would-be bank thieves out, after all. If it was quick and easy to drill into, the bank would have gone with a different door.

Buck did his best to keep his flashlight trained on where the drill slowly chewed into the metal. It might’ve been the second worst part of the whole ordeal, beat only by Hen being the one trapped in the vault. Luckily, Eddie was far too used to him and so he didn’t get upset when the flashlight beamed occasionally bounced around.

The moment the drill reached the other side was obvious, because Eddie suddenly leaned forward a little with the sudden absence of resistance before pulling back. He shut the drill off and took it out of the hole, allowing Buck to feed the hose through it. With an order from Bobby over the radio, the lights came back on. Another order had Buck cranking the O2.

“We’re wide open, Cap,” Buck reported.

“Okay, now what?” Eddie asked. He was frowning at the screen showing the inside of the vault. It had gone dark with everything else, and came back on when the lights did.

“Now we get in there,” Bobby said.

Buck and Eddie shared a look. They’d tried everything to get into that vault, only to fail each and every time.

“How are we gonna do that?” Buck asked.

“We’re gonna crack this safe.”

It sounded so simple when he said it.

The captain ended up calling Michael for help. Buck wasn’t sure how he did it, but the architect came through with the blueprints of the bank and got a date out of it. Apparently the south wall of the building was there before the bank and the vault. More to the point, it wasn’t made to be a vault.

“We start drilling here and here,” Bobby told them, gesturing to the spots on the wall. They’d gathered outside the bank, having ditched the SCBA and the cleansuits in favor of their regular turnouts. “Creating a crumple zone.”

“And then what?” Eddie asked.

“Then, I have an idea. But we’ve got to soften it up first.”

“Soften up a foot of concrete?” Chimney sounded dubious, but he hadn’t been wasting his time trying to break through a vault door.

“It beats three layers of tempered steel,” Buck pointed out. “Let’s do it.”

It was as simple of a plan as anything that involved breaking down a wall. They took the drill to the spots Bobby pointed out, and then took a hammer and a prise bar to the holes. When they could see the inside of the vault, they stuck the prise bar through it and made sure it was hooked nicely to the other side before tying a rope to the end, attached to the winch on the front of the fire engine.

Chimney was sent inside to keep an eye on the video feed of the vault. The moment he radioed that he was in position, they turned on the winch. It rattled, it groaned, it let out a grinding noise. But nothing gave. Or at least, the wall wasn’t going to give before the winch did.

And then, Bobby’s phone rang. He gestured for Buck and Eddie to hold on as he answered it. It was not a happy phone call, judging from the grave expression that settled across his face.

“Patient zero, the armored car courier, just died at the hospital,” he told them.

“Do they know what it was yet?” Eddie asked.

“No,” Bobby said. “But they did say no one else is exhibiting symptoms.”

“Yeah, no one else except them,” Buck said, gesturing at the wall, on the other side of which was Hen and the bank manager. “Okay, no, screw this.”

“Buck…”

“No, stand back!”

He jumped into the driver’s seat of the engine, an idle thought about Austin’s current presence running through his head. Bobby and Eddie scrambled out of the way as he put the truck into reverse and hit the gas. A several ton vehicle had much more force behind it, and a piece of the wall came flying.

When Buck joined Bobby and Eddie at the hole they had just made, it was in time to see the vault door opening and Chimney rushing inside to check on Hen. Hen, who was waking up and seemed perfectly fine as they loaded her into the ambulance, if a bit groggy and tired. Same for the bank manager.

It wasn’t a nerve toxin, despite Eddie’s initial fears, because apparently all the tests came back negative. Which meant they had absolutely no idea what killed the courier or got the others sick. But at least the driver of the armored car was able to get out at some point, because he wasn’t in the driver’s seat while they were packing everything away.

“That might have been the weirdest day I’ve had since joining the LAFD,” Buck declared once he and everyone else arrived back at the station and were climbing out of the trucks. “And we have had a few.”

“Seriously,” Eddie agreed. “It was like a bank robbery with no robbers.”

As if they had been waiting for the cue, police cars squealed to a stop outside the station. One, unmarked except for the flashing lights, pulled into the station’s bay. Two detectives, a man and a woman, hopped out and were soon joined by uniformed officers.

“Step away from the vehicles,” the male detective ordered.

“Can I help you?” Bobby asked as he and everyone else moved to do as ordered.

“You and your people just stay where we can see you, Captain,” he said. 

He motioned at the two officers flanking him and the trio walked past the gathered group of firefighters. They went directly for the compartments on the engine, opening them and pulling out the bags they store their small pieces of equipment in. They laid them out on the floor and started unzipping them.

“Excuse me,” Bobby said. “You want to tell me what’s going on?”

In answer, one of the officers upended a bag. Bundles of cold, hard cash fell out into a pile. Buck and the others all stared at it in stunned silence.

Chapter End Notes
