Chapter 31

Chapter Notes

Content warning for references to and discussion of past sex work. Also I feel like I should put a warning label for the amount of ACAB present in this chapter?

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Now

Despite being his brother’s ex-girlfriend, Buck had never actually met Attica Rose before this day. Danny dated her during that period of time Buck lived with Connor and everyone else at the knock-off frat house in Arcadia, so his path never crossed with hers. She was beautiful, though. Beautiful like a lioness crouched in the tall grass just before she pounced on a wildebeest.

The bags of cash in the compartments of their fire engine were pretty damning, and pretty damn confusing, but they hadn’t been arrested. They were, however, strongly advised to not leave town and politely asked to come in for questioning at their earliest convenience. When he told Danny about what was going on, his big brother had told him to,

“Keep your fucking mouth shut and don’t say a damn word to anyone.”

That was pretty much an impossibility for Buck, and they both knew it. That was where Attica came in. Danny had called her pretty much the moment Buck was done explaining everything, and she was willing to help. There was something about her working in the public defender’s office and doing it on the city’s dime.

So that was how Buck ended up meeting her outside the police station, having arrived at the time he’d been told. She was already there when he arrived, wearing a smart looking pantsuit and calmly waiting with a leather briefcase in hand outside the entrance doors.

“You must be Evan,” Attica said, holding out her hand.

“Buck, but yeah.” He shook her hand and then followed her inside. “Thank you for doing this.”

“You’re just lucky your brother is such a good lay.”

“I didn’t need to know that,” he whined.

“And I didn’t need to spend an entire date listening to Dan drone on about you becoming a firefighter, too, and yet.” The moment they got to the police receptionist, her entire demeanor changed. She broke out into a version of that oh-so-familiar customer service smile that showed a lot of teeth. “Hi! My client, Firefighter Evan Buckley, is here as requested.”

“Detectives Wash and Mercer are busy with another one,” the receptionist said, she sounded bored and barely bothered to look up from her computer. “Wait over there.”

Buck and Attica went over to the nearby chairs and sat down, Attica immediately pulling out her phone. Maybe to keep track of hours worked for billing purposes, Buck didn’t know.

“The fact that we’re having to wait on them means either they’re having a very good time questioning someone,” Attica commented. “Or…”

The receptionist got up and went down the hall. Moments later, a door opened up somewhere. A voice that Buck recognized as the male detective from the night before shouted,

“Just say something!”

“Or not so good,” Attica finished.

“What does that mean for me?” Buck asked, craning his neck like if he stretched it far enough he could see what was happening. “I mean, if they’re angry before I even open my mouth.”

“It’ll be fine.”

They weren’t sitting there a minute later before Austin Jones came strolling out, merrily whistling. He came to a stop in front of them and waved.

“Hey, Buck,” he said, and then pointed at Attica. “The ex-girlfriend?”

“And you’re the boyfriend,” she said in return. “Sorry, the fake boyfriend.”

“Probably a good thing you’re here,” Austin said, not rising to the bait. “That one couldn’t keep his mouth shut to save his life.”

“Rude,” Buck grumbled, even though he knew it was probably true. He was a talker when he was nervous.

“They’ve already decided we’re all guilty as sin,” the other man said. “And I’m pretty sure they think they’re smarter than they are. So have fun in there.”

The smile that broke out across Attica’s face was not kind.

“Oh,” she said, “I think I will.”

Shortly after Austin left them, a uniformed officer came to fetch Buck. He directed the two of them to one of the interrogation rooms and left them alone. There was a metal table in the middle of the room, perpendicular to the window with the one-way glass. There were also only three chairs, two on one side and one on the other, which had Attica scowling. Then she just shrugged and sat on the side with the two chairs, gesturing for Buck to take the seat next to her.

“They’re going to try and upset you,” Attica told Buck while they waited. “They’ll claim your friends said something incriminating against you to get you to say something incriminating. They’ll make accusations and come up with hypotheticals to get you to react. If they had anything concrete against you, you’d be in handcuffs already. Just keep to the facts as you know them, don’t elaborate, don’t give unnecessary details.”

“Danny said to keep my mouth shut and not say a word.”

“It’s good advice,” she said. “They’re probably going to make us wait in here for a bit. Let us stew while they watch.”

Attica turned slightly in her seat and cheerfully waved at the window. Buck couldn’t see anything except their reflection, but a couple minutes later the door to the interrogation room opened again. The same two detectives from last night came in carrying folders, already looking very unhappy.

“I’m Detective Mercer,” the woman introduced herself as they laid the folders down on the table. She took the chair while the other detective remained standing. “My partner is Detective Wash.”

“Attica Rose,” the lawyer said, standing briefly to shake their hands. “I don’t know why there isn’t a fourth chair. Surely you weren’t expecting my client to arrive here without a lawyer?”

Her tone was perfectly polite, but even Buck could catch the razor sharp edge of the words.

“Of course not.” The words came out of Wash’s mouth through gritted teeth. He’d been the one that Buck heard shouting at Austin. “We just have a few questions that we hope your client can answer.”

“Ask away,” was the attorney’s sunny reply.

“Let’s start with how three-hundred thousand dollars in cash ended up in the One Eighteen’s ladder truck,” Mercer said, opening up one of the folders and laying out stills from the bank’s security footage. “Any ideas?”

“Nope,” Buck said with a shrug.

“You’re with the One Eighteen,” Wash said. “You were at the bank. You have no idea how the money ended up in your truck?”

“Not my truck.”

The detectives waited, clearly expecting him to say more. Except Attica had said to not go into any details, and Buck was trusting her advice on this matter. So he simply sat there and gave his best smile, while the detectives’ scowls grew deeper.

Mercer pulled a sheet of paper from the folder.

“Looks like the money trouble is over,” she read. “That’s the text your sister sent you. Right after she took the call and sent your squad to the bank. Care to explain?”

“Where did you get that text?” Attica asked with a curious frown. “I know it isn’t from my client’s phone records, because I don’t recall any subpoena or warrant being filed for them. I actually don’t recall seeing any subpoena or warrant being filed in this investigation so far.”

At the twin glares the detectives gave her, she simply smiled brighter.

“I take my job very seriously,” Attica said. “I would hope that you and your colleagues do the same.”

Danny was an idiot for letting her get away.

“What we take very seriously is someone breaking into a bank vault.” Mercer laid out another photo, this one of the hole in the wall.

“We had two people stuck in there, and one victim in severe distress. Who died at the hospital,” Buck said, trying to keep his cool. “Bobby - Captain Nash - decided we didn’t have time to waste waiting for them to get the vault door open, so we went for the wall.”

“Did Captain Nash seem like he was acting differently before the call?” Wash asked. He sounded like he was trying for something friendly, except he was still very clearly annoyed over how things had been going so far.

“Nope.”

“What about anyone else?

“It was Chimney’s first day, he was just excited to be back,” Buck said. “Hen seemed a bit cranky that morning.”

“What about Mr. Diaz?”

Buck felt himself stiffen in his seat. He knew the detectives saw it, because their fake smiles suddenly grew very real.

“What about Eddie?” he asked.

“We’re just curious,” Mercer said, leaning in a little. “You both lived in El Paso, didn't you? Went to the same high school. How long have you two known each other?”

“A few years,” Buck answered. “We lost touch for a while.”

“He was the first to suggest it was a nerve agent.”

“And everyone figured he knew what he was talking about,” Wash said. “Right? Since he was in the military.”

“He’s got a special needs kid, doesn’t he?” The tone of Mercer’s voice would probably come across as kind if it wasn’t for the undercurrent of smugness. “Separated from his wife. I’m sure three-hundred grand would solve a lot of his problems.”

“Being a single parent is hard,” Buck said. “But he’s a good dad.”

“You and your brother would know about that,” Wash said. “The difficulties of being a single parent, I mean. He was your guardian while you lived in El Paso, wasn’t he?”

“Yeah, but what-”

“Did you know he was arrested on suspicion of solicitation at one point? The charges were dropped, but the record from El Paso is still there.”

“Solicitation?” Buck repeated, confused. He wasn’t aware his brother had ever been arrested. “What, like those “No Solicitors” signs? The people who go door-to-door trying to sell you things?”

“No, Mr. Buckley,” Wash said. “Solicitation like-”

“What does this have to do with anything?” Attica demanded. She sounded furious. “You just said the charges were dropped. What does any of this have to do with a bank robbery?”

“It’s just interesting,” Mercer said. “What single parents will do to make ends meet.”

“What are you talking about?” Buck asked. He turned to Attica. “What are they talking about?”

“Nothing that matters,” she said, too busy trying to kill the detectives with her eyes to look at him. “Nothing that is relevant to why we’re here today.”

“Fine,” Wash said. He pulled out more photos, this time showing someone in one of their yellow cleansuits climbing into the back of the armored car. “What’s relevant is that your client appears to have conspired with one or more of his coworkers for a robbery.”

“Wait.” Buck shook his head. “The money came from the armored car?”

“Yes. While you and your friends were distracting everyone, you had someone else send the driver away and then empty the back of the car.”

“The armored car driver?” Nothing was making sense anymore. “I thought he couldn’t leave the truck.”

“According to him, a firefighter told him he needed to be decontaminated. So he gets out, goes to get decontaminated, and leaves the money unguarded.”

“Just as you and your friends planned,” Mercer said.

“I’m so confused,” Buck complained. “Can you start over?”

“I’m confused as well,” Attica said. “Do you have any proof that my client conspired with anyone to do anything except his job? Because this all sounds like conjecture and supposition.”

The attorney took their grumpy silence to mean that they, in fact, did not have any proof. She was quite vicious when she cut off any further questions, clearly still upset over whatever they tried bringing up about Danny. Buck didn’t say anything as she herded him out of the room and the building, not until they were standing on the sidewalk outside.

“What did they mean in there?” he asked her. “About Danny being arrested for solicitation?”

“I don’t know,” Attica said. “I do know that it doesn’t matter and it’s nothing you should worry about.”

“Okay, but-”

“They’ll probably keep giving you and your friends grief,” she said, talking over him. “They don't have anything, they know they don’t have anything, and they’re upset about it. That’s never meant anything good for my other clients.”

“So what do I do?”

“Get my number from Dan, and keep your mouth shut if they ambush you.”

Daniel, as a general rule, did not like cops, with Athena Grant as the one and only exception. He particularly did not like cops throwing the more unfortunate parts of his past in his brother’s face.

“I don’t want to know what they were talking about,” Attica said. She’d called him after she and Buck were done with the detectives. “Your brother was definitely caught off-guard, had no clue what they meant. He might ask you.”

He thanked her for the heads up and waited for Buck to get home. When he did, Daniel could just tell that his brother was chewing at the bit to ask him about whatever the detectives dug up. But he didn’t, for whatever reason, just complained about still being confused. That day, at least.

The next day, however, Daniel was not so lucky.

“Were you ever arrested?” Buck asked, in the middle of lunch.

Daniel dropped his half eaten sandwich on his plate and pushed it away, appetite gone just as suddenly as the question had come.

“Once,” he answered, because he promised his baby brother that he would try and do better. And lying about this, however easier it might be, did not strike him as trying to do better.

“When?”

“Your sophomore year. Never made it to court.”

“They said the charges were dropped,” Buck said, idly tearing apart a piece of lettuce that had fallen onto his plate. “Charges of solicitation.”

Daniel closed his eyes. Oh, but he hated cops.

“They didn’t have evidence.”

“Evidence that you were, what? Paying someone for sex?”

Buck must have spent the last day on his phone or laptop, going down rabbit holes, to know that’s what the charges meant.

“No.” Daniel opened his eyes, but he kept them focused on his unfinished lunch. “Not for paying.”

His brother was silent for a moment, and then another. And then, an anguished whisper of,

“Danny.”

“It was work,” Daniel said, picking up the sandwich again and tearing into it. “It got you new clothes, it put food on the table. It bought us our couch.”

“Danny.” Buck looked the picture of heartbroken. “Was it because-?”

“It was my choice,” Daniel said, cutting the question off. “Every step of the way. I chose to get you the hell out of Hershey, and I wouldn’t change that for anything in the world. I did what I had to do to make sure you were taken care of, and I wouldn't change that, either.”

Buck looked like he was about to cry, so Daniel got up and rounded the table. He perched on the edge and pressed his face into Buck’s hair.

“None of it was your fault,” Daniel said. “And if you start blaming yourself for anything, I have baby photos of you that I could send to Austin to share with everyone.”

“You wouldn’t.” The words were quiet, and maybe a bit wet, but Buck was laughing a little too.

“Try me.”

Oh, but he hated cops. He hated them for the guilty air that Buck carried around the rest of the day. And he especially hated them for the way they pounded on his door the next morning while he was getting ready for work.

“What?” Daniel demanded, a bit grumpy, when he answered the door. 

A group of uniformed officers were standing in the hall. The lead one handed him a paper and started to just walk inside, but Daniel flung his arm and blocked him as he read through the legalese.

“We have a warrant to search these premises, sir,” the officer said, a bit haughtily.

“A warrant to search apartment Three Forty-one,” Daniel agreed. As a single entity, the cops all turned their gaze to his door with the very obvious number of 314. “I’m not even sure this building has an apartment Three Forty-one, but if you find it, you’re more than welcome to search through it.”

He handed the warrant back to the cop, who took it with a disgruntled scowl, and then slammed the door in their faces. Because he could, and because it brought him joy to do so.

Despite Hen explaining things to everyone, Buck was still terribly confused about what happened. He was pretty sure he got most of it down, though, and that it went like this:

Marty the mechanic and Franklin the bank manager were brothers-in-law. Or, used to be brothers-in-law. Marty’s crazy sister, the one who sent him to the acupuncturist for his arthritis, was Franklin’s ex-wife. They ran into each other again, somehow, and hatched a plan.

Franklin used the scorpion venom that Marty’s “witch doctor” guy was giving him for his arthritis to stage a medical emergency that would trigger a HAZMAT response and seal the perimeter. Marty then got the driver out of the truck, and the money onto the engine. Except that Franklin apparently had his own theft planned. He was retiring that day, so he took advantage of the fact that they had to cut the power to the vault to steal a bunch of diamonds, and then he told the police where to find the money Marty stole.

And then, or so they figured, Marty found out about the doublecross and killed Franklin in revenge.

Except, there was still a detail that Buck was still caught on.

“How’d he get the diamonds out?” Buck asked, once everything was explained to him. The others all looked at him questioningly. “I- I mean, everyone thought he was the victim of  a nerve agent. They took his and Hen’s clothes before loading them onto the ambulances, right?”

“Right,” Hen confirmed. “That gown was breezy.”

“So, how did he get the diamonds out?” he asked again. “If they were in his clothes, they would’ve found them. But they’re still missing, so…”

Buck trailed off as the others all looked at each other and then, in unison, said,

“Huh.”

(The answer, according to the autopsy report, was that Franklin swallowed them. And then died from acute intestinal blockage.)

Buck knew, the moment he got to the station, that something was wrong. It was a feeling in the air, maybe. A quality of the atmosphere. Maybe he, like the dogs people compared him to, could sense the impending disaster. If only he knew what the disaster was going to be.

He and the others climbed the stairs to the loft, to find Bobby sitting at the dining table. The captain wasn’t dressed in his uniform, and there wasn’t the usual smell of breakfast that typically welcomed them in the mornings.

“Why are you sitting up here in your civvies?” Chimney voiced the question they were all wondering. “And how come I’m not smelling my breakfast.”

“Maybe you should all sit down,” Bobby said.

“This doesn’t feel good,” Hen said.

Buck could only agree, a terrible feeling bubbling up in his stomach.”

“I know the shift’s about to start, but I wanted to be here to tell you this in person.” Bobby looked at them all, one by one, and took a breath. And then he told them, “As of this morning, pending further investigation, I am officially suspended from my duties here at the One Eighteen.”

Chapter 32

Now

It was a very beautiful day for a trip to the beach. The sky was clear, the sun was shining. There wasn’t a massive crowd as there typically might be, so luck was on their side, too. Eddie leaned back against the blanket they’d spread over the sand, unable to help the smile that broke across his face as he watched Chris diligently building a sand castle.

“Look at him,” Eddie said. “Feel like I won the lottery.”

The night before, they’d responded to a guy who won the lottery and whose life had not taken a positive turn for it. So he’d leaped out of a window, feet first, after tossing all his money into the air, and landed on a car.

“Our son seems happy,” Shannon commented, sitting on the blanket next to him.

“He was born happy.”

“I have no idea where he gets it.”

Eddie turned his face to grin at her.

“From me,” he said. “Obviously.”

“You?” Shannon scoffed. “Mr. Broody?”

“Hopefully he gets your brains,” Eddie told her, going back to watching Chris. “He can’t get by just on my good looks.”

“God knows he better hope he’s not stuck with your math skills.”

“Hey! I get by okay.”

“I’m fairly certain I kept you and Evan from flunking math, our senior year.”

Eddie just shrugged, because he was fairly certain of that, too.

“Speaking of Evan,” Shannon said. “You didn’t invite him this time?”

“I did. He, um.” Eddie ran his hand through his hair. “He said he didn’t want to come.”

She nodded to herself. He could feel her eyes boring holes into the side of his face, even as he kept his own on their son.

“What are we doing?” she suddenly asked.

“What do you mean?” he asked in turn, looking at her again.

“I need to know what you want.”

“God, you…” Eddie’s laugh was just short of bitter. “You sound like Buck”

“And did you at least answer him?”

“What I want is this,” he told her, waving an arm at everything around them. “A nice day on the beach with my son and his mother.”

“Is that what I am to you? Christopher’s mother? Because if that’s what it is, fine. I just need to know.”

Shannon really was sounding like Buck, enough to give Eddie deja vu. It was almost, beat for beat, the exact same conversation the two of them had those weeks ago. And just like with Buck, Eddie’s answer was,

“I don’t know.”

He sat up, brushed sand off the blanket.

“These last few months have been good. Really good,” he told her. “I just… I don’t know. I guess I’m waiting for a sign, something to know which way to step.”

“Okay.” Shannon gave a slow nod. “How about this for a sign? I want a divorce.”

“What!?” he yelped, only just managing to keep his voice low enough that it didn’t grab his son’s attention. “Why?”

“Seriously?” She rolled her eyes. “Eddie…”

“I just- I thought- These last few months have been good. Haven’t they?”

“Do you know what these last few months have shown me?” Shannon asked, but did not wait for his answer. “How things could have been. How things should have been.”

“I don’t understand,” Eddie told her.

“We both made so many mistakes,” she said. “And only one of them I don’t regret, because I will never, ever regret having Chris. Even when I was a shitty mom who ran from her son.”

“Shannon-”

“But what I really, truly regret is that he didn’t grow up with three parents.”

“Three?”

“You, me, Evan,” she said, like it was ever that simple. “I’ve spent the last couple of months wondering how things might’ve gone if I’d talked to you both that day. Or if you’d been a bit braver, if you’d listened to your parents less. How much happier we both would have been if our choices hadn’t sent Evan running, if we hadn’t gotten married. Whatever it is we’re doing, whatever it is we’ve been doing, it isn’t helping. It isn’t helping us, it isn’t helping Chris.”

“Shannon,” he said again, because that was all he could think to say.

“I want him to have three parents that love him,” she said. “What do you want, Eddie?”

Interim Captain Han was no one’s friend, and he would probably end up costing Chimney all of his by the time this whole mess was cleared up. Buck wasn’t sure what the man’s problem was, but he became a raging asshole the moment he was given power. He made them stand at attention for lineups, insisted on showing them how to “properly” roll up a hose like they were all probies and not just Eddie. And for some reason, he was fixated on Buck.

Chimney made a point of asking if Buck- Sorry, if Buckley, because Interim Captain Han couldn’t lower himself to calling him by his nickname, was paying attention to his needless demonstration of hose rolling. He zeroed in on him during the lineups, while others like Hen and Austin got away with leaving or just not showing up at all. He also tried to make Buck wash his car. His personal car, not the captain’s truck he kept driving to scenes in.

(Because God forbid he ride in the trucks with the rest of them.)

Key word there: Chimney tried to make Buck wash his car. Buck simply told him, in the politest fashion, to fuck off. Not in those words, obviously. But there was a limit to what he was willing to put up with, and there was that knowledge in the back of his mind that Danny did something to his last bully to make them leave him alone. There was a special kind of confidence that came from knowing he had someone in his corner, ready to go to bat for him, no matter what.

“Do I need to come down there?” Danny asked. They were having supper while Buck complained about Chimney. “Talk some sense to him?”

Buck glanced over at the baseball bat, looking oh-so-innocent where it sat by the door.

“You know, you never told me what you did to that kid who told everyone I had eye herpes.”

“Eye herpes?” Austin asked. He’d come over to eat and complain about Chimney, too.

Buck couldn’t help but look at the man’s relationship with his brother differently, now that he knew about that. Couldn’t help but wonder if Austin knew about it, too. Couldn’t help but wonder if maybe what Danny was doing with him was similar to what he used to do in El Paso.

They insisted, after all, that they weren’t really dating. That they both got something out of pretending to date. But Danny also said Austin wasn’t interested in anyone like that, so Buck didn’t think there was any sex happening. Which meant it wasn’t quite the same as what Danny used to do. It wasn’t quite the same as what Danny had to do, after he shouldered the burden of raising his kid brother on his own.

If Buck said he wasn’t feeling a terrible guilt every time he thought back to his high school years now, every time he remembered all the things his big brother paid for with money that he wrongly assumed came from his firefighter salary, then he’d be a liar. All the fees that came with Buck playing football, a stack of presents for him every Christmas, his first jeep. Danny said it was his choice, that he wouldn’t do anything differently, but Buck still felt so fucking guilty for it.

A kick to his shin tore Buck out of his thoughts. Danny was scowling at him from across the table, as if he knew what was filling Buck’s head at that moment.

“There was this little shit back in Hershey,” he told Austin. “Spread a rumor that Buck’s birthmark was eye herpes. Got everyone to laugh at him and avoid him.”

“And was his body ever found?”

“I didn’t do anything to him.” Danny rolled his eyes. “I just had a talk with him.”

“Every time he saw me after that, he ran the other way,” Buck said. “Pretty sure he got in trouble a few times for it, because he refused to sit next to me.”

“It was an informative talk.”

The front door opened and Buck twisted around in his seat to see Maddie walking in. She looked a bit harried as she balanced on first one leg and then the other to slip off her shoes, dumping her purse and keys on the table just beyond the door. Then she paused as she took in their other guest at the table.

“You’ve never met properly,” Danny said, and pointed with his fork. “Maddie, this is Austin.”

“Oh, uh. Hi?” Maddie awkwardly waved on her way to the kitchen area, where the food Buck cooked was still warm on the stove. “It’s nice to finally meet Dan’s not-boyfriend.”

“We were just talking about my old bully, back in elementary,” Buck told her, leaning back in his chair.

“The eye herpes kid?” she checked as she loaded up a plate. “I remember Dan and I running into him and his mother at the store, one day. The kid burst into tears and hid behind her.”

Buck and Austin turned their heads to stare at Danny.

“It was a very informative talk,” he said. “Seriously, Buck, do you need me to stop by the station? Say hi to everyone?”

“Why would you need to go by his station and say hi?” Maddie sat down in the empty chair beside Buck. “And how did you make that sound so ominous?”

“It’s a skill.” Danny shrugged. “And, uh…”

He and Buck shared a look, but it was Austin that answered. Because, as Buck was quickly figuring out the more they actually interacted, the guy had few fucks to give.

“Chimney,” he said. “Your ex’s head is getting a little too big for the captain’s hat he’s wearing.”

“Oh,” Maddie said, poking at her food. “Well, I’m- I’m sure he’s just adjusting. He’ll calm down once he has.”

“God, I hope so,” Buck groaned. “I’m thinking I’ll swing by Bobby’s, find out how long he’ll be out. Because I don’t know if I’ve got it in me to wait until Chimney’s ‘calmed down.’”

“Oh, I’m sure Chimney’s not that bad.”

“Chimney isn’t, but Interim Captain Han definitely is.”

Talking to Bobby proved to be fruitless. The captain - and he was still Buck’s captain, the brass and Interim Captain Han be damned - shot down Buck’s offer of talking to the chief on Bobby’s behalf. He also, much like Maddie, refused to believe that Chimney was really as bad as Buck claimed.

“It’s only been two shifts,” he said.

“He’s your friend,” he said.

The atmosphere at the station on the third shift of Interim Captain Han’s reign of terror was grim. Things weren’t quite as bad as the two shifts before it, Chimney no longer being as anal as he had been, no longer wielding his temporary authority like a cudgel. He’d even deigned to ride in the engine with them, instead of the captain’s truck. But the damage had already been done, and people were stepping warily around him.

At one point, early in the day, they were sent to a chocolate factory where someone fell into a vat of the stuff. Buck filled the engine’s cab with chatter about the many, many field trips he’d gone on to Hershey Park as a kid. Not that he was much help when they got there, mostly working with Eddie to keep the guy from sinking completely. It was Chimney who saved the day with confectionery chemistry and allowed them to get the guy out.

(Buck was fairly certain he called Bobby for help.)

There was something going on with Eddie, too. The man was acting weird around Buck, weirder than normal. Actively avoiding him, actually, when they had downtime at the station. Buck almost asked him what was up, because he was curious and worried. But the man never did tell him what he wanted, from Buck or even in general. Without that answer, Buck wasn’t sure where he and Eddie stood. He needed to know, before he did anything else.

But just because Chimney was doing better, it did not mean Interim Captain Han was suddenly picture-perfect. That was never more clear than when they were called out for a mail bomb. 

Athena met them when they arrived, the cops having beaten them there and already clearing out the neighbors. Their victim, a heavily burnt woman, was on her porch with her husband kneeling beside her. There were scorch marks on the ceiling above her, the wall behind her, the floor below her. There was charred debris everywhere.

“She’s not breathing!” the husband told them when they rushed up. “I tried talking to her, but she didn’t answer.”

“She can’t hear you because her eardrums are blown out,” Chimney told him. “Let’s turn her.”

On a count of three, they turned the woman onto her back. Her eyes, wide with terror, bounced around as she struggled for air. Chimney put on a stethoscope and pressed the head of it to her burnt chest.

“Eddie and I can handle this,” Hen told him, but he ignored her.

“Breathing’s shallow,” he announced as he took off the stethoscope and dug through his medical bag. He pulled out a pair of scissors and began cutting the woman’s shirt open. “We’re looking at a bilateral pneumothorax. We don’t release the pressure, she could suffocate.”

“I did my share of these in combat,” Eddie said. Buck had no doubt about that. Actually, out of all of them, Buck figured that Eddie probably had the most experience with this exact scenario.

Not that Interim Captain Han was listening to the same voice of reason as Buck.

“Get some fentanyl,” Chimney ordered. “Slow drip.”

“Chim, there’s post-attack protocol,” Hen tried. “You’re the captain.”

“I’m also the most experienced paramedic on-site,” he said, and while Buck knew that it wasn’t the time to point out that Chimney couldn’t be both their captain and a paramedic, not now, it was very tempting. “Four-inch catheter, ten gauge. Buck, Eddie, there could be a secondary device. We need to get everyone off the X.”

Buck nodded, turned to the husband.

“Sir, is there anyone else in the house?”

“No, no,” the husband said, shaking his head. “Is she gonna make it? Please tell me she’s gonna make it?”

“Why don’t you come with us,” Eddie said. He and Buck began to work together to herd the guy away. “We can check you out.”

“No, no, she has to know!” the husband said, struggling to stay with his wife. “We weren’t just going out to dinner, I had tickets for Fiji!”

“You can tell her yourself later, I promise.”

While they got the guy off the porch, the bomb squad was finally arriving. They piled out of their vans and rushed inside, in search of any secondary devices. The wife screamed, something that had the husband renewing his struggles to get to her, and then Chimney was giving the all-clear to move her.

They got her onto the gurney, and then the gurney into the ambulance. The husband climbed in with her, and the doors closed behind them as he clutched her burnt hand in his.

Eddie, for some reason, kept staring through the window at them. He jumped when Buck slapped a hand down on his shoulder.

“Everything okay?”

“Yeah,” Eddie said, tearing his gaze from the couple. “Everything’s fine.”

Buck didn’t buy that for a second.

“Hey, mijo?” Eddie poked his head into Chris’ room. “Can we, uh. Can I talk to you about something?”

Chris lifted his head from the Legos he was putting together. Eddie was fairly certain this particular set was a birthday gift from Danny, of all people. His son looked at him from behind his glasses and gave a little nod.

Eddie crept into the room and knelt down next to him, reaching up to brush some of Chris’ hair back.

“Okay,” he said, the word coming out shaky. “So you know how your mom and I don’t live together? I live here, she lives at her home, and sometimes you go between us?”

“Yeah,” Chris said, nodding again.

“If that didn’t change, if your mom and I never lived together again, would that be okay with you?”

“Is she going to leave again?” His son was very quiet when he asked.

“No, no, buddy. She’s gonna stay,” Eddie promised him. “It’ll just be… Instead of us living together again, like how it was before she left, it’ll just be like how it is now, your mom with her own house and me here with you. Would that be okay?”

Another nod from his son.

“And if…” Eddie hesitated, a tendril of fear creeping up his throat even now, even here. He swallowed it down as best he could. God, he was so tired of being afraid.  “And if there was, uh. If there was someone else, too. If it was you and your mom and me, plus someone else. Would… Would that be okay?”

“Like when Buck went to places with us?” Chris asked.

“Yeah, buddy. Exactly like that,” Eddie said. “That would be okay? If- If Buck…”

Chris nodded again, much more energetically than before.

“Yeah! He’s my best friend.”

Eddie couldn’t help but laugh.

“Yeah, he’s mine too…”

Chapter 33

Chapter Notes

content warning: discussion and references to past sex work

Then

The first time Eddie met Evan was in first period biology. Well, technically, it was in the minutes before first period started. The teacher, Mr. Wade, had stuck notecards with their names on the tables, already assigning seats before they even started the year. They were in alphabetical order, because teachers could be just as lazy as their students. Eddie found his name, dropped his bag to the ground, flung himself into the chair, and laid his head on the tabletop with a groan.

“Not a morning person?” a voice asked to his left. The accent was out of place.

Eddie just let out a muffled groan and shook his head, making the other boy laugh.

His morning had started far too early. With Dad on yet another work trip and Mom in no hurry to start the morning, he’d had to wrangle Sophia and Adriana out of bed and get them ready for school. They whined about having to get up and then whined some more about getting Poptarts for breakfast, but Eddie had learned his lesson there and knew better than to try making them something more. And then one of them, he was pretty sure it was Adriana, even if she wasn’t the one sitting behind him, kept kicking at his seat the entire time Mom drove them.

“Edmundo,” the other boy said, reading the notecard stuck to his side of the table. “Nice to meet you.”

“Eddie,” he corrected, still not bothering to lift his head up. The table was comfortable, maybe he’d take a small nap before the bell rang.

“You’re like a zombie, man.” The other boy laughed again when Eddie just let out another muffled grunt. “I’m Evan.”

Eddie lifted a hand and gave Evan a feeble wave, before letting it flop back down by his side. Maybe now that introductions were done, the other boy would fall silent and let him nap for a bit.

“There’re a lot less cowboy hats here than I imagined.” Nope. He was gonna keep talking, apparently. “And you all talk weird.”

“You talk weird,” Eddie grumbled, feeling strangely insulted. 

He lifted his head, accepting the fact that he was not getting any peace this morning. It was the first time he actually looked at the other boy. Evan was…  Well, if Evan was a girl, he would call him cute. But he wasn’t a girl, so Eddie couldn’t call him that. Even if it was still true. There was a pair of pink splotches just over his left eye, above and below his eyebrow. Evan clearly knew when he noticed them, because he suddenly tensed up as if waiting for a comment.

He relaxed, though, when the only thing that came out of Eddie’s mouth was,

“Where are you from?”

“Hershey, P.A.,” was the cheery reply.

Eddie blinked, his groggy mind whirring.

“Like… The chocolate?”

“Exactly!”

“So you’re from Pennsylvania,” Eddie said, squinting at Evan. “Does that mean you’re Amish?”

“What!? No!”

They talked a bit more while other students started filling the seats around them. Eddie learned that Evan moved to El Paso in June; that it was just him and his big brother, Danny, and that Danny was going to be a firefighter; and that he had a sister living in Boston with her boyfriend, who Evan didn’t like much. Eddie also learned that, if left to his own devices, Evan could carry on a conversation with a wall.

It was nice, because all Eddie had to do was give a grunt once in a while, occasionally peppering in actual words when pressed.

The bell rang, signaling the start of class, and Mr. Wade closed the door behind him as he walked in. Things went the way they almost always did on the first day of school, with introductions being made and the syllabus being handed out. They were given those cards to put down their emergency contacts, which Eddie saw his new friend - because Evan insisted they were friends, now - frown at.

When the next ball rang, Eddie stuffed everything into his school bag and was out the door. The next class was pre-algebra, a couple halls away, and he was already dreading it. Math was not his strong suit in middle school, and he very much doubted that it improved any over the summer.

He claimed a seat in the back corner and waited for everyone else to come in. Just as the bell rang, just before Mrs. Moore could close the door, a sheepish looking Evan managing to make it in. Eddie saw his eyes light up when the other boy saw him, and Evan made a beeline for the empty seat beside him.

“Fancy seeing you here,” he whispered as he dug through his own bag. He frowned and the look he gave Eddie was similar to a puppy that piddled on the carpet and knew it was in trouble. “Hey, uh. Could I borrow a pencil?”

“Didn’t you have one five minutes ago?”

“Lost it.”

“In the last five minutes?”

Evan simply shrugged. Eddie rolled his eyes, dug out a spare pencil, and handed it to him.

Their second period went the same as their first, with introductions and going over the syllabus. Once again, when the bell rang, everyone hurried to gather their things and get out. Evan was up and heading for the door before Eddie, who was getting up from his desk when he spotted the pencil left behind on Evan’s. He rolled his eyes and grabbed it, figuring that Evan could just borrow someone else’s for his next class.

Not that it mattered, because they ended up sitting together there, too.

Now

“Can I ask you a question?” Buck asked out of the blue.

“You just did.” Daniel raised his head from the flashcards he was going through in preparation for the test coming up, grinning at the dirty look his brother was giving him. “Shoot.”

“It’s, uh. It’s about…” The way Buck sounded, and looked, anxious and unsure told Daniel what topic they were on.

“Oh,” he said, putting down the cards and leaning back into the cushions of the couch they were sitting on. “Like I said, shoot.”

“You said… When I came out to you in high school, you said you’d kissed lots of boys,” Buck said, and then wetted his lips. “How- How many of them were…”

Daniel let out a sigh.

“At the time? All of them.”

Buck looked as if he’d just seen someone punt a puppy into traffic.

“Obviously I’ve kissed guys without being paid for it since then,” Daniel told him. His brother clearly did his best not to flinch at the bluntness, which was kind of him. “You’ve seen me do it.”

“I’ve seen you kiss Austin,” Buck said, like a brat. “I don’t think that counts.”

“Did you forget that time I took you to a gay bar for your birthday?”

“...oh yeah.”

“Oh yeah,” Daniel mockingly repeated.

“So do you, um.” Buck hesitated until Daniel nodded for him to go on. “Do you, y’know, still…?”

“No. Not since before we left El Paso.”

A scowl bloomed across his brother’s face before it was turned away from him.

“You mean since before I left El Paso,” was said to the coffee table.

“Buck…” Daniel sighed. “It was my choice. And it wasn’t any different from, I dunno. Picking up a second job as a waiter or something. Except that it paid better.”

“Yeah, being a waiter is exactly the same as being a who-”

“Don’t.” The word came out more cutting than Daniel intended. Buck’s mouth shut with a click and a flush crept up his neck as he ducked his head.  “You’re right, it isn’t the same. For one, I would probably have been on my feet more as a waiter.”

Buck looked like he was surprised by the laughter that burst from his own mouth. Daniel just smiled at him. Then he reached over, gently took Buck’s chin between his thumb and forefinger and made him turn his head to face him.

“I told you,” Daniel said. “It was my choice, and I don’t regret it.”

“But-”

“And,” he said, talking over his brother. “I also told you what would happen if you started blaming yourself.”

The scowl from earlier came back.

“You wouldn’t,” Buck said.

“Oh, I’m gonna,” Daniel said with a grin. “I’ll let you choose: the picture of you with those awful highlights, or the one I took of you after Delia and Celia tried their hand as makeup artists?”

Buck opened his mouth, no doubt to tell him exactly where to shove both of those pictures, but he was interrupted by knocking on the door. It was firm, as if the person on the other side was there with a purpose. The two brothers frowned at each other, and then Buck got up to answer it.

“You!” Eddie announced as he waltzed inside like he owned the place. He turned on his heel to face Buck, who was still standing holding the door.

“What?” Buck sounded as confused as Daniel felt. He closed the door, though he kept his eyes on Eddie.

“My answer, what I want. It’s you.”

“Eddie…”

This seemed like something that Daniel should not be here for. However much he might want to take his bat and threaten Eddie’s livelihood before whatever this was progressed any further, he knew that he couldn’t. Or, well, he could, but Buck wouldn’t appreciate it.

He started gathering up all of his study materials. The movement must have spooked Eddie, because he jumped like a startled rabbit.

“Danny!” he yelped. “I didn’t…”

“Don’t mind me, I’ll just go to Austin’s,” Daniel said. “Even though this is my apartment, and if anything, the two of you should be taking this elsewhere.”

An awkward silence - for them, not for him - fell over the apartment as he got everything stowed away in his bag. Eddie’s eyes tracked him as he got up and pulled on his shoes and grabbed his keys. At the door, he stopped and told Buck,

“You know where the bat is if you need it.”

“Get out,” was his brother’s reply.

The building that Austin’s apartment was in wasn’t very far from Daniel’s own. It was a short drive, and he was soon at Austin’s door. At this point of their relationship, fake or not, Daniel had no problem with just letting himself inside. The other man was on his couch, feet propped up on his own coffee table, and flipping through channels on the TV.

“Did Buck put a sock on the door?” he asked.

“Worse,” Daniel said, toeing off his shoes to place them with Austin’s. “My apartment’s about to host the climax of a Nicholas Sparks movie.”

“Have you ever watched a Nicholas Sparks movie? Pretty sure someone dies during every climax.”

Daniel shrugged, because no, he actually hadn’t seen any of those movies.

“I don’t think I’d complain if Eddie died dramatically,” he said, taking a seat on the couch beside Austin. He started pulling out his study materials, laying them on the table next to the other man’s feet. “S’long as there wasn’t a mess.”

“Okay, you never said what the deal was between Buck and Eddie,” Austin said. “You just said it wasn’t your place, and then we made them both cry when we wouldn’t let them keep making out.”

Daniel let out a groan.

“They used to date in high school.”

“Oh.” The word was drawn out. “That explains so much. What happened?”

“Eddie’s an idiot, and he’s lucky Buck made me promise not to hurt him.”

“Ah. So now the two of them are at your place, doing…?”

“Talking. Maybe, I don’t know.” Daniel sighed and, much like his mood, sank down. “Pretty sure Eddie was about to try and get Buck back, before I left.”

Silence followed that statement, and Daniel turned his head to see Austin frowning at him.

“What?” he asked.

“You don’t seem very happy about that,” Austin said. “I mean, I understand being upset, maybe. I was upset every time my sister went back to the guy she dated before Emmett, no matter how many times he broke her heart. But you seem… More than upset.”

“The last time things between the two of them exploded, Buck fled the state and I barely saw him for years after. I’m not really looking forward to a repeat.”

Austin nodded to himself. One bob of his head, two. And then suddenly he was wrapping his arms around Daniel and manhandling him.

“Wh- What are you doing?” Daniel yelped.

“I have no interest in sex, and when it comes to romance, I can take it or leave it,” Austin said. They were both stretched out on the couch now, Daniel reclining against this chest. “But I am a pretty big fan of cuddling, and you seem to be in need of it.”

“Oh, um. Okay.”

Austin went back to flipping through channels, one arm still wrapped around Daniel, before finally settling on a rerun of some medical show. It took a bit for Daniel to relax, but he eventually sank back against the other man and let their legs tangle together as he stretched his out.

It was actually kinda nice.

Awkward silence filled the apartment after Danny closed the door.

“So,” Buck said, turning back to Eddie. A very nervous and uncertain looking Eddie. “Me. You want me.”

“Yes.”

“What’s changed?” he asked him. “Between when I first asked and now?”

Eddie shrugged.

“Shannon, she uh…” He ducked his head. “She asked for a divorce-”

“Oh!” Buck threw up his arms and put space between himself and the bat, because he was suddenly so very tempted to use it. “ So I’m a consolation prize, okay.”

“No!” Eddie nearly shouted. “No, no, that isn’t-” He dragged a hand through his hair, leaving it messy looking. “She told me what she wanted, Chris with three loving parents. And she- She talked about how he could’ve had that already, except that I… I panicked. I was afraid. And I ruined everything.”

“Yeah,” Buck agreed. “You did.”

Eddie closed his eyes, wincing as if the words were a punch to his face.

“And I- I am-” He opened them again, shook his head, crossed the space separating himself and Buck. Eddie reached up and gently cupped Buck’s face with his hands. “I am so tired of being afraid.”

“Eddie…”

“I’ve been afraid since, I don’t know. Since the first time you kissed me, or since Timothy in Freshman year, or- or since Dad first told me how wrong people like us are. It’s like- It’s like I’ve been less than half myself for so long, and I am… You terrify me, Evan. Wanting you terrifies me. And I am just so tired of being afraid.”

Eddie pulled Buck closer, so their foreheads were resting against one another and they were breathing each other’s air.

“I want you,” Eddie said. “It’s always been you.”

“I love you,” Buck told him. He heard Eddie’s sharp intake of breath, because it was the first time either of them had ever said the words to the other. So many years later, and maybe far too late. “I never stopped loving you, even when it hurt. But I- I don’t think I can do this again, Eddie.”

“Please-”

“I can’t go back to how it was.” He reached up, gripped Eddie’s wrists and pulled his hands away, and stepped back. “I can’t go back to hiding in the closet because you’re afraid of coming out. I won’t hurt myself like that, Eddie. If we’re going to be together, I want to be able to tell people we’re together. I want to take you on an actual date, and not something we can just pretend we’re doing as friends. I want to be able to hold your hand when we’re out somewhere.”

“Okay, okay,” Eddie said. “Okay. Then- Then-” He took a breath, and then another. “Then we’ll go on a date.”

“Eds.”

“If that’s what it takes to prove it to you, then we’ll go on a date,” he said. “Somewhere fancy, somewhere public. Because I told you, I want you, and I am tired of being afraid.”

When Buck didn’t say anything, Eddie’s face began to crumple.

“Please, Evan.”

Buck closed his eyes and took a breath and chose to trust.

“Okay,” he agreed. “Somewhere fancy, somewhere public.”

Chapter 34

Chapter Notes

Content Warning: Character Death =)

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Now

Eddie did not linger long in Buck and Danny’s apartment after Buck agreed to the date. It felt anti-climactic, almost, to leave without even a kiss. But he knew that they weren’t there again, not yet. Not until he proved to Buck that he wasn’t going to panic and ruin everything again. Not until he proved that to himself.

And Eddie was on the verge of panicking at the thought of this date. At the thought of being seen in public with Buck while holding hands. At the thought of what his father might say if he found out. When he found out. It was an anxious thrum beneath his skin the entire drive to Abuela’s, where he’d dropped off Chris while he went to tear his own heart out in front of Buck.

“Eddito! You’re back just in time,” Abuela said, hugging him when he stepped through her door. “We’re just about to have supper, Chris is washing up. Did you do whatever it was you needed doing?”

“Yeah, Abuela, I did.” He chewed on the inside of his cheek and avoided her eyes. “Uh, do you think you could watch him again in a couple nights?”

Eddie could ask Carla to watch him, but then she had arrangements with other clients to consider, plus her own family. He’d have to wait for a day she had free, or for her to rearrange things, and he wasn’t certain his courage wouldn’t fail him if he waited too long. He could ask Shannon, she’d probably be willing, but it seemed somehow inappropriate to have his wife watch their son while he went on a date with his ex.

“You know I am always willing to have Chris over,” Abuela told him. “Especially if it means you’re getting time for yourself. What’s the occasion?”

“I’m, uh. It’s- It’s a date,” he managed to get out. He cleared his throat, said it again a bit clearer. “It’s a date.”

Abuela let out a hum.

“With Shannon?” she asked, eying him curiously.

It would be so much simpler to just agree. The lie was on the tip of Eddie’s tongue, ready to fall out. But he gripped the last vestiges of his courage and he swallowed it back, and he said,

“No.”

Another hum.

“With Evan, then?” Abuela asked.

Eddie could swear he felt his heart stop.

“Abuela, I-”

He fell silent when she reached up and cupped his cheek. The smile she wore was small and kind.

“I remember,” she said, thumb stroking across his skin. “How happy you sounded whenever you talked about him when you were a boy. And every time your tia and I went down to visit, I saw the way you looked at him. It was the way your abuelo used to look at me.”

Eddie swallowed, his throat suddenly so very dry. He opened his mouth to say something, but his voice was gone.

“Did you think I would care?” she asked him. “Did you think I would love you differently? Love you less?”

“Don’t you?” he managed to get out in a hoarse whisper.

“Silly boy,” Abuela tutted, patting his cheek. “Tell Evan that he will be coming for dinner.”

“Yes, Abuela.”

“Good. Now go make sure Chris is done washing up, and then you do the same.”

She patted his cheek again, before letting her arm drop and going into the kitchen. Eddie lingered where he stood, feeling dazed, before he finally gathered himself back up to track down his son. When it was his turn to wash up, Eddie had to spend several minutes scrubbing at his face.

“So I have a job interview on Friday,” Maddie announced out of the blue. She’d come over because both she and Daniel, whose schedule had been weird since starting the paramedic classes, had that morning off. “A nursing job.”

Daniel lifted his head up from his textbook with a frown, spitting out the lid of the highlighter he was using to mark the pages.

“A nursing job,” he repeated. “What, gotten tired of being a dispatcher?”

“I don’t know. Maybe?” Maddie shrugged. “No, that isn’t it. I took the job because I wanted to keep helping people, but with Doug out there…”

“But he isn’t now,” Daniel said. “You made sure of that.”

Maddie grimaced at the reminder, and his fingers twitched with the urge to wipe the pain from her face.

“Yeah. So there’s nothing stopping me from being a nurse again,” she said. “And when I was a nurse, I knew how things… Ended, I guess. I could see the difference I was making. I miss that. I miss being able to actually help.”

“Kinda feel like you’re selling yourself and your coworkers short, there.”

“Isn’t that why you’re studying to become a paramedic? To be able to do more?”

“Not really,” Daniel said with a shrug. “I’ll still be driving the engine for the most part. This is just… Because I want to, really. Because it’s the closest I can get to becoming a doctor without going to school for it.”

“You could still go to school for it,” was Maddie’s predictable response. “There’s nothing stopping you.”

“I don’t even have my Bachelor’s, Mads,” he reminded her for the umpteenth time. Though it had been awhile since they last hit upon this topic of conversation. “This is faster, easier, and cheaper.”

“Okay, but-”

Whatever she was about to say got interrupted by her phone ringing. Daniel was hit with a wave of deja vu as she took it out to answer, so heavy he could swear he caught the faint scent of chocolate and peanut butter in the air.

“Hey, Josh,” Maddie said. Daniel rolled his eyes and went back to his textbook as he listened to half the conversation. “Oh, I’m with Dan… Coffee? Sure… Okay, see you there.”

“So,” he said, once she ended the call. “I’m being ditched?”

“He invited you, too,” she said as she got up. “His treat.”

Well, with an offer like that, how could Daniel refuse?

They met Josh at some cafe downtown. Josh already had their orders made and paid for, thanks to Maddie texting him on the way, so it was simply a matter of grabbing them and finding a seat. Josh seemed to have already decided on that, too, leading them to an empty table in the middle of everything. 

“It’s good to see you again in better circumstances, Dan,” the guy said

“Same,” Daniel agreed.

“I’m always down for coffee,” Maddie said as they took their seats. “But what’s really going on?”

Was there something else going on? Daniel was just there for the free drink and food. Though it did make sense, considering Josh had been willing to pay for hot chocolate and a slice of coffee cake and a pair of croissants just for Daniel. There was absolutely an ulterior motive at hand. Then again, it could also be explained away by the not very subtle once-over Josh gave him when they arrived.

“Remember your first call?” Josh asked Maddie.

“Anaphylactic shock, seafood allergy.”

“And do you remember the first lesson I taught you on that call?”

“To listen.”

“Terrific! Do that now,” Josh told her. “Sue and I were on the phone for a long time last night, trying to find a way to convince you of the difference you’ve made.”

“I appreciate that you and Sue were talking,” Maddie said, but trailed off when Josh shook his head.

“I never said that Sue and I were talking to each other.”

“We’re gonna get through this together,” a woman said behind them.

Daniel watched Maddie perk up and then leap to her feet.

“Nicole?” she asked, sounding shocked. “Wait, is Sam…?”

“He’s okay, the police got there in time and I got home in one piece. Because of you,” Nicole said. “Hearing your voice kept me focused, gave me hope.”

Oh, Daniel could see what was going on now. Especially with the self-satisfied smile that crept across Josh’s face as another woman and her husband stood up and talked about how Maddie got them to help when the woman was unconscious and in labor. And then another man, who talked about how Maddie saved his life and helped get home for Christmas. And then another, who said Maddie saved his marriage, and his wife joined him to say she was literally on the edge until Norman-

Wait.

Hold on.

Something about this was very- Oh.

“You!” he said, dropping his croissant as he started to his feet. “You pointed a gun at my baby brother!”

The woman - Lola, if he remembered right - hid behind her husband, eyes going wide.

“Okay, simmer down, Rambo,” Josh said, lunging forward to grab his arm and tug him back down. “You’re ruining the moment.”

“Fuck the moment,” Daniel grumbled.

He didn’t stop glaring at Lola, however, not even as some woman named Gloria - a dispatcher Maddie told him about forever ago, who kept hanging up on people - came in to start extolling Maddie’s virtues, too. Daniel kept trying to kill the woman with his eyes right up until she scurried out of the cafe like a little roach, dragging Norman along behind her.

“She’s gone, Dan,” Maddie said, sounding both amused and annoyed with him. “You’ve driven the great evil away, you can relax now.”

“Why isn’t she in jail?” 

“Who knows,” Josh said.

When Daniel finally tore his eyes away from the cafe doors to look at his sister, he thought she looked… Lighter, maybe? Happier, definitely. Even if she had obviously been crying. He grabbed a napkin and handed it to her, which she immediately used to start dabbing at her eyes.

“I told you that you were selling yourself short,” he said.

Maddie smacked at his arm.

“Tomorrow night,” Eddie blurted out.

It was somehow the first time since their shift started that he managed to catch Buck alone. The fact that it was in the supply closet was as ironic as it was unintentional.

Buck looked up from the shelf he was going through, trying to find everything Hen had sent him to fetch for the ambulance, to simply stare at him.

“Our date,” Eddie clarified. "I’ll pick you up.”

“Does Shannon have Chris that night?” Buck asked, letting out a small cheer when he found what he was looking for. “Or is Carla pulling overtime?”

“No, I asked Abuela if she could.”

“And what does she think you’re doing while she has Chris?”

“Going on a date with you.”

Buck froze, and then slowly turned his head to face Eddie. He looked surprised and taken aback.

“You told your abuela?” he asked. Eddie just shrugged. “Huh. So, um. You promised me fancy and public.”

“Well, I couldn’t find anywhere too fancy on short notice,” Eddie said, feeling even more self-conscious than he already was. He’d never actually planned a date before. “But I- I didn’t want to wait too long.”

Buck nodded, as if he understood the unspoken part of the reasoning. Maybe he did. Maybe he was expecting Eddie to freak out and cancel at the last minute.

“You’re not taking me to Denny’s again, are you?”

“No, no Denny’s this time,” Eddie assured him. “I’ll save that for the second date.”

“Look at you, so certain there’ll even be a second date,” Buck teased. “You said it wasn’t anywhere too fancy, so what should I be expecting? Would a suit and tie be too much?”

“Nice shirt and pants?” Eddie shrugged. “There’s a wine menu.”

The other man let out a laugh, which made him feel better about things.

“Because a wine menu means the place is fancy.”

“Well, the options weren’t just red or white when I looked, so…”

The alarm ended their conversation and sent them running for the trucks, Buck dumping the supplies he’d been gathering in a pile on the nearest shelf to grab when they got back.

They arrived at Rose Avenue, the site of a car barreling through a crowd of people at a crosswalk. When they arrived, Chimney immediately began ordering them around, sending Buck and Eddie to check on the driver of the car. She was young looking, and clearly shaken up, the hood of her red car crumpled and smoking from the impact.

“Ma’am, can you hear me?” Eddie asked, unzipping and digging through his medical bag. On the other side, Buck was slipping into the passenger seat.

“I, um. I tried to stop, but I didn’t see any of them,” she said. “Are they okay?”

“They’re being taken care of,” Buck told her. “Right now, we need to see if you’re okay.”

“M- My neck hurts.”

“Okay, we’re gonna put this on you as a precaution,” Eddie told her, beginning to wrap a c-collar around her neck. “Until the doctors at the hospital can check you out.”

“There was a lady,” she said. “Is she alright?”

The question had Buck looking back to where Chimney and the others were working. Whatever he saw, it wasn’t good. Eddie figured that out from the set of his shoulders and the drop of his face and the way he whispered,

“Oh God.”

“Buck? What’s going on?”

Buck didn’t answer right away, and so Eddie turned to look for himself. His blood ran cold at the sight of just who Chimney and Hen were working on, and he pushed himself away from the car in that direction. He ignored the way Buck called for him, the way he tried to hold him back. Chimney stopped him just short of his goal.

“How bad is it?” Eddie demanded.

“Bad,” the interim captain answered.

“Spinal injury?”

“Maybe worse.”

Eddie shoved his way past and knelt next to where Shannon was lying awkwardly on the pavement. He took her hand, and her returning grip was weak. On the other side of her, Hen was checking her vitals.

“Hey,” Shannon said, voice barely above a whisper. “You’re here.”

“I’m here.”

“God, this is so embarrassing.”

“Vitals trending downward,” Hen announced as she tore off her stethoscope.

They got her on a backboard and then on a gurney, and from there loaded up onto the ambulance. Hen climbed in first, and then Eddie climbed in after with Chimney. They didn’t intubate yet, despite Hen saying they needed to, despite knowing what it meant. Because they also knew that once that tube went in, it would probably never be coming back out.

“I don’t feel anything,” Shannon told him, voice shaking. “That can’t be good right?”

Eddie silently shook his head.

“I’m sorry, I’m leaving again,” she said. “Eddie, Eddie listen to me. There’s- There’s a letter. For Chris. I wrote it after I left, I never sent it. I wanted him to know it wasn’t his fault.”

“I’ll find it,” Eddie promised.

Shannon set out a shuddering breath and the monitor tracking her vitals began to scream. Chimney gave the order and Hen began to intubate.

The tube went in.

It didn’t come back out.

At the hospital, once the doctors declared what they all already knew to be true, once she was moved to the morgue, he was given her belongings. Everything Shannon had with her when she was struck by the car, sans one single shoe. All of it tucked neatly away into a clear plastic package that he had to sign for.

Eddie clutched it as he went to the waiting room, where the others all stood up at the sight of him with a weighty silence. He clutched it when Buck stepped forward to put an arm around his shoulders, and when suddenly Bobby was there, too, wrapping him into a hug that felt nothing like his own father’s had. He clutched it the entire way back to the station, a trip that was a blur in his memories, and only set it down because he couldn’t hold it and change his clothes at the same time.

When he was done, he sat down on the locker room bench and picked the package up again. Simply stared at it, at what was inside of it, for he didn’t know how long.

“Eds?” Buck was kneeling in front of him. When had that happened?

“I have to tell Christopher,” Eddie whispered. “I don’t- I don’t know how I’m…”

“Do you want me to go with you?”

“Please,” he quietly begged, and heard his voice break on the word.

“I’ll handle Chimney.” Jones’ voice came from the locker room door, and for once the suddenness of it did not make Eddie jump. “I think I saw Hen go after him.”

“Thanks, Austin,” Buck said, though his blue eyes didn’t once move from Eddie’s face. “My jeep or your truck?”

Eddie wetted his lips, took stock of the way he felt so distant from himself.

“Your jeep,” he answered.

“Okay.”

Buck was still in his uniform, though that didn’t seem to bother him any. He simply grabbed Eddie’s bag and got him to his feet, gently herding him through the bay and out of the station. This trip was less of a blur, and after he told Buck where to go he took the time to call Abuela and tell her he was coming. To tell her why he was coming.

She was sitting outside on her porch when they arrived, Christopher with her. The boy smiled at the sight of his dad, all sunshine and cheer. Eddie knelt in front of him, Buck a comforting presence at his back, and said,

“Chris, your mom…”

Buck pushed the apartment door open, feeling the gloomy mood following him inside. After a few hours spent at Isabel’s, not the way he wanted to see her again, he ferried Eddie and Chris to the station to pick up Eddie’s truck, taking the opportunity to change into his civvies. Now that he was home, he toed off his shoes, tossed his keys onto the table by the door, and went to drop his bag by the washer for later.

The sight on the couch drew him to a stop.

“Uh, hi?” he said, peering down at Danny and Austin. More specifically, at the way the two were stretched out on the couch and cuddling, with Danny’s back against Austin’s chest.

“How’s Eddie and Chris?” Danny asked, neither of them making a move to untangle themselves.

“They’re… Doing,” Buck answered with a shrug.

“How are you doing?” Danny asked him, and then tilted his head up to tell Austin, “He knew her in school, too.”

Austin let out a silent “ah” as he nodded.

“Guess the cat was out of the bag after the brownies,” Buck grumbled, and then louder, “I don’t know. We were friends because of Eddie, and I’m pretty sure I hated her a little afterwards. Are we just gonna ignore whatever you two are doing right now?”

He watched Danny and Austin share a look and then the both of them shrugged.

“Okay then,” he said. “Whatever floats your- Oh, shit.”

On the TV, the news was talking about a mail bomb being sent to someone’s house. A second mail bomb, not the one the 118 responded to. Austin frowned and stretched his arm out for the remote so he could turn up the volume.

“A retiree attempted to open it,” the newscaster reported over scenes of paramedics rushing by the camera with a gurney. “That man, whose name is being withheld, fights for life at this very hour.”

Buck didn’t have to look at Danny or Austin to know they both shared his apprehension.

Chapter End Notes

I've had "What Sarah Said" in the playlist this entire time.

Chapter 35

Chapter Notes

Content Warning: the use of a (non-English) homophobic slur

Now

This was not how Buck wanted to see the rest of the Diaz family again.

Yesterday was Shannon’s funeral. Buck hadn’t been able to go, as much as he’d wanted to be there for both Eddie and Christopher’s sakes. But with the package bombs driving the city into a panic, and thus getting them bombarded with calls, the station simply couldn’t afford to be three men down. So he’d managed to postpone dealing with Ramon and Helena Diaz again, and he had a funny story - involving a bomb scare that turned out to be a little girl’s unwanted lunch - to tell both Eddie and Danny.

Now, though, they were having a… Post-funeral gathering? He didn’t think it was a wake, ‘cause he was fairly certain those happened before the funeral. It was basically just an excuse for all the Diazes to get together, much like graduations and birthdays and holidays. It was being held at Isabel’s, and while Buck didn’t have to go and wasn’t sure if he even wanted to, the old woman had somehow gotten hold of his phone number and pretty much demanded his presence.

He wanted to drag Danny along with him, because he would very much like to not suffer through this alone and it would be nice to have someone to hide behind, but his brother was busy. Hopefully with passing the last of his paramedic tests.

“Good, you listened,” was how Pepa greeted him when she answered her mother’s door, and then she frowned at the tupperware in his hands. “Did you bring food? To Mama’s house?”

Buck sheepishly held it out to her. He’d made sopapillas to bring over, because they were quick and easy to make, and also because being raised by WASPs left him with ingrained habits he might never break. The old woman peeked at what was inside, rolled her eyes, and took the tupperware from him.

“Out back,” she said, grabbing his arm and practically dragging him inside. “Everyone else is already here.”

“Yes, ma’am,” he said, because he knew better than to argue with her.

The backyard was crowded. There weren’t as many Diazes as there could be, mainly just Eddie’s parents and sisters, along with Pepa’s kids and grandkids. Still, though, they filled the space. Buck felt a bit self-conscious, the only thing keeping him from hesitating being the way Pepa practically shoved him out the back door.

Helena was closest, standing by the table that held the food. She turned and smiled in slight confusion at the sight of him. That smile slipped away as her eyes tracked over his face, pausing on his birthmark.

“Evan?” she said. “I didn’t realize you were in town.”

“Lived here for a while now,” Buck said.

“And he never once said hello,” Pepa complained, smacking him on the arm on her way to put down the sopapillas he brought. “Eddie!”

Eddie, standing by the grill with his father, his abuela, and one of his sisters, turned at the sound of his name. The smile that bloomed across his face was bright enough to rival the one his son usually wore. He waved and motioned for Buck to come over.

Buck paused, though, to check on Chris. The kid was sitting with a few of his cousins, his normal sunshine clouded over with gloom. While the others were playing cards and stuffing themselves, he was simply poking at the food on his plate.

“Hey, buddy,” Buck said, kneeling down beside his chair. Chris lifted his head, managing a small smile. “How are you holding up?”

The kid just shrugged.

“That’s what I figured. I’m sorry I wasn’t there yesterday.”

“It’s okay,” Chris said, voice quiet. “Dad said you had to work.”

“I got to see what happens when a tuna fish sandwich explodes,” Buck told him.

“Bet that was messy.” Eddie said, coming to stand beside them. He reached down to brush his hand over Chris’ hair. “Kinda glad I missed it.”

“I mostly feel bad for the school’s janitor,” Buck said. He stood up, spotted the plate Eddie was holding, and let out a happy noise. “Street corn!”

He went to grab one, only for Eddie to smack his hand away.

“They literally just came off the grill,” he scolded, and then pulled Buck into a one-armed hug. “Thanks for coming.”

“‘Course,” Buck said, and peered past Eddie as they separated. “Hi, Ramon. And… Adriana? Is that you?”

Ramon, like his wife, did not seem particularly pleased to see him. Once again, Buck was left with the feeling that the pair of them saw him and knew something. He just didn’t know what that something was.

“It’s me,” Adriana said, friendlier than her parents. Much friendlier: she was blatantly checking him out. “You grew up hot.”

“Are you saying I wasn’t hot before?” Buck teased.

“Fine, hotter,” she said, and then called for, “Sophia! Come look! It’s Evan Buckley, all grown up.”

“You stop that,” Isabel said, swatting at her granddaughter. She frowned at Buck. “You are much too skinny, still. Come, come, the food’s over here.”

“There’s a cooler with drinks, too,” Eddie said as they followed his abuela. He lowered his voice and added, “There’s beer. Y’know, for the sake of our sanity.”

“Chris is growing up way too fast,” Helena commented when Buck and Eddie reached the food table. She had a beer from the cooler, which she handed to Eddie after he sat down the platter of street corn. Buck couldn’t help but notice that there wasn’t one for him, and rolled his eyes as he dug one out. “He was so brave yesterday at the funeral.”

Buck frowned at the comment, pausing as he twisted off the cap of his drink. Why did Chris have to be brave at his mother’s funeral? If it had been Buck’s mother- No, wrong comparison. If it had been Danny’s funeral, or Maddie’s, Buck would have been the exact opposite of whatever Helena fucking Diaz considered “brave.”

(It was a toss-up whether he’d spend the entire funeral completely catatonic or wailing at the top of his lungs.)

“Bravest kid I know,” Eddie said, seemingly oblivious to the wrongness of his mother’s statement.

“Just like his father,” Helena said, carrying a pitcher of tea to the table Pepa was sitting at.

“His mom was pretty brave, too.”

“How?” Ramon demanded, standing at the other end of that table from them. “By running out on him?”

“Ramon,” Isabel scolded, carrying a plate to the seat next to her daughter.

“Papi, we’re not doing this,” Eddie said. He pulled out a chair as well and threw himself into it.

Buck leaned against the back of it, sipping at his beer and choosing to keep his mouth shut. If he started speaking, he ran the risk of agreeing with Ramon. And that would just be unacceptable.

“I apologize,” Ramon said, though he did not look sorry at all.

“Look, honey,” Helena said, taking a seat, too. “We know Shannon loved Christopher.”

“‘Course, Mom,” Eddie said.

“But she’s gone, and you’re a single father again,” she said. Buck’s fingers tightened around his drink, and he shook his head at Isabel when she opened her mouth. Now was not the time, even he knew that. Plus, that date got canceled, so technically… “And the hours you work, Eddie? Come home.”

“To El Paso?”

“Texas has fires, too,” Ramon said from his place at the head of the table, like this was his house. “You could join a department there.”

“He’s right,” Buck said. Eddie tilted back his head to frown up at him. “You should apply to Houston, it’s right on the Gulf.”

That frown turned into an amused grin, because Houston was just about as far from El Paso as Los Angeles was. Ramon and Helena knew that, too, and glared at Buck for the comment.

“Would be stupid of me to move now, though,” Eddie said, turning back to his parents. “Since I’m so close to earning my shield. Do you want me to just throw away the last year of my life?”

Helena and Ramon averted their gazes, and Buck was sure he made the realization at the same time Eddie did.

“That’s why you flew down here,” Eddie said. “Not for the funeral, but to bring us back. Was that the plan?”

“Yeah,” Helena eagerly admitted. “We could help you, and Christopher would be close to family-”

“Um,” Buck said. He awkwardly pointed at the very upset looking Isabel and Pepa. Maybe Helena had just forgotten that she was sitting across from her sister- and mother-in-law? In her mother-in-law’s backyard?

“Thank you, Evan,” Pepa said, glaring at Helena. “Helena needs reminding of these things, on occasion. Although I would think that my brother would help her remember.”

“We have a life here,” Eddie told his parents. “I won’t uproot him again.”

“Christopher hasn’t been here long enough to put down roots,” Ramon argued. “He spent the first six years of his life in El Paso, with us.”

“With you?" Buck blurted out. “Did you stop going on all those work trips after Chris was born?”

He should not have said that. He absolutely should not have said that. Even if it had Pepa making a poor attempt at hiding her laughter.

“This has nothing to do with you,” Ramon said, and if he was capable of it, he’d probably be vaporizing Buck with laser vision or something. And then he said, under his breath but still loud enough for Buck to hear, “Puto.”

Well, that answered that question.

“Ramon!” Isabel snapped. It was the angriest Buck had ever heard her sound. “Not in my home.”

“I’m gonna fix myself a plate and then go sit with the cool kids,” Buck announced, pushing himself away from Eddie’s chair. “Eds, you wanna come with?”

“Yeah.” His voice was rough with anger, but Buck could hear the hurt that it was covering up. Eddie paused as he stood, telling his parents, “Christopher and I are staying in Los Angeles. I chose this life for a reason.”

“You can choose another one,” Ramon said. His eyes flickered over to Buck. “A better one.”

“C’mon,” Buck said, tugging at Eddie’s shoulder. “I want some of this street corn before it all goes away. Oh! And your abuela made tamales.”

“Yeah,” Eddie said, still glaring at his father. “Okay…”

Buck managed to escape the Diazes late in the evening. He would’ve managed it earlier, but Chris looked him in the eye and asked, in the quietest and sweetest voice, if Buck would come home with them. And since Buck was merely human, and also had a weak spot the exact size and shape of one Christopher Diaz, he ended up following Eddie to his place.

If Ramon or Helena had anything to say about that, no words were uttered where Buck could hear.

After Chris was tucked into bed, and after an awkward goodbye and goodnight with Eddie, Buck slipped out and headed home. Danny was lounging on the couch when he got in, a bag of chips balanced on his stomach and some medical drama playing on the TV. His brother didn’t even look up at him, just lifted a hand and waved.

“How did it go today?” Buck asked him. The baseball bat was sitting by the door, and he grabbed it before walking all the way in. He looked around their apartment, trying to figure out where the best place was to hide it, not that it mattered with his brother right there.

“It went,” Danny said. “I’ll get the results in a few days. What about you? How was the Great Diaz Reunion?”

“Uh, well.” What to say, what to say… “It… Went?”

Danny lifted his head to look at him, and then frowned.

“Why do you have my bat?” he asked.

“For reasons.”

The frown morphed into a scowl.

“Buck,” his brother said, voice stern. “What happened? Do I need to murder Eddie?”

“No!” Buck was quick to say. “No, no. No murdering. Definitely not of Eddie. It’s just, uh. I always thought that his parents looked at me and just knew, y’know?”

“Okay…”

“And it turns out that I was right! Because his dad called me a slur,” Buck said. Danny silently stared at him, face going utterly blank. It was rather scary. “Not that I’ve never been called a slur before. I mean, there was this one time in Florida, I was making out with a drag queen outside of-”

“Buck,” Danny interrupted. He moved the chips and sat up, and then held out his hand. “Give me my bat.”

“Nope.” Buck shook his head. “I am not gonna do that.”
