“Buck. Give me my bat.”

“Nope!” He lifted the bat above his head, as if that would keep it out of his brother’s reach, and headed for his room. “The bat’s sleeping with me tonight!”

“Hey.” Buck kicked out, hitting Eddie’s shin beneath the table. The other man looked up from his phone with a scowl. Well, he’d already been scowling, that was why Buck kicked him. “What’s up?”

“Mom,” was the answer. “She’s still trying to convince me to move back to El Paso.”

It had been a couple days since the gathering at Isabel’s, and a couple days since Buck confiscated the bat to prevent Danny from murdering Ramon. Said bat was currently tucked away in his locker, because he figured that was a better place to keep it than anywhere at the apartment. 

“And your dad?” Buck asked.

Eddie gave a shrug.

“The same,” he said and then, after he glanced around to make sure no one was nearby, he lowered his voice and asked, “Do you think he knew? About…?”

They hadn’t really talked about what Ramon had said, not before now. It had remained an unspoken thing hanging between them, adding even more awkwardness to their interactions. In a lot of ways, Buck was left feeling as if they’d taken fifty steps back from the progress they had made. There had been no mention of another attempt at a date, though he didn’t know if that was because of Ramon or because of Shannon.

“I mean, I always figured they knew about me,” Buck told me. “Especially with the way they acted like Shannon’s biggest fans, our senior year. But I don’t think they ever knew about us. They wouldn’t have let me through the front door if they knew I was corrupting their baby boy.”

It was meant to make Eddie laugh. All the comment achieved, though, was a deeper frown. Eddie opened his mouth, though Buck never got to hear what he was going to say. The alarm rang out above them. They both scrambled to their feet and rushed for the trucks.

Somehow, Buck ended up sitting in the front seat of the ladder truck. Eddie was in the engine, with Hen and Chimney and Austin. It wasn’t often that Buck ended up separated from his normal group, and it wasn’t usual, but it happened sometimes. They couldn’t be picky about where they sat when they were hurrying to an emergency.

They were going through a tunnel when dispatch radioed them, saying they had Bobby on the line. That he had something important to tell them. Buck heard Chimney’s confused response over their shared channel, asking for dispatch to repeat.

And then the world exploded.

There was heat, there was fire, there was noise.

The next thing Buck knew, he was on the ground. He didn’t know how he ended up on the ground, or why he wasn’t in the truck. His head hurt and there was… Blood? Was he bleeding?

His headset was on the ground in front of him. How did he end up on the ground?

He was just in the truck.

His leg. There was something heavy on his leg.

Everything hurt.

His vision was blurry, with everything refusing to come completely into focus.

There was someone coming towards him, out of the smoke. A kid wearing some kind of vest and holding something in his hand. He came to a stop just in front of Buck, looking down and regarding him with detached curiosity, and said,

“You’re new.”

Chapter 36

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Now

The station was quiet, far too quiet. All the sounds that typically filled it, of people going about their assigned tasks or using the exercise equipment, all the chatter and laughter and even shouting, it was all absent. The station wasn’t empty, Daniel knew that. They hadn’t been sent out on a call, they’d actually had a bit of downtime.

But the station, and everyone in it, was far too quiet.

Curiosity was not a trait that his brother monopolized, and it did not take Daniel long to find where all of his coworkers had gathered. The den area of the building, with its couches and the TV and game system, was crowded. Every single person there was focused on the screen and none of them noticed he was there.

“Something interesting happen?” Daniel asked.

Mehta whirled around, eyes going wide.

“Shit, Buckley.” He glanced at the TV, then back to Daniel. “Shit, you shouldn’t-”

Daniel ignored him, because now that the captain had moved, Daniel could see what they were watching. A ladder truck on its side, people on the ground, smoke everywhere. There was someone pacing. But what caught his attention was the number emblazoned upon the side of the truck.

“Is that…?” Daniel stepped closer, dodged Mehta when he tried to stop him. He could feel everyone’s eyes on him. He lifted his hand, fingers hovering just over the screen. “What’s-?”

“If you’re just joining us,” the newscaster said, “witnesses are reporting that this LAFD ladder truck, belonging to station house One Eighteen, was hit by some kind of explosive as it was making its way to a call. Now, as you can see, there’s a firefighter pinned under that truck…”

Daniel was blocking the television, he knew. He also didn’t care. His phone was ringing, Maddie’s ringtone, and he didn’t care about that, either. All he cared about was his brother on the screen, trapped beneath the truck. All he cared about was his baby brother, right in front of him and yet so far away, hurting.

Daniel stood there, and he watched, because there was nothing else that he could do.

Eddie had wanted to run for Buck the moment the explosion happened. He’d tried to, in fact, when Austin pulled the engine to a halt and they all jumped out. He’d started to rush in that direction, only to be grabbed by multiple pairs of hands. Multiple, because it took all of them to hold him back.

Now they were huddling behind the engine. Austin had a firm grip on his arm like he was concerned that Eddie might try running again. It was a fair worry, Eddie could admit, except he also wasn’t an idiot. The moment he saw the kid pacing in front of the ladder truck, in front of Buck; the moment he saw the vest the kid was wearing and the trigger in his hand, Eddie knew.  

He wanted to run to Buck, to see how badly he was hurt, to reassure him everything was going to be okay. But he knew it wasn’t safe, not until the scene was cleared. And so, however much it was killing him, he stayed where he was and watched.

It did not take long for others to reach them, either. Police, bomb squad, FBI, ATF. They circled the 118’s trucks, the engine still standing and the ladder still smoldering, and set up a perimeter that Eddie wasn’t sure would be big enough. But none of them were doing anything to rescue Buck and the others, either.

It was frustrating for all of them.

“Dispatch, this is Captain One Eighteen,” Chimney said into his radio. “What is the play? I’ve got people dying in the street.”

“Hold your position, One Eighteen,” was the reply.

It was a reply that had Eddie grinding his teeth, because inaction was not something he was good with. He needed something, anything, that he could do instead of simply standing there and watching Buck squirm in agony where he was pinned.

“Get me the captain!” the little shit in the vest shouted. “Where’s the captain!?”

One of the guys that was thrown at the truck, Eddie couldn’t tell who it was with all the smoke and so much of his attention focused on Buck, started dragging themselves away to safety. The kid took offense to this, stomping up to the guy. Eddie wasn’t certain what the kid was going to do, but it seemed to be Chimney’s limit.

He stepped out from behind the perceived safety of the engine, ignoring the way everyone was telling him to stop, and approached the kid with his hands up.

“I’m the captain!” Chimney said, raising his voice to be heard. “I’m the captain, so please just let me help them, okay? Please.”

“No, no.” The kid in the vest shook his head. “I don’t want you. I want Captain Nash.”

That was problematic. Eddie wasn’t certain where Bobby even was right at that moment. Watching this play out from his apartment, maybe? From Athena’s house? And Bobby was suspended, currently just a civilian, would they even let him-?

“Freddie!”

Or Bobby could be right there, stepping out of the smoke. The kid - Freddie, apparently - turned to face him. Even Buck pushed himself up a little, twisting around as best he could to see the captain. His head swayed as he held it up, and the blood running down his face was even more visible now.

If it wasn’t for Austin’s grip on his arm, Eddie probably would have ran for him, to hell with what the kid might do.

“Thought you’d be on the truck,” Freddie said, voice just loud enough to carry over for Eddie and the rest.

“I’m here now,” Bobby responded as he approached them, arms held out to appear non-threatening. “It’s what you wanted, so what’s next?”

“I wanted you dead.”

“What about them?” Bobby asked, and then pointed at Buck. “What about him? His brother and sister, they’re probably watching this right now. He never did anything to you, he wasn’t even a firefighter when your father burned down that restaurant.”

“Collateral damage,” Freddie answered and in that moment, Eddie had never understood Danny more. What he wouldn’t do to have something in his hands right then, a baseball bat or a halligan, to bash that little shit’s head in.

“Is that how you see yourself?” Bobby circled the kid. Or maybe it was better to describe it as a spiral, since he got ever closer to him. “An unintended victim in all this?”

“Stop,” Freddie demanded, holding up the trigger. His thumb was already pressing down on it. Eddie’s heart leaped into his throat, because Freddie was right next to where Buck was pinned. “One more step, we all go boom.”

“You got dealt a bad hand, and I’m sorry about that,” Bobby told him. He actually sounded like it, too, which made him a significantly better person than Eddie, because Eddie wasn’t feeling sorry for the kid at all. Not now, not at this moment. “But what you did with it was your choice. You stopped being a victim the moment you left that first bomb.”

“That lawyer! She-”

“Did her job. We were all doing our jobs.”

“Destroying my family!” the kid shot back. “Mom and I lost everything! She was in so much pain.”

“Want to make it worse?” Bobby asked, nodding to somewhere behind Freddie. When Eddie looked, he saw a woman being escorted by Athena. “You want her to watch you die?”

Freddie turned to look and Bobby lunged forward, wrapping an arm around the kid and his hand around the trigger. The police, useless as they had been up to that point, finally swarmed in. There was a handover, and they were soon carting Freddie away, but Eddie didn’t care about that anymore.

All he cared about was getting to Buck.

Austin’s grip had loosened, and so Eddie was free to do what he’d been wanting to do since this whole thing started. He wasn’t the first one to Buck, Chimney was closer, but he didn’t take long to get there, sliding to a stop on his knees like he was back in high school and playing baseball again.

“Buck, how are we doing?” Hen asked, digging through her bag. Eddie’s own had been left, forgotten, by the engine.

“Kind of numb,” was Buck’s weak response. “Eddie?”

“I’m right here.” Eddie took one of Buck’s hands in his own, and used his other to feel at his pulse. He didn’t like what he found. “Skin’s cold and pale.”

“Running two lines,” Hen said. “And pushing sodium bicarbonate. Just hang in there, Buckaroo.”

“I want Danny,” was mostly whispered to the pavement as Buck lowered his head back down. Eddie ran his hand through Buck’s hair, uncaring of both the blood and anyone that was watching. Fuck them, and fuck whatever they might think.

“I know,” he said, not once stopping the motion of his hand in Buck’s hair. Not even when Hen put the c-collar around Buck’s neck. “We’ll get you out of this and then you’ll see him, okay?”

Behind him, Eddie could hear Chimney relaying updates over the radio, asking for assistance in getting Buck freed and for a path to be cleared to the nearest trauma center. He heard Bobby conferring with him, and then heard them start motivating people to help lift the truck. Other first responders, firefighters and police, gathered around them and jostled for a spot to grab hold of the truck.

Eddie started to his feet so he could help, but stopped as Buck’s grip on his hand tightened.

“Buck, I need to…” he trailed off when Buck still didn’t let go, instead gripped even tighter, and so he knelt back down beside him. “Okay. I’m right here, I’m not going anywhere.”

At Bobby’s word, they began to lift the truck. And Buck began to scream. He screamed and he screamed, one of the worst sounds Eddie had ever heard, until they gave up and lowered the truck back down. Not that they managed to lift it very far.

“You’ve got to lift higher!” Hen told them.

“One more time, guys,” Bobby said and began to count.

“Hang in there,” Eddie told Buck.

Bobby hit three, and the screaming began again. And still, they didn’t manage to lift the ladder enough for Buck’s leg to be free.

“She’s too heavy,” Bobby said, once their second attempt was a clear failure.

“We got anything on the truck we can use for leverage?” Eddie asked him, one hand still trapped by Buck’s grip and the other once again running through Buck’s hair. 

Buck himself was gasping for air.

“No.” Bobby shook his head. “We need more people.”

“I’ll radio again,” Chimney announced. “Dispatch, this is the captain…”

He trailed off. When Eddie looked up, he saw the man’s attention was caught on something off to the side of them. It was caught on the crowd of bystanders breaking the barricades to come help them. Eddie could only gape as these total strangers filled in any gaps they could. 

This time, when Bobby gave the count and Buck began to scream, Eddie could see the truck lift off his leg. He snaked his arm around Buck and pulled and tugged, until his foot was clear. The ladder truck, when they let it fall back to the ground, did so with a metallic groan and a resounding thud.

They worked together to get him onto the backboard, and from there onto the gurney and to the ambulance. Eddie’s hand left Buck’s only once, and Buck blindly grabbed in search of him again. Eddie didn’t let go of it again until he had to, when they were passing him off to the doctors.

The first thing Buck saw when he woke up was the fluorescent lights above him. The first thing he heard was Danny’s voice, saying,

“Whenever the horse stopped, which it did very often, he fell off in front. And when it went on again, which it generally did rather suddenly, he fell off behind.” Danny was sitting in a chair right beside the bed, a sleeping Chris in his lap, as he held open a copy of Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland and Through the Looking Glass that Buck recognized as the one he gave the boy for Christmas. Chris’ crutches were leaning against the side of the bed. “Otherwise he kept on pretty well, except that he had a habit of now and then falling off sideways. And as he generally did this on the side on which Alice was walking, she soon found that it was the best plan not to walk quite close to the horse.”

“I’m afraid you’ve not had much practice in riding,” Buck quoted. The words came out as a croak, and trailed off into a cough at the end.

Danny’s head shot up.

“Buck!” He closed the book, placing it onto the small table that sat beside the bed. “I need- I should text the others. Alma’ll kill me if I don’t let her know, and Maddie’ll help.”

“Tia Alma’s here?”

“She heard about the truck and booked a red-eye.”

The mention of the truck brought the reality of everything crashing back down. Buck’s eyes fell to his leg, casted up to his thigh and held in traction above a pillow. It didn’t hurt, something he attributed to the pleasant floaty feeling he was just noticing. But the sight of it, the memory, had his breath coming short.

“Okay, okay, no.” Danny adjusted Chris, so he could lean over and grab Buck’s shoulder. “It’s gonna be alright. You hear me? It’s alright. It’s gonna be alright.”

“Is it?” Buck asked him. “Did you talk to the doctor? Did he say anything about how the surgery went?”

“They put in a rod to hold the bone in place,” his brother answered. Buck tried to hide the disappointment at the answer, or rather at the lack of one, but knew it showed when Danny said, “You were expecting more.”

“When I got wheeled in, he said he didn’t know how it was gonna go,” Buck told him. “He was confident I’d walk again, but not… He didn’t know if I’d work again.”

“He didn’t say one way or the other,” Danny said. “But that’s a worry for later, okay? They won’t even let you get out of bed on your own yet, let’s focus on getting that far.”

In Danny’s lap, Chris began to shift. He yawned and raised a little fist to rub at his eyes, before blinking them open. When he saw Buck, his face broke into a smile made of pure sunshine.

“Buck! You’re awake!” he cheered. “We were reading to you, Danny said it made you feel better.”

“He was right,” Buck said, and tried for a smile of his own. It didn’t feel like a grimace, so he counted it as a win. “It did.”

Chris tilted his head back to fix Danny with a stern look, so similar to Eddie that it knocked Buk for a loop.

“Did you tell Dad yet?” he demanded to know. “You promised.”

“I know,” Danny said. “I was about to.”

“Is Eddie here?” Buck asked.

“He’s supposed to be getting us food and something to drink,” Danny answered. “Maddie and Alma went with him to supervise. Or just to torment him, I’m not sure which. Honestly, I kinda hope it’s the latter.”

That’s… That was probably not good, for neither him nor Eddie. But the floaty feeling kept that particular worry at bay for the moment.

Chris reached up to tug on Danny’s sleeve. Danny, in turn, looked down at him with raised eyebrows and asked,

“Yes?”

“I want to give Buck a hug.”

“Alright,” Danny said with a nod. “We’ve gotta be careful, though.”

Buck watched, feeling fit to burst with fondness, as Danny lifted Chris up and placed him on the bed, the pair of them taking care that Chris didn’t pull at anything important. And then Chris shuffled over, to wrap his tiny arms around Buck’s neck. Buck, because he wasn’t a heartless monster, hugged him back.

“Thanks, buddy.”

The door eased open, catching everyone’s attention. The first one through was Eddie, who paused, eyes going wide, before hurrying forward. He tossed a bag of skittles at Danny, sat a styrofoam cup on the table, and grabbed at Buck’s hand. His voice trembled with relief when he said,

“You’re awake!”

“So everyone keeps telling me,” Buck said, smiling up at him.

“You were supposed to tell us!” That was Tia Alma. When Buck turned his head, he saw her smacking Danny on the shoulder as Maddie looked on in amusement.

“He literally just woke up,” Danny said, raising his arms to shield himself. “I was about to tell you, I swear. Right, Chris?”

“I told him to tell Dad,” Chris said, tossing Buck’s brother under the bus. Danny squawked in offense.

“So.” Maddie was looking between Buck and Eddie, an eyebrow raised and lips curved into a smile. For some reason, Eddie was doing his best to avoid her gaze. “High school, huh?”

Ah.

Chapter End Notes

For a fun fact, I've had the image of Buck waking up after the ladder truck to Daniel reading Alice in Wonderland/Through the Looking Glass in my head since Spare Parts.

Chapter 37

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Now

There was a new ladder truck sitting in the bay when Eddie walked into the station. Or, well, it was new to the station, enough so that Chimney was still putting gear away in their proper compartments. He kinda doubted they’d give a brand new ladder truck to a station that had two of them break in as many months. Its cab was painted a bright white, which just wouldn’t do; not only did it stick out next to the engine and ambulances, but fire trucks were supposed to be red.

Still, though, he found himself letting out an appreciative whistle as he trailed a finger down its side.

“It’s shiny,” Eddie commented.

“It’s a loaner,” Chimney said.

“A loaner? For real?” 

“I’m kidding, we own it. But! With the caveat that we are not allowed to-” Chimney held up his hand and started ticking things off on his fingers. “Flip, crash, or blow her up any time soon.”

“But breaking the ladder itself is okay?” Eddie asked.

“I have no plans to dangle another shark from the end of it.” At the sound of Bobby’s voice, Eddie spun on a heel and spotted him - in uniform! - striding towards them. “I didn’t make any promises about the other things, though.”

“You’re back!” Eddie slid his work bag off his shoulder, letting it hit the floor, so he could give the man a hug.

“I am back,” Bobby confirmed.

“It’s kind of hard to fire a guy who was a hero on the six o’clock news,” Chimney pointed out. “Imagine the letters they would get.”

“Not to mention the fiery tweet-storm that’d rain down on their heads, starting with me.” Hen joined their little group, bypassing Chimney and Eddie to give Bobby a hug of her own. “Welcome back, Cap. About damn time.”

“Thank you,” Bobby said. “It feels great to be home.”

“Admit it,” Chimney broke in. “You miss Captain Han just a little.”

“No one misses Captain Han,” Eddie told him.

“Not even a little,” Hen said. “But things are finally getting back to the way they should be.”

Eddie’s eyes immediately slid to the empty space beside him and the glaring absence they were all avoiding, the gaping hole in their circle where Buck should be.

“Are they?” he asked.

Hen let out a sigh and amended her statement with,

“Almost.”

Getting out of the hospital was supposed to be freeing, but so far it had just made Buck exhausted. He grumped and grumbled and complained about being wheelchaired out, because what was the point of giving him crutches if they weren’t going to let him use them? But getting into Danny’s car was a feat of gymnastics that took about all he had. Getting out of the car, when they arrived at their apartment building, was much the same, simply in reverse.

And it turned out that hobbling along with the crutches took a lot of effort, actually. Especially when you weren’t really used to them yet. Christopher simply made it look easy.

“Okay, gimpy,” Danny said, pushing their door open. “Limp yourself to the couch. Try not to fall and break anything else.”

“Daniel, be nice,” Alma chided. She was in their kitchen, apparently using up all their tupperware. “Your brother’s hurt.”

“Yeah, Danny.” Buck paused in hopping his way across the room to stick his tongue out at his brother. “You hafta be nice to me, now.”

“I am always nice. Even when you’re lame.”

“I am not-” Buck paused, glancing down at his leg. “Oh my God! That is so rude.”

“If you were a horse at the Sunland Derby…”

“Rude.” Buck finished the journey to the couch, and then tried to figure out what to do with his crutches as he sat down. Danny solved that, taking them from him and then hovering nearby while Buck hopped on his good leg a couple times to back up to the cushions, lowering himself down with a grimace and a grunt. “I’m gonna tell Austin you were being mean to me.”

“Like Austin would care.”

Damn, Danny had a point there.

“Fine, I’ll tell Maddie.”

“She’ll just help me make fun of you.”

So rude! So hateful!

“Tia!” Buck whined, throwing his head back to look for her. “Danny’s being mean to me!”

“I swear, the calendar said it’s been nine years.” Alma shook her head. “Delia and Celia are graduating this year, so it must be right. But listening to you…”

“I was crushed by municipal equipment,” Buck said, gesturing at his leg where it stretched the length of the couch. “Why are you two being so mean to me?”

Danny scoffed as he set the crutches within Buck’s reach.

“I practically carried you out of my car, so I don’t think you have a leg to stand on.”

“I have exactly one leg to stand on!”

“If you two are done.” Alma sounded like she was trying not to laugh. “I put meals for the next few days in the fridge.”

“You didn’t have to do that,” Danny told her.

“Yes I did. I remember how you were, both when you first moved in next door and when he was gone,” she said. “I don’t trust you not to starve if he can’t make it to the stove.”

“I can make dinner,” he complained. At the synchronous looks of disbelief from both Alma and Buck, he rolled his eyes and said, “I can order dinner. And it’s not like I’d die of scurvy or anything. Pizza sauce is made from tomatoes, right?”

“And he had guardianship of me,” Buck told Alma, pointing at him. “It’s a miracle I lived long enough to be crushed by a fire truck.”

“Luckily for you both, the food in the fridge just needs reheating,” she said, pointedly turning away just as Danny wrapped an arm around Buck’s neck and started knuckling the top of his head. “And Isabel promised to make sure you were kept fed when that ran out.”

Danny and Buck stopped their little squabble to share a worried look.

“Isabel?” Buck hesitantly asked, unsure if he actually wanted the answer. “Eddie’s abuela, Isabel?”

“Si. I ran into her at the hospital, and we exchanged phone numbers.”

Oh, that was not good. Buck adored them both, but the idea of them being friends was a horrifying thing to consider. On the other hand, Isabel’s food was delicious, so maybe it would be worth it.

“What time’s your flight back tomorrow?” Danny asked. Buck figured he was eager to put a few hundred miles between the two women, before they joined forces to mother hen them together.

“Around ten,” Alma answered. “I’d stay longer, but the girls’ graduation is at the end of the week. Plus, I’m pretty sure they’re eating Iggy alive.”

Iggy - Ignacio - was Alma’s current boyfriend. They’d been together about a year now and just from the sound of him, from how Alma talked about him, Buck already liked him much more than some of the other guys she’d been with. Especially Marcos.

“It’s fine, I think we can manage without you,” Danny assured her. “I’m spending some of my vacation days to make sure this one doesn’t trip and die on the way to the toilet.”

“I can go to the bathroom on my own,” Buck complained. 

Danny stared down at him and crossed his arms.

“Alright,” he said. “Prove it.”

“...I don’t have to go right now.”

“That’s what I thought.”

“What are you writing?” Buck asked, craning his neck as he tried to see.

“Don’t-” Daniel started to say, and then spat the marker’s cap out of his mouth. “Don’t worry about it.”

“It’s my leg and my cast, I have every right to be worried,” his brother said. “Especially since we’re about to go to Eddie’s shield ceremony. Please don’t be drawing a dick.”

“I am not drawing a dick.”

They were sitting on the couch, Buck’s leg in Daniel’s lap. Both of them were dressed in their uniforms, complete with ties. Or at least Daniel was dressed; Buck’s shirt was hanging open and unbuttoned, and he didn’t have his pants on. Maddie was trying to fix a pair to put over the cast.

“Trying” being the operative word. She’d been using a pair of scissors to cut off the leg, but from the sound of fabric tearing, she had clearly lost her patience.

“Hey!” Buck’s worry switched from what Daniel was doing to Maddie. “Come on, be careful.”

“Oh, were you gonna sew these two pieces back together?” Maddie’s question was dripping with sarcasm as she finished removing the leg. “I don’t think so.”

“Doesn’t mean you had to rip them.”

Maddie lifted the pants up. All three of them grimaced.

“Uh-huh, looks terrible,” Buck complained.

“Yeah,” Daniel agreed. “I don’t think I ever want to go under your knife if that’s how the results look.”

“It’ll be fine,” their sister insisted. “We’re tucking it into the top of the cast anyway. Not that I even know why we’re taking you. You shouldn’t be on your feet, you need to be healing.”

“This is more important,” Buck said. “I promised both of them that I’d be there.”

Daniel let out a snort and shook his head.

“Eddie would understand. Christopher, too, I’m sure.”

“You know,” Maddie said, dropping the butchered pants in her lap. “I’d thought it was a coincidence that his first name was your middle name. Now that I know, I guess it kinda makes sense.”

Thanks to Alma not knowing better, Maddie now knew that Buck and Eddie had been friends in high school. Alma hadn’t let slip the fact that they were exes, though. Daniel wasn’t sure if Buck was grateful for that or not, they hadn’t really talked about it.

“His middle name’s Daniel,” Buck said, and Daniel looked up from his artistry so quickly he felt his neck pop.

“His middle name is what!?”

“That was pretty much my reaction.”

Daniel blinked at his brother, allowing that information to soak in.

“Huh,” was all he could think to say to that.

“And anyway,” Buck said, moving on from that particular revelation far sooner than Daniel felt ready for. “If I break anything else, they can just fix it with the other stuff.”

Maddie, who’d started using the scissors to trim the loose threads from her hack job, frowned.

“What other stuff?” she asked.

“They talked about him having another surgery,” Daniel answered, putting marker back to plaster.

“The doctor wasn’t happy with what he saw on the x-rays,” Buck explained, looking uncomfortable with the topic. “So we talked about him going back in. Replace the rod and do some bone grafts.”

“Do they think it’s a delayed union or a nonunion?” Maddie asked. Daniel looked up, realized she was looking at him as she asked instead of Buck, and frowned. “I mean, if it’s just healing slower than expected, waiting a few weeks couldn’t hurt.”

“I’m not waiting,” Buck told her, and the tone he used made it clear he intended for that to be the end of the discussion. “The sooner I have the surgery, the sooner I can go back to work.”

“So the doctor wants you to wait,” Maddie said. Daniel glanced between them, uncertain whether he should step in or not. “You should listen to him, okay? This is your health we’re talking about, your ability to walk. We’re talking about the rest of your life.”

“Being a firefighter is my life,” Buck told her. Daniel turned his frown on him. “I’m only useful for two things, Maddie. Being a firefighter is one of them, and without that…”

“That’s not true,” Daniel told him. Buck just shook his head and looked away.

“You’ll still be Buck, and we’ll all love you,” Maddie said. She wasn’t quite getting it, Daniel thought. She wasn’t quite understanding what this was about. Then again, Daniel wasn’t sure he did, either. “There are lots of other important things you can do with your life.”

Then again, again, it was clear he understood better than she did.

“It’s his body and his choice, Mads,” Daniel said, breaking in before this could become an actual argument. “If he wants to have the surgery, then we’re going to support him in that. Right?”

Maddie pursed her lips. She didn’t agree, but she also didn’t push, so Daniel declared it a win in his head.

“Alright,” he said, looking around for the lid to the marker. “I’m done.”

“What did you draw?” Buck asked, pushing himself up to see. “I’m serious, it better not be a- Oh.”

It was a (very poor) artist’s rendition of a Fragile: Handle With Care label. It came complete with what Daniel hoped looked like a wine glass with a crack running through it. He wasn’t sure if it actually came out that way, though.

“If anyone ignores the warning label,” he said, capping the marker. “I claim full rights to hit them with the bat.”

It was a declaration that earned him eye rolls from both his siblings, so at least he brought them to an accord about something.

The bay of Station 118 was set up for a fancy reception. The ambulances were parked outside, the trucks moved a bit farther apart to allow for two rows of tables, plus chairs. There was a longer table in the back with food that Buck hoped to God that Bobby had made. At least some of it.

Eddie’s immediate family, plus Pepa and Isabel, were in attendance. There was Carla, too, and everyone else on their shift plus some of their families. Sitting at Buck’s table - at the front and therefore closest to the entrance - was Danny and Maddie. There was also Chimney, on Maddie’s side, and Austin, on Danny’s.

Danny and Austin were actually sitting rather close, almost on top of each other, with the latter’s arm casually slung around the back of Danny’s chair. They were both ignoring the pointed looks Maddie kept throwing them, along with the confused and somewhat weirded out ones from Chimney.

Standing in front of them all was Bobby, in his full dress uniform, and Eddie, looking as handsome as always. Bobby was giving a speech, one about choice and duty to their fellow firefighters.

“Today we welcome into those ranks a new brother,” Bobby said, winding down. “After much hard work and dedication, I am proud to officially declare that your probationary period is at an end. Welcome to the Los Angeles Fire Department, Firefighter Diaz.”

“Thank you, sir,” Eddie said, shaking his hand.

Everyone applauded, and Buck heard Helena cheer behind him. Christopher got up from his chair, firefighter helmet in hand, and started making his way down the aisle between the tables. Buck’s heart melted at the sight. Eddie met his son partway, kneeling down to ask,

“What do you got for me?”

“I got your helmet.”

“Yeah?” Eddie was grinning, large and bright, as he took it from him and then wrapped his arms around the boy, lifting him up into a hug. “Thank you so much.”

The Diaz family swarmed him, as Buck figured they would, as Bobby squeezed past to start serving food. Ramon gave his son a perfunctory handshake and shoulder pat, and Buck couldn’t help but notice how blank faced Eddie was during it

After them, everyone else got up, too. Either to line up for food or for their own chance to congratulate Eddie. Buck waited, wary about his leg and crutches with the mass of bodies. He waited too long, apparently, because Eddie came to him.

“Congratulations, Firefighter Diaz,” Buck said with a grin, grabbing his crutches from next to him and leveraging himself to his feet. Or, well, foot, as the case may be.

“Thank you, Firefighter Buckley,” Eddie shot back.

Buck rolled his eyes, clutched his crutches beneath his armpits and went for a hug. Tried to, anyway, because Eddie stepped back suddenly.

“Eds?” he asked, feeling a bit hurt.

“I, uh.” Eddie reached out, took Buck’s tie between his fingers. As he watched, Eddie took a breath and straightened his shoulders, standing with his spine straight. “I promised you somewhere fancy, somewhere public.”

“Oh, yeah, I- Obviously I don’t expect you to…”

Eddie moved, dropping Buck’s tie so it fell back against his chest, and then he reached for Buck. Buck, in turn, let his words trail off and simply waited to see what he was going to do. He did not expect the gentle way Eddie’s palm cradled his face, did not expect the soft lips that met his.

Oh, Buck thought.

“Oh,” he said, when Eddie leaned back again.

They looked at each other, both of them silent and with Eddie on the verge of panicking. And then Buck surged forward, dropping his crutches so they hit the ground with a clatter. He gripped Eddie’s shirt, trusting the man to hold his weight as their mouths crashed together again. Eddie’s hands went to his hips, holding him steady.

There was a commotion somewhere nearby. Angry words - Ramon and Helena, Isabel and Pepa, even Danny and Hen - but Buck ignored it. Hoped that Eddie ignored it, too. He was kissing Eddie again, the rest of the world could go to hell.

(Later, he would learn how Danny and Hen worked together to almost literally throw Ramon out on his ass, with Isabel wagging a finger in his face the entire way. But that was later.)

 

Fin.

Chapter End Notes

While waiting for Part 3, have some homework: bone marrow and stem cell transplants are used to treat leukemia, like with Daniel. What health problems, dear readers, can disqualify a person from donating? =)
